This customary formality struck me as nothing extraordinary, but the aspect of the inn had something exceedingly chilling and forbidding in it, and I observed the men shut to the great yard-gates as soon as we were entered. Parties of French horsemen, the Captain said, were about the country, and one could not take too many precautions against such villains.
We went into supper, after the two sergeants had taken charge of our horses; the Captain, also, ordering one of them to take my valise to my bedroom. I promised the worthy fellow a glass of schnapps for his pains.

===Chinese===

如今我既是绅士，听这些故事也能带着钦慕听了：可就在三个星期之前，上一章末尾记的那层感想还压在我心头——记得那会儿我想的是：荣耀归大将，给小兵的只有侮辱和鞭子。
“对了，您的公文送给谁？”军官问。
这又是个不好回答的问题，我决意随口报一个；就说了“给罗尔斯将军”。这位将军我一年前见过，便把脑子里头一个名字抛了出去。我朋友全然满意，我们继续赶路，直到天晚；两匹马乏了，双方说好歇脚。
“有一家顶好的客栈。”上尉说，这时我们骑到一处我看去极冷清的地方。
“在德国这算顶好，”我说，“搁老爱尔兰可过不了关。科尔巴赫只差一里格：加把劲赶到科尔巴赫去。”
“您不想见识一下全欧洲最美的女人么？”军官说。“啊！你这滑头，我看这一条才动得了你。”说实在的——我也不怕认——这样的提议我一向欢迎。“这家人既是店主，也是种地的好手。”上尉说；那地方看去确实更像一座农场，不像客栈院子。我们进了一道大门，门里一圈围墙的场院，一头是房子——一处灰扑扑的破败所在。院里停着两辆带篷的大车，马在近旁棚下吃着草料，场院里晃着几个汉子，还有两名普鲁士军服的军士；两人都朝我那位上尉朋友碰了碰帽檐。这一套例行的客套我不以为奇，可那客栈的气象里有种说不出的阴冷拒人，我还注意到：我们刚一进院，那几条大门就叫人关严了。上尉说，法国骑兵一队一队在乡下游荡，对这班恶棍，怎么提防都不为过。
两名军士牵走我们的马之后，我们进去吃晚饭；上尉还吩咐其中一个把我的旅行袋拿到我卧室去。我许了这位值价的伙计一杯烧酒，谢他的辛苦。

===Original===

A dish of fried eggs-and-bacon was ordered from a hideous old wench that came to serve us, in place of the lovely creature I had expected to see; and the Captain, laughing, said, “Well, our meal is a frugal one, but a soldier has many a time a worse;” and, taking off his hat, sword-belt, and gloves, with great ceremony, he sat down to eat. I would not be behindhand with him in politeness, and put my weapon securely on the old chest of drawers where his was laid.
The hideous old woman before mentioned brought us in a pot of very sour wine, at which and at her ugliness I felt a considerable ill-humour.
“Where’s the beauty you promised me?” said I, as soon as the old hag had left the room.
“Bah!” said he, laughing, and looking hard at me: “it was my joke. I was tired, and did not care to go farther. There’s no prettier woman here than that. If she won’t suit your fancy, my friend, you must wait a while.”
This increased my ill-humour.
“Upon my word, sir,” said I sternly, “I think you have acted very coolly!”
“I have acted as I think fit!” replied the captain.
“Sir,” said I, “I’m a British officer!”
“It’s a lie!” roared the other, “you’re a *deserter*! You’re an impostor, sir; I have known you for such these three hours. I suspected you yesterday. My men heard of a man escaping from Warburg, and I thought you were the man. Your lies and folly have confirmed me. You pretend to carry despatches to a general who has been dead these ten months: you have an uncle who is an ambassador, and whose name forsooth you don’t know. Will you join and take the bounty, sir; or will you be given up?”
“Neither!” said I, springing at him like a tiger. But, agile as I was, he was equally on his guard. He took two pistols out of his pocket, fired one off, and said, from the other end of the table where he stood dodging me, as it were﻿—
“Advance a step, and I send this bullet into your brains!” In another minute the door was flung open, and the two sergeants entered, armed with musket and bayonet to aid their comrade.
The game was up. I flung down a knife with which I had armed myself; for the old hag on bringing in the wine had removed my sword.
“I volunteer,” said I.
“That’s my good fellow. What name shall I put on my list?”
“Write Redmond Barry of Bally Barry,” said I haughtily; “a descendant of the Irish kings!”

===Chinese===

一个丑老婆子来伺候点菜，端上来一盆煎蛋咸肉——全不是我指望见的那个可人儿；上尉笑着说：“行，饭食是寒素了点，可当兵的比这差的日子有的是。”随即郑重其事摘下帽、解下佩剑带和手套，坐下吃饭。我不肯在礼数上输他，也把我的家伙稳稳搁上那口旧五斗橱——他的就搁在那上头。
前述那个丑老婆子又端来一壶酸得厉害的酒；对着那酒、对着那丑，我心里升起老大一股无明火。
“您许我的美人儿呢？”老婆子一出屋我就说。
“呸！”他笑着，眼睛死死盯住我，“那是我的玩笑。我乏了，懒得再赶。这儿再没有比她俊的女人。她若不中您的意，我的朋友，您只好等一等。”
这下火上浇油。
“老实说，先生，”我板起脸，“我看您办得未免太冷！”
“我照我看妥当的办！”上尉回。
“先生，”我说，“我是英国军官！”
“放屁！”对方吼起来，“你是逃兵！你是骗子，先生；这三个钟头我早认出你了。昨天我就疑心。我的人听说瓦尔堡逃出来一个人，我就想是你。你那些谎和蠢话坐实了。你装着给一位死了十个月的将军送公文；你有个当大使的伯父，偏又不知道他姓甚名谁。先生，你是领赏投军呢，还是被交出去？”
“都不！”我说，猛虎似的朝他扑去。可我虽敏捷，他防备得也一般快。他从口袋里拔出两支手枪，放响一支，从桌子那头站着——像存心逗我——说：
“再上前一步，我叫这颗子弹进你的脑门！”话音未落，门被推开，两名军士端着上了刺刀的滑膛枪进来帮他们的伙计。
这局棋完了。我丢下手里的刀——那老婆子端酒进来时，早把我的剑撤走了。
“我自愿投军。”我说。
“这才是好小子。名单上给你写什么名字？”
“写‘巴利巴里的雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）’，”我傲然说，“爱尔兰诸王的后裔！”

===Original===

“I was once with the Irish brigade, Roche’s,” said the recruiter, sneering, “trying if I could get any likely fellows among the few countrymen of yours that are in the brigade, and there was scarcely one of them that was not descended from the kings of Ireland.”
“Sir,” said I, “king or not, I am a gentleman, as you can see.”
“Oh! you will find plenty more in our corps,” answered the Captain, still in the sneering mood. “Give up your papers, Mr. Gentleman, and let us see who you really are.”
As my pocketbook contained some banknotes as well as papers of Mr. Fakenham’s, I was not willing to give up my property; suspecting very rightly that it was but a scheme on the part of the Captain to get and keep it.
“It can matter very little to you,” said I, “what my private papers are: I am enlisted under the name of Redmond Barry.”
“Give it up, sirrah!” said the Captain, seizing his cane.
“I will not give it up!” answered I.
“*Hound!* do you mutiny?” screamed he, and, at the same time, gave me a lash across the face with the cane, which had the anticipated effect of producing a struggle. I dashed forward to grapple with him, the two sergeants flung themselves on me, I was thrown to the ground and stunned again; being hit on my former wound in the head. It was bleeding severely when I came to myself, my laced coat was already torn off my back, my purse and papers gone, and my hands tied behind my back.
The great and illustrious Frederick had scores of these white slave-dealers all round the frontiers of his kingdom, debauching troops or kidnapping peasants, and hesitating at no crime to supply those brilliant regiments of his with food for powder; and I cannot help telling here, with some satisfaction, the fate which ultimately befell the atrocious scoundrel who, violating all the rights of friendship and good-fellowship, had just succeeded in entrapping me. This individual was a person of high family and known talents and courage, but who had a propensity to gambling and extravagance, and found his calling as a recruit-decoy far more profitable to him than his pay of second captain in the line. The sovereign, too, probably found his services more useful in the former capacity. His name was Monsieur de Galgenstein, and he was one of the most successful of the practisers of his rascally trade.

===Chinese===

“我从前在爱尔兰旅，罗什旅，干过这行，”募兵贩子冷笑，“想在旅里你那几个同胞中间捞几个像样的小子——那里面没有一个不是爱尔兰国王的后裔。”
“先生，”我说，“为王的也罢，不为王的也罢，我是绅士，您瞧得见。”
“哦！我们团队里您这样的还多得很。”上尉答，还是那副冷笑。“交出您的证件，绅士先生，看看您到底是谁。”
我的皮夹子里除了费克纳姆先生的文件还有几张钞票，我不愿交出我的财产；而且疑心得很对——这不过是上尉捞一把、昧下来的算计。
“我的私人证件于您无足轻重，”我说，“我以雷德蒙·巴里之名入伍。”
“交出来，小子！”上尉抓起手杖。
“我不交！”我答。
“狗东西！你要造反？”他尖叫着，一杖抽在我脸上；这一下正等着制造一场扭打。我抢上前去揪他，两名军士扑到我身上，我被掼倒在地，又昏了过去——正打在头上旧伤上。醒过来时血淌得很凶，镶绦子的军衣早叫人从我背上剥去，钱袋证件没了，双手反绑在身后。
那位伟大辉煌的腓特烈，王国四境边上豢养着几十个这等白奴贩子：腐蚀军队也罢，绑架农夫也罢，什么罪行都不皱眉，只为他那些光辉的团队添炮灰。那恶棍，践踏友谊与江湖的一切道义、方才把我诱进了罗网——他的下场，我在这里带着几分快意交代一句。此人出身高门，才具胆识有名，就是嗜赌，挥霍；干这勾兵的行当，远比他当步兵上尉那份薪俸进项丰，于是他就干了。君王那边，多半也觉得他干前一行更为有用。他名叫加尔根施泰因先生，是这行无耻营生里最得手的角色之一。

===Original===

He spoke all languages, and knew all countries, and hence had no difficulty in finding out the simple braggadocio of a young lad like me.
About 1765, however, he came to his justly merited end. He was at this time living at Kehl, opposite Strasburg, and used to take his walk upon the bridge there, and get into conversation with the French advanced sentinels; to whom he was in the habit of promising “mountains and marvels,” as the French say, if they would take service in Prussia. One day there was on the bridge a superb grenadier, whom Galgenstein accosted, and to whom he promised a company, at least, if he would enlist under Frederick.
“Ask my comrade yonder,” said the grenadier; “I can do nothing without him. We were born and bred together, we are of the same company, sleep in the same room, and always go in pairs. If he will go and you will give him a captaincy, I will go too.”
“Bring your comrade over to Kehl,” said Galgenstein, delighted. “I will give you the best of dinners, and can promise to satisfy both of you.”
“Had you not better speak to him on the bridge?” said the grenadier. “I dare not leave my post; but you have but to pass, and talk over the matter.”
Galgenstein, after a little parley, passed the sentinel; but presently a panic took him, and he retraced his steps. But the grenadier brought his bayonet to the Prussian’s breast and bade him stand: that he was his prisoner.
The Prussian, however, seeing his danger, made a bound across the bridge and into the Rhine; whither, flinging aside his musket, the intrepid sentry followed him. The Frenchman was the better swimmer of the two, seized upon the recruiter, and bore him to the Strasburg side of the stream, where he gave him up.
“You deserve to be shot,” said the general to him, “for abandoning your post and arms; but you merit reward for an act of courage and daring. The King prefers to reward you,” and the man received money and promotion.
As for Galgenstein, he declared his quality as a nobleman and a captain in the Prussian service, and applications were made to Berlin to know if his representations were true. But the King, though he employed men of this stamp (officers to seduce the subjects of his allies) could not acknowledge his own shame.

===Chinese===

他各国话都通，各国都到过，所以不难识破我这样一个小年轻那点可怜的牛皮。
不过到1765年上下，他得了他应得的下场。其时他住在凯尔，斯特拉斯堡对岸，惯常在桥头散步，同法国前哨的哨兵攀谈；惯常对他们许“金山银山、奇遇奇迹”——用法国人的话说——只要肯到普鲁士当兵。一天，桥上站着一个威武的掷弹兵，加尔根施泰因凑上去，许他至少一个连长，只要他到腓特烈麾下入伍。
“问那边的我伙计，”掷弹兵说，“没他我不行。我们生在一处长在一处，同一个连，睡同一间屋，走道儿从来成双。他若去，您又肯给他一个上尉，我也去。”
“把你伙计带到凯尔来，”加尔根施泰因喜出望外，“我请你们吃顶好的饭，保管叫你们两个都满意。”
“您在桥上同他说不好么？”掷弹兵说，“我不敢离哨；您只消走过来，把话说了就是。”
加尔根施泰因略作寒暄，跨过了哨兵；可立刻一阵恐慌袭来，他又往回走。掷弹兵却把刺刀顶住普鲁士人的胸口，叫他站住：他是他的俘虏了。
那普鲁士人眼见危险，一纵身越过桥栏跳进莱茵河；那位无畏的哨兵掠下枪，也跟着跳了下去。法国人水性更好，揪住募兵贩子，把他拖到斯特拉斯堡一岸，交了上去。
“你弃哨弃枪，”将军对那哨兵说，“本该枪毙；可你这一勇敢大胆之举，又该受赏。国王选择奖你。”那士兵得了钱，升了职。
至于加尔根施泰因，他申明自己是贵族、普军上尉；斯特拉斯堡行文柏林，查问他的话虚实。国王虽豢养这号人物（派去勾引盟邦子民的军官），自己的丑却认不得。

===Original===

Letters were written back from Berlin to say that such a family existed in the kingdom, but that the person representing himself to belong to it must be an impostor, for every officer of the name was at his regiment and his post. It was Galgenstein’s death-warrant, and he was hanged as a spy in Strasburg.
---
“Turn him into the cart with the rest,” said he, as soon as I awoke from my trance.

===Chinese===

柏林回文说：王国里确有这样一家人家；但自称出于这家的，必是冒牌，因为这一姓的军官个个都在团队任上。这等于加尔根施泰因的死刑判决书；他在斯特拉斯堡以间谍罪绞死了。
---
我刚从昏迷里醒转，他就说：“把他也扔上车，同其余的一起。”

## VII Barry Leads a Garrison Life, and Finds Many Friends There / 七 巴里过起驻防生活，在那儿交了许多朋友
===Original===

After the war our regiment was garrisoned in the capital, the least dull, perhaps, of all the towns of Prussia: but that does not say much for its gaiety. Our service, which was always severe, still left many hours of the day disengaged, in which we might take our pleasure had we the means of paying for the same. Many of our mess got leave to work in trades; but I had been brought up to none: and besides, my honour forbade me; for as a gentleman, I could not soil my fingers by a manual occupation. But our pay was barely enough to keep us from starving; and as I have always been fond of pleasure, and as the position in which we now were, in the midst of the capital, prevented us from resorting to those means of levying contributions which are always pretty feasible in wartime, I was obliged to adopt the only means left me of providing for my expenses: and in a word became the *Ordonnanz*, or confidential military gentleman, of my captain. I spurned the office four years previously, when it was made to me in the English service; but the position is very different in a foreign country; besides, to tell the truth, after five years in the ranks, a man’s pride will submit to many rebuffs which would be intolerable to him in an independent condition.
The captain was a young man and had distinguished himself during the war, or he would never have been advanced to rank so early. He was, moreover, the nephew and heir of the Minister of Police, Monsieur de Potzdorff, a relationship which no doubt aided in the young gentleman’s promotion. Captain de Potzdorff was a severe officer enough on parade or in barracks, but he was a person easily led by flattery. I won his heart in the first place by my manner of tying my hair in queue (indeed, it was more neatly dressed than that of any man in the regiment), and subsequently gained his confidence by a thousand little arts and compliments, which as a gentleman myself I knew how to employ. He was a man of pleasure, which he pursued more openly than most men in the stern Court of the King; he was generous and careless with his purse, and he had a great affection for Rhine wine: in all which qualities I sincerely sympathised with him; and from which I, of course, had my profit.

===Chinese===

战后，我们团驻防在都城——普鲁士各镇里，它算最不乏味的了：可这也道不出它多少热闹。我们的军务素来严酷，倒仍有一日许多钟头闲着，只要有钱，尽可去寻欢作乐。伙食团里许多人请了假去做营生；可我什么手艺也没学过；再说，我的体面也不许：身为绅士，我岂能叫手艺脏了我的手指。可我们的钧银只够饿不死；我一向贪图享乐，如今驻在京城的地处，又断绝了战时向来行得通的打秋丰的路数，我只得掠剩下的唯一一条路子应付开销：一句话，当了我上尉的亲随武弁（Ordonnanz）——心腹的军中听差。四年前在英国军中，这差事给我我是不屑的；可在异国，处境大不相同；再说句实话，在行伍里混过五年，人的傲气也肯受许多委屈了——若在自立门户的光景里，这些委屈他一刻也受不了。
上尉是个年轻人，战争中出过风头，不然升迁断不至这样早。他又是警务大臣波茨多夫先生的侄子兼继承人，这层关系无疑帮了这位年轻绅士的晋升。波茨多夫上尉在操场上、营房里是够严的军官，可他吃捧。我先凭我打辫子的手法赢得他的心（说真的，全团没有谁的头梳得比我更齐整），随后又用上千样小手腕和恭维，渐渐得了他的信任——这些手段，我自己既是绅士，自然懂得使。他是个寻欢作乐的人，在国王那冷峻的宫廷里，他行乐比多数人放得开；出手阔绰，银钱上不留心；又极爱莱茵酒：这几样品性我都真心同他共鸣；当然，我也从中得我的利。

===Original===

He was disliked in the regiment, because he was supposed to have too intimate relations with his uncle the Police Minister; to whom, it was hinted, he carried the news of the corps.
Before long I had ingratiated myself considerably with my officer, and knew most of his affairs. Thus I was relieved from many drills and parades, which would otherwise have fallen to my lot, and came in for a number of perquisites; which enabled me to support a genteel figure and to appear with some éclat in a certain, though it must be confessed very humble, society in Berlin. Among the ladies I was always an especial favourite, and so polished was my behaviour amongst them, that they could not understand how I should have obtained my frightful nickname of the Black Devil in the regiment. “He is not so black as he is painted,” I laughingly would say; and most of the ladies agreed that the private was quite as well-bred as the captain: as indeed how should it be otherwise, considering my education and birth?
When I was sufficiently ingratiated with him, I asked leave to address a letter to my poor mother in Ireland, to whom I had not given any news of myself for many many years; for the letters of the foreign soldiers were never admitted to the post, for fear of appeals or disturbances on the part of their parents abroad. My captain agreed to find means to forward the letter, and as I knew that he would open it, I took care to give it him unsealed; thus showing my confidence in him. But the letter was, as you may imagine, written so that the writer should come to no harm were it intercepted. I begged my honoured mother’s forgiveness for having fled from her; I said that my extravagance and folly in my own country I knew rendered my return thither impossible; but that she would, at least, be glad to know that I was well and happy in the service of the greatest monarch in the world, and that the soldier’s life was most agreeable to me: and, I added, that I had found a kind protector and patron, who I hoped would some day provide for me as I knew it was out of her power to do. I offered remembrances to all the girls at Castle Brady, naming them from Biddy to Becky downwards, and signed myself, as in truth I was, her affectionate son, Redmond Barry, in Captain Potzdorffs company of the Bülowisch regiment of foot in garrison at Berlin.

===Chinese===

团里人不喜欢他，因为他同他那位警务大臣伯父过从太密，据说——人们这么喃咕——团里的动静都由他递给伯父。
不久我在我军官跟前大大得宠，他的事我知晓了大半。于是许多本该派到我头上的操练和检阅都免了，还沾了不少外快；这使我能撑起一副体面身架，在柏林（Berlin）——得承认，很寒微的——某一个圈子里，出入也算有点声光。太太小姐堆里我向来是特别的宠儿，我在她们中间举止那样文雅，她们全不解我怎么会在团里落下“黑魔鬼”那样吓人的诨名。“他不像画的那样黑。”我总笑着说；多数太太也承认：这小兵的教养不输上尉；想想我的出身和教养，这还能是别的样么？
我在他跟前宠得够了，便请准给爱尔兰（Ireland）我可怜的母亲去一封信；许多许多年没给她我的音讯了。外国兵的信是从来进不了邮路的，就怕他们在国外的父母闹出呈控或乱子。我的上尉答应设法转递；我知道他必拆看，索性不封口交给他，示我以信任。可那封信，诸位想得到，写得就算中途失落也伤不着写信人一根毫毛。我求慈母恕我不辞而别；我说，我在本国的荒唐和蠢行，我知道叫我再无颜回去；可她至少可以高兴：我在天下最伟大的君王麾下当差，安然快活，大兵的日子于我再合意不过；我又添上一句：我遇着一位仁厚的保护人和恩主，指望他有一天会替我挣个出身——这一层我知道她是无力的。我向布雷迪堡的姑娘们一一致意，从比迪到贝基一个个点名，落款署的我确实的身份：她的爱子雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry），驻柏林布洛维奇步兵团波茨多夫上尉连。

===Original===

Also I told her a pleasant story about the King kicking the Chancellor and three judges downstairs, as he had done one day when I was on guard at Potsdam, and said I hoped for another war soon, when I might rise to be an officer. In fact, you might have imagined my letter to be that of the happiest fellow in the world, and I was not on this head at all sorry to mislead my kind parent.
I was sure my letter was read, for Captain Potzdorff began asking me some days afterwards about my family, and I told him the circumstances pretty truly, all things considered. I was a cadet of a good family, but my mother was almost ruined and had barely enough to support her eight daughters, whom I named. I had been to study for the law at Dublin, where I had got into debt and bad company, had killed a man in a duel, and would be hanged or imprisoned by his powerful friends, if I returned. I had enlisted in the English service, where an opportunity for escape presented itself to me such as I could not resist; and hereupon I told the story of Mr. Fakenham of Fakenham in such a way as made my patron to be convulsed with laughter, and he told me afterwards that he had repeated the story at Madame de Kamake’s evening assembly, where all the world was anxious to have a sight of the young Englander.
“Was the British Ambassador there?” I asked, in a tone of the greatest alarm, and added, “For Heaven’s sake, sir, do not tell my name to him, or he might ask to have me delivered up: and I have no fancy to go to be hanged in my dear native country.” Potzdorff, laughing, said he would take care that I should remain where I was, on which I swore eternal gratitude to him.
Some days afterwards, and with rather a grave face, he said to me, “Redmond, I have been talking to our colonel about you, and as I wondered that a fellow of your courage and talents had not been advanced during the war, the general said they had had their eye upon you: that you were a gallant soldier, and had evidently come of a good stock; that no man in the regiment had had less fault found with him; but that no man merited promotion less. You were idle, dissolute, and unprincipled; you had done a deal of harm to the men; and, for all your talents and bravery, he was sure would come to no good.”
“Sir!”

===Chinese===

我还讲了一段趣事给她听：国王一脚把大法官和三位法官踢下楼梯——我在波茨坦站岗那天他真这么干过——又说巴望再打一仗，好升我做军官。总之，你尽可把我的信当作天下头号乐天派写的；在这一头上骗骗我慈祥的母亲，我心里毫无歉意。
我断定信被读过了，因为几天后波茨多夫上尉问起我的家世，我便把实情原原本本——方方面面掂量过——告诉了他。我是个好人家的小儿子，母亲几乎破产，勉强养活她的八个女儿，名字我一一报了。我在都柏林（Dublin）学过法律，在那里欠了债、交了坏朋友，一场决斗杀了个人；我若回去，他那些有势力的朋友不绞死我也得关死我。我投了英军；一个抵挡不住的机会放我脱了身——说到这里，我把费克纳姆先生那段故事讲了一版，讲得我恩主笑得直不起腰；他后来说，他在卡马凯夫人的晚会上照讲了一遍，满场的人都急着要见识见识我这个年轻的英国人。
“英国大使当时在场么？”我带着最大的惊惶问，又添一句：“看在老天份上，先生，千万别把我的名字告诉他，不然他兴许要求把我交出去：我可没兴致回亲爱的祖国去上绞架。”波茨多夫笑着应承，保管我留在原地；我便对他赌了永世的感激。
几天之后，他脸色相当郑重地对我说：“雷德蒙，我就你同我们上校谈了谈；我纳闷，你这样有胆有才的家伙，战争中怎么没得升迁。将军说，他们早留意你了：你是个勇兵，显然出身良家；团里数你受的挑剔最少；可也数你最不该升。你懒愒、放荡、没原则；你把兵带坏了不少；你虽有才有勇，他断言你成不了好事。”
“先生！”

===Original===

said I, quite astonished that any mortal man should have formed such an opinion of me, “I hope General Bülow is mistaken regarding my character. I have fallen into bad company, it is true; but I have only done as other soldiers have done; and, above all, I have never had a kind friend and protector before, to whom I might show that I was worthy of better things. The general may say I am a ruined lad, and send me to the d﻿—﻿—﻿l: but be sure of this, I would go to the d﻿—﻿—﻿l to serve *you*.” This speech I saw pleased my patron very much; and, as I was very discreet and useful in a thousand delicate ways to him, he soon came to have a sincere attachment for me. One day, or rather night, when he was tête-à-tête with the lady of the Tabaks Rath von Dose for instance, I﻿—But there is no use in telling affairs which concern nobody now.
Four months after my letter to my mother, I got, under cover to the Captain, a reply, which created in my mind a yearning after home, and a melancholy which I cannot describe. I had not seen the dear soul’s writing for five years. All the old days, and the fresh happy sunshine of the old green fields in Ireland, and her love, and my uncle, and Phil Purcell, and everything that I had done and thought, came back to me as I read the letter; and when I was alone I cried over it, as I hadn’t done since the day when Nora jilted me. I took care not to show my feelings to the regiment or my captain: but that night, when I was to have taken tea at the Garden-house outside Brandenburg Gate, with Fraulein Lottchen (the Tabaks Rathinn’s gentlewoman of company), I somehow had not the courage to go; but begged to be excused, and went early to bed in barracks, out of which I went and came now almost as I willed, and passed a long night weeping and thinking about dear Ireland.
Next day, my spirits rose again and I got a ten-guinea bill cashed, which my mother sent in the letter, and gave a handsome treat to some of my acquaintance. The poor soul’s letter was blotted all over with tears, full of texts, and written in the wildest incoherent way. She said she was delighted to think I was under a Protestant prince, though she feared he was not in the right way: that right way, she said, she had the blessing to find, under the guidance of the Reverend Joshua Jowls, whom she sat under.

===Chinese===

我叫道，任何一个凡人竟对我形成了这样的见解，实在叫我愕然，“我希望比洛将军看错了我的品行。我交了坏朋友，不假；可我干的不过是别的兵也干的；尤其，我从没有过一位仁厚的朋友和保护人，好叫我证明自己配得上更好的东西。将军尽可以说我是个毁了的小子，把我打发去见鬼；可有一样您放明白：为您效劳，见鬼我也去。”这番话我看很中恩主的意；加上我在上千件微妙事上对他那样知趣、那样有用，他不久当真对我生出一份真感情。譬如有一天——不如说一夜——他正同烟草顾问冯·多泽太太两个对坐密谈，我——可如今讲这些同谁都不相干的事，没有用处。
给我母亲去信四个月后，我经上尉转手收到一封回信；它在我心里勾起的乡愁和伤感，我没法描画。这可爱的老人的笔迹，我五年没见了。旧日的一切，爱尔兰旧日绿野上鲜亮快活的太阳，她的爱，我舅舅，菲尔·珀塞尔，我做过想过的一切，读信时光全回来了；独自一人时，我对着信哭了——自诺拉甩了我那天起，我不曾这样哭过。我留神不在团里、不在上尉跟前露这心思：可当夜本该去勃兰登堡门外花园馆子同小洛特欣小姐（那位烟草顾问夫人的伴娘）喝茶的，我竟没了勇气去；告了假，早早回营房睡下——那营房我如今进出几乎随心——哭一阵想一阵，把大半夜交给了亲爱的爱尔兰。
第二天我的兴致又回来了：我把母亲随信寄来的一张十几尼的汇票兑了，请我相熟的一些人阔气地乐了一场。那可怜人的信叫泪水洇得一塌糊涂，满纸圣经经文，写得颠三倒四。她说，想到我在一位新教君王麾下，她欢喜；只怕那位君王走的路子不正——那正路，她说，托上帝的福，她已在可敬的乔舒亚·焦尔斯牧师指导之下寻着了；她就在他座下听道。

===Original===

She said he was a precious chosen vessel; a sweet ointment and precious box of spikenard; and made use of a great number more phrases that I could not understand; but one thing was clear in the midst of all this jargon, that the good soul loved her son still, and thought and prayed day and night for her wild Redmond. Has it not come across many a poor fellow, in a solitary night’s watch, or in sorrow, sickness, or captivity, that at that very minute, most likely, his mother is praying for him? I often have had these thoughts; but they are none of the gayest, and it’s quite as well that they don’t come to you in company; for where would be a set of jolly fellows then?﻿—as mute as undertakers at a funeral, I promise you. I drank my mother’s health that night in a bumper, and lived like a gentleman whilst the money lasted. She pinched herself to give it me, as she told me afterwards; and Mr. Jowls was very wroth with her. Although the good soul’s money was very quickly spent, I was not long in getting more; for I had a hundred ways of getting it, and became a universal favourite with the Captain and his friends. Now, it was Madame von Dose who gave me a Frederic-d’or for bringing her a bouquet or a letter from the Captain; now it was, on the contrary, the old Privy Councillor who treated me with a bottle of Rhenish, and slipped into my hand a dollar or two, in order that I might give him some information regarding the liaison between my captain and his lady. But though I was not such a fool as not to take his money, you may be sure I was not dishonourable enough to betray my benefactor; and he got very little out of *me*. When the Captain and the lady fell out, and he began to pay his addresses to the rich daughter of the Dutch Minister, I don’t know how many more letters and guineas the unfortunate Tabaks Rathinn handed over to me, that I might get her lover back again. But such returns are rare in love, and the Captain used only to laugh at her stale sighs and entreaties. In the house of Mynheer Van Guldensack I made myself so pleasant to high and low, that I came to be quite intimate there: and got the knowledge of a state secret or two, which surprised and pleased my captain very much.

===Chinese===

她说他是重价掠选的器皿；是一盒真哪哒香膏，一只贵重的玉瓶；还用了许许多多我不懂的词；可这一片胡话当中有一件事清楚：这好人仍然爱她的儿子，日日夜夜为她野性的雷德蒙挂念、祈祷。多少可怜人，在孤零零的一班夜哨里，在忧患、疾病、监禁中，岂不都忽然想到：多半就在此刻，他的母亲正为他祈祷？这样的念头我常有；可它们一点也不快活，幸亏它们不赶上你正同人在一处；不然一伙乐呵呵的汉子该成什么样？——我告诉你，哑得同出殓的杠夫一样。那晚我满满一杯为母亲的健康干了，趁手头有钱，活得像个绅士。那笔钱是她从牙缝里省给我的——她后来这样告诉我；焦尔斯先生为这事对她好一顿恼。好人的这笔钱花完得很快，可我捾钱也不慢；捾钱的门路我有上百样，上尉同他的朋友们把我当作通用的宠儿。这一阵是多泽太太，为替她捎一束花、一封上尉的信，赏我一枚腓特烈金币；那一阵反过来，是老枢密顾问请我喝一瓶莱茵酒，塞我一两块银元，要我给他通点消息，关于我上尉同他太太的私情。可我虽不至于蠢到不收他的钱，诸位放心，我还不至于不体面到出卖我的恩主；他从来从我这里捞不着东西。等到上尉同那位太太闹翻，转而去向荷兰公使的阔小姐献殷勤，那倒霉的烟草顾问夫人又不知塞给我多少封信、多少几尼，求我把她的情郎弄回来。可爱情里这样的回头是稀罕的；上尉只把她那隔年的叹息和哀告当笑话。在范·古尔登萨克老爷家里，我上上下下伺候得那样可意，竟成了那里的熟客：还探得一两桩国家机密，叫我上尉又惊又喜。

===Original===

These little hints he carried to his uncle, the Minister of Police, who, no doubt, made his advantage of them; and thus I began to be received quite in a confidential light by the Potzdorff family, and became a mere nominal soldier, being allowed to appear in plain clothes (which were, I warrant you, of a neat fashion), and to enjoy myself in a hundred ways, which the poor fellows my comrades envied. As for the sergeants, they were as civil to me as to an officer: it was as much as their stripes were worth to offend a person who had the ear of the Minister’s nephew. There was in my company a young fellow by the name of Kurz, who was six feet high in spite of his name, and whose life I had saved in some affair of the war. What does this lad do, after I had recounted to him one of my adventures, but call me a spy and informer, and beg me not to call him *du* any more, as is the fashion with young men when they are very intimate. I had nothing for it but to call him out; but I owed him no grudge. I disarmed him in a twinkling; and as I sent his sword flying over his head, said to him, “Kurz, did ever you know a man guilty of a mean action who can do as I do now?” This silenced the rest of the grumblers; and no man ever sneered at me after that.
No man can suppose that to a person of my fashion the waiting in antechambers, the conversation of footmen and hangers-on, was pleasant. But it was not more degrading than the barrack-room, of which I need not say I was heartily sick. My protestations of liking for the army were all intended to throw dust into the eyes of my employer. I sighed to be out of slavery. I knew I was born to make a figure in the world. Had I been one of the Neiss garrison, I would have cut my way to freedom by the side of the gallant Frenchman; but here I had only artifice to enable me to attain my end, and was not I justified in employing it? My plan was this: I may make myself so necessary to M. de Potzdorff, that he will obtain my freedom. Once free, with my fine person and good family, I will do what ten thousand Irish gentlemen have done before, and will marry a lady of fortune and condition. And the proof that I was, if not disinterested, at least actuated by a noble ambition, is this.

===Chinese===

这一点点风声他带给伯父警务大臣，大臣无疑拿去派了他的用场；于是我渐渐被波茨多夫一家当作心腹接待，成了名义上的兵：准我穿便衣（我担保，款式顶时兴），准我以一百种方式逍遥——我的苦伙计们眼红得很。至于军士们，待我客气得同待军官一样：得罪一个在部长侄子耳边说得上话的人，他们的袖杠可担待不起。我们连里有个年轻家伙，姓库尔茨——人可同姓作对，足六英尺高——战争中什么事里我救过他一命。我把一段历险讲给他听之后，这小子干了什么？骂我是奸细、是眼线，求我别再同他“你”呀“你”的相称——年轻人交情深了兴这样。我别无办法，只有约他出来说。可我不记他的仇。眨眼间我缴了他的械；趁他的剑飞过头顶，我对他说：“库尔茨，你可认识哪个干过下作事的男人，能像我这样干这一手？”这一下把其余嘀咕的人全镇住了；此后再没有人讥诣过我。
谁也不会当真以为：像我这样品级的人，在前厅里候着，同听差和食客闲谈，是件乐事。可这也不比营房更辱没人——营房我早已腻透了，不必说。我那些喜欢行伍的表白，全是为蒙蔽我东家撒的烟幕。我渴望脱离奴役。我知道我生来要在世上成个人物。倘若我当年在尼斯守军里，早学那位勇敢的法国人，一路杀出自由来了；可在这里，只有心机能叫我达到目的，用心机难道不名正言顺么？我的算盘是：把自己弄成波茨多夫先生离不得的人，他就会替我弄来自由。一旦自由，凭我漂亮的人品、好好的家世，我就干一万爱尔兰绅士先行干过的事：娶一位有钱有地位的太太。而证明我纵非无私、至少怀着一股高贵雄心的，是这一桩：

===Original===

There was a fat grocer’s widow in Berlin with six hundred thalers of rent, and a good business, who gave me to understand that she would purchase my discharge if I would marry her; but I frankly told her that I was not made to be a grocer, and thus absolutely flung away a chance of freedom which she offered me.
And I was grateful to my employers; more grateful than they to me. The Captain was in debt, and had dealings with the Jews, to whom he gave notes of hand payable on his uncle’s death. The old Herr von Potzdorff, seeing the confidence his nephew had in me, offered to bribe me to know what the young man’s affairs really were. But what did I do? I informed Monsieur George von Potzdorff of the fact; and we made out, in concert, a list of little debts, so moderate, that they actually appeased the old uncle instead of irritating, and he paid them, being glad to get off so cheap.
And a pretty return I got for this fidelity. One morning, the old gentleman being closeted with his nephew (he used to come to get any news stirring as to what the young officers of the regiment were doing: whether this or that gambled; who intrigued, and with whom; who was at the ridotto on such a night; who was in debt, and whatnot; for the King liked to know the business of every officer in his army), I was sent with a letter to the Marquis d’Argens (that afterwards married Mademoiselle Cochois the actress), and, meeting the Marquis at a few paces off in the street, gave my message, and returned to the Captain’s lodging. He and his worthy uncle were making my unworthy self the subject of conversation.
“He is noble,” said the Captain.
“Bah!” replied the uncle (whom I could have throttled for his insolence). “All the beggarly Irish who ever enlisted tell the same story.”
“He was kidnapped by Galgenstein,” resumed the other.
“A kidnapped deserter,” said M. Potzdorff; “*la belle affaire!*”
“Well, I promised the lad I would ask for his discharge; and I am sure you can make him useful.”
“You *have* asked his discharge,” answered the elder, laughing. “*Bon Dieu!* You are a model of probity! You’ll never succeed to my place, George, if you are no wiser than you are just now. Make the fellow as useful to you as you please. He has a good manner and a frank countenance. He can lie with an assurance that I never saw surpassed, and fight, you say, on a pinch.

===Chinese===

柏林有个胖胖的杂货铺寡妇，六百塔勒租金，一份好铺面；她示意，只要我肯娶她，她肯出钱赎我的兵役；可我坦白告诉她，我天生不是当杂货铺老板的料——就这样，她递到我手上的自由，叫我干干脆脆甩掉了。
我对我东家们是感激的；比他们对我感激得多。上尉负着债，同犹太人打交道，开的票据都写明他伯父死后兑付。老波茨多夫老爷见侄子这样信得过我，出钱贿赂我，要打听年轻人的账目到底如何。可我干了什么？我把这事一五一十报给了乔治·冯·波茨多夫先生；我们两个合拟了一份小小债务的清单，数目克制得竟叫老伯父不但不冒火，反而消了气，照单付了——捾了这样大的便宜，他乐得。
而我这份忠心换来的报答可漂亮。一天早晨，老绅士正同侄子关起门密谈（他惯来打听团里年轻军官们的动静：哪个赌钱；谁同谁有染；哪晚谁在化装舞会；谁欠了债，等等——国王喜欢知道他军中每个军官的底细），我奉命送一封信给达让侯爵（就是后来娶了女演员科舒瓦小姐的那位）；在街上几步外遇上侯爵，传了话，回到上尉寓所。他同他那位值价的伯父，正拿我不值价的本人作话头。
“他是贵族。”上尉说。
“呸！”伯父答（他那放肆，我真想掐死他），“投军的爱尔兰叫花子，个个都是这套话。”
“他是叫加尔根施泰因拐来的。”另一个接着说。
“拐来的逃兵，”波茨多夫先生说，“好大一桩事！”
“唉，我应承过这小子，替他求开革；您准能把他派上用场。”
“你已经求过他的开革了，”老的那位笑着答，“老天爷！你可真是廉正的模范！乔治，你要是只这点见识，我的位子你永远接不上。这小子你随怎么用。他有好仪态，坦率的面相。撒谎撒得那样笃定，我没见过第二个；急了还能打——你说的。

===Original===

The scoundrel does not want for good qualities; but he is vain, a spendthrift, and a *bavard*. As long as you have the regiment *in terrorem* over him, you can do as you like with him. Once let him loose, and the lad is likely to give you the slip. Keep on promising him; promise to make him a general, if you like. What the deuce do I care? There are spies enough to be had in this town without him.”
It was thus that the services I rendered to M. Potzdorff were qualified by that ungrateful old gentleman; and I stole away from the room extremely troubled in spirit, to think that another of my fond dreams was thus dispelled; and that my hopes of getting out of the army, by being useful to the Captain, were entirely vain. For some time my despair was such, that I thought of marrying the widow; but the marriages of privates are never allowed without the direct permission of the King; and it was a matter of very great doubt whether His Majesty would allow a young fellow of twenty-two, the handsomest man of his army, to be coupled to a pimple-faced old widow of sixty, who was quite beyond the age when her marriage would be likely to multiply the subjects of His Majesty. This hope of liberty was therefore vain; nor could I hope to purchase my discharge, unless any charitable soul would lend me a large sum of money; for, though I made a good deal, as I have said, yet I have always had through life an incorrigible knack of spending, and (such is my generosity of disposition) have been in debt ever since I was born.
My captain, the sly rascal! gave me a very different version of his conversation with his uncle to that which I knew to be the true one; and said smilingly to me, “Redmond, I have spoken to the Minister regarding thy services,3 and thy fortune is made. We shall get thee out of the army, appoint thee to the police bureau, and procure for thee an inspectorship of customs; and, in fine, allow thee to move in a better sphere than that in which Fortune has hitherto placed thee.”
Although I did not believe a word of this speech, I affected to be very much moved by it, and of course swore eternal gratitude to the Captain for his kindness to the poor Irish castaway.
“Your service at the Dutch Minister’s has pleased me very well. There is another occasion on which you may make yourself useful to us; and if you succeed, depend on it your reward will be secure.”

===Chinese===

这恶棍好货色不缺；就是虚荣、挥霍、碎嘴。只要拿团队吊在他头上吓着，你要他怎样就怎样。一旦放脱，这小子多半给你溜之大吉。尽管许他；许他做将军也行。关我什么事？这城里没有他，奸细也多的是。”
我给波茨多夫先生效的力，就得了那位没良心的老先生这样一番评语；我从房里溜出来，心里懊丧极了：又一场好梦这样破灭；想靠对上尉有用脱出军籍的指望，全是白想。有一阵我绝望到想娶那寡妇；可大兵娶妻，非得国王直接恩准不可；陛下肯不肯恩准一个二十二岁的小伙子——全军数第一的美男子——去配一个六十岁、麻子脸的老寡妇，大是疑问：那年纪早过了替陛下增添子民的指望。这条自由路于是也断了；赎身的路也断了，除非哪位善人肯借我一大笔钱：因为，虽说我进项不少——前面交代过——可我平生有一副无可救药的花钱的本事，又（我天性这样慷慨）打娘胎起就欠债。
我的上尉，那只狡猾的促狭鬼！把他同伯父的谈话对我另讲了一版，同我知道的真话全然两样；他笑吟吟对我说：“雷德蒙，我就你的劳绩同大臣谈过了，你的造化来了。我们把你弄出军队，安插到警察局，再给你谋个海关稽查；总而言之，许你挪进一个比命运至今安置你的高些的圈子。”
这番话我半个字不信，可我装作大为感动，当然又对上尉的仁厚——对这可怜的爱尔兰落难人——赌了一回永世的感激。
“你在荷兰公使家的差使我顶满意。另有一桩事，你还能替我们出力；办成了，包管你的酬劳跑不了。”

===Original===

“What is the service, sir?” said I; “I will do anything for so kind a master.”
“There is lately come to Berlin,” said the Captain, “a gentleman in the service of the Empress-Queen, who calls himself the Chevalier de Balibari, and wears the red riband and star of the Pope’s order of the Spur. He speaks Italian or French indifferently; but we have some reason to fancy this Monsieur de Balibari is a native of your country of Ireland. Did you ever hear such a name as Balibari in Ireland?”
“Balibari? Balyb﻿—?” A sudden thought flashed across me. “No, sir,” said I, “I never heard the name.”
“You must go into his service. Of course you will not know a word of English: and if the Chevalier asks as to the particularity of your accent, say you are a Hungarian. The servant who came with him will be turned away today, and the person to whom he has applied for a faithful fellow will recommend you. You are a Hungarian; you served in the Seven Years’ War. You left the army on account of weakness of the loins. You served Monsieur de Quellenberg two years; he is now with the army in Silesia, but there is your certificate signed by him. You afterwards lived with Doctor Mopsius, who will give you a character, if need be; and the landlord of the Star will, of course, certify that you are an honest fellow: but his certificate goes for nothing. As for the rest of your story, you can fashion that as you will, and make it as romantic or as ludicrous as your fancy dictates. Try, however, to win the Chevalier’s confidence by provoking his compassion. He gambles a great deal, and *wins*. Do you know the cards well?”
“Only a very little, as soldiers do.”
“I had thought you more expert. You must find out if the Chevalier cheats; if he does, we have him. He sees the English and Austrian envoys continually, and the young men of either Ministry sup repeatedly at his house. Find out what they talk of; for how much each plays, especially if any of them play on parole: if you can read his private letters, of course you will; though about those which go to the post, you need not trouble yourself; we look at them there. But never see him write a note without finding out to whom it goes, and by what channel or messenger. He sleeps with the keys of his despatch-box on a string round his neck. Twenty Frederics, if you get an impression of the keys. You will, of course, go in plain clothes.

===Chinese===

“什么差使，先生？”我说，“这样仁厚的主人，我什么都干。”
“近来到柏林来了一位绅士，”上尉说，“在女皇麾下当差，自称巴利巴里骑士，佩着教皇马刺勋位的红绶带和星章。他意大利语法语说得一样好；可我们有几分理由疑心，这位巴利巴里先生是你们爱尔兰的本地产人。你在爱尔兰听过巴利巴里这样的姓么？”
“巴利巴里？巴利巴——？”一个念头闪过我心头。“没有，先生，”我说，“这姓我没听过。”
“你必须进他家当差。你自然一个英文字不能懂；骑士若问起你口音的特别处，就说你是匈牙利人。跟他来的仆人今天就会被打发走；他托人找一个可靠家伙，那受托的人会举荐你。你是匈牙利人；打过七年战争。因为腰部衰弱离了军。你在奎伦贝格先生家当过两年差；他如今在西里西亚军中，喏，这是他签名的品行证明。你随后在莫普修斯医生家过日子；需要的话他肯给你作保；星客栈的老板自然肯证明你是个老实人——不过他那张纸不值什么。至于故事其余的部分，你爱怎么编就怎么编，爱多浪漫多浪漫，爱多滑稽多滑稽。只是，要想法子激起骑士的怜悯，赢他的信任。他赌得凶，而且赢。牌你熟么？”
“略知一点，当兵人的那点。”
“我当你精些呢。你要查明骑士出千不出千；他若出千，我们就拿住他了。他同英国、奥地利两边的公使不断来往，两边的年轻人夜夜在他家夜宴。查他们谈些什么；各人下多大的注，尤其有没有谁打欠条赌的：他的私信你若能看，当然看；至于走邮路的那些，不必费心；我们在那边看。可他但凡写一张条子，你务必查明给谁、走什么路子、什么信使。他睡觉时公文箱的钥匙拿带子挂在脖子上。钥匙拓下印模，二十枚腓特烈。你自然穿便衣去。

===Original===

You had best brush the powder out of your hair, and tie it with a riband simply; your moustache you must of course shave off.”
With these instructions, and a very small gratuity, the Captain left me. When I again saw him, he was amused at the change in my appearance. I had, not without a pang (for they were as black as jet, and curled elegantly), shaved off my moustaches; had removed the odious grease and flour, which I always abominated, out of my hair; had mounted a demure French grey coat, black satin breeches, and a maroon plush waistcoat, and a hat without a cockade. I looked as meek and humble as any servant out of place could possibly appear; and I think not my own regiment, which was now at the review at Potsdam, would have known me. Thus accoutred, I went to the Star Hotel, where this stranger was﻿—my heart beating with anxiety, and something telling me that this Chevalier de Balibari was no other than Barry, of Ballybarry, my father’s eldest brother, who had given up his estate in consequence of his obstinate adherence to the Romish superstition. Before I went in to present myself, I went to look in the remises at his carriage. Had he the Barry arms? Yes, there they were: argent, a bend gules, with four escallops of the field﻿—the ancient coat of my house. They were painted in a shield about as big as my hat, on a smart chariot handsomely gilded, surmounted with a coronet, and supported by eight or nine Cupids, cornucopias, and flower-baskets, according to the queer heraldic fashion of those days. It must be he! I felt quite feint as I went up the stairs. I was going to present myself before my uncle in the character of a servant!
“You are the young man whom M. de Seebach recommended?”
I bowed, and handed him a letter from that gentleman, with which my captain had taken care to provide me. As he looked at it I had leisure to examine him. My uncle was a man of sixty years of age, dressed superbly in a coat and breeches of apricot-coloured velvet, a white satin waistcoat embroidered with gold like the coat. Across his breast went the purple riband of his order of the Spur; and the star of the order, an enormous one, sparkled on his breast.

===Chinese===

顶好把你头发上的粉刷掉，拿根缎带简简单单系住；小胡子自然得刮掉。”
交代完这些，外加一笔小得可怜的犒赏，上尉离开了我。等我再见他，他瞧我这变样乐了。我刮掉了小胡子——不免心疼（它们黑得同漆一样，卷得漂亮）；除掉了头发里那叫我一直厌恶的油腻和粉；换上一件谦卑的法国灰外衣、黑缎马裤、栗色长毛绒背心，一顶不佩帽徽的帽子。我看上去驯顺谦卑，同一个落了差事的仆人所能有的模样一般无二；我想，连我自己那个团——这时正在波茨坦受阅——也认不出我了。这副行头进了星客栈，那位生客就下榻在此——我心头乱跳，有个声音告诉我：这位巴利巴里骑士，不是别人，正是巴利巴里的巴里，我父亲的长兄；他当年因死抱罗马公教的迷信，舍了家产。进屋投名之前，我先到车棚里看他的马车。可有巴里家的纹章？有，正在那儿：银地红斜带，缀四枚本色的扇贝——我家的古老纹记。画在一面同我帽子差不多大的盾上，装在一辆漂亮地镀了金的车上，顶着一顶冠，八个九个小爱神、丰饶角、花篮拥着——照那个年代古怪的纹章风尚。必是他！上楼时我几乎晕了。我要以仆人的身份，站到我伯父面前了！
“你就是泽巴赫先生荐来的年轻人？”
我一躬身，递上那位先生的信——上尉周到地替我备好了。他看信时，我得了工夫打量他。我伯父六十岁年纪，穿得华丽之极：杏黄色天鹅绒的外衣和马裤，白缎背心，同外衣一样金线绣花。胸前横着他马刺勋位的紫绶带；星章——大得吓人——在胸前放光。

===Original===

He had rings on all his fingers, a couple of watches in his fobs, a rich diamond solitaire in the black riband round his neck, and fastened to the bag of his wig; his ruffles and frills were decorated with a profusion of the richest lace. He had pink silk stockings rolled over the knee, and tied with gold garters; and enormous diamond buckles to his red-heeled shoes. A sword mounted in gold, in a white fish-skin scabbard; and a hat richly laced, and lined with white feathers, which were lying on a table beside him, completed the costume of this splendid gentleman. In height he was about my size, that is, six feet and half an inch; his cast of features singularly like mine, and extremely distingué. One of his eyes was closed with a black patch, however; he wore a little white and red paint, by no means an unusual ornament in those days; and a pair of moustaches, which fell over his lip and hid a mouth that I afterwards found had rather a disagreeable expression. When his beard was removed, the upper teeth appeared to project very much; and his countenance wore a ghastly fixed smile, by no means pleasant.
It was very imprudent of me; but when I saw the splendour of his appearance, the nobleness of his manner, I felt it impossible to keep disguise with him; and when he said, “Ah, you are a Hungarian, I see!” I could hold no longer.
“Sir,” said I, “I am an Irishman, and my name is Redmond Barry, of Ballybarry.” As I spoke, I burst into tears; I can’t tell why; but I had seen none of my kith or kin for six years, and my heart longed for someone.

===Chinese===

他十根手指根根戴戒指；表袋里揣着两只表；黑丝带上、假发的发袋上别着一颗贵重的独粒钻石；袖口和胸褶缀着满得不能再满的上等花边。粉红丝袜翻卷过膝，金袜带扎着；红跟鞋上一对大得吓人的钻石扣。一口金装柄的剑，白鱼皮剑鞘；一顶绶带重重、衬白羽毛的帽子搁在身边桌上——这位华丽绅士的行头至此才算齐全。身量同我相仿，也就是六英尺半；眉眼同我出奇地像，而且极有贵气。只是一只眼蒙着黑罩；脸上敷着一点红白——那年头这绝非罕见的修饰；一部小胡子垂在唇上，遮住的那张嘴，我后来发现表情相当讨厌。胡子一去，门牙显得暴出；一脸僵死的假笑，绝说不上好看。
我未免太鲁莽了；可一见他那煊赫的气派、高贵的仪态，我觉得再没法同他装假；等他说出：“啊，我看你是匈牙利人！”我再也撑不住了。
“先生，”我说，“我是爱尔兰人，我叫雷德蒙·巴里，巴利巴里的。”话一出口，我眼泪直涌；说不上为什么；六年了，我没见过一个亲人，我的心渴望一个亲人。

## VIII Barry’s Adieu to Military Profession / 八 巴里告别军人职业
===Original===

You who have never been out of your country, know little what it is to hear a friendly voice in captivity; and there’s many a man that will not understand the cause of the burst of feeling which I have confessed took place on my seeing my uncle. He never for a minute thought to question the truth of what I said. “Mother of God!” cried he, “it’s my brother Harry’s son.” And I think in my heart he was as much affected as I was at thus suddenly finding one of his kindred; for he, too, was an exile from home, and a friendly voice, a look, brought the old country back to his memory again, and the old days of his boyhood. “I’d give five years of my life to see them again,” said he, after caressing me very warmly. “What?” asked I. “Why,” replied he, “the green fields, and the river, and the old round tower, and the burying-place at Ballybarry. ’Twas a shame for your father to part with the land, Redmond, that went so long with the name.”
He then began to ask me concerning myself, and I gave him my history at some length; at which the worthy gentleman laughed many times, saying, that I was a Barry all over. In the middle of my story he would stop me, to make me stand back to back, and measure with him (by which I ascertained that our heights were the same, and that my uncle had a stiff knee, moreover, which made him walk in a peculiar way), and uttered, during the course of the narrative, a hundred exclamations of pity, and kindness, and sympathy. It was “Holy Saints!” and “Mother of Heaven!” and “Blessed Mary!” continually; by which, and with justice, I concluded that he was still devotedly attached to the ancient faith of our family.
It was with some difficulty that I came to explain to him the last part of my history, viz., that I was put into his service as a watch upon his actions, of which I was to give information in a certain quarter. When I told him (with a great deal of hesitation) of this fact, he burst out laughing, and enjoyed the joke amazingly. “The rascals!” said he; “they think to catch me, do they? Why, Redmond, my chief conspiracy is a faro-bank. But the King is so jealous, that he will see a spy in every person who comes to his miserable capital in the great sandy desert here. Ah, my boy, I must show you Paris and Vienna!”

===Chinese===

你们这些从未出过国门的人，不大知道在囚禁中听见一个友好嗓音是什么滋味；许多人也不会理解，为什么我一见舅舅，就爆发了我方才承认的那场感情。他一刻也没想过怀疑我话的真假。“上帝之母！”他喊道，“这是我兄弟哈里的儿子。”我心底相信，他同我一样动情——就这样突然找到了一个骨肉；他自己也是离乡的人，一句友好的话、一个眼色，就把故国、把他少年时代的老日子，重新带回他记忆里。“情愿拿五年寿命再看它们一眼，”他热热烈烈抚爱过我之后说。“看什么？”我问。“呃，”他答，“绿色的田野，那条河，那座老圆塔，还有巴利巴里的墓地。你父亲把那片同姓氏流传了那么久的土地脱手，雷德蒙，真是不像话。”
他随后问起我的来历，我把自己的经历相当详细地讲给他听；这位厚道绅士听了几回大笑，说我浑身上下就是个巴里。讲到一半他还打断我，叫我同他背靠背站着比身高（由此我查明我们两个一般高，还查出我舅舅膝盖有毛病，走路因此有个特别的姿势），叙述进行中，他发出上百声怜惜、慈爱和同情的感叹。“神圣的圣徒！”“上天之母！”“蒙福的马利亚！”不绝于口；由此我公道地断定，他仍然虔诚信奉我们家的古教。
我颇费了些力气，才把经历中最后一段向他讲明：我是被安插到他身边当坐探的，要向某个地方报告他的行动。我（带着满口迟疑）把这件事告诉他时，他放声大笑，把这玩笑受用了个够。“这伙无赖！”他说。“他们想抓我，是吧？雷德蒙，我的头号阴谋是一家法罗赌场。可国王疑心太重，凡到这座大沙漠里他那倒霉京城来的人，他个个当奸细看。啊，孩子，我一定带你看看巴黎和维也纳！”

===Original===

I said there was nothing I longed for more than to see any city but Berlin, and should be delighted to be free of the odious military service. Indeed, I thought, from his splendour of appearance, the knickknacks about the room, the gilded carriage in the remise, that my uncle was a man of vast property; and that he would purchase a dozen, nay, a whole regiment of substitutes, in order to restore me to freedom.
But I was mistaken in my calculations regarding him, as his history of himself speedily showed me. “I have been beaten about the world,” said he, “ever since the year 1742, when my brother your father (and Heaven forgive him) cut my family estate from under my heels, by turning heretic, in order to marry that scold of a mother of yours. Well, let bygones be bygones. ’Tis probable that I should have run through the little property as he did in my place, and I should have had to begin a year or two later the life I have been leading ever since I was compelled to leave Ireland. My lad, I have been in every service; and, between ourselves, owe money in every capital in Europe. I made a campaign or two with the Pandours under Austrian Trenck. I was captain in the Guard of His Holiness the Pope, I made the campaign of Scotland with the Prince of Wales﻿—a bad fellow, my dear, caring more for his mistress and his brandy-bottle than for the crowns of the three kingdoms. I have served in Spain and in Piedmont; but I have been a rolling stone, my good fellow. Play﻿—play has been my ruin; that and beauty” (here he gave a leer which made him, I must confess, look anything but handsome; besides, his rouged cheeks were all beslobbered with the tears which he had shed on receiving me). “The women have made a fool of me, my dear Redmond. I am a softhearted creature, and this minute, at sixty-two, have no more command of myself than when Peggy O’Dwyer made a fool of me at sixteen.”
“ ’Faith sir,” says I, laughing, “I think it runs in the family!” and described to him, much to his amusement, my romantic passion for my cousin, Nora Brady. He resumed his narrative.
“The cards now are my only livelihood. Sometimes I am in luck, and then I lay out my money in these trinkets you see. It’s property, look you, Redmond; and the only way I have found of keeping a little about me. When the luck goes against me, why, my dear, my diamonds go to the pawnbrokers, and I wear paste.

===Chinese===

我说我别无所求，只盼看一座柏林以外的任何城市，巴不得脱离这可憎的军籍。说真的，瞧他那身光鲜、满屋子的小摆设、车棚里那辆镀金马车，我当舅舅是位家财万贯的人，为赎我的自由，买上一打、整整一团替身他都肯。
可我对他这笔账算错了，他自述的经历很快让我明白。“我自1742年起就在世界各地颠沛，”他说，“那一年，你父亲——我兄弟（上帝饶恕他）——改宗当了异端，为娶你那位泼妇母亲，把我脚底下的家产抽走了。罢了，既往不咎。换了我，多半也会像他那样把那份小家当荡光，也不过比实际晚一两年，去过我被迫离开爱尔兰以来一直过的这种日子。孩子，哪一国的军队我几乎都干过；咱们私下说，欧洲哪座京城我都欠着钱。我随奥地利特伦克手下的潘杜尔兵打过一两仗。我在教皇陛下卫队里当过上尉。我随威尔士亲王打过苏格兰战役——那是个坏东西，亲爱的，他心疼情妇和白兰地瓶，胜过三顶王冠。我在西班牙干过，在皮埃蒙特干过；可我一直是块滚石，好朋友。赌——赌毁了我；还有美色”（说到这里他斜眼一笑，我得承认，那副样子说不上好看；况且他敷粉的两颊还糊满了方才接我时的眼泪）。“女人们把我当傻子耍，我亲爱的雷德蒙。我是个心软的造物，此刻六十二岁了，自控的本事同佩吉·奥德怀尔十六岁耍我那时候不相上下。”
“真的，先生，”我笑着说，“我看这是家传！”随后把我对表妹诺拉·布雷迪那段罗曼蒂克的痴情讲给他听，逗得他大为开心。他接着叙述。
“如今纸牌是我唯一的生计。手气顺的时候，我就把钱换成你眼前这些小玩意儿。这也是产业，你瞧，雷德蒙；是我攒下点东西的唯一法门。手气背了，呃，亲爱的，我的钻石就进当铺，我戴玻璃的。金匠摩西朋友今天就要来拜访我；因为上礼拜牌运一直同我作对，今晚赌场的本钱我还得筹呢。你懂牌吗？”
我答，当兵的怎么玩我就会怎么玩，本事不大。
“明儿早上咱们练练，孩子，”他说，“我教你一两手有用的东西。”
有这样的求学机会我自然高兴，忙不迭表示领受舅舅教诲之乐。

===Original===

Friend Moses the goldsmith will pay me a visit this very day; for the chances have been against me all the week past, and I must raise money for the bank tonight. Do you understand the cards?”
I replied that I could play as soldiers do, but had no great skill.
“We will practise in the morning, my boy,” said he, “and I’ll put you up to a thing or two worth knowing.”
Of course I was glad to have such an opportunity of acquiring knowledge, and professed myself delighted to receive my uncle’s instruction.
The Chevalier’s account of himself rather disagreeably affected me. All his show was on his back, as he said. His carriage, with the fine gilding, was a part of his stock in trade. He *had* a sort of mission from the Austrian Court:﻿—it was to discover whether a certain quantity of alloyed ducats which had been traced to Berlin, were from the King’s treasury. But the real end of Monsieur de Balibari was play. There was a young attaché of the English embassy, my Lord Deuceace, afterwards Viscount and Earl of Crabs in the English peerage, who was playing high; and it was after hearing of the passion of this young English nobleman that my uncle, then at Prague, determined to visit Berlin and engage him. For there is a sort of chivalry among the knights of the dice-box: the fame of great players is known all over Europe. I have known the Chevalier de Casanova, for instance, to travel six hundred miles, from Paris to Turin, for the purpose of meeting Mr. Charles Fox, then only my Lord Holland’s dashing son, afterwards the greatest of European orators and statesmen.
It was agreed that I should keep my character of valet; that in the presence of strangers I should not know a word of English; that I should keep a good lookout on the trumps when I was serving the champagne and punch about; and, having a remarkably fine eyesight and a great natural aptitude, I was speedily able to give my dear uncle much assistance against his opponents at the green table. Some prudish persons may affect indignation at the frankness of these confessions, but Heaven pity them! Do you suppose that any man who has lost or won a hundred thousand pounds at play will not take the advantages which his neighbour enjoys? They are all the same. But it is only the clumsy fool who *cheats*; who resorts to the vulgar expedients of cogged dice and cut cards.

===Chinese===

骑士这番自述叫我听了相当不受用。他那一身风光全在背上，如他自己所说。那辆镀金漂亮的马车是他营业本钱的一部分。他确实从奥地利宫廷领着一桩差事：——查明那批被追踪到柏林的掺假杜卡特究竟出不出自国王的金库。但巴利巴里先生的真正目的在赌。英国使馆有位年轻随员，迪尤斯埃斯勋爵——后来在英国贵族院封子爵、再封克拉布斯伯爵——正赌得凶；我舅舅当时在布拉格，听到这位英国青年贵族的赌名，决意到柏林来会他。骰盅骑士们自有一套骑士风：大赌客的名声传遍全欧。我就认得卡萨诺瓦骑士，为一会查尔斯·福克斯先生——当时还不过是霍兰勋爵家那位神气的公子，后来成了欧洲最伟大的演说家和政治家——从巴黎专程赶六百里到都灵。
谈定的章程是：我照旧当我的跟班；有生人在场，我一个英文单词也不认得；斟香槟、端潘趣的当儿，我要好好盯着王牌——我天生眼力极尖，又极有禀赋，不多时便能替亲爱的舅舅在绿台面上给对手们大大的助力。有些假正经的人听了这些自白的坦率，怕要摆出义愤；上帝可怜他们吧！你以为一个在赌桌上输赢过十万镑的人，会不占邻座所占的便宜？天下赌客一色一样。只是那笨手笨脚的蠢货才*出千*——才用灌铅骰子、切口纸牌这类下等手段。

===Original===

Such a man is sure to go wrong some time or other, and is not fit to play in the society of gallant gentlemen; and my advice to people who see such a vulgar person at his pranks is, of course, to back him while he plays, but never﻿—never to have anything to do with him. Play grandly, honourably. Be not, of course, cast down at losing; but above all, be not eager at winning, as mean souls are. And, indeed, with all one’s skill and advantages, winning is often problematical; I have seen a sheer ignoramus that knows no more of play than of Hebrew, blunder you out of five thousand pounds in a few turns of the cards. I have seen a gentleman and his confederate play against another and *his* confederate. One never is secure in these cases: and when one considers the time and labour spent, the genius, the anxiety, the outlay of money required, the multiplicity of bad debts that one meets with (for dishonourable rascals are to be found at the play-table, as everywhere else in the world), I say, for my part, the profession is a bad one; and, indeed, have scarcely ever met a man who, in the end, profited by it. I am writing now with the experience of a man of the world. At the time I speak of I was a lad, dazzled by the idea of wealth, and respecting, certainly too much, my uncle’s superior age and station in life.
There is no need to particularise here the little arrangements made between us; the playmen of the present day want no instruction, I take it, and the public have little interest in the matter. But simplicity was our secret. Everything successful is simple. If, for instance, I wiped the dust off a chair with my napkin, it was to show that the enemy was strong in diamonds; if I pushed it, he had ace, king; if I said, “Punch or wine, my Lord?” hearts was meant; if “Wine or punch?” clubs. If I blew my nose, it was to indicate that there was another confederate employed by the adversary; and *then*, I warrant you, some pretty trials of skill would take place. My Lord Deuceace, although so young, had a very great skill and cleverness with the cards in every way; and it was only from hearing Frank Punter, who came with him, yawn three times when the Chevalier had the ace of trumps, that I knew we were Greek to Greek, as it were.

===Chinese===

这种人早晚要栽，上不了堂堂绅士的赌桌；有人看见这种俗物耍把戏，我的忠告自然是：他押什么你跟什么——可千万、千万别同他沾边。要赌，就赌得堂皇、光明。输了自然别垂头丧气；更要紧的是，赢了别猴急，小家子气的人才那样。何况任你本事多大、便宜多多，赢常常没个准；我见过一个对赌博的理解不比对希伯来文多的十足外行，几圈牌就把你五千镑胡撈了去。我见过一位绅士带着他的搭档，同另一位及其搭档对赌。这种局面谁也保不了险；想想搭进去的光阴和力气、天才、焦灼、本钱，还有一路遇上的烂账（赌桌上同世上任何地方一样，不名誉的无赖总有），要我说，这门营生是门坏营生；而且说真的，靠它到底占了便宜的，我几乎没见过一个。我现在写下这些话，是凭一个阅历世故之人的身份。可我说到的那个年月，我还是个少年，被财富的念想晃花了眼，对舅舅的年辈与地位，敬意也未免太重了些。
我们爷俩之间的那些小安排，此处不必细表；如今的赌客无需人教，我想公众对此也没什么兴味。只说一句：简单是我们的诀窍。凡成功的东西都简单。比方说，我用餐巾掸一掸椅子上的灰，是示意敌方方块硬；我把椅子推一推，是他手里幺、王双头；我说“潘趣还是酒，大人？”指的是红心；说“酒还是潘趣？”是梅花。我暌鼻子，是示意对方那边另有一名搭档；到了那一步，我担保，就有好戏看了，各显神通。迪尤斯埃斯勋爵虽年轻，一副牌上手无论哪路功夫都极精；我是听他随行的弗兰克·庞特在骑士摸到王牌时连打三个呵欠，才明白这回是希腊人碰上了希腊人。

===Original===

My assumed dullness was perfect; and I used to make Monsieur de Potzdorff laugh with it, when I carried my little reports to him at the Garden-house outside the town where he gave me rendezvous. These reports, of course, were arranged between me and my uncle beforehand. I was instructed (and it is always far the best way) to tell as much truth as my story would possibly bear. When, for instance, he would ask me, “What does the Chevalier do of a morning?”
“He goes to church regularly” (he was very religious), “and after hearing mass comes home to breakfast. Then he takes an airing in his chariot till dinner, which is served at noon. After dinner he writes his letters, if he have any letters to write: but he has very little to do in this way. His letters are to the Austrian envoy, with whom he corresponds, but who does not acknowledge him; and being written in English, of course I look over his shoulder. He generally writes for money. He says he wants it to bribe the secretaries of the Treasury, in order to find out really where the alloyed ducats come from; but, in fact, he wants it to play of evenings, when he makes his party with Calsabigi, the lottery-contractor, the Russian attachés, two from the English embassy, my Lords Deuceace and Punter, who play *a jeu d’enfer*, and a few more. The same set meet every night at supper: there are seldom any ladies; those who come are chiefly French ladies, members of the corps de ballet. He wins often, but not always. Lord Deuceace is a very fine player. The Chevalier Elliot, the English Minister, sometimes comes, on which occasion the secretaries do not play. Monsieur de Balibari dines at the missions, but *en petit comité*, not on grand days of reception. Calsabigi, I think, is his confederate at play. He has won lately; but the week before last he pledged his solitaire for four hundred ducats.”
“Do he and the English attachés talk together in their own language?”
“Yes; he and the envoy spoke yesterday for half-an-hour about the new danseuse and the American troubles: chiefly about the new danseuse.”
It will be seen that the information I gave was very minute and accurate, though not very important.

===Chinese===

我装出的那副木讷天衣无缝；每回我把小报告送到城外花园小屋——波茨多夫先生同我约定的地方——总把他逗得直笑。这些报告，自然是我同舅舅预先编好的。我受的指点（也永远是上策）是：在不穿帮的前提下，实话尽量多说。比如他问我：“骑士早晨都做些什么？”
“他按时上教堂”（他虔信得很），“望过弥撒回家用早餐。然后乘他的四轮车兜风到正午的午饭。饭后写信——若有信可写：这方面他营生很少。信是写给奥地利公使的，两人通信，公使却不认他；信用英文写的，我自然从他肩膀后面看。他多半是要钱。说是拿去贿赂国库的秘书们，好查明掺假杜卡特的真正来路；其实是要来晚上赌的，他每晚上同卡尔萨比吉——彩票承包人——俄国的随员们、英国使馆的两位、迪尤斯埃斯和庞特两位勋爵开赌局，玩的是地狱快的输赢，另外还有几位。同一伙人每夜共进夜宵：女客很少；来的多是法国太太，歌剧院芭蕾班的。他常赢，不总赢。迪尤斯埃斯勋爵是位很了不起的赌客。英国公使埃利奥特爵士有时来，他一到，秘书们就不下场。巴利巴里先生到各公使馆赴宴，但只小聚，大宴会日不去。我猜卡尔萨比吉是他赌桌上的搭档。他近来赢了；上上礼拜他当了四百杜卡特，押的是他那枚独粒钻。”
“他同英国随员们用他们本国话交谈么？”
“谈；昨天他同公使谈了半个钟头，谈新来的舞女和美洲的乱子：多半谈新来的舞女。”
诸位看得出，我送去的情报细而准，只是无关宏旨。

===Original===

But such as it was, it was carried to the ears of that famous hero and warrior the Philosopher of Sans Souci; and there was not a stranger who entered the capital but his actions were similarly spied and related to Frederick the Great.
As long as the play was confined to the young men of the different embassies, His Majesty did not care to prevent it; nay, he encouraged play at all the missions, knowing full well that a man in difficulties can be made to speak, and that a timely rouleau of Frederics would often get him a secret worth many thousands. He got some papers from the French house in this way: and I have no doubt that my Lord Deuceace would have supplied him with information at a similar rate, had his chief not known the young nobleman’s character pretty well, and had (as is usually the case) the work of the mission performed by a steady roturier, while the young brilliant bloods of the suite sported their embroidery at the balls, or shook their Mechlin ruffles over the green tables at faro. I have seen many scores of these young sprigs since, of these and their principals, and, *mon Dieu!* what fools they are! What dullards, what fribbles, what addle-headed simple coxcombs! This is one of the lies of the world, this diplomacy; or how could we suppose, that were the profession as difficult as the solemn red-box and tape-men would have us believe, they would invariably choose for it little pink-faced boys from school, with no other claim than mamma’s title, and able at most to judge of a curricle, a new dance, or a neat boot?
When it became known, however, to the officers of the garrison that there was a faro-table in town, they were wild to be admitted to the sport; and, in spite of my entreaties to the contrary, my uncle was not averse to allow the young gentlemen their fling, and once or twice cleared a handsome sum out of their purses. It was in vain I told him that I must carry the news to my captain, before whom his comrades would not fail to talk, and who would thus know of the intrigue even without my information.
“Tell him,” said my uncle.
“They will send you away,” said I; “then what is to become of me?”
“Make your mind easy,” said the latter, with a smile; “you shall not be left behind, I warrant you. Go take a last look at your barracks, make your mind easy; say a farewell to your friends in Berlin.

===Chinese===

可就是这样货色，也一字一句递到了那位赫赫有名的英雄、勇士、无忧宫哲学家耳朵里；凡进了这座京城的生客，行止没有不这样被窥探了报给腓特烈大王的。
只要赌局限于各使馆的年轻人，陛下懒得禁止；不但不禁，还鼓励各使馆赌，他深知陷入窘境的人不难撬开嘴，一卷及时递上的腓特烈金币，常常换回值几千金币的秘密。法国使馆的几份文件他就是这么弄到手的；我也毫不怀疑，迪尤斯埃斯勋爵本来也会照同样的价钱向他供情报，若不是他的上司对这位青年贵族的成色摸得很清，并且（这也是惯常的情形）使馆的实务都由一个稳重的平民包办，随员里那班鲜衣怒马的贵公子只管在舞会上抖他们的绣花衣，或是在法罗台子上把他们的梅克林花边袖口伸出去。这班花花芽子，我后来见过好几十，还有他们的上司——我的老天！多蠢的一伙！多钝、多虚浮、多糊涂的绣花枕头！外交这行是世上谎言之一；要不然怎么解释：倘若这行当真像那些捧红皮文件盒和公文带的先生们装的那么难，他们怎么回回挑的都是些刚出学堂的粉脸娃娃，唯一的本钱是妈妈的头衔，至多会鉴赏一驾双轮轻车、一支新舞步、一只漂亮的靴子？
后来，城里驻军的军官们打听到市里有法罗赌台，都疯了似的想进场；我不住劝阻，舅舅却不介意放这些年轻绅士乐一乐，还从他们钱包里一两次捞了可观的一笔。我告诉他，这消息我必定带到上尉那里，他的同僚们在他面前少不得谈论，这样他不用我报也就知情了——全属白费。
“告诉他，”舅舅说。
“他们会把你赶走的，”我说；“那我怎么办？”
“把心放宽，”他说，带着一个微笑；“我不会撇下你，我担保。去最后看一眼你的营房，把心放宽；同柏林的朋友们道个别。

===Original===

The dear souls, how they will weep when they hear you are out of the country; and, as sure as my name is Barry, out of it you shall go!”
“But how, sir?” said I.
“Recollect Mr. Fakenham of Fakenham,” said he knowingly. “ ’Tis you yourself taught me how. Go get me one of my wigs. Open my despatch-box yonder, where the great secrets of the Austrian Chancery lie; put your hair back off your forehead; clap me on this patch and these moustaches, and now look in the glass!”
“The Chevalier de Balibari,” said I, bursting with laughter, and began walking the room in his manner with his stiff knee.
The next day, when I went to make my report to Monsieur de Potzdorff, I told him of the young Prussian officers that had been of late gambling; and he replied, as I expected, that the King had determined to send the Chevalier out of the country.
“He is a stingy curmudgeon,” I replied; “I have had but three Frederics from him in two months, and I hope you will remember your promise to advance me!”
“Why, three Frederics were too much for the news you have picked up,” said the Captain, sneering.
“It is not my fault that there has been no more,” I replied. “When is he to go, sir?”
“The day after tomorrow. You say he drives after breakfast and before dinner. When he comes out to his carriage, a couple of gendarmes will mount the box, and the coachman will get his orders to move on.”
“And his baggage, sir?” said I.
“Oh! that will be sent after him. I have a fancy to look into that red box which contains his papers, you say; and at noon, after parade, shall be at the inn. You will not say a word to anyone there regarding the affair, and will wait for me at the Chevalier’s rooms until my arrival. We must force that box. You are a clumsy hound, or you would have got the key long ago!”
I begged the Captain to remember me, and so took my leave of him. The next night I placed a couple of pistols under the carriage seat; and I think the adventures of the following day are quite worthy of the honours of a separate chapter.

===Chinese===

那些可爱的灵魂，听说你出了国，不知要怎么哭呢；我姓巴里，说话算话——你是一定要出去的！”
“可怎么出去呢，先生？”我说。
“想想法肯纳姆家的法肯纳姆先生，”他会意地说。“那法子还是你自己教我的。去，给我取一顶假发来。把我那只公文箱打开——奥地利公使馆的大机密全在那儿；把额头前的头发往后拢；给我贴上这块膏药、安上这两撇髦，现在照照镜子！”
“巴利巴里骑士！”我喊，笑破了肚子，随即学他那条僵膝的走法，在屋里踱起他的步子来。
第二天，我到波茨多夫先生处回报，说起了新近赌钱的普鲁士青年军官们；不出所料，他答说国王已决意把骑士驱逐出境。
“他是个吝啬的老财迷，”我答，“两个月统共才给我三个腓特烈，您答应提拨我的话，希望您记得！”
“呃，就你打听来的这点货色，三个腓特烈已经太多了。”上尉冷笑。
“没攒出更多的货可不是我的错，”我答。“他几时走，先生？”
“后天。你说他早饭后、午饭前驾车兜风。他出来上车的时候，两名宪兵登上车座，车夫自会领命开车。”
“那他的行李呢，先生？”我问。
“哦！随后给他送去。你说的那只装文件的红匣子，我有意亲自看看；正午阅兵后我到客栈。你在那里对谁也别提这件事一个字，在骑士的寓所里等我到。那只箱子咱们得撬。你这笨猎狗——不然你早把钥匙弄到手了！”
我求上尉记着我的好处，就此告辞。当夜我把两支手枪塞进车座底下；我想，第二天那场奇遇，满配得上一章的体面。

## XIX Conclusion / 十九 终章
===Original===

If the world were not composed of a race of ungrateful scoundrels, who share your prosperity while it lasts, and, even when gorged with your venison and Burgundy, abuse the generous giver of the feast, I am sure I merit a good name and a high reputation: in Ireland, at least, where my generosity was unbounded, and the splendour of my mansion and entertainments unequalled by any other nobleman of my time. As long as my magnificence lasted, all the country was free to partake of it; I had hunters sufficient in my stables to mount a regiment of dragoons, and butts of wine in my cellar which would have made whole counties drunk for years. Castle Lyndon became the headquarters of scores of needy gentlemen, and I never rode a-hunting but I had a dozen young fellows of the best blood of the country riding as my squires and gentlemen of the horse. My son, little Castle Lyndon, was a prince; his breeding and manners, even at his early age, showed him to be worthy of the two noble families from whom he was descended: I don’t know what high hopes I had for the boy, and indulged in a thousand fond anticipations as to his future success and figure in the world. But stern Fate had determined that I should leave none of my race behind me, and ordained that I should finish my career, as I see it closing now﻿—poor, lonely, and childless. I may have had my faults; but no man shall dare to say of me that I was not a good and tender father. I loved that boy passionately; perhaps with a blind partiality: I denied him nothing. Gladly, gladly, I swear, would I have died that his premature doom might have been averted. I think there is not a day since I lost him but his bright face and beautiful smiles look down on me out of heaven, where he is, and that my heart does not yearn towards him. That sweet child was taken from me at the age of nine years, when he was full of beauty and promise: and so powerful is the hold his memory has of me that I have never been able to forget him; his little spirit haunts me of nights on my restless solitary pillow; many a time, in the wildest and maddest company, as the bottle is going round, and the song and laugh roaring about, I am thinking of him. I have got a lock of his soft brown hair hanging round my breast now: it will accompany me to the dishonoured pauper’s grave; where soon, no doubt, Barry Lyndon’s worn-out old bones will be laid.

===Chinese===

倘若这世界不是由一伙忘恩负义的恶棍组成——他们趁你走运时来分你的富贵，吃足了你的鹿肉和勃艮第酒，还要咒骂宴席的慷慨主人——那我自信配得上好名声和大名望：至少在爱尔兰，我的慷慨没有边，我的宅邸和排场的阔气，同代的贵族没有一个比得上。我的豪奢维持一日，全国就有一日可来同享；我马厩里的猎马足以装备一团龙骑兵，地窖里的酒桶够几个郡的人喝醉好几年。林登城堡成了几十位穷绅士的总部；我每出猎，必有一打全国最好血统的年轻人做我的扈从和马弁。我的儿子，小林登城堡，是一位亲王；他的教养举止，小小年纪已配得上他出身的两族贵门：我对这孩子抱着多高的指望，对他的前程和日后在世界上的地位，做过一千种痴心的盘算，只有天知道。可严酷的命运判定了我不留一个骨肉在后头，判定了我要这样收场，如我此刻眼见它收拢——贫穷、孤独、无子。我的过错也许有；可谁也别敢说我不是个慈爱的父亲。我狂热地爱那孩子；爱得也许盲目偏心：他要什么我从不拒绝。我起誓，我千情愿万情愿替他一死，换他免于夭折。自失了他，我想没有一天，他那明亮的脸、那好看的笑不从他在的天上望下来，我的心不朝他牵去。那可爱的孩子九岁上离开了我，正当他满怀美好与前程：他的记忆攫住我这样紧，我从没能忘掉他；夜里，在我翻腾的孤枕上，他那小魂灵缠着我；多少回，在最狂最闹的酒席上，酒瓶传着，歌声笑声喧腾，我想的是他。我如今胸口还挂着一绺他柔软的棕发：它将伴我进那不体面的贫民坟；不久，毫无疑问，巴里·林登这副朽骨也将安放在那里。

===Original===

My Bryan was a boy of amazing high spirit (indeed how, coming from such a stock, could he be otherwise?), impatient even of my control, against which the dear little rogue would often rebel gallantly; how much more, then, of his mother’s and the women’s, whose attempts to direct him he would laugh to scorn. Even my own mother (“Mrs. Barry of Lyndon” the good soul now called herself, in compliment to my new family) was quite unable to check him; and hence you may fancy what a will he had of his own. If it had not been for that, he might have lived to this day: he might﻿—but why repine? Is he not in a better place? would the heritage of a beggar do any service to him? It is best as it is﻿—Heaven be good to us!﻿—Alas! that I, his father, should be left to deplore him.
It was in the month of October I had been to Dublin, in order to see a lawyer and a moneyed man who had come over to Ireland to consult with me about some sales of mine and the cut of Hackton timber; of which, as I hated the place and was greatly in want of money, I was determined to cut down every stick. There had been some difficulty in the matter. It was said I had no right to touch the timber. The brute peasantry about the estate had been roused to such a pitch of hatred against me, that the rascals actually refused to lay an axe to the trees; and my agent (that scoundrel Larkins) declared that his life was in danger among them if he attempted any further despoilment (as they called it) of the property. Every article of the splendid furniture was sold by this time, as I need not say; and as for the plate, I had taken good care to bring it off to Ireland, where it now was in the best of keeping﻿—my banker’s, who had advanced six thousand pounds on it: which sum I soon had occasion for.
I went to Dublin, then, to meet the English man of business; and so far succeeded in persuading Mr. Splint, a great shipbuilder and timber-dealer of Plymouth, of my claim to the Hackton timber, that he agreed to purchase it offhand at about one-third of its value, and handed me over five thousand pounds: which, being pressed with debts at the time, I was fain to accept. *He* had no difficulty in getting down the wood, I warrant. He took a regiment of shipwrights and sawyers from his own and the King’s yards at Plymouth, and in two months Hackton Park was as bare of trees as the Bog of Allen.

===Chinese===

我的布赖恩是个心气极高的孩子（出身高门，还能是别的样子么？），连我的管束都嫌烦，这可爱的小捣蛋常好汉似的造反；对他母亲的、女人们的，就更不用说了，谁想指点他，他就笑谁。连我自己的母亲（这位好人如今自称“林登的巴里太太”，恭维我的新门第）也全然辖不住他；由此诸位可以想见他的主意有多大。若不是为了那股子主意，他兴许活到今天：兴许——可何必怨命？他不是在更好的去处么？叫花子的遗产于他何益？这样最好——上天可怜我们！——唉！偏偏留下我这做父亲的来哀恸他。
十月里，我去了一趟都柏林，见一位律师和一位有钱人——对方专程来爱尔兰，同我商谈我的几处变卖和哈克顿林子的采伐；那地方我恨，钱我又大大地缺，我打定主意砍它个一根不剩。这桩事有些周折。人说我无权动那林子。庄园四下的刁民被我激起的仇恨这样深，那伙无赖竟真的拒绝朝树上抡斧子；我的代理人（那个恶棍拉金斯）声言，他若再敢动手“劫掠”——他们的说法——产业，性命难保。华贵家具这时已一件不剩全卖了，这不必说；至于银器，我早小心运到了爱尔兰，如今存放在最稳妥的地方——我的银行家那里，他凭这个预支了我六千镑：这笔款子我不久就有了用场。
于是我去都柏林会那位英国生意人；我居然说动了普利茅斯的大造船商兼木材商斯普林特先生，让他认下我对哈克顿木材的权利，当场同意按约值三分之一买断，付了我五千镑：其时债主催逼，我只得领受。他伐那片木头可没半点难处，我担保。他从普利茅斯自家的和王家的船厂带来整整一团造船工和锯木工，两个月工夫，哈克顿园林光得同艾伦沼泽一样。

===Original===

I had but ill luck with that accursed expedition and money. I lost the greater part of it in two nights’ play at Daly’s, so that my debts stood just as they were before; and before the vessel sailed for Holyhead, which carried away my old sharper of a timber-merchant, all that I had left of the money he brought me was a couple of hundred pounds, with which I returned home very disconsolately: and very suddenly, too, for my Dublin tradesmen were hot upon me, hearing I had spent the loan, and two of my wine-merchants had writs out against me for some thousands of pounds.
I bought in Dublin, according to my promise, however﻿—for when I give a promise I will keep it at any sacrifices﻿—a little horse for my dear little Bryan; which was to be a present for his tenth birthday, that was now coming on: it was a beautiful little animal and stood me in a good sum. I never regarded money for that dear child. But the horse was very wild. He kicked off one of my horse-boys, who rode him at first, and broke the lad’s leg; and, though I took the animal in hand on the journey home, it was only my weight and skill that made the brute quiet.
When we got home I sent the horse away with one of my grooms to a farmer’s house, to break him thoroughly in, and told Bryan, who was all anxiety to see his little horse, that he would arrive by his birthday, when he should hunt him along with my hounds; and I promised myself no small pleasure in presenting the dear fellow to the field that day: which I hoped to see him lead some time or other in place of his fond father. Ah me! never was that gallant boy to ride a fox-chase, or to take the place amongst the gentry of his country which his birth and genius had pointed out for him!
Though I don’t believe in dreams and omens, yet I can’t but own that when a great calamity is hanging over a man he has frequently many strange and awful forebodings of it. I fancy now I had many. Lady Lyndon, especially, twice dreamed of her son’s death; but, as she was now grown uncommonly nervous and vapourish, I treated her fears with scorn, and my own, of course, too. And in an unguarded moment, over the bottle after dinner, I told poor Bryan, who was always questioning me about the little horse, and when it was to come, that it was arrived; that it was in Doolan’s farm, where Mick the groom was breaking him in.

===Chinese===

那趟晦气的差事和那笔钱，我手气坏透了。两晚在戴利俱乐部输掉了大半，债台照旧一分未动；载走我那老滑头木材商的船开往霍利黑德之前，他带来的钱我手上只剩两三百镑，我揣着它灰溜溜回了家：而且回得突然，因为都柏林的商人们听说我把借款花光了，追得很紧，两家酒商已经开出了几张几千镑的拘捕令。
不过在都柏林，我照着许过的诺言——我许下的诺言，不惜任何代价也要兑现——给我亲爱的布赖恩买了一匹小马；那是他十岁生日的礼物，眼看就要到了：那是匹漂亮的小牲口，也花了我好一笔钱。为那亲爱的孩子，我从不把钱放在心上。可那马野得很。先骑它的小马夫被它一脚踢下来，摔断了腿；回家路上虽由我亲自执辔，全凭我的骑术和分量才镇住那畜生。
到家后我把马交给一个马夫送到一家农户去好好驯，告诉急不可耐想见小马的布赖恩，马他生日前准到，到时候可以随我的猎犬出猎；我暗自盘算着那天在田野上把这位亲爱的小家伙引荐给众人，不知有多少得意：我指望他有朝一日接他慈父的班，领这一方乡绅。唉！那英气勃勃的孩子，再不能纵马逐狐，再不能在他血脉与天资早已指明的乡绅之列里占一个座位了！
我虽不信梦兆，可不能不承认：大难悬顶的人，常有许多古怪可怖的预感。如今想来，我那时就有许多。林登夫人尤其两次梦见儿子死；可她如今变得异常地神经质、爱闹虚火，我眗笑她的恐惧，自然也眗笑我自己的。一次饭后持杯，口没遮拦，我告诉了可怜的布赖恩——他老缠着我问小马、问它几时来——说马到了，在杜兰家的田庄，马夫米克正驯着。

===Original===

“Promise me, Bryan,” screamed his mother, “that you will not ride the horse except in company of your father.” But I only said, “Pooh, madam, you are an ass!” being angry at her silly timidity, which was always showing itself in a thousand disagreeable ways now; and, turning round to Bryan, said, “I promise your Lordship a good flogging if you mount him without my leave.”
I suppose the poor child did not care about paying this penalty for the pleasure he was to have, or possibly thought a fond father would remit the punishment altogether; for the next morning, when I rose rather late, having sat up drinking the night before, I found the child had been off at daybreak, having slipt through his tutor’s room (this was Redmond Quin, our cousin, whom I had taken to live with me), and I had no doubt but that he was gone to Doolan’s farm.
I took a great horsewhip and galloped off after him in a rage, swearing I would keep my promise. But, Heaven forgive me! I little thought of it when at three miles from home I met a sad procession coming towards me: peasants moaning and howling as our Irish do, the black horse led by the hand, and, on a door that some of the folk carried, my poor dear dear little boy. There he lay in his little boots and spurs, and his little coat of scarlet and gold. His dear face was quite white, and he smiled as he held a hand out to me, and said painfully, “You won’t whip me, will you, papa?” I could only burst out into tears in reply. I have seen many and many a man dying, and there’s a look about the eyes which you cannot mistake. There was a little drummer-boy I was fond of who was hit down before my company at Kuhnersdorf; when I ran up to give him some water, he looked exactly like my dear Bryan then did﻿—there’s no mistaking that awful look of the eyes. We carried him home and scoured the country round for doctors to come and look at his hurt.
But what does a doctor avail in a contest with the grim invincible enemy? Such as came could only confirm our despair by their account of the poor child’s case. He had mounted his horse gallantly, sat him bravely all the time the animal plunged and kicked, and, having overcome his first spite, ran him at a hedge by the roadside. But there were loose stones at the top, and the horse’s foot caught among them, and he and his brave little rider rolled over together at the other side.

===Chinese===

“答应我，布赖恩，”他母亲尖声喊，“除了同你父亲在一起，不许你骑那匹马。”我只说：“呸，夫人，您是头蠢驴！”我恼她这愚蠢的胆怯——如今它总以一千种不痛快的方式露头；又转向布赖恩说：“不许我许可就上马，我保证您大人吃一顿好鞭子。”
我猜这可怜的孩子没把这代价放在心上，或者认定慈父终究免了罚；因为第二天早晨——头晚我喝到半夜，起得迟——发现孩子天一亮就没影了，从他家庭教师的房里溜出去的（那是雷德蒙·奎因，我们的表亲，我接到家里住的），我毫不怀疑他去了杜兰田庄。
我抓起一根大马鞭，怒冲冲打马追去，赌咒要兑现我的诺言。可上天饶恕我！离家三英里，我哪想到这句诺言应在此处——迎面来了一队凄惨的人：庄稼人按我们爱尔兰的规矩哭嚎着，那匹黑马被人牵着，一副门板上，抬着我的可怜亲爱的孩子。他躺在那里，穿着小马靴、小马刺，穿着红金两色的小外套。他那亲爱的脸惨白，朝我伸出一只手，微笑着，费力地说：“您不会鞭我了吧，爸爸？”我能作的回答只有放声大哭。垂死的人我见得多了，眼睛里有那么一种神气，错不了。库讷斯多夫战斗里，连里有个我喜欢的小鼓手被打倒在阵前；我跑上去给他水喝，他那眼神正同我亲爱的布赖恩此刻一模一样——眼睛里那可怕的神气，错不了。我们把他抬回家，四乡八里搜请医生来看他的伤。
可同那位阴沉的不敌之敌相争，医生何用？来的诸位，只能就这可怜孩子的情况证实我们的绝望。他英武地上了马，那牲口尥蹶子的整段时间，他都勇敢地坐稳了，等它撒过头一阵野，驱它冲向路边一道树篱。可篱顶浮着碎石，马蹄插进石缝，它同它勇敢的小骑手一齐翻滚到了那一边。

===Original===

The people said they saw the noble little boy spring up after his fall and run to catch the horse; which had broken away from him, kicking him on the back, as it would seem, as they lay on the ground. Poor Bryan ran a few yards and then dropped down as if shot. A pallor came over his face, and they thought he was dead. But they poured whisky down his mouth, and the poor child revived: still he could not move; his spine was injured; the lower half of him was dead when they laid him in bed at home. The rest did not last long, God help me! He remained yet for two days with us; and a sad comfort it was to think he was in no pain.
During this time the dear angel’s temper seemed quite to change: he asked his mother and me pardon for any act of disobedience he had been guilty of towards us; he said often he should like to see his brother Bullingdon. “Bully was better than you, papa,” he said; “he used not to swear so, and he told and taught me many good things while you were away.” And, taking a hand of his mother and mine in each of his little clammy ones, he begged us not to quarrel so, but love each other, so that we might meet again in heaven, where Bully told him quarrelsome people never went. His mother was very much affected by these admonitions from the poor suffering angel’s mouth; and I was so too. I wish she had enabled me to keep the counsel which the dying boy gave us.
At last, after two days, he died. There he lay, the hope of my family, the pride of my manhood, the link which had kept me and my Lady Lyndon together. “Oh, Redmond,” said she, kneeling by the sweet child’s body, “do, do let us listen to the truth out of his blessed mouth: and do you amend your life, and treat your poor loving fond wife as her dying child bade you.” And I said I would: but there are promises which it is out of a man’s power to keep; especially with such a woman as her. But we drew together after that sad event, and were for several months better friends.
I won’t tell you with what splendour we buried him. Of what avail are undertakers’ feathers and heralds’ trumpery? I went out and shot the fatal black horse that had killed him, at the door of the vault where we laid my boy. I was so wild, that I could have shot myself too. But for the crime, it would have been better that I should, perhaps; for what has my life been since that sweet flower was taken out of my bosom?

===Chinese===

人们说，他们眼看那高贵的小男孩摔倒后一跃而起，跑去追马；马挣脱了他，看样子在他们倒地时踢中了他的背。可怜的布赖恩跑了几步，就像中了枪似的栽倒。他脸上罩上一层灰白，人们只当他死了。可他们往他嘴里灌了威士忌，可怜的孩子缓了过来：人还是不能动；脊柱伤了；抬回家放上床时，他的下半身已经死了。余下的没有拖多久，上帝怜我！他还同我们待了两天；想想他不觉得疼，也算一个惨然的安慰。
那两天里，这亲爱的天使性子仿佛全变了：他为忤逆过母亲和我，求我们饶恕；他常说想见哥哥布灵登。“布利比你好，爸爸，”他说；“他不像你那样骂人，你不在家的时候，他给我讲了、教了我好多好东西。”他用两只冰凉的小手，一手拉住母亲，一手拉住我，求我们别再吵，要彼此相爱，好在天国重聚——布利告诉他，好吵架的人永远进不了天国。这些话从那受难的小天使口里出来，他母亲大为感动；我也是。可惜她没能让我守住这垂死孩子的忠告。
两天后，他死了。他躺在那里——我家门的希望，我盛年的骄傲，维系我和林登夫人的那条纽带。“哦，雷德蒙，”她跪在爱儿尸身旁说，“听一听、听一听他蒙福口中的真话吧：改一改你的生活，照你垂死的孩子嘱咐的，好好待你那可怜的、爱你的、疼你的妻子。”我说我会的：可有些诺言，人力守不住；尤其对着她这样的女人。不过那场伤心事以后我们亲近了些，有几个月是较好的朋友。
我们怎样风光地葬了他，我不说了。棺材铺的羽毛、纹章铺的破烂，有何用处？我走到安放我孩子的墓窖门口，一枪打死了那匹害他丧命的黑马。我疯得几乎想连自己也一枪打死。若不算罪，那样也许倒好些；自从那朵鲜花从我怀中摘去，我的日子成了什么？

===Original===

A succession of miseries, wrongs, disasters, and mental and bodily sufferings which never fell to the lot of any other man in Christendom.
Lady Lyndon, always vapourish and nervous, after our blessed boy’s catastrophe became more agitated than ever, and plunged into devotion with so much fervour, that you would have fancied her almost distracted at times. She imagined she saw visions. She said an angel from heaven had told her that Bryan’s death was as a punishment to her for her neglect of her firstborn. Then she would declare Bullingdon was alive; she had seen him in a dream. Then again she would fall into fits of sorrow about his death, and grieve for him as violently as if he had been the last of her sons who had died, and not our darling Bryan; who, compared to Bullingdon, was what a diamond is to a vulgar stone. Her freaks were painful to witness, and difficult to control. It began to be said in the country that the Countess was going mad. My scoundrelly enemies did not fail to confirm and magnify the rumour, and would add that I was the cause of her insanity: I had driven her to distraction, I had killed Bullingdon, I had murdered my own son; I don’t know what else they laid to my charge. Even in Ireland their hateful calumnies reached me: my friends fell away from me. They began to desert my hunt, as they did in England, and when I went to race or market found sudden reasons for getting out of my neighbourhood.
