No man on the European Continent held a higher position than Redmond Barry then; and when the Duke of Courland lost, he was pleased to say that we had won nobly; and so we had, and spent nobly what we won.
At this period my uncle, who attended mass every day regularly, always put ten florins into the box. Wherever we went, the tavern-keepers made us more welcome than royal princes. We used to give away the broken meat from our suppers and dinners to scores of beggars who blessed us. Every man who held my horse or cleaned my boots got a ducat for his pains. I was, I may say, the author of our common good fortune, by putting boldness into our play. Pippi was a fainthearted fellow, who was always cowardly when he began to win. My uncle (I speak with great respect of him) was too much of a devotee, and too much of a martinet at play ever to win *greatly*. His moral courage was unquestionable, but his daring was not sufficient. Both of these my seniors very soon acknowledged me to be their chief, and hence the style of splendour I have described.
I have mentioned H.I.H. the Princess Frederica Amelia, who was affected by my success, and shall always think with gratitude of the protection with which that exalted lady honoured me.

===Chinese===

想当年我们迎战那位胆大包天的阿列克西斯·科斯洛夫斯基，一注赢了他7,000路易；那一场倘若输了，我们第二天便得上街讨饭；他输了，不过典掉一座庄子、几百个农奴，小焉者也。在托普利茨，库尔兰公爵带着14名仆从，每人拎4袋弗罗林，向我们赌庄叫阵，要同那些封口的袋子对赌，我们怎样答复的？“爵爷，”我们说，“我们庄上现银不过80,000弗罗林；开3个月期票，可以凑到200,000。殿下的钱袋若不满80,000，我们奉陪。”我们果真奉陪了；鏖战11个钟头，庄本一度只剩203个达克特，末了我们从他手里赢了17,000弗罗林。这*才*称得上胆气了吧？这*一行当*，难道不需要技巧、坚忍和勇敢？4顶戴王冠的头颅临桌观局，还有一位帝国公主——当我翻出红心爱司，惹得“帕罗利”热泪滚滚。彼时彼刻，欧洲大陆上没有谁的地位高过雷德蒙·巴里；库尔兰公爵输了，还屈尊称许我们赢得高贵；我们也确乎赢得高贵，赢来的钱也花得高贵。
那一阵子，我叔叔天天准点到堂望弥撒，捐箱里从不吝惜10个弗罗林。无论行到何处，酒馆掌柜迎我们比迎王孙公子还热络。我们正餐夜宴的残肴剩馔，常散给几十名乞丐，他们都为我们祝福。替我牵过马、替我擦过靴的，人人得一枚达克特的辛苦钱。可以说，我们这份共同的财运是我一手做成的——我把胆气注入了我们的赌局。皮皮是个软心肠的孬种，一开头赢钱，反倒先怯了阵。我叔叔（我说这话满怀着敬意）过于虔信，上了赌桌又过于拘泥成法，永远赢不到*大*钱。他持守道义的勇气无可置疑，只是胆略不足。这两位前辈不久便公推我为首，我前面描写的这一派豪奢，正由此而来。
我已提到过弗雷德里卡·阿梅莉亚公主殿下，她为我的成功所动；那位高贵的夫人在我身上施与的恩遇，我也将永远怀着感激追念。

===Original===

She was passionately fond of play, as indeed were the ladies of almost all the Courts in Europe in those days, and hence would often arise no small trouble to us; for the truth must be told, that ladies love to play, certainly, but not to *pay*. The point of honour is not understood by the charming sex; and it was with the greatest difficulty, in our peregrinations to the various Courts of Northern Europe, that we could keep them from the table, could get their money if they lost, or, if they paid, prevent them from using the most furious and extraordinary means of revenge. In those great days of our fortune, I calculate that we lost no less than fourteen thousand louis by such failures of payment. A princess of a ducal house gave us paste instead of diamonds, which she had solemnly pledged to us; another organised a robbery of the Crown jewels, and would have charged the theft upon us, but for Pippi’s caution, who had kept back a note of hand “her High Transparency” gave us, and sent it to his ambassador; by which precaution I do believe our necks were saved. A third lady of high (but not princely) rank, after I had won a considerable sum in diamonds and pearls from her, sent her lover with a band of cutthroats to waylay me; and it was only by extraordinary courage, skill, and good luck, that I escaped from these villains, wounded myself, but leaving the chief aggressor dead on the ground: my sword entered his eye and broke there, and the villains who were with him fled, seeing their chief fall. They might have finished me else, for I had no weapon of defence.
Thus it will be seen that our life, for all its splendour, was one of extreme danger and difficulty, requiring high talents and courage for success; and often, when we were in a full vein of success, we were suddenly driven from our ground on account of some freak of a reigning prince, some intrigue of a disappointed mistress, or some quarrel with the police minister. If the latter personage were not bribed or won over, nothing was more common than for us to receive a sudden order of departure; and so, perforce, we lived a wandering and desultory life.
Though the gains of such a life are, as I have said, very great, yet the expenses are enormous.

===Chinese===

她酷嗜牌戏——那年月，欧洲几乎各国宫廷的夫人们大都如此——由此便常给我们招来不小的麻烦；因为实话实说：太太小姐们爱赌，固然千真万确，却不爱*付账*。荣誉的规矩，这可爱的性别是不甚了了的；我们游历北欧各国宫廷时，费尽九牛二虎之力，才拦得住她们上桌；她们输了，才讨得回钱；她们付了账，又拦不住她们使出最凶悍、最离奇的报复手段。在我们鸿运亨通的那段大日子里，我算下来，单是这类收不回的账，我们便损失了不下14,000路易。一位公爵王族的公主，交不出郑重抵押给我们的钻石，竟拿假宝石来充数；另一位设局盗了王冠上的珠宝，险些把这窃案栽到我们头上，多亏皮皮存了个心眼，扣下“透亮殿下”亲手立给我们的一张期票，送到了他本国大使手里；我深信，正是这一手谨慎，救了我们的脖颈。第三位爵位很高（却非王族）的夫人，在我从她手里赢走一大注钻石珍珠之后，派她的情人领着一伙刺客半路埋伏我；全凭过人的胆量、剑术加好运气，我才从那伙恶徒手中脱身：我自己带了一处伤，却教为首的凶徒横尸当场——我的剑刺进他的眼窝，断在里头；余党见头领倒地，一哄而散。不然他们准能结果了我，因为我手里再无兵刃护身。
由此可见，我们这行生涯，排场尽管堂皇，却是险到极处、难到极处，非有上乘的才具与胆魄不能成功；多少回我们正连战连捷，忽因在位君侯的一时兴到、某个失意情妇的诡计，或是同警务大臣的一场龃龉，被逐出地盘。这后一位大人若是没打点到、没拉拢住，再没有比一道限期离境的突如其来之令更常见的了；于是我们只得常年过着萍踪靡定的生涯。
这种生涯，进项固然，如我所说，大得很，花销却也大得骇人。

===Original===

Our appearance and retinue was too splendid for the narrow mind of Pippi, who was always crying out at my extravagance, though obliged to own that his own meanness and parsimony would never have achieved the great victories which my generosity had won. With all our success, our capital was not very great. That speech to the Duke of Courland, for instance, was a mere boast as far as the two hundred thousand florins at three months were concerned. We had no credit, and no money beyond that on our table, and should have been forced to fly if his Highness had won and accepted our bills. Sometimes, too, we were hit very hard. A bank is a certainty, *almost*; but now and then a bad day will come; and men who have the courage of good fortune, at least, ought to meet bad luck well: the former, believe me, is the harder task of the two.
One of these evil chances befell us in the Duke of Baden’s territory, at Mannheim. Pippi, who was always on the lookout for business, offered to make a bank at the inn where we put up, and where the officers of the Duke’s cuirassiers supped; and some small play accordingly took place, and some wretched crowns and louis changed hands: I trust, rather to the advantage of these poor gentlemen of the army, who are surely the poorest of all devils under the sun.
But, as ill luck would have it, a couple of young students from the neighbouring University of Heidelberg, who had come to Mannheim for their quarter’s revenue, and so had some hundred of dollars between them, were introduced to the table, and, having never played before, began to win (as is always the case). As ill luck would have it, too, they were tipsy, and against tipsiness I have often found the best calculations of play fail entirely. They played in the most perfectly insane way, and yet won always. Every card they backed turned up in their favour. They had won a hundred louis from us in ten minutes; and, seeing that Pippi was growing angry and the luck against us, I was for shutting up the bank for the night, saying the play was only meant for a joke, and that now we had had enough.

===Chinese===

我们这副排场和随从阵仗，皮皮那副小心眼的见识看不过去，总嚷嚷我奢侈，却又不得不认账：靠他那份悭吝小气，一辈子也赢不来我凭慷慨豪爽赢下的那些大捷。饶是我们百战百胜，本钱却算不得雄厚。比如对库尔兰公爵的那番话，所谓3个月期票200,000弗罗林，纯属虚声。我们既没有赊借的门路，桌上之外也没有一个子儿，殿下倘若赢了、收了我们的期票，我们便只有一逃了之。有时我们也挨狠命的闷棍。坐庄是一桩稳当买卖，*差不多*稳当；可恶日子时不时总有一两日；至少，有胆量承当好运的人，也该有气度承当厄运：请信我，这两桩差事里，前一件更难。
在巴登公爵的领地曼海姆，我们便撞上了这样一场厄运。皮皮素来四处寻摸生意，提议在我们落脚的旅馆里开一庄小赌——公爵的胸甲骑兵军官们常在那家旅馆用晚餐——于是小小赌了几场，几枚可怜的克朗和路易换了主人：我相信，便宜多半落在了那班军中可怜的绅士们手里，普天之下，实在再没有比他们更穷的可怜虫了。
偏偏倒运，邻府海德堡大学的两个年轻学生到曼海姆来领这一季的进项，两人身上凑了好几百块银元，被人引到了赌桌上；他们从未上过赌场，一坐下便赢（新手从来如此）。也偏偏倒运，他们都喝醉了；而依我多年的经验，再精妙的赌局盘算，一碰上醉意便全盘失灵。他们赌得全无章法到了疯狂的地步，可偏偏张张都赢。他们押哪张，哪张便开出来。10分钟工夫，他们已从我们手里赢了100路易；我见皮皮上了火、运势又不顺，便主张当夜封庄，只说赌本图个玩笑，如今尽兴了。

===Original===

But Pippi, who had quarrelled with me that day, was determined to proceed, and the upshot was, that the students played and won more; then they lent money to the officers, who began to win, too; and in this ignoble way, in a tavern room thick with tobacco-smoke, across a deal table besmeared with beer and liquor, and to a parcel of hungry subalterns and a pair of beardless students, three of the most skilful and renowned players in Europe lost seventeen hundred louis! I blush now when I think of it. It was like Charles XII or Richard Coeur de Lion falling before a petty fortress and an unknown hand (as my friend Mr. Johnson wrote), and was, in fact, a most shameful defeat.
Nor was this the only defeat. When our poor conquerors had gone off, bewildered with the treasure which fortune had flung in their way (one of these students was called the Baron de Clootz, perhaps he who afterwards lost his head at Paris), Pippi resumed the quarrel of the morning, and some exceedingly high words passed between us. Among other things I recollect I knocked him down with a stool, and was for flinging him out of the window; but my uncle, who was cool, and had been keeping Lent with his usual solemnity, interposed between us, and a reconciliation took place, Pippi apologising and confessing he had been wrong.
I ought to have doubted, however, the sincerity of the treacherous Italian; indeed, as I never before believed a word that he said in his life, I know not why I was so foolish as to credit him now, and go to bed, leaving the keys of our cashbox with him. It contained, after our loss to the cuirassiers, in bills and money, near upon £8,000 sterling. Pippi insisted that our reconciliation should be ratified over a bowl of hot wine, and I have no doubt put some soporific drug into the liquor; for my uncle and I both slept till very late the next morning, and woke with violent headaches and fever: we did not quit our beds till noon. He had been gone twelve hours, leaving our treasury empty; and behind him a sort of calculation, by which he strove to make out that this was his share of the profits, and that all the losses had been incurred without his consent.
Thus, after eighteen months, we had to begin the world again. But was I cast down? No.

===Chinese===

可皮皮那天同我拌过嘴，铁了心要赌下去。结局是：学生越赌越赢；他们又借钱给军官们，军官们也赢开了；就这样，在一间烟气腾腾的酒馆房间里，隔着一张泼满啤酒酒渍的松木桌，对着一伙饥肠辘辘的下级军官和两个嘴上无毛的学生，欧洲最负盛名的三位赌坛高手，输了1,700路易！如今想起这事我还脸红。这好比查理十二世或狮心王理查殒命于一座无名小堡垒、一个无名小卒之手（我朋友约翰逊先生如此写过），实实在在是一场奇耻大辱。
败仗还不止这一场。我们那几位可怜的征服者，被命运劈面掷来的财宝弄得晕头转向，扬长而去之后（其中一个学生唤作克卢茨男爵，也许就是后来在巴黎丢掉脑袋的那一位），皮皮重把清晨的口角拾了起来，我们之间当下恶言相加。记得诸般情节之中，有一桩是我抄起一只圆凳把他掼翻在地，还要把他从窗口掷出去；亏得我叔叔头脑冷静——他正以素来的庄严守着四旬斋——横身隔在中间，一场和解方才达成：皮皮赔了不是，认了错。
然而我本该疑一疑这意大利奸人的诚意；说来也怪，他生平的话我从不曾信过半个字，不知怎么这回竟糊涂到信了他，把钱箱钥匙留在他手上，自家上床睡了。输给胸甲骑兵之后，那箱子里票据连同现钱，将近8,000英镑。皮皮非要斟一碗热酒，把这番和解坐实不可；我毫不怀疑他在酒里搀了蒙汗药：因为叔叔同我一觉直睡到第二天日上三竿，醒来头痛欲裂，周身发热，挨到晌午才离床。皮皮已经去了12个钟头，金库给他卷了个精光；身后只留下一页算草，左涂右算，务要证明这是他名下应得的红利，而一切亏空都是不曾禀过他闯下的祸。
就这样，18个月的营生过后，我们只得重打锣鼓另开张。可我灰心了么？没有。

===Original===

Our wardrobes still were worth a very large sum of money; for gentlemen did not dress like parish-clerks in those days, and a person of fashion would often wear a suit of clothes and a set of ornaments that would be a shop-boy’s fortune; so, without repining for one single minute, or saying a single angry word (my uncle’s temper in this respect was admirable), or allowing the secret of our loss to be known to a mortal soul, we pawned three-fourths of our jewels and clothes to Moses Löwe the banker, and with the produce of the sale, and our private pocket-money, amounting in all to something less than 800 louis, we took the field again.

===Chinese===

好在我们的衣箱行头仍值一大注钱；那年头的绅士，穿著可不像教区文书那般寒酸，一位时麾人物的衣裳首饰，往往抵得上铺子里小伙计的身家；于是，不曾有一分钟的怨叹，不曾吐一句气话（这方面我叔叔的涵养令人叹服），也不曾让我们折本的秘密走漏给一个活人知晓，我们把四分之三的珠宝衣饰押给了银行家摩西·勒韦，变卖的价款，连同各自的私房零用，统共凑得不足800路易，重开旗鼓，再上战场。

## XIII I Continue My Career as a Man of Fashion / 十三 我继续我的时兴人营生
===Original===

I find I have already filled up many scores of pages, and yet a vast deal of the most interesting portion of my history remains to be told, viz. that which describes my sojourn in the kingdoms of England and Ireland, and the great part I played there; moving among the most illustrious of the land, myself not the least distinguished of the brilliant circle. In order to give due justice to this portion of my Memoirs, then﻿—which is more important than my foreign adventures can be (though I could fill volumes with interesting descriptions of the latter)﻿—I shall cut short the account of my travels in Europe, and of my success at the Continental Courts, in order to speak of what befell me at home. Suffice it to say that there is not a capital in Europe, except the beggarly one of Berlin, where the young Chevalier de Balibari was not known and admired; and where he has not made the brave, the highborn, and the beautiful talk of him. I won 80,000 roubles from Potemkin at the Winter Palace at Petersburg, which the scoundrelly favourite never paid me; I have had the honour of seeing his Royal Highness the Chevalier Charles Edward as drunk as any porter at Rome; my uncle played several matches at billiards against the celebrated Lord C﻿—﻿— at Spa, and I promise you did not come off a loser. In fact, by a neat stratagem of ours, we raised the laugh against his Lordship, and something a great deal more substantial. My Lord did not know that the Chevalier Barry had a useless eye; and when, one day, my uncle playfully bet him odds at billiards that he would play him with a patch over one eye, the noble lord, thinking to bite us (he was one of the most desperate gamblers that ever lived), accepted the bet, and we won a very considerable amount of him.
Nor need I mention my successes among the fairer portion of the creation. One of the most accomplished, the tallest, the most athletic, and the handsomest gentlemen of Europe, as I was then, a young fellow of my figure could not fail of having advantages, which a person of my spirit knew very well how to use. But upon these subjects I am dumb. Charming Schuvaloff, black-eyed Sczotarska, dark Valdez, tender Hegenheim, brilliant Langeac!﻿—ye gentle hearts that knew how to beat in old times for the warm young Irish gentleman, where are you now?

===Chinese===

我发觉自己已写满了数十页，而生平中最有趣的那一大段还没讲：即我在英格兰、爱尔兰两国侨居的经历，和我在那里扮演的重要角色——出入全国最显赫的人物之间，而我自己在那圈光华里也不算最暗的一个。为给回忆录的这一部分以应得的笔墨——它比我的海外历险重要得多（虽然后者我也能写出好几卷有趣的描写）——我且把在欧洲（Europe）的游历、在大陆宫廷的得意略过去，讲讲国内落在我头上的事。只需说一句：欧洲除柏林那座乞丐都城之外，没有一座京城里，小巴利巴里骑士不是无人不知、无人不羡的；没有一处，他不曾叫勇士、贵种和美人拿他当话讲。在彼得堡冬宫，我从波将金手里赢了八万卢布——那无赖宠臣至今没付；我有幸在罗马见过殿下查理·爱德华骑士醉得同任何一个脚夫一样；我伯父在斯帕同著名的C——勋爵赛过几场台球，我担保，没输。说真的，靠我们一条妙计，我们叫勋爵大人成了笑柄，还搭上比笑柄实在得多的一些东西。勋爵不知道巴里骑士有一只眼是废的；一天，我伯父打趣同他赌台球，赌注带彩：他蒙一只眼同他打。这位贵勋爵只想咬我们一口（他是天下最亡命的赌徒之一），应了赌；我们从他身上赢下相当可观的一笔。
至于我在造物那较美的一半中间的战绩，也不必细表。当时的我，是欧洲最有教养、最高大、最矫健、最英俊的绅士之一；我这副身板的年轻人，不可能没有种种便宜，而以我的气魄，又深知怎么用这些便宜。可在这些题目上，我哑口。迷人的舒瓦洛夫，黑眼睛的肖塔尔斯卡，深色的瓦尔德斯，温柔的赫根海姆，明艳的朗热亚克！——你们这些曾为热烈年轻的爱尔兰绅士怦然跳动过的温柔心儿，如今都在哪儿？

===Original===

Though my hair has grown grey now, and my sight dim, and my heart cold with years, and ennui, and disappointment, and the treachery of friends, yet I have but to lean back in my armchair and think, and those sweet figures come rising up before me out of the past, with their smiles, and their kindnesses, and their bright tender eyes! There are no women like them now﻿—no manners like theirs! Look you at a bevy of women at the Prince’s, stitched up in tight white satin sacks, with their waists under their arms, and compare them to the graceful figures of the old time! Why, when I danced with Coralie de Langeac at the fêtes on the birth of the first Dauphin at Versailles, her hoop was eighteen feet in circumference, and the heels of her lovely little *mules* were three inches from the ground; the lace of my jabot was worth a thousand crowns, and the buttons of my amaranth velvet coat alone cost eighty thousand livres. Look at the difference now! The gentlemen are dressed like boxers, Quakers, or hackney-coachmen; and the ladies are not dressed at all. There is no elegance, no refinement; none of the chivalry of the old world, of which I form a portion. Think of the fashion of London being led by a Br-mm-l!4 a nobody’s son: a low creature, who can no more dance a minuet than I can talk Cherokee; who cannot even crack a bottle like a gentleman; who never showed himself to be a man with his sword in his hand: as we used to approve ourselves in the good old times, before that vulgar Corsican upset the gentry of the world! Oh, to see the Valdez once again, as on that day I met her first driving in state, with her eight mules and her retinue of gentlemen, by the side of yellow Mançanares! Oh, for another drive with Hegenheim, in the gilded sledge, over the Saxon snow! False as Schuvaloff was, ’twas better to be jilted by her than to be adored by any other woman. I can’t think of any one of them without tenderness. I have ringlets of all their hair in my poor little museum of recollections. Do you keep mine, you dear souls that survive the turmoils and troubles of near half a hundred years? How changed its colour is now, since the day Sczotarska wore it round her neck, after my duel with Count Bjernaski, at Warsaw.
I never kept any beggarly books of accounts in those days. I had no debts. I paid royally for everything I took; and I took everything I wanted.

===Chinese===

如今我的头发已灰，眼睛已花，心已叫岁月、叫厌倦、叫失意、叫朋友的负义焐冷了；可我只要往安乐椅里一靠，一想，那些甜蜜的身影就从往昔里升起在我眼前，带着她们的笑、她们的好、她们明亮的温柔的眼睛！如今再没有那样的女人——再没有那样的举止！你去看亲王府上那一群裹在紧绷绷白缎袍里的女人，腰提到了胳肢窝底下，再拿她们同旧日那裊娜的身影比一比！想当年，凡尔赛庆贺头一位王太孙诞生的庆典上，我同科拉莉·德·朗热亚克跳舞，她的裙环周长十八英尺，她那双可爱小山羊鞋的鞋跟离地三寸；我胸前花边的领结值一千克朗，单我那件苋红色天鹅绒外衣上的纽扣，就花掉八万里弗尔。再看如今的差别！绅士们穿得像拳师、教友会徒、雇马车夫；女士们干脆等于没穿。没有雅致，没有精微；没有旧世界——我也是其中一份子——的骑士风。想想伦敦的时髦竟由一个布—尔—尔来领！4一个无名小卒的儿子：一个下作东西，跳小步舞的本事，比我讲彻罗基话的本事高不到哪儿去；连像绅士那样开一瓶酒都不会；一辈子不曾手中有剑地证明自己是个男人——在往日的好时光里，我们都是那样自证的，直到那个科西嘉的粗人把世上的绅士翻了船！哦，再看一眼瓦尔德斯吧，像我初见她那天，衣香鬓影，八匹骡子、一队绅士前呼后拥，行在黄沙的曼萨纳雷斯河边！哦，再同赫根海姆乘一次镀金的雪橇，滑过萨克森的雪原！舒瓦洛夫尽管负心，被她甩，也强似被任何别的女人爱。想起她们中的哪一个，我心里都是软的。在我那可怜的小小回忆博物馆里，藏着她们每人的一绺头发。你们呢，熬过了将近半世纪风浪与患难的亲爱灵魂，可还藏着我的？如今它的颜色变了多少——自从肖塔尔斯卡在我华沙同比耶纳斯基伯爵决斗之后，把它戴在颈上的那天。
那些日子我从不记什么寒酸的账。我不欠债。拿什么，我付的都是王爷价；我要什么，就拿什么。

===Original===

My income must have been very large. My entertainments and equipages were those of a gentleman of the highest distinction; nor let any scoundrel presume to sneer because I carried off and married my Lady Lyndon (as you shall presently hear), and call me an adventurer, or say I was penniless, or the match unequal. Penniless! I had the wealth of Europe at my command. Adventurer! So is a meritorious lawyer or a gallant soldier; so is every man who makes his own fortune an adventurer. My profession was play: in which I was then unrivalled. No man could play with me through Europe, *on the square*; and my income was just as certain (during health and the exercise of my profession) as that of a man who draws on his Three-per-cents, or any fat squire whose acres bring him revenue. Harvest is not more certain than the effect of skill is: a crop is a chance, as much as a game of cards greatly played by a fine player: there may be a drought, or a frost, or a hailstorm, and your stake is lost; but one man is just as much an adventurer as another.
In evoking the recollection of these kind and fair creatures I have nothing but pleasure. I would I could say as much of the memory of another lady, who will henceforth play a considerable part in the drama of my life﻿—I mean the Countess of Lyndon; whose fatal acquaintance I made at Spa, very soon after the events described in the last chapter had caused me to quit Germany.
Honoria, Countess of Lyndon, Viscountess Bullingdon in England, Baroness Castle Lyndon of the kingdom of Ireland, was so well known to the great world in her day, that I have little need to enter into her family history; which is to be had in any peerage that the reader may lay his hand on. She was, as I need not say, a countess, viscountess, and baroness in her own right. Her estates in Devon and Cornwall were among the most extensive in those parts; her Irish possessions not less magnificent; and they have been alluded to, in a very early part of these Memoirs, as lying near to my own paternal property in the kingdom of Ireland: indeed, unjust confiscations in the time of Elizabeth and her father went to diminish my acres, while they added to the already vast possessions of the Lyndon family.

===Chinese===

我的进项必定极大。我的宴饮车马，是最高等贵人的排场；也不容哪个恶棍放冷箭，说我“捾走并娶了”林登伯爵夫人（Lady Lyndon）——这个诸位马上要听——就骂我冒险家，说我身无分文、说这桩婚事门不当户不对。身无分文！全欧洲的财富听我调度。冒险家！有功的律师、勇敢的士兵也是冒险家；凡自己挣家业的，个个是冒险家。我的职业是赌，而那时我独步。凭规规矩矩，全欧洲没人赢得了我；我的进项（趁健康、趁开业的当口）同吃三厘公债的人一样稳，同哪个体面乡绅靠田亩收租一样稳。收成未必比手艺的效验更稳：庄稼同高手打好一副牌一样是碰运气；兴许一场旱、一场霜、一场雹，你的本就折了；这一个人同那一个人，一样都是冒险。
追忆这些温存可人的造物，我满怀的只有愉快。换一位太太，我的记忆就不能这样说了；从今往后，她在我这出人生戏里要占重要的一角——我指林登伯爵夫人；上一章讲的那些事逼我离开德国之后不久，我就在斯帕同她结下了那段要命的相识。
霍诺丽亚，林登伯爵夫人，英格兰的布林登子爵夫人，爱尔兰王国的林登堡男爵夫人——她当日在上流社会里那样有名，她的家世我无须多讲；任何一部贵族谱牒，读者伸手就翻得到。不必说，伯爵、子爵、男爵，都是她本人的名衔。她在德文和康沃尔的产业，是那一带数一数二的大；爱尔兰的领地同样豪阔；本书极前面提过，那些地紧挨着我先父在爱尔兰的产业：说真的，伊丽莎白和她父亲年间的非法没收，削了我的田亩，添了林登家本已浩大的产业。

===Original===

The Countess, when I first saw her at the assembly at Spa, was the wife of her cousin, the Right Honourable Sir Charles Reginald Lyndon, Knight of the Bath, and Minister to George II and George III at several of the smaller Courts of Europe. Sir Charles Lyndon was celebrated as a wit and bon vivant: he could write love-verses against Hanbury Williams, and make jokes with George Selwyn; he was a man of *vertu* like Harry Walpole, with whom and Mr. Grey he had made a part of the grand tour; and was cited, in a word, as one of the most elegant and accomplished men of his time.
I made this gentleman’s acquaintance as usual at the play-table, of which he was a constant frequenter. Indeed, one could not but admire the spirit and gallantry with which he pursued his favourite pastime; for, though worn out by gout and a myriad of diseases, a cripple wheeled about in a chair, and suffering pangs of agony, yet you would see him every morning and every evening at his post behind the delightful green cloth: and if, as it would often happen, his own hands were too feeble or inflamed to hold the box, he would call the mains, nevertheless, and have his valet or a friend to throw for him. I like this courageous spirit in a man; the greatest successes in life have been won by such indomitable perseverance.
I was by this time one of the best-known characters in Europe; and the fame of my exploits, my duels, my courage at play, would bring crowds around me in any public society where I appeared. I could show reams of scented paper, to prove that this eagerness to make my acquaintance was not confined to the gentlemen only; but that I hate boasting, and only talk of myself in so far as it is necessary to relate myself’s adventures: the most singular of any man’s in Europe. Well, Sir Charles Lyndon’s first acquaintance with me originated in the right honourable knight’s winning 700 pieces of me at picquet (for which he was almost my match); and I lost them with much good-humour, and paid them: and paid them, you may be sure, punctually. Indeed, I will say this for myself, that losing money at play never in the least put me out of good-humour with the winner, and that wherever I found a superior, I was always ready to acknowledge and hail him.

===Chinese===

我在斯帕的舞会上初见伯爵夫人时，她是她表亲查尔斯·雷金纳德·林登爵士的太太——尊贵的巴斯骑士，乔治二世、三世驻欧洲几个较小宫廷的公使。查尔斯·林登爵士以才子和美食家闻名：写情诗赛得过汉伯里·威廉斯，说笑话接得上乔治·塞尔温；他同亨利·沃尔波尔一样是个雅玩家，同沃尔波尔、同格雷先生一起游学过全欧；一句话，他被推为他那个时代最风雅最全才的人物之一。
我同这位先生的交情照例从赌桌开始——他是赌桌的常客。说真的，你不能不佩服他追逐心头之好的那股气与胆：痛风同百病把他磨垮了，坐着轮椅推来推去，疼得死去活来，可每天早晚，你总见他在那块可爱绿呢后面坚守岗位；倘若——这也常有——他自己的手太虚、肿得握不住骰盒，他照样报点，叫贴身仆人或哪位朋友替他掷。我喜欢男人身上这股狠劲；人生最大的成功，多半是这等不屈不挠挣来的。
这时我已是欧洲数一数二的名人；我历险的名声、我的决斗、我赌桌上的胆量，使我一在任何公开场合露面，人群就围拢来。我能亮出一令一令洒了香水的手纸，证明这份结识我的热忱不限于先生们；可我恨自吹，谈自己只到叙述自己历险所需的限度为止——欧洲任何人的历险都不及我的离奇。总之，查尔斯·林登爵士同我相识，起头是这位尊贵骑士在皮克牌上赢了我七百枚（这门他几乎同我旗鼓相当）；我输得和颜悦色，照付；照付，诸位放心，分毫不欠。说真的，我为自己讲一句：赌桌上输钱从不曾叫我冲赢家变过半分脸色；无论哪里遇上强手，我总乐于认账、乐于致敬。

===Original===

Lyndon was very proud of winning from so celebrated a person, and we contracted a kind of intimacy; which, however, did not for a while go beyond pump-room attentions, and conversations over the supper-table at play: but which gradually increased, until I was admitted into his more private friendship. He was a very free-spoken man (the gentry of those days were much prouder than at present), and used to say to me in his haughty easy way, “Hang it, Mr. Barry, you have no more manners than a barber, and I think my black footman has been better educated than you; but you are a young fellow of originality and pluck, and I like you, sir, because you seem determined to go to the deuce by a way of your own.” I would thank him laughingly for this compliment, and say, that as he was bound to the next world much sooner than I was, I would be obliged to him to get comfortable quarters arranged there for me. He used also to be immensely amused with my stories about the splendour of my family and the magnificence of Castle Brady: he would never tire of listening or laughing at those histories.
“Stick to the trumps, however, my lad,” he would say, when I told him of my misfortunes in the conjugal line, and how near I had been winning the greatest fortune in Germany. “Do anything but marry, my artless Irish rustic” (he called me by a multiplicity of queer names). “Cultivate your great talents in the gambling line; but mind this, that a woman will beat you.”
That I denied; mentioning several instances in which I had conquered the most intractable tempers among the sex.
“They will beat you in the long run, my Tipperary Alcibiades. As soon as you are married, take my word of it, you are conquered. Look at me. I married my cousin, the noblest and greatest heiress in England﻿—married her in spite of herself almost” (here a dark shade passed over Sir Charles Lyndon’s countenance). “She is a weak woman. You shall see her, sir, *how* weak she is; but she is my mistress. She has embittered my whole life. She is a fool; but she has got the better of one of the best heads in Christendom. She is enormously rich; but somehow I have never been so poor as since I married her. I thought to better myself; and she has made me miserable and killed me. And she will do as much for my successor, when I am gone.”
“Has her Ladyship a very large income?” said I.

===Chinese===

林登以赢了这样一个名人得意非凡，我们结下一份交情；起初不外乎泵室里的殷勤、赌桌上夜宵时的谈锋：渐渐加深，直到我进了他更私交的圈子。他是个说话没遮拦的人（那年代的缙绅比今日傲慢得多），惯用他那又倨傲又随和的腔调对我说：“见鬼，巴里先生，你的教养不多于一个剃头匠，我想我的黑脚夫受的教育都比你好；可你是个有原创、有胆量的年轻人，我喜欢你，先生，因为你像是打定了主意，要照你自己的路子下地狱。”我笑着谢他这番夸奖，说：他既注定比我先去那个世界，就烦他先替我安排个舒服的住处。他听我讲我家的煊赫、布雷迪堡的豪阔，也总是乐不可支；那些故事他听不厌，笑不厌。
“记住要紧牌，我的小子，”每当我对他讲我在婚姻线上的失手、讲我离德意志头一份大家业多么近，他就说，“什么都干，就是别结婚，我天真的爱尔兰乡巴佬”（他给我起过一大串怪名字）。“好好使你在赌这门行当里的大才；只记住一样：女人会打败你。”
我不服，举了我降服过的那性别里几头最烈的脾气作证。
“长远算，她们必打败你，我的蒂珀雷里的亚西比德。你一结婚，记住我的话，你就完了。看我。我娶了我表亲，英格兰最高贵最豪的继承女——几乎是不顾她本人娶来的”（说到这里，查尔斯·林登爵士脸上掠过一层暗影）。“她是个软弱的女人。你会见着她的，先生，看看她怎样软弱；可她是我的主子。她把我一辈子搅苦了。她是个傻子；可她斗赢了基督世界里最好的头脑之一。她富得吓人；可不知怎么，自打娶了她，我从没这样穷过。我想往上爬；她把我弄得凄惨，把我杀了。我死之后，她对我的后任也会照样来一遍。”
“夫人的进项很大么？”我说。

===Original===

At which Sir Charles burst out into a yelling laugh, and made me blush not a little at my gaucherie; for the fact is, seeing him in the condition in which he was, I could not help speculating upon the chance a man of spirit might have with his widow.
“No, no!” said he, laughing. “Waugh hawk, Mr. Barry; don’t think, if you value your peace of mind, to stand in my shoes when they are vacant. Besides, I don’t think my Lady Lyndon would *quite* condescend to marry a﻿—”
“Marry a what, sir?” said I, in a rage.
“Never mind what: but the man who gets her will rue it, take my word on’t. A plague on her! had it not been for my father’s ambition and mine (he was her uncle and guardian, and we wouldn’t let such a prize out of the family), I might have died peaceably, at least; carried my gout down to my grave in quiet, lived in my modest tenement in Mayfair, had every house in England open to me; and now, now I have six of my own, and every one of them is a hell to me. Beware of greatness, Mr. Barry. Take warning by me. Ever since I have been married and have been rich, I have been the most miserable wretch in the world. Look at me. I am dying a worn-out cripple at the age of fifty. Marriage has added forty years to my life. When I took off Lady Lyndon, there was no man of my years who looked so young as myself. Fool that I was! I had enough with my pensions, perfect freedom, the best society in Europe; and I gave up all these, and married, and was miserable. Take a warning by me, Captain Barry, and stick to the trumps.”
Though my intimacy with the knight was considerable, for a long time I never penetrated into any other apartments of his hotel but those which he himself occupied. His lady lived entirely apart from him; and it is only curious how they came to travel together at all. She was a goddaughter of old Mary Wortley Montagu: and, like that famous old woman of the last century, made considerable pretensions to be a bluestocking and a *bel esprit*. Lady Lyndon wrote poems in English and Italian, which still may be read by the curious in the pages of the magazines of the day. She entertained a correspondence with several of the European *savans* upon history, science, and ancient languages, and especially theology. Her pleasure was to dispute controversial points with abbés and bishops; and her flatterers said she rivalled Madam Dacier in learning.

===Chinese===

这话引得查尔斯爵士一声长嚎般的大笑，笑得我相当脸红；因为实情是：看他那副光景，我不由得盘算，一个有血性的男人在他寡妇身上有几分指望。
“不，不！”他笑着说，“哇嗬嗬，巴里先生；你若还爱惜你的清宁，趁我这双鞋还空着，趁早别打主意。再说，我想林登夫人也不见得肯屈尊嫁给一个——”
“嫁给一个什么，先生？”我火了。
“什么且不管；娶了她的人有他后悔的，拿我的话作准。这瘟婆娘！若不是我父亲的野心加我的（他是她伯父兼监护人，我们不肯这样一份大注流出家门），我至少可以安安静静死掉；安安静静把我的痛风带进坟，住我梅费尔那所朴素宅子，英格兰家家大门为我敞开；如今呢，如今我有了六所宅子，每一所于我都是一座地狱。当心伟大这东西，巴里先生。拿我作前车。打结婚、发财那天起，我就成了世上最凄惨的可怜虫。瞧我。五十岁，磨成残废在死。婚姻给我这条命添了四十岁。娶林登夫人那时节，同岁数的人里，没有谁瞧着比我年轻的。我多蠢！我有俸金，有完全的自由，有欧洲最好的交际；这些我全扔了，结了婚，落得凄惨。拿我作前车，巴里船长，记住要紧牌。”
我同这位骑士交情虽厚，很长一段日子里，他旅馆里除他自己住的几间，我从不曾进过别的。他的太太同他完全分居；也奇怪这一对怎么会凑在同一趟旅行里。她是老玛丽·沃特利·蒙塔古的教女；同上世纪那位有名的老太太一样，颇有几分自称女才子、女雅士的意思。林登夫人用英文和意大利文写诗，好奇的读者如今还能在当日的杂志页上读到。她同欧洲好几位学者通信，谈历史、科学、古文，尤其神学。她的乐趣是同修道院长、主教们辩争教的难点；捧她的人说，论学问她赛得过达西耶夫人。

===Original===

Every adventurer who had a discovery in chemistry, a new antique bust, or a plan for discovering the philosopher’s stone, was sure to find a patroness in her. She had numberless works dedicated to her, and sonnets without end addressed to her by all the poetasters of Europe, under the name of Lindonira or Calista. Her rooms were crowded with hideous China magots, and all sorts of objects of *vertu*.
No woman piqued herself more upon her principles, or allowed love to be made to her more profusely. There was a habit of courtship practised by the fine gentlemen of those days, which is little understood in our coarse downright times: and young and old fellows would pour out floods of compliments in letters and madrigals, such as would make a sober lady stare were they addressed to her nowadays: so entirely has the gallantry of the last century disappeared out of our manners.
Lady Lyndon moved about with a little court of her own. She had half-a-dozen carriages in her progresses. In her own she would travel with her companion (some shabby lady of quality), her birds, and poodles, and the favourite savant for the time being. In another would be her female secretary and her waiting-women; who, in spite of their care, never could make their mistress look much better than a slattern. Sir Charles Lyndon had his own chariot, and the domestics of the establishment would follow in other vehicles.
Also must be mentioned the carriage in which rode her Ladyship’s chaplain, Mr. Runt, who acted in capacity of governor to her son, the little Viscount Bullingdon﻿—a melancholy deserted little boy, about whom his father was more than indifferent, and whom his mother never saw, except for two minutes at her levée, when she would put to him a few questions of history or Latin grammar; after which he was consigned to his own amusements, or the care of his governor, for the rest of the day.
The notion of such a Minerva as this, whom I saw in the public places now and then, surrounded by swarms of needy abbés and schoolmasters, who flattered her, frightened me for some time, and I had not the least desire to make her acquaintance.

===Chinese===

凡是有新化学发现、新出土半身像、或点金石计划的冒险家，必在她那里找到女恩主。题献给她的著作无数；欧洲的打油诗人给她写的十四行没有个完，称她林多尼拉，或卡莉丝塔。她的房间里挤满丑陋的中国瓷偶和各式雅玩。
论立身的大道理，没有哪位太太比她更自负；论任人向她献殷勤，也没有哪位比她更大方。当年时兴绅士们有一种献殷勤的路数，我们这粗直的年头已不大懂了：少年郎和老先生会倾泻出成河的恭维，信里、情诗里，如今哪位正经太太读了都要瞠目：上个世纪的骑士风，就这样整个从我们风俗里退光了。
林登夫人出行，自带一个小朝廷。她巡游一趟，车驾六七乘。她自己的车里坐着她的女伴（哪位寒酸的命妇）、她的鸟、卷毛狗，和时下的那位宠学者。另一乘里是她的女秘书和侍女们；饶她们怎样经心，也从来没能把她们的主母拾掇得比一个懒婆娘体面多少。查尔斯·林登爵士自有他的轿车；宅子的仆从再乘别的车跟上。
还该提一提夫人的牧师伦特先生坐的那一乘：他兼任她儿子小布林登（Bullingdon）子爵的监护人——一个忧忧郁郁没人管的小男孩，父亲对他漠不关心犹有过之，母亲除了每天接见那两分钟，从不见他；两分钟里问几句历史或拉丁文法，随后这一天就把他还给他自己的玩物，或他的监护人。
这样一位密涅瓦——我如今在公共场合偶尔望见她，身边围着一大群穷院长、穷教书匠，人人捧她——有一阵真把我吓住了，我丝毫没有结识她的意思。

===Original===

I had no desire to be one of the beggarly adorers in the great lady’s train﻿—fellows, half friend, half lackey, who made verses, and wrote letters, and ran errands, content to be paid by a seat in her Ladyship’s box at the comedy, or a cover at her dinner-table at noon. “Don’t be afraid,” Sir Charles Lyndon would say, whose great subject of conversation and abuse was his lady: “my Lindonira will have nothing to do with you. She likes the Tuscan brogue, not that of Kerry. She says you smell too much of the stable to be admitted to ladies’ society; and last Sunday fortnight, when she did me the honour to speak to me last, said, ‘I wonder, Sir Charles Lyndon, a gentleman who has been the King’s ambassador can demean himself by gambling and boozing with low Irish blacklegs!’ Don’t fly in a fury! I’m a cripple, and it was Lindonira said it, not I.”
This piqued me, and I resolved to become acquainted with Lady Lyndon; if it were but to show her Ladyship that the descendant of those Barrys, whose property she unjustly held, was not an unworthy companion for any lady, were she ever so high. Besides, my friend the knight was dying: his widow would be the richest prize in the three kingdoms. Why should I not win her, and, with her, the means of making in the world that figure which my genius and inclination desired? I felt I was equal in blood and breeding to any Lyndon in Christendom, and determined to bend this haughty lady. When I determine, I look upon the thing as done.
My uncle and I talked the matter over, and speedily settled upon a method for making our approaches upon this stately lady of Castle Lyndon. Mr. Runt, young Lord Bullingdon’s governor, was fond of pleasure, of a glass of Rhenish in the garden-houses in the summer evenings, and of a sly throw of the dice when the occasion offered; and I took care to make friends with this person, who, being a college tutor and an Englishman, was ready to go on his knees to anyone who resembled a man of fashion. Seeing me with my retinue of servants, my vis-à-vis and chariots, my valets, my hussar, and horses, dressed in gold, and velvet, and sables, saluting the greatest people in Europe as we met on the course, or at the Spas, Runt was dazzled by my advances, and was mine by a beckoning of the finger.

===Chinese===

我不想加入贵夫人随从里那班寒酸的崇拜者——半友半仆的家伙，做诗，写信，跑腿，得的报酬是喜剧院夫人包厢里的一个座、正午她饭桌上的一个位。“别怕，”查尔斯·林登爵士惯说——他的太太是他谈论和咒骂的大题目——“我的林多尼拉不会理你。她喜欢托斯卡纳腔，不喜欢克里腔。她说你身上马厩味太重，进不得太太们的交际；前个礼拜日——她赏脸同我说话的最后一天——还说：‘真奇怪，查尔斯·林登爵士，当过国王大使的绅士，竟会同爱尔兰下三滥的黑腿子们又赌又灌！’别暴跳！我是瘸子，再说，这是林多尼拉说的，不是我。”
这话激起了我，我拿定主意要结识林登夫人；哪怕只为让夫人知道：她不义霸占着的那份巴里家产业的后人，配得上任何太太做伴，任她门第多高。何况我那位骑士朋友眼看要死：他的寡妇将是三王国里最肥的彩头。我为什么不赢她，连她在手，在世上挣出我的天分和志向所要求的那份局面？我自觉论血统论教养，不输基督世界任何一个林登；我拿定主意要折服这位倨傲的夫人。我一旦拿定，事情就算办成了。
我同伯父把这事合计了一番，很快定了接近林登堡（Castle Lyndon）这位庄严夫人的路数。伦特先生，小布林登勋爵的监护人，好消遣，好夏夜在花园馆子里来一杯莱茵酒，有机会还好偷偷掷一把骰子；我留意同这位结交——他既是学院导师又是英国人，见了像时兴人的，就肯下跪。他见我仆从前呼后拥，双座车、四轮车、贴身仆人、骠骑兵装的马夫、成群的马，遍身金绦、天鹅绒、黑貂，在跑马场、在斯帕，一路同欧洲最了不起的人物頷首为礼，早叫我这份垂青晃花了眼；我手指一勾，他就是我的人。

===Original===

I shall never forget the poor wretch’s astonishment when I asked him to dine, with two counts, off gold plate, at the little room in the casino: he was made happy by being allowed to win a few pieces of us, became exceedingly tipsy, sang Cambridge songs, and recreated the company by telling us, in his horrid Yorkshire French, stories about the gyps, and all the lords that had ever been in his college. I encouraged him to come and see me oftener, and bring with him his little viscount; for whom, though the boy always detested me, I took care to have a good stock of sweetmeats, toys, and picture-books when he came.
I then began to enter into a controversy with Mr. Runt, and confided to him some doubts which I had, and a very very earnest leaning towards the Church of Rome. I made a certain abbé whom I knew write me letters upon transubstantiation, etc., which the honest tutor was rather puzzled to answer. I knew that they would be communicated to his lady, as they were; for, asking leave to attend the English service which was celebrated in her apartments, and frequented by the best English then at the Spa, on the second Sunday she condescended to look at me; on the third she was pleased to reply to my profound bow by a curtsey; the next day I followed up the acquaintance by another obeisance in the public walk; and, to make a long story short, her Ladyship and I were in full correspondence on transubstantiation before six weeks were over. My Lady came to the aid of her chaplain; and then I began to see the prodigious weight of his arguments: as was to be expected. The progress of this harmless little intrigue need not be detailed. I make no doubt every one of my readers has practised similar stratagems when a fair lady was in the case.
I shall never forget the astonishment of Sir Charles Lyndon when, on one summer evening, as he was issuing out to the play-table in his sedan-chair, according to his wont, her Ladyship’s barouche and four, with her outriders in the tawny livery of the Lyndon family, came driving into the courtyard of the house which they inhabited; and in that carriage, by her Ladyship’s side, sat no other than the “vulgar Irish adventurer,” as she was pleased to call him: I mean Redmond Barry, Esquire.

===Chinese===

我永远忘不了那可怜虫第一次应我之邀、同两位伯爵一道、就着金餐具、在赌馆小间吃饭时那副受宠若惊：又让他赢了我们几枚，把他乐得不行，喝得酩酊，唱剑桥歌，又用他那可怕的约克郡法语，给我们讲学院工友的故事，讲他学院里出过的历任贵人，把满座逗得直乐。我怂恿他常来，把他的小子爵也带来；虽说那孩子一向讨厌我，他一来，我必备下满满的糖果、玩物和画书。
随后我开始同伦特先生辩起教义，向他吐露了我的几点疑虑，和一颗非常非常热切倾向罗马公教的心。我叫一位相识的修道院长给我写信，谈变体论等等；这位诚实的导师回起来相当犯难。我知道这些信必会转到他女主人手里——果然转了；因为经我请求，去参加在她公寓里举行的英国礼拜——当时斯帕最好的英国人都去——第二个礼拜日，她屈尊看了我一眼；第三个礼拜日，她赏脸用一屈膝答了我深深一躬；次日我趁热打铁，在公共散步道上又行了一个大礼；长话短说，不出六个礼拜，夫人同我已就变体论通信甚欢。我的夫人赶来给她的牧师助阵；于是我立刻看出他那些论证的千钧分量——这是意料中事。这段无害小计谋的进展，不必细表。我的读者们，凡遇一位可人的太太，想必人人都使过同样的计。
我永远忘不了查尔斯·林登爵士那一惊：一个夏天的傍晚，他照例坐轿子出来上赌桌，夫人的四马大车——前导骑穿着林登家黄褐号衣——驶进他们住处的院子；车里，夫人身旁坐着的，不是别人，正是她口中的“粗俗爱尔兰冒险家”：我是说，雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）绅士。

===Original===

He made the most courtly of his bows, and grinned and waved his hat in as graceful a manner as the gout permitted; and her Ladyship and I replied to the salutation with the utmost politeness and elegance on our parts.
I could not go to the play-table for some time afterwards for Lady Lyndon and I had an argument on transubstantiation, which lasted for three hours; in which she was, as usual, victorious, and, in which her companion, the Honourable Miss Flint Skinner, fell asleep; but when, at last, I joined Sir Charles at the casino, he received me with a yell of laughter, as his wont was, and introduced me to all the company as Lady Lyndon’s interesting young convert. This was his way. He laughed and sneered at everything. He laughed when he was in a paroxysm of pain; he laughed when he won money, or when he lost it: his laugh was not jovial or agreeable, but rather painful and sardonic.
“Gentlemen,” said he to Punter, Colonel Loder, Count du Carreau, and several jovial fellows with whom he used to discuss a flask of champagne and a Rhenish trout or two after play, “see this amiable youth! He has been troubled by religious scruples, and has flown for refuge to my chaplain, Mr. Runt, who has asked for advice from my wife, Lady Lyndon; and, between them both, they are confirming my ingenious young friend in his faith. Did you ever hear of such doctors, and such a disciple?”
“ ’Faith, sir,” said I, “if I want to learn good principles, it’s surely better I should apply for them to your lady and your chaplain than to you!”
“He wants to step into my shoes!” continued the knight.
“The man would be happy who did so,” responded I, “provided there were no chalk-stones included!” At which reply Sir Charles was not very well pleased, and went on with increased rancour. He was always free-spoken in his cups; and, to say the truth, he was in his cups many more times in a week than his doctors allowed.
“Is it not a pleasure, gentlemen,” said he, “for me, as I am drawing near the goal, to find my home such a happy one; my wife so fond of me, that she is even now thinking of appointing a successor? (I don’t mean you precisely, Mr. Barry; you are only taking your chance with a score of others whom I could mention.) Isn’t it a comfort to see her, like a prudent housewife, getting everything ready for her husband’s departure?”

===Chinese===

他行了顶宫廷气的一躬，就痛风容许的程度，体面地咧嘴、挥帽；夫人同我也以我方十二分的客气与风雅还礼。
随后好一阵子我上不了赌桌，因为林登夫人和我就变体论辩了三个钟头；照例她赢；她的女伴，可敬的弗林特·斯金纳小姐，睡着了；等我终于到赌馆同查尔斯爵士会合，他照例一声狂笑接待我，向满座引见：这位是林登夫人有趣的年轻皈依者。他就是这个脾气。样样他都要笑，都要讥。剧痛发作他笑；赢钱他笑，输钱也笑：他的笑不带喜气，不叫人舒服，倒叫人难受，透着讥诣。
“先生们，”他对庞特、洛德上校、迪卡罗伯爵和几位常在牌后共品一瓶香槟、一两条莱茵鳟的快活人说，“瞧这位可爱的少年！他叫宗教的良心折磨了，逃到我的牧师伦特先生那里求庇护；伦特先生又请教了我的太太林登夫人；两位合力，正给我这位乖巧的小朋友坚他的信仰。你们见过这样的博士、这样的门徒么？”
“说真的，先生，”我说，“我要学好道理，向您夫人和您的牧师讨教，总比向您讨教强！”
“他想穿我的鞋！”骑士接着说。
“能穿上的那个人有福气，”我答，“只要鞋里不含痛风石！”这一答查尔斯爵士不大受用，说得更毒了。他三杯下肚照例口没遮拦；说真话，一礼拜里他下肚的次数，远比他的医生准许的多。
“先生们，”他说，“我眼看快到站了，家里这样一团和气，太太这样疼我——这会儿就在张罗着定后任了（我不是单说你，巴里先生；同你一起排队的，我一口气能报出二十个），做丈夫的还没动身，太太已经像个会当家的主妇，样样替他打点妥当——这难道不是一桩安慰？”

===Original===

“I hope you are not thinking of leaving us soon, knight?” said I, with perfect sincerity; for I liked him, as a most amusing companion.
“Not so soon, my dear, as you may fancy, perhaps,” continued he. “Why, man, I have been given over any time these four years; and there was always a candidate or two waiting to apply for the situation. Who knows how long I may keep you waiting?” and he *did* keep me waiting some little time longer than at that period there was any reason to suspect.
As I declared myself pretty openly, according to my usual way, and authors are accustomed to describe the persons of the ladies with whom their heroes fall in love; in compliance with this fashion, I perhaps should say a word or two respecting the charms of my Lady Lyndon. But though I celebrated them in many copies of verses, of my own and other persons’ writing; and though I filled reams of paper in the passionate style of those days with compliments to every one of her beauties and smiles, in which I compared her to every flower, goddess, or famous heroine ever heard of﻿—truth compels me to say that there was nothing divine about her at all. She was very well; but no more. Her shape was fine, her hair dark, her eyes good, and exceedingly active; she loved singing, but performed it as so great a lady should, very much out of tune. She had a smattering of half-a-dozen modern languages, and, as I have said before, of many more sciences than I even knew the names of. She piqued herself on knowing Greek and Latin; but the truth is, that Mr. Runt used to supply her with the quotations which she introduced into her voluminous correspondence. She had as much love of admiration, as strong, uneasy a vanity, and as little heart, as any woman I ever knew. Otherwise, when her son, Lord Bullingdon, on account of his differences with me, ran﻿—but that matter shall be told in its proper time. Finally, my Lady Lyndon was about a year older than myself; though, of course, she would take her Bible oath that she was three years younger.
Few men are so honest as I am; for few will own to their real motives, and I don’t care a button about confessing mine. What Sir Charles Lyndon said was perfectly true. I made the acquaintance of Lady Lyndon with ulterior views. “Sir,” said I to him, when, after the scene described and the jokes he made upon me, we met alone, “let those laugh that win.

“您不至于打算很快就丢下我们吧，骑士？”我说，十足的诚心；我喜欢他，他是顶有趣的一个伴。
“没有那么快，亲爱的，未必如你想的那么快，”他接着说，“嗦，伙计，这四年里我随时被人报过病危；候补的人永远有一两个在那儿递呈子。谁知道还叫你等多久？”——他也果真叫我多等了一阵，比那时节谁有理由料想的都久。
我既照素来的脾气放言无忌；而作家们惯于给他们主人公爱上的太太描画容貌——依着这个时尚，关于林登夫人的姿色，我或者也该说上一两句。可虽然我用自己同旁人的手笔，写了许多诗行歌咏她；虽然我拿当年那火热的文体，写满一令一令的纸，把她的一颦一笑逐项恭维，把她比作听过的每一朵花、每一位女神、每一个著名女杰——实话逼着我说：她身上没有半点神圣。她是很不坏；仅此而已。身段好，头发黑，眼睛不错，极活泛；她爱唱，可唱得同她这样大的贵妇相称——荒腔走板得厉害。半打现代语言她都懂个皮毛；又如前述，好些门科学的名字她都知道，比我认得的还多。她自负懂希腊文拉丁文；实情是，她那浩繁通信里引的书句，都由伦特先生供给。论爱受人追捧、论虚荣之炽与难安、论心肠之薄，我认得的太太里没有谁超过她。其余的——比如她的儿子布林登勋爵，因为我同他的那些过节，一气——但那件事到时候再讲。末了：林登夫人约比我大一岁；当然，她肯按着圣经起誓她小三岁。
像我这样诚实的人少有；肯认自己真实动机的人少有，而我认我的一丝不挂。查尔斯·林登爵士说的全是实情。我结识林登夫人，怀着下一步的算盘。“先生，”那一场戏、那一番取笑过后，我们单独见面时我对他说，“谁笑到最后谁笑。

===Original===

You were very pleasant upon me a few nights since, and on my intentions regarding your lady. Well, if they *are* what you think they are﻿—if I *do* wish to step into your shoes, what then? I have no other intentions than you had yourself. I’ll be sworn to muster just as much regard for my Lady Lyndon as you ever showed her; and if I win her and wear her when you are dead and gone, *corbleu*, knight, do you think it will be the fear of your ghost will deter me?”
Lyndon laughed as usual; but somewhat disconcertedly: indeed I had clearly the best of him in the argument, and had just as much right to hunt my fortune as he had.
But one day he said, “If you marry such a woman as my Lady Lyndon, mark my words, you will regret it. You will pine after the liberty you once enjoyed. By George! Captain Barry,” he added, with a sigh, “the thing that I regret most in life﻿—perhaps it is because I am old, blasé, and dying﻿—is, that I never had a virtuous attachment.”
“Ha! ha! a milkmaid’s daughter!” said I, laughing at the absurdity.
“Well, why not a milkmaid’s daughter? My good fellow, I *was* in love in youth, as most gentlemen are, with my tutor’s daughter, Helena, a bouncing girl; of course older than myself” (this made me remember my own little love-passages with Nora Brady in the days of my early life), “and do you know, sir, I heartily regret I didn’t marry her? There’s nothing like having a virtuous drudge at home, sir; depend upon that. It gives a zest to one’s enjoyments in the world, take my word for it. No man of sense need restrict himself, or deny himself a single amusement for his wife’s sake: on the contrary, if he select the animal properly, he will choose such a one as shall be no bar to his pleasure, but a comfort in his hours of annoyance. For instance, I have got the gout: who tends me? A hired valet, who robs me whenever he has the power. My wife never comes near me. What friend have I? None in the wide world. Men of the world, as you and I are, don’t make friends; and we are fools for our pains. Get a friend, sir, and that friend a woman﻿—a good household drudge, who loves you. *That* is the most precious sort of friendship; for the expense of it is all on the woman’s side. The man needn’t contribute anything. If he’s a rogue, she’ll vow he’s an angel; if he’s a brute, she will like him all the better for his ill-treatment of her.
