前几天您拿我、拿我对夫人的打算开心，开心得很。好——倘若我的打算真是您想的那样；倘若我真想穿您的鞋，又怎样？我除您自己当初抱过的，没有别的打算。我敢起誓：我对林登夫人的敬意，凑得齐您历来给她看过的那么多；等我赢了您、又死了之后赢了她戴上——凭上帝，骑士，您当是怕您的鬼，就能拦住我么？”
林登照例笑；笑得有点局促；说真的，这番道理我分明占了他的上风：我打我的家业，同他打他的一样名正言顺。
可有一天他说：“你若娶了我林登夫人这样的女人，记住我的话，你要后悔。你会想念你一度享有的自由。老天！巴里船长，”他叹一口气又添一句，“我这辈子最悔的一件事——也许因为我老了、腻了、快死了——是我不曾有过一次正经的恋爱。”
“哈！哈！牛奶厂姑娘的女儿！”我笑这荒唐话。
“嗦，牛奶厂姑娘的女儿怎么就不行？我的好朋友，我年轻时同多数绅士一样，恋爱过——爱上我导师的女儿海伦娜，一个壮健的姑娘；当然比我大”（这话叫我记起我早年同小诺拉·布雷迪的那段情事），“先生，你可知道，我真心后悔没娶她？家里有个正经的苦役，什么都比不上，先生；这话你记牢。它给你世上的享乐添一味佐料，信我的话。明白人一点不必为太太拘束自己，不必为她戒一样消遣：恰恰相反，只要选得当，他挑的那一个，该是他行乐无碍、烦闷有慰的一个。比如我害痛风：谁伺候我？雇来的贴身仆人，一得空就偷我。我太太从不进我的门。我有什么朋友？天底下没有一个。你我这样的世界中人交不成朋友；交了也是白痴。去交个朋友，先生，交个女人的——一个爱你的、会当家的苦役。那才是顶金贵的友谊；花费全在女人那一头。男人一个子儿不必出。他若是流氓，她赌咒他是天使；他若是畜生，他越虐待她，她越喜欢他。

===Original===

They like it, sir, these women. They are born to be our greatest comforts and conveniences; our﻿—our moral bootjacks, as it were; and to men in your way of life, believe me such a person would be invaluable. I am only speaking for your bodily and mental comfort’s sake, mind. Why didn’t I marry poor Helena Flower, the curate’s daughter?”
I thought these speeches the remarks of a weakly disappointed man; although since, perhaps, I have had reason to find the truth of Sir Charles Lyndon’s statements. The fact is, in my opinion, that we often buy money very much too dear. To purchase a few thousands a year at the expense of an odious wife, is very bad economy for a young fellow of any talent and spirit; and there have been moments of my life when, in the midst of my greatest splendour and opulence, with half-a-dozen lords at my levée, with the finest horses in my stables, the grandest house over my head, with unlimited credit at my banker’s, and﻿—Lady Lyndon to boot, I have wished myself back a private of Bülow’s, or anything, so as to get rid of her. To return, however, to the story. Sir Charles, with his complication of ills, was dying before us by inches! and I’ve no doubt it could not have been very pleasant to him to see a young handsome fellow paying court to his widow before his own face as it were. After I once got into the house on the transubstantiation dispute, I found a dozen more occasions to improve my intimacy, and was scarcely ever out of her Ladyship’s doors. The world talked and blustered; but what cared I? The men cried fie upon the shameless Irish adventurer; but I have told my way of silencing such envious people: and my sword had by this time got such a reputation through Europe, that few people cared to encounter it. If I can once get my hold of a place, I keep it. Many’s the house I have been to where I have seen the men avoid me. “Faugh! the low Irishman,” they would say. “Bah! the coarse adventurer!” “Out on the insufferable blackleg and puppy!” and so forth. This hatred has been of no inconsiderable service to me in the world; for when I fasten on a man, nothing can induce me to release my hold: and I am left to myself, which is all the better.

===Chinese===

她们就爱这个，先生，这些女人。她们生来就是我们最大的安慰与便利；是我们的——我们的道德上的脱靴器；信我，对走你这条路的人，这样一个人抵得金不换。我纯粹为你身心的舒坦着想，记着。我当年怎么就没娶可怜的海伦娜·弗劳尔，牧区副牧师的女儿？”
我把这些话当作一个病弱的失意人的牢骚；虽然后来，我也许有了理由，尝出查尔斯·林登爵士这些话的实味。依我看，实情是：我们常常把钱买得太贵。为一年几千镑，赔上一个可憎的太太，对任何有才有胆的年轻人都是最坏的算盘；我这一生有过那样的时辰——在我最煊赫最豪阔的顶点，接见厅里站着六位勋爵，马厩里是我最好的马，头顶是我最气派的宅子，银行里是我无限的信用，外加——林登夫人——我倒甘愿回去当比洛团的列兵，当什么都行，只要脱开她。闲话收住。查尔斯爵士带着他一身凑齐的病，正一寸一寸死在我们眼前！一个年轻漂亮的家伙当着他的面追求他的寡妇，我毫不疑心这于他不会十分愉快。自打变体论那场争端把我送进他家门，我又找出一打机会加深交情，几乎不曾出过夫人的门槛。世人议论、咆哮；我管它做什么？男人们骂：无耻的爱尔兰冒险家！可我讲过我镇这类眼红人的法子；何况我的剑这时已名震欧洲，肯来领教的没有几个。我一旦抓住一个地方，就不放手。多少人家，我进门只见男人们躲着我。“呸！下等爱尔兰人。”他们说。“呸！粗坯冒险家！”“滚出去，狂妄的黑腿子、小狗子！”云云。这种仇恨在世上于我颇有大用：因为我一旦咬住一个人，什么都不能叫我松口；而人们索性不理我——那更好。

===Original===

As I told Lady Lyndon in those days, with perfect sincerity, “Calista” (I used to call her Calista in my correspondence)﻿—“Calista, I swear to thee, by the spotlessness of thy own soul, by the brilliancy of thy immitigable eyes, by everything pure and chaste in heaven and in thy own heart, that I will never cease from following thee! Scorn I can bear, and have borne at thy hands. Indifference I can surmount; ’tis a rock which my energy will climb over, a magnet which attracts the dauntless iron of my soul!” And it was true, I wouldn’t have left her﻿—no, though they had kicked me downstairs every day I presented myself at her door.
That is my way of fascinating women. Let the man who has to make his fortune in life remember this maxim. *Attacking* is his only secret. Dare, and the world always yields: or, if it beat you sometimes, dare again, and it will succumb. In those days my spirit was so great, that if I had set my heart upon marrying a princess of the blood, I would have had her!
I told Calista my story, and altered very very little of the truth. My object was to frighten her: to show her that what I wanted, that I dared; that what I dared, that I won; and there were striking passages enough in my history to convince her of my iron will and indomitable courage. “Never hope to escape me, madam,” I would say: “offer to marry another man, and he dies upon this sword, which never yet met its master. Fly from me, and I will follow you, though it were to the gates of Hades.” I promise you this was very different language to that she had been in the habit of hearing from her Jemmy-Jessamy adorers. You should have seen how I scared the fellows from her.
When I said in this energetic way that I would follow Lady Lyndon across the Styx if necessary, of course I meant that I would do so, provided nothing more suitable presented itself in the interim. If Lyndon would not die, where was the use of my pursuing the Countess? And somehow, towards the end of the Spa season, very much to my mortification I do confess, the knight made another rally: it seemed as if nothing would kill him. “I am sorry for you, Captain Barry,” he would say, laughing as usual. “I’m grieved to keep you, or any gentleman, waiting. Had you not better arrange with my doctor, or get the cook to flavour my omelette with arsenic?

===Chinese===

我那些日子对林登夫人说的话，句句出于至诚：“卡莉丝塔”（通信里我惯这样唤她）——“卡莉丝塔，我凭你灵魂的无瑕、凭你那双不容抗拒的眼的辉光、凭天上和你心里一切纯洁贞净的事物起誓：追随你的脚步，我永不停歇！轻蔑我能受，也受过了，就从你手里。淡漠我能翻越：那是一座山岩，我的精力会攀它而过；那是一块磁石，吸的是我灵魂里无畏的铁！”而这是真话，我不会离开她——不，哪怕我天天上门、他们天天把我踢下楼梯。
这就是我迷女人的路数。要在这世上挣出前程的男人，记住这条格言：进攻，是他唯一的秘诀。敢，世界总是让步的；或者，它偶尔打你一顿，再敢，它就降了。那些日子我的气魄那样大，倘若我一心要娶一位王室的公主，我也能娶到！
我把我的故事讲给卡莉丝塔听，同真事出入极小。我的目的是吓住她：叫她明白，我要的，我敢；我敢的，我赢；我生平够多的惊心段落，足以叫她信我这铁的意志、不屈的胆量。“休想逃出我的手心，夫人，”我惯说，“你若应许嫁别的男人，他必死在这口剑下——它还不曾遇过主人。你若从我身边飞走，我必追你，追到冥府的门口。”我担保，这同她从她那些嗲声嗲气的崇拜者惯听的语言，全然两样。你该看看我怎么把那班家伙从她身边吓跑。
我以这样烈性的话说，必要时不惜追随林登夫人跨过冥河——当然，我的意思是：只要这期间没有更合适的出现，我必照办。林登不死，我追这伯爵夫人何用？可不知怎么，斯帕旺季临尾时——我认了，大拂我的意——骑士又回了一阵阳：他像是怎么也死不了。“我替你难过，巴里船长，”他照例笑着说，“叫你、叫哪位绅士久候，我心里过意不去。你同我的医生商量商量，岂不更好？或者买通厨子，往我的蛋卷里搀点砒霜？”

===Original===

What are the odds, gentlemen,” he would add, “that I don’t live to see Captain Barry hanged yet?”
In fact, the doctors tinkered him up for a year. “It’s my usual luck,” I could not help saying to my uncle, who was my confidential and most excellent adviser in all matters of the heart. “I’ve been wasting the treasures of my affections upon that flirt of a countess, and here’s her husband restored to health and likely to live I don’t know how many years!” And, as if to add to my mortification, there came just at this period to Spa an English tallow-chandler’s heiress, with a plum to her fortune; and Madame Cornu, the widow of a Norman cattle-dealer and farmer-general, with a dropsy and two hundred thousand livres a year.
“What’s the use of my following the Lyndons to England,” says I, “if the knight won’t die?”
“Don’t follow them, my dear simple child,” replied my uncle. “Stop here and pay court to the new arrivals.”
“Yes, and lose Calista forever, and the greatest estate in all England.”
“Pooh, pooh! youths like you easily fire and easily despond. Keep up a correspondence with Lady Lyndon. You know there’s nothing she likes so much. There’s the Irish abbé, who will write you the most charming letters for a crown apiece. Let her go; write to her, and meanwhile look out for anything else which may turn up. Who knows? you might marry the Norman widow, bury her, take her money, and be ready for the Countess against the knight’s death.”
And so, with vows of the most profound respectful attachment, and having given twenty louis to Lady Lyndon’s waiting-woman for a lock of her hair (of which fact, of course, the woman informed her mistress), I took leave of the Countess, when it became necessary for her return to her estates in England; swearing I would follow her as soon as an affair of honour I had on my hands could be brought to an end.
I shall pass over the events of the year that ensued before I again saw her. She wrote to me according to promise; with much regularity at first, with somewhat less frequency afterwards. My affairs, meanwhile, at the play-table went on not unprosperously, and I was just on the point of marrying the widow Cornu (we were at Brussels by this time, and the poor soul was madly in love with me,) when the *London Gazette* was put into my hands, and I read the following announcement:﻿—

===Chinese===

“先生们，”他还添一句，“赔率几何：我等不到看巴里船长上绞架那一天？”
总之，医生们又把他修修补补拖了一年。“我素来的运气，”我不由得对伯父说——一切心上的事，他都是我的心腹军师，“我把感情库里的珍宝，全浪费在那个轻佻的伯爵夫人身上，她丈夫倒还了阳，保不齐还要活不知多少年！”像是嫌我不够晦气，恰在这时节，斯帕又来了位英国蜡烛铺的继承人，一份家私足有一百万；还有科尔尼夫人，诺曼底牲口贩子兼包税人的寡妇，带着一身水肿和一年二十万里弗尔。
“骑士不死，我跟着林登一家去英格兰干什么？”我说。
“那就别跟，我亲爱的傻孩子，”伯父答，“留下，向新来的献殷勤。”
“是啊，永远丢了我的卡莉丝塔，丢全英格兰最大的产业。”
“呸，呸！你们这样的后生，火来得快，泄得也快。同林登夫人保持通信。你知道她顶喜欢这个。有那位爱尔兰修道院长，一克朗一封，替你写顶迷人的信。放她走；给她写信，同时留意别的动静。谁知呢？你兴许娶了那位诺曼底寡妇，把她埋了，收下她的钱，等骑士一死，正好接着对付伯爵夫人。”
于是，怀着最深沉虔敬的眷恋的誓言，又给了林登夫人贴身侍女二十路易，换夫人的一绺头发（这事那女人当然报了主母），我向伯爵夫人辞行——她那时须回英格兰的产业去了——赌咒说，只等我手头一场荣誉之债了结，立刻追随她去。
再见到她之前那一年里的事，我略过。她照约给我写信：起初勤，随后略疏。我赌桌上的营生进行得不算坏，我正要娶寡妇科尔尼（其时我们已到布鲁塞尔，那可怜人爱我爱得发疯），《伦敦公报》递到我手里，我读到了下面这则讣闻：

===Original===

> “Died at Castle-Lyndon, in the kingdom of Ireland, the Right Honourable Sir Charles Lyndon, Knight of the Bath, member of Parliament for Lyndon in Devonshire, and many years His Majesty’s representative at various European Courts. He hath left behind him a name which is endeared to all his friends for his manifold virtues and talents, a reputation justly acquired in the service of His Majesty, and an inconsolable widow to deplore his loss. Her Ladyship, the bereaved Countess of Lyndon, was at the Bath when the horrid intelligence reached her of her husband’s demise, and hastened to Ireland immediately in order to pay her last sad duties to his beloved remains.”

That very night I ordered my chariot and posted to Ostend, whence I freighted a vessel to Dover, and travelling rapidly into the West, reached Bristol; from which port I embarked for Waterford, and found myself, after an absence of eleven years, in my native country.

===Chinese===

> “查尔斯·林登爵士，尊贵的巴斯骑士，德文郡林登选区议员、历任陛下驻欧陆各廷代表，于爱尔兰王国林登堡邸逝世。他以多方面的德行与才具深惬亲友，以恭王事而得令名，身后遗一恸不欲生之寡妇。其遗孀林登伯爵夫人闻此噩耗时正在巴斯，即刻驰赴爱尔兰，料理先夫遗体，以尽哀思。”

当夜我就叫人套车，驿站直奔奥斯坦德，从那里雇船到多佛，兼程西进，抵布里斯托尔；再由该港登船去沃特福德——阔别十一年之后，重新踏上了故土。

## XV I Pay Court to My Lady Lyndon / 十五 我向我的林登夫人献殷勤
===Original===

As my uncle’s attainder was not reversed for being out with the Pretender in 1745, it would have been inconvenient for him to accompany his nephew to the land of our ancestors; where, if not hanging, at least a tedious process of imprisonment, and a doubtful pardon, would have awaited the good old gentleman. In any important crisis of my life, his advice was always of advantage to me, and I did not fail to seek it at this juncture, and to implore his counsel as regarded my pursuit of the widow. I told him the situation of her heart, as I have described it in the last chapter; of the progress that young Poynings had made in her affections, and of her forgetfulness of her old admirer; and I got a letter, in reply, full of excellent suggestions, by which I did not fail to profit. The kind Chevalier prefaced it by saying, that he was for the present boarding in the Minorite convent at Brussels; that he had thoughts of making his salut there, and retiring forever from the world, devoting himself to the severest practices of religion. Meanwhile he wrote with regard to the lovely widow: it was natural that a person of her vast wealth and not disagreeable person should have many adorers about her; and that, as in her husband’s lifetime she had shown herself not at all disinclined to receive my addresses, I must make no manner of doubt I was not the first person whom she had so favoured; nor was I likely to be the last.
“I would, my dear child,” he added, “that the ugly attainder round my neck, and the resolution I have formed of retiring from a world of sin and vanity altogether, did not prevent me from coming personally to your aid in this delicate crisis of your affairs; for, to lead them to a good end, it requires not only the indomitable courage, swagger, and audacity, which you possess beyond any young man I have ever known” (as for the “swagger,” as the Chevalier calls it, I deny it *in toto*, being always most modest in my demeanour); “but though you have the vigour to execute, you have not the ingenuity to suggest plans of conduct for the following out of a scheme that is likely to be long and difficult of execution.

===Chinese===

我伯父因1745年随“觊觎者”起事、褯夺令未曾撤销，陪他侄儿回我们祖先的土地，诸多不便；在那边，即便不上绞架，至少也有一场旷日持久的监禁和一道靠不住的赦书等着这位好老头。每逢我人生的重要关口，他的主意于我总有好处；此际我自然去讨教，为追那寡妇的事求他的方略。我把她心上的局面——上一章已描画过——把小波因宁斯在她感情里的进展、把她对旧情人的忘却，一一告诉了他；回信来了，满纸妙策，我无一条不照办。好心的骑士开头写道：他目前寄住布鲁塞尔的小修会修道院，有意在那里“留名”，从此退出尘世，专务最严格的宗教功课。至于那位可爱的寡妇，他写道：她那样豪富、人又不算讨厌，身边自然仰慕者成群；何况她丈夫在世时，她既全不拒绝受我的殷勤，那我断不必疑心我是她头一个垂青的人；也多半不是末一个。
“亲爱的孩子，”他添写道，“要不是我这脖子上这圈难看的褯夺令，要不是我已拿定主意、整个退出这罪恶虚荣的世界，真想亲自来帮你渡过你事情的这个微妙关口；因为要引它到善终，光有你那份不折不挠的勇气、派头、胆量——你胜过我认得的任何年轻人——（至于‘派头’，骑士这样叫它，我一概否认；我举止向来自谦）还不够；“你虽有力气执行，却没有那份灵机，替这样一桩旷日持久、荆棘丛生的计划，一条条想出行止的章程来。

===Original===

Would you have ever thought of the brilliant scheme of the Countess Ida, which so nearly made you the greatest fortune in Europe, but for the advice and experience of a poor old man, now making up his accounts with the world, and about to retire from it for good and all?
“Well, with regard to the Countess of Lyndon, your manner of winning her is quite *en l’air* at present to me; nor can I advise day by day, as I would I could, according to circumstances as they arise. But your general scheme should be this. If I remember the letters you used to have from her during the period of the correspondence which the silly woman entertained you with, much high-flown sentiment passed between you; and especially was written by her Ladyship herself: she is a bluestocking, and fond of writing; she used to make her griefs with her husband the continual theme of her correspondence (as women will do). I recollect several passages in her letters bitterly deploring her fate in being united to one so unworthy of her.
“Surely, in the mass of billets you possess from her, there must be enough to compromise her. Look them well over; select passages, and threaten to do so. Write to her at first in the undoubting tone of a lover who has every claim upon her. Then, if she is silent, remonstrate, alluding to former promises from her; producing proofs of her former regard for you; vowing despair, destruction, revenge, if she prove unfaithful. Frighten her﻿—astonish her by some daring feat, which will let her see your indomitable resolution: you are the man to do it. Your sword has a reputation in Europe, and you have a character for boldness; which was the first thing that caused my Lady Lyndon to turn her eyes upon you. Make the people talk about you at Dublin. Be as splendid, and as brave, and as odd as possible. How I wish I were near you! You have no imagination to invent such a character as I would make for you﻿—but why speak; have I not had enough of the world and its vanities?”
There was much practical good sense in this advice; which I quote, unaccompanied with the lengthened description of his mortifications and devotions which my uncle indulged in, finishing his letter, as usual, with earnest prayers for my conversion to the true faith.

===Chinese===

若不是我这个正在同世界清账、即将一去不返的可怜老人的主意和阅历，你可曾想出过伊达伯爵夫人那条妙计——那条差一点叫你做成全欧洲头一份大家业的计？
“好，关于林登伯爵夫人：你赢得她的路数，此刻在我还是悬空的；我也不能像巴不得的那样，逐日相机指点。可你的大章法应该如此。我记得她同你那阵子通信——那傻女人拿它吊你——中间许多高调的深情往来；尤其是夫人亲笔写的那些：她是个女才子，爱写信；她惯拿她同丈夫的苦楚当通信的常题（女人们都这样）。她信里有好几段，哀叹自己竟同这样配不上她的人拴在一处，字字怨毒。
“你手里那一大叠她的短简，总够叫她落把柄的了。细细过一遍；掠出段落，拿它吓她。起初，用情人那不容置疑的口吻写信给她——一个对她有全部权利的情人。她若不理，就诘责，援引她从前的许诺；摆出她从前待你情分的凭据；赌咒说：她若负心，便是绝望、便是毁灭、便是报复。吓住她——用一桩大胆的奇举震住她，叫她看看你不折不挠的决心：你正是干这个的人。你的剑名震欧洲；你有一副大胆的名声——这原是林登夫人头一眼看上你的缘由。在都柏林（Dublin）叫众人拿你当话讲。要阔气，要胆壮，要古怪，越出格越好。我多想在你身边！你那点想象力，编不出我替你编的角色——唉，说它做什么；这世界同它的虚荣，我还没受够么？”
这封信里着实有实惠的道理；我引了它，只略去我伯父末尾又一大量篇苦修和灵修——他照例以恳求我皈依真教的热诚祷告收束。

===Original===

But he was constant to his form of worship; and I, as a man of honour and principle, was resolute to mine; and have no doubt that the one, in this respect, will be as acceptable as the other.
Under these directions it was, then, I wrote to Lady Lyndon, to ask on my arrival when the most respectful of her admirers might be permitted to intrude upon her grief? Then, as her Ladyship was silent, I demanded, Had she forgotten old times, and one whom she had favoured with her intimacy at a very happy period? Had Calista forgotten Eugenio? At the same time I sent down by my servant with this letter a present of a little sword for Lord Bullingdon, and a private note to his governor; whose note of hand, by the way, I possessed for a sum﻿—I forget what﻿—but such as the poor fellow would have been very unwilling to pay. To this an answer came from her Ladyship’s amanuensis, stating that Lady Lyndon was too much disturbed by grief at her recent dreadful calamity to see anyone but her own relations; and advices from my friend, the boy’s governor, stating that my Lord George Poynings was the young kinsman who was about to console her.
This caused the quarrel between me and the young nobleman; whom I took care to challenge on his first arrival at Dublin.
When the news of the duel was brought to the widow at Castle Lyndon, my informant wrote me that Lady Lyndon shrieked and flung down the journal, and said, “The horrible monster! He would not shrink from murder, I believe;” and little Lord Bullingdon, drawing his sword﻿—the sword I had given him, the rascal!﻿—declared he would kill with it the man who had hurt Cousin George. On Mr. Runt telling him that I was the donor of the weapon, the little rogue still vowed that he would kill me all the same! Indeed, in spite of my kindness to him, that boy always seemed to detest me.

===Chinese===

可他守他的教门守得牢；我，一个荣誉与原则之士，守我的也守得牢；我不疑心：在这一层上，这一位同那一位，一样蒙悦纳。
就是按着这些指示，我给林登夫人（Lady Lyndon）去信：问她，她最恭顺的崇拜者到了之后，几时容许他闯进她的悲伤？夫人不答；我便质问：她忘了旧时光，忘了那个她在极幸福的一段日子里许以亲密的人了么？卡莉丝塔忘了尤金尼奥么？随信我打发仆人送上一件给布林登（Bullingdon）勋爵的小剑作礼，另附给他监护人的一张私条——顺便说，这人的期票握在我手里，数目记不清了，总之是那可怜人极不愿付的一笔。回信来了，是夫人的代笔：说林登夫人遭此惨祸，哀痛方深，除至亲外一概不见；我那位朋友、孩子的监护人来信说：前去安慰她的年轻亲眷，是乔治·波因宁斯勋爵。
这就成了我同那年轻贵族的衅端；他一到都柏林，我便寻着由头下了战书。
决斗的消息传到林登堡（Castle Lyndon）的寡妇那里，报信人写信告诉我：林登夫人尖叫一声，把报纸摔在地上，说：“可怕的怪物！我看他杀人都不会眨眼。”小布林登勋爵抽出剑——我送他的那把，这小滑头！——声称要用它杀了伤乔治表哥的人。伦特先生告诉他武器是我送的，那小坏蛋仍旧赌咒：照样要杀我！说真的，任凭我怎样疼他，那孩子见了我总是恨。

===Original===

Her Ladyship sent up daily couriers to inquire after the health of Lord George; and, thinking to myself that she would probably be induced to come to Dublin if she were to hear that he was in danger, I managed to have her informed that he was in a precarious state; that he grew worse; that Redmond Barry had fled in consequence: of this flight I caused the *Mercury* newspaper to give notice also, but indeed it did not carry me beyond the town of Bray, where my poor mother dwelt; and where, under the difficulties of a duel, I might be sure of having a welcome.
Those readers who have the sentiment of filial duty strong in their mind, will wonder that I have not yet described my interview with that kind mother whose sacrifices for me in youth had been so considerable, and for whom a man of my warm and affectionate nature could not but feel the most enduring and sincere regard.
But a man, moving in the exalted sphere of society in which I now stood, has his public duties to perform before he consults his private affections; and so, upon my first arrival, I despatched a messenger to Mrs. Barry, stating my arrival, conveying to her my sentiments of respect and duty, and promising to pay them to her personally so soon as my business in Dublin would leave me free.
This, I need not say, was very considerable. I had my horses to buy, my establishment to arrange, my entrée into the genteel world to make; and, having announced my intention to purchase horses and live in a genteel style, was in a couple of days so pestered by visits of the nobility and gentry, and so hampered by invitations to dinners and suppers, that it became exceedingly difficult for me during some days to manage my anxiously desired visit to Mrs. Barry.
It appears that the good soul provided an entertainment as soon as she heard of my arrival, and invited all her humble acquaintances of Bray to be present: but I was engaged subsequently to my Lord Ballyragget on the day appointed, and was, of course, obliged to break the promise that I had made to Mrs. Barry to attend her humble festival.

===Chinese===

夫人天天派信使上去问乔治勋爵的伤势；我心想，她若听说他病危，多半肯来都柏林，便设法叫人报给她：他危殆了；又报：他重了；又报：雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）因此逃逸了——这逃逸，我又叫《信使报》也登了告白；其实它没把我带出布雷小镇，我可怜的母亲住在那里；决斗多有不便的日子，那里我准保有一份欢迎。
凡孝亲之念强的读者，必纳闷我怎么还不描写同那位慈母的会面：她年轻时为我牺牲那样多，以我这样热烈多情的天性，对她的眷爱自然最久最真。
可一个在我如今这样显赫圈子里走动的人，先公事，后私情；所以一到任，我即刻打发一名信使去巴里太太处，报我的到达，致我的敬悃，许她：都柏林的事务一松动，便亲上门叩安。
这事务，不必说，相当可观。我要买马，要安家，要打进上流社会；我放出要买马、要过绅士日子的风声不出两天，贵族缙绅的拜帖就把我说住了，晚饭夜宵的请柬把我围困住了，一连几天，我那心焦盼望的巴里太太之访，竟没法成行。
那好人儿一听我到了，立刻备下一桌席，把她布雷的贫贱相识全请了来；可日子正撞上巴利拉格特勋爵先期的约，我自然只好毁了对巴里太太应下的、赴她那朴素庆典的话。

===Original===

I endeavoured to sweeten the disappointment by sending my mother a handsome satin sack and velvet robe, which I purchased for her at the best mercers in Dublin (and indeed told her I had brought from Paris expressly for her); but the messenger whom I despatched with the presents brought back the parcels, with the piece of satin torn halfway up the middle: and I did not need his descriptions to be aware that something had offended the good lady; who came out, he said, and abused him at the door, and would have boxed his ears, but that she was restrained by a gentleman in black; who I concluded, with justice, was her clerical friend Mr. Jowls.
This reception of my presents made me rather dread than hope for an interview with Mrs. Barry, and delayed my visit to her for some days further. I wrote her a dutiful and soothing letter, to which there was no answer returned; although I mentioned that on my way to the capital I had been at Barryville, and revisited the old haunts of my youth.
I don’t care to own that she is the only human being whom I am afraid to face. I can recollect her fits of anger as a child, and the reconciliations, which used to be still more violent and painful: and so, instead of going myself, I sent my factotum, Ulick Brady, to her; who rode back, saying that he had met with a reception he would not again undergo for twenty guineas; that he had been dismissed the house, with strict injunctions to inform me that my mother disowned me forever. This parental anathema, as it were, affected me much, for I was always the most dutiful of sons; and I determined to go as soon as possible, and brave what I knew must be an inevitable scene of reproach and anger, for the sake, as I hoped, of as certain a reconciliation.
I had been giving one night an entertainment to some of the genteelest company in Dublin, and was showing my Lord Marquess downstairs with a pair of wax tapers, when I found a woman in a grey coat seated at my doorsteps: to whom, taking her for a beggar, I tendered a piece of money, and whom my noble friends, who were rather hot with wine, began to joke, as my door closed and I bade them all good night.

===Chinese===

我设法把这份失望抹甜些：差人给母亲送去一件漂亮的缎袍和一件天鹅绒外氅——都柏林头等绸缎庄买的（我还告诉她是我特意从巴黎给她捎的）；可送礼的差人抱着包裹回来了：那块缎子，当胸撕开了一半。不必他描画我也明白：什么东西得罪了那位好太太。差人说，她出来在门口把他骂了个狗血淋头，要不是一个黑衣绅士拦着，连耳朵都要给他一记——我断得不错，是她的教友焦尔斯先生。
这份见面礼的下场，叫我与其盼、不如怕同巴里太太见面，把行期又拖了几天。我给她去了一封子孝温存的信，没有回音；虽然我提了：赴都途中我到过巴里维尔，重访了我少年时代的老地方。
我不怕认：天下人里，我只怕见她一个。我还记得小时候她发火的阵仗，同随后的和好——那往往更凶、更痛；于是我不自己去，打发我的总管尤利克·布雷迪去；他骑马回来，说那趟差给他两几尼他也不愿再受第二回：他被轰出了门，还掠来死命令：母亲永远不认我这个儿子了。这道近乎绝亲的诅咒，叫我好不难受：我一向是顶孝顺的儿子；我决计尽早走一趟，硬着头皮领受那一场必有的责备和怒火，指望换来一场同样必有的和解。
一天夜里，我正宴请都柏林最上流的一伙客人，亲手执一对蜡台送侯爵大人下楼，发现门阶上坐着一个披灰斗篷的女人：我只当她是讨饭的，递过去一枚钱；我那几位灌得半醉的贵友，趁我关门道晚安的当口，拿她打起趣来。

===Original===

I was rather surprised and affected to find afterwards that the hooded woman was no other than my mother; whose pride had made her vow that she would not enter my doors, but whose natural maternal yearnings had made her long to see her son’s face once again, and who had thus planted herself in disguise at my gate. Indeed, I have found in my experience that these are the only women who never deceive a man, and whose affection remains constant through all trials. Think of the hours that the kind soul must have passed, lonely in the street, listening to the din and merriment within my apartments, the clinking of the glasses, the laughing, the choruses, and the cheering.
When my affair with Lord George happened, and it became necessary to me, for the reasons I have stated, to be out of the way; now, thought I, is the time to make my peace with my good mother: she will never refuse me an asylum now that I seem in distress. So sending to her a notice that I was coming, that I had had a duel which had brought me into trouble, and required I should go into hiding, I followed my messenger half-an-hour afterwards: and, I warrant me, there was no want of a good reception, for presently, being introduced into an empty room by the barefooted maid who waited upon Mrs. Barry, the door was opened, and the poor mother flung herself into my arms with a scream, and with transports of joy which I shall not attempt to describe﻿—they are but to be comprehended by women who have held in their arms an only child after a twelve years’ absence from him.
The Reverend Mr. Jowls, my mother’s director, was the only person to whom the door of her habitation was opened during my sojourn; and he would take no denial. He mixed for himself a glass of rum-punch, which he seemed in the habit of drinking at my good mother’s charge, groaned aloud, and forthwith began reading me a lecture upon the sinfulness of my past courses, and especially of the last horrible action I had been committing.
“Sinful!” said my mother, bristling up when her son was attacked; “sure we’re all sinners; and it’s you, Mr. Jowls, who have given me the inexpressible blessing to let me know *that*. But how else would you have had the poor child behave?”
“I would have had the gentleman avoid the drink, and the quarrel, and this wicked duel altogether,” answered the clergyman.

===Chinese===

过后我才知道，那蒙头的女人不是别人，正是我母亲：她的傲气叫她赌咒不进我的门，她的母性又叫她渴再见儿子一面，于是蒙起脸，坐到了我的大门口。说真的，凭我一生的阅历：唯有这样的女人从不欺男人，任凭什么磨难，情分不变。想想那好心的灵魂独自在街上坐过的那几个钟头，听我屋里的喧闹和欢笑，听杯盏的叮当、笑声、合唱和喝彩。
乔治勋爵那桩事一出，我因前述的缘故须得避一避；我想：眼下正是同我好母亲言和的时机：如今我落了难，她断不会拒绝我藏身。于是我送去口信，说我这就来；说我打了一场决斗、惹了祸，须躲一躲；半个钟头后，我跟着信使到了。我管保，招待不缺：光脚的使女——伺候巴里太太的那位——把我让进一间空屋，门一开，可怜的母亲一声喊，扑进我怀里，那阵欢喜若狂，我不去描画——只有把独子搂在怀里、阔别十二年的母亲，才懂得它。
我母亲的神师焦尔斯牧师，是我蛰居期间她家唯一开门放进来的人；还由不得拒绝。他自己调了一杯朗姆潘趣——看惯了是拿我好母亲请客的——大声叹着气，随即对我开讲：讲我过往行径的罪，尤其讲我新近犯下的那桩可怕行为。
“有罪！”我母亲一听儿子挨骂就炸了毛，“咱们哪个不是罪人？还不就是你，焦尔斯先生，叫我得了那个说不出的福，明白了这一条。可你倒说说，这可怜的孩子还能怎么样？”
“我倒要这位先生戒了酒、戒了口角、整个戒了这邪恶的决斗。”牧师答。

===Original===

But my mother cut him short, by saying such sort of conduct might be very well in a person of his cloth and his birth, but it neither became a Brady nor a Barry. In fact, she was quite delighted with the thought that I had pinked an English marquis’s son in a duel; and so, to console her, I told her of a score more in which I had been engaged, and of some of which I have already informed the reader.
As my late antagonist was in no sort of danger when I spread that report of his perilous situation, there was no particular call that my hiding should be very close. But the widow did not know the fact as well as I did: and caused her house to be barricaded, and Becky, her barefooted serving-wench, to be a perpetual sentinel to give alarm, lest the officers should be in search of me.
The only person I expected, however, was my cousin Ulick, who was to bring me the welcome intelligence of Lady Lyndon’s arrival; and I own, after two days’ close confinement at Bray, in which I narrated all the adventures of my life to my mother, and succeeded in making her accept the dresses she had formerly refused, and a considerable addition to her income which I was glad to make, I was very glad when I saw that reprobate Ulick Brady, as my mother called him, ride up to the door in my carriage with the welcome intelligence for my mother, that the young lord was out of danger; and for me, that the Countess of Lyndon had arrived in Dublin.
“And I wish, Redmond, that the young gentleman had been in danger a little longer,” said the widow, her eyes filling with tears, “and you’d have stayed so much the more with your poor old mother.” But I dried her tears, embracing her warmly, and promised to see her often; and hinted I would have, mayhap, a house of my own and a noble daughter to welcome her.
“Who is she, Redmond dear?” said the old lady.
“One of the noblest and richest women in the empire, mother,” answered I. “No mere Brady this time,” I added, laughing: with which hopes I left Mrs. Barry in the best of tempers.
No man can bear less malice than I do; and, when I have once carried my point, I am one of the most placable creatures in the world. I was a week in Dublin before I thought it necessary to quit that capital. I had become quite reconciled to my rival in that time; made a point of calling at his lodgings, and speedily became an intimate consoler of his bedside.

===Chinese===

可我母亲把他拦腰截断：说那一套行为，安在他那教门、他那出身的人身上尽好，安在布雷迪家、巴里家的人身上，断乎不可。说真的，她一想到我在决斗里捅了英国侯爵的儿子，欢喜得不得了；为慰她的心，我又给她讲了二十来场我打过的，有几场前面已告诉过读者。
我散布险讯的时候，我那位对头根本毫无危险，我的躲藏原不必躲得多严。可寡妇不知这底细：叫人把门户堵死，又叫她的光脚使女贝琪做永久的哨兵，随时报警，生怕军官来拿我。
我等的只有一个人：我表弟尤利克，等他带来林登夫人抵埠的好消息；在布雷严密蛰居的两天里，我把平生的历险讲给母亲听，到底叫她收下了先前拒收的衣裳，又欢欢喜喜给她添了一注进项——这钱我给得甘愿；随后，见那“浪子”（我母亲这样叫他）尤利克·布雷迪坐着我的马车驰到门口，给母亲掠来好消息：小勋爵脱险了；给我掠来：林登伯爵夫人到了都柏林——我说不出的高兴。
“雷德蒙，我倒巴望那位小先生多危险几天，”寡妇眼里涌着泪说，“你也好多陪你可怜的老母亲几天。”我搂住她亲热，替她拭了泪，许她常来看她；又漏了点口风：兴许我自有宅子、自有一位高贵的儿媳妇迎她。
“她是谁呀，亲爱的雷德蒙？”老太太问。
“全帝国最高贵最有钱的女人之一，母亲。”我笑着补一句：“这回可不光是布雷迪家的。”我带着这份指望，把巴里太太留在顶好的心情里，走了。
没人比我更不计仇；目的一旦到手，我是世上最好说话的人。我在都柏林又住了一礼拜，才觉得该离京了。这期间我同我的情敌完全言归于好：特意到他的寓所问安，很快成了他病榻边一位亲近的安慰者。

===Original===

He had a gentleman to whom I did not neglect to be civil, and towards whom I ordered my people to be particular in their attentions; for I was naturally anxious to learn what my Lord George’s position with the lady of Castle Lyndon had really been, whether other suitors were about the widow, and how she would bear the news of his wound.
The young nobleman himself enlightened me somewhat upon the subjects I was most desirous to inquire into.
“Chevalier,” said he to me one morning when I went to pay him my compliments, “I find you are an old acquaintance with my kinswoman, the Countess of Lyndon. She writes me a page of abuse of you in a letter here; and the strange part of the story is this, that one day when there was talk about you at Castle Lyndon, and the splendid equipage you were exhibiting in Dublin, the fair widow vowed and protested she never had heard of you.
“ ‘Oh yes, mamma,’ said the little Bullingdon, ‘the tall dark man at Spa with the cast in his eye, who used to make my governor tipsy and sent me the sword: his name is Mr. Barry.’
“But my Lady ordered the boy out of the room, and persisted in knowing nothing about you.”
“And are you a kinsman and acquaintance of my Lady Lyndon, my Lord?” said I, in a tone of grave surprise.
“Yes, indeed,” answered the young gentleman. “I left her house but to get this ugly wound from you. And it came at a most unlucky time too.”
“Why more unlucky now than at another moment?”
“Why, look you, Chevalier, I think the widow was not unpartial to me. I think I might have induced her to make our connection a little closer: and faith, though she is older than I am, she is the richest party now in England.”
“My Lord George,” said I, “will you let me ask you a frank but an odd question?﻿—will you show me her letters?”
“Indeed I’ll do no such thing,” replied he, in a rage.
“Nay, don’t be angry. If *I* show you letters of Lady Lyndon’s to me, will you let me see hers to you?”
“What, in Heaven’s name, do you mean, Mr. Barry?” said the young gentleman.
“*I* mean that I passionately loved Lady Lyndon. I mean that I am a﻿—that I rather was not indifferent to her. I mean that I love her to distraction at this present moment, and will die myself, or kill the man who possesses her before me.”
“*You* marry the greatest heiress and the noblest blood in England?” said Lord George haughtily.

===Chinese===

他有一位随身绅士，我不曾怠慢，殷勤得体；又吩咐我的人对他格外照应：因为我自然急于探明，乔治勋爵同林登堡那位夫人到底到了哪一步，寡妇身边还有没有别的求婚人，她听了他的伤讯会怎样。
年轻的贵族本人，把我最想问的几桩，替我解说了几分。
“骑士，”一天早晨我去问安，他对我说，“我发觉你原是我家亲戚林登伯爵夫人的旧识。她这里一封信，写了整页骂你的话；妙的是：有一天林登堡里谈起你，谈起你在都柏林摆出的煊赫车马，那位美丽的寡妇赌咒发誓，从没听说过你。
“‘哦，听说过的，妈妈，’小布林登说，‘就是斯帕那个高个黑脸、眼睛有点斜的先生，惯把我监护人灌醉、还送我剑的；他姓巴里。’
“可夫人把孩子喝出屋去，一口咬定对你一无所知。”
“您也是林登夫人的亲戚和相识么，勋爵？”我说，声调庄重而惊讶。
“正是。”年轻绅士答，“我出她家门，就为从您手里吃了这一剑。偏偏又赶在最不巧的时候。”
“怎么这会儿比别的时候更不巧？”
“嗦，您看，骑士，我看那寡妇对我并非无意。我想我本可以说动她把我们的关系拉得更近些；说真的，她虽然比我大，她可是如今英格兰最有钱的一注。”
“乔治勋爵，”我说，“容我问您一个坦率却古怪的问题——可否把她的信给我看看？”
“决不。”他火了。
“嗦，别恼。若我把林登夫人给我的信给您看，您肯让我看写您的么？”
“您到底是什么意思，巴里先生？”年轻贵族说。
“我的意思是：我热烈地爱过林登夫人。意思是：我——我对她从前并非无意。意思是：此刻我爱她爱得发狂，谁先我得到她，我就同谁拼命，不是他死就是我死。”
“您——娶英格兰头号的继承产、头名的贵血？”乔治勋爵傲然说。

===Original===

“There’s no nobler blood in Europe than mine,” answered I: “and I tell you I don’t know whether to hope or not. But this I know, that there were days in which, poor as I am, the great heiress did not disdain to look down upon my poverty: and that any man who marries her passes over my dead body to do it. It’s lucky for you,” I added gloomily, “that on the occasion of my engagement with you, I did not know what were your views regarding my Lady Lyndon. My poor boy, you are a lad of courage and I love you. Mine is the first sword in Europe, and you would have been lying in a narrower bed than that you now occupy.”
“Boy!” said Lord George: “I am not four years younger than you are.”
“You are forty years younger than I am in experience. I have passed through every grade of life. With my own skill and daring I have made my own fortune. I have been in fourteen pitched battles as a private soldier, and have been twenty-three times on the ground, and never was touched but once; and that was by the sword of a French *maître-d’armes*, whom I killed. I started in life at seventeen, a beggar, and am now at seven-and-twenty, with twenty thousand guineas. Do you suppose a man of my courage and energy can’t attain anything that he dares, and that having claims upon the widow, I will not press them?”
This speech was not exactly true to the letter (for I had multiplied my pitched battles, my duels, and my wealth somewhat); but I saw that it made the impression I desired to effect upon the young gentleman’s mind, who listened to my statement with peculiar seriousness, and whom I presently left to digest it.
A couple of days afterwards I called to see him again, when I brought with me some of the letters that had passed between me and my Lady Lyndon. “Here,” said I, “look﻿—I show it you in confidence﻿—it is a lock of her Ladyship’s hair; here are her letters signed Calista, and addressed to Eugenio. Here is a poem, ‘When Sol bedecks the mead with light, And pallid Cynthia sheds her ray,’ addressed by her Ladyship to your humble servant.”
“Calista! Eugenio! Sol bedecks the mead with light?” cried the young lord. “Am I dreaming? Why, my dear Barry, the widow has sent me the very poem herself! ‘Rejoicing in the sunshine bright, Or musing in the evening grey.’ ”
I could not help laughing as he made the quotation.

===Chinese===

“欧洲没有比我更高贵的血。”我答，“我告诉你，我不知道该不该指望。可这一层我知道：从前有些日子，尽管我穷，那位大继承女并不屑于小看我这个穷；谁娶她，得从我的尸首上跨过去。算你走运，”我阴沉沉地补一句，“同你交手那回，我还不知道你对林登夫人的算盘。我可怜的孩子，你是个有胆的少年，我喜欢你。我这口剑是欧洲第一剑；不然，你如今躺的床，还要再窄一截。”
“孩子！”乔治勋爵说，“我跟你差不离四岁。”
“论阅历你小我四十岁。人生的每一级台阶我都走过。凭我自己的本事和胆量，我挣出了自己的家业。我当列兵打过十四场大会战，决斗场上过二十三回，只挨过一回——还是个法国剑术师，我把他结果了。我十七岁起家，是个叫花子；如今二十七，两万几尼。你想，我这样的人品和魄力，还怕得不到他敢要的东西？既有权利在那寡妇身上，我会不追到底？”
这番话同字面上的实情不无出入（会战、决斗和家私，我都翻了几番）；可我看得出，它在年轻绅士心上留下了我要的印象：他听我这番申报，神情异常郑重；我随即告辞，留他慢慢消化。
两天后我再看他去，随身带着几封我同林登夫人往来的信。“瞧，”我说，“看——拿给你看，只出于信任——这是夫人的头发一绺；这是她署名卡莉丝塔、写给尤金尼奥的信；这是一首诗，‘当太阳为草场镀上光，惨淡的辛西娅洒下她的辉’，夫人写给您卑微的仆人的。”
“卡莉丝塔！尤金尼奥！‘当太阳为草场镀上光’？”年轻的勋爵喊，“我在做梦么？嗦，亲爱的巴里，那寡妇把同一首诗原样也寄给了我！‘或在明亮的阳光里欢畅，或在黄昏的灰色里冥想。’”
他一引原句，我禁不住笑了。

===Original===

They were, in fact, the very words *my* Calista had addressed to me. And we found, upon comparing letters, that whole passages of eloquence figured in the one correspondence which appeared in the other. See what it is to be a bluestocking and have a love of letter-writing!
The young man put down the papers in great perturbation. “Well, thank Heaven!” said he, after a pause of some duration﻿—“thank Heaven for a good riddance! Ah, Mr. Barry, what a woman I *might* have married had these lucky papers not come in my way! I thought my Lady Lyndon had a heart, sir, I must confess, though not a very warm one; and that, at least, one could *trust* her. But marry her now! I would as lief send my servant into the street to get me a wife, as put up with such an Ephesian matron as that.”
“My Lord George,” said I, “you little know the world. Remember what a bad husband Lady Lyndon had, and don’t be astonished that she, on her side, should be indifferent. Nor has she, I will dare to wager, ever passed beyond the bounds of harmless gallantry, or sinned beyond the composing of a sonnet or a billet-doux.”
“My wife,” said the little lord, “shall write no sonnets or billets-doux; and I’m heartily glad to think I have obtained, in good time, a knowledge of the heartless vixen with whom I thought myself for a moment in love.”
The wounded young nobleman was either, as I have said, very young and green in matters of the world﻿—for to suppose that a man would give up forty thousand a year, because, forsooth, the lady connected with it had written a few sentimental letters to a young fellow, is too absurd﻿—or, as I am inclined to believe, he was glad of an excuse to quit the field altogether, being by no means anxious to meet the victorious sword of Redmond Barry a second time.
When the idea of Poynings’ danger, or the reproaches probably addressed by him to the widow regarding myself, had brought this exceedingly weak and feeble woman up to Dublin, as I expected, and my worthy Ulick had informed me of her arrival, I quitted my good mother, who was quite reconciled to me (indeed the duel had done that), and found the disconsolate Calista was in the habit of paying visits to the wounded swain; much to the annoyance, the servants told me, of that gentleman.

===Chinese===

那正是我的卡莉丝塔写给我的原句。我们把两边的信一对，发现整段整段的锦绣文章，在两头的通信里轮番出场。瞧瞧当女才子、爱写信是什么光景！
年轻人把信纸放下，心里大乱。“好，谢天谢地！”隔了好一阵他说，“谢天谢地，甩得好！啊，巴里先生，若不是这几张救命纸，我差一点娶了怎样的一个女人！先生，我原以为林登夫人有颗心——我认了，不怎么热的心——至少，总还信得过。如今还要娶她！与其将就那样一位以弗所的贞妇，我还不如打发仆人上街替我讨个老婆呢。”
“乔治勋爵，”我说，“您太不懂世界。想想林登夫人碰上的是怎样一个坏丈夫；她那头落到冷淡，不必惊怪。而且我敢打赌，她从不曾越出无害的调情的界限；要犯罪，也只到写一首十四行、一张情书为止。”
“我的太太，”小勋爵说，“什么十四行情书都不许写；我一时当自己爱上了这没心肝的泼妇，又及时认清了她——想起这个，我打心眼里高兴。”
这位挂彩的年轻贵族，要么如我方才所说，世事上太嫩——因为想一个男人竟会放弃一年四万镑，只为他看中的那位太太给某个年轻人写过几封缠绵的信，未免荒唐——要么，如我更相信的，正巴不得有个借口整个退出赛场：他实在无意同得胜的雷德蒙·巴里的剑第二次照面。
波因宁斯险讯一起、多半还有他在寡妇跟前对我的那番数落，把这位弱不禁风的妇人照我预料的拽到了都柏林；我那位值价的尤利克报了她到的信，我便辞别我那完全言归于好的好母亲（言和正是那场决斗办成的），随即探明：我那愁苦的卡莉丝塔正频频探望那位挂彩的情郎——仆人们告诉我，情郎烦得很。

===Original===

The English are often absurdly high and haughty upon a point of punctilio; and, after his kinswoman’s conduct, Lord Poynings swore he would have no more to do with her.
I had this information from his Lordship’s gentleman; with whom, as I have said, I took particular care to be friends; nor was I denied admission by his porter, when I chose to call, as before.
Her Ladyship had most likely bribed that person, as I had; for she had found her way up, though denied admission; and, in fact, I had watched her from her own house to Lord George Poynings’ lodgings, and seen her descend from her chair there and enter, before I myself followed her. I proposed to await her quietly in the anteroom, to make a scene there, and reproach her with infidelity, if necessary; but matters were, as it happened, arranged much more conveniently for me; and walking, unannounced, into the outer room of his Lordship’s apartments, I had the felicity of hearing in the next chamber, of which the door was partially open, the voice of my Calista. She was in full cry, appealing to the poor patient, as he lay confined in his bed, and speaking in the most passionate manner. “What can lead you, George,” she said, “to doubt of my faith? How can you break my heart by casting me off in this monstrous manner? Do you wish to drive your poor Calista to the grave? Well, well, I shall join there the dear departed angel.”
“Who entered it three months since,” said Lord George, with a sneer. “It’s a wonder you have survived so long.”
“Don’t treat your poor Calista in this cruel cruel manner, Antonio!” cried the widow.
“Bah!” said Lord George, “my wound is bad. My doctors forbid me much talk. Suppose your Antonio tired, my dear. Can’t you console yourself with somebody else?”
“Heavens, Lord George! Antonio!”
“Console yourself with Eugenio,” said the young nobleman bitterly, and began ringing his bell; on which his valet, who was in an inner room, came out, and he bade him show her Ladyship downstairs.
Lady Lyndon issued from the room in the greatest flurry. She was dressed in deep weeds, with a veil over her face, and did not recognise the person waiting in the outer apartment. As she went down the stairs, I stepped lightly after her, and as her chairman opened her door, sprang forward, and took her hand to place her in the vehicle. “Dearest widow,” said I, “his Lordship spoke correctly.

英国人在小节上动不动就傲得可笑；受了表亲夫人这一场，波因宁斯勋爵赌咒再不与她相干。
这消息我从他勋爵的贴身绅士处得来——我说过，我特意同他交了朋友；我登门时，门房照旧放我进去，从没拦过。
夫人多半同我一样，把那门房也买通了；因为虽说主人挡驾，她照样上了楼；说真的，我从她自己的宅子一路跟到乔治·波因宁斯勋爵的寓所，亲眼见她下了轿进门，才跟着进去的。我本打算安安静静在前厅等她，必要时当场发作一场，拿负心数落她；偏巧事情替我安排得更顺手：我未经通传，走进勋爵公寓的外间，有幸隔着半开的门，在隔壁房间里听见了我的卡莉丝塔的嗓音。她正连哭带诉，向着卧床不起的可怜人大发其情。“乔治，”她说，“什么能叫你疑我的真心？你怎能这样狠心甩掉我，叫我心碎？你是要逼你可怜的卡莉丝塔进坟墓么？好，好，我到那边去陪那位先走的天使。”
“先进去三个月的那位。”乔治勋爵讥诣道，“你竟活得这么久，也算奇迹。”
“别这样残忍残忍地待你可怜的卡莉丝塔，安东尼奥！”寡妇喊。
“呸！”乔治勋爵说，“我的伤重。医生不许我多说话。就当你的安东尼奥腻了，亲爱的。你就不能换个别人安慰自己？”
“天哪，乔治勋爵！安东尼奥！”

Console yourself with Eugenio!” She was too frightened even to scream, as her chairman carried her away. She was set down at her house, and you may be sure that I was at the chair-door, as before, to help her out.
“Monstrous man!” said she, “I desire you to leave me.”
“Madam, it would be against my oath,” replied I; “recollect the vow Eugenio sent to Calista.”
“If you do not quit me, I will call for the domestics to turn you from the door.”
“What! when I am come with my Calista’s letters in my pocket, to return them mayhap? You can soothe, madam, but you cannot frighten Redmond Barry.”
“What is it you would have of me, sir?” said the widow, rather agitated.
“Let me come upstairs, and I will tell you all,” I replied; and she condescended to give me her hand, and to permit me to lead her from her chair to her drawing-room.
When we were alone I opened my mind honourably to her.
“Dearest madam,” said I, “do not let your cruelty drive a desperate slave to fatal measures. I adore you. In former days you allowed me to whisper my passion to you unrestrained; at present you drive me from your door, leave my letters unanswered, and prefer another to me. My flesh and blood cannot bear such treatment. Look upon the punishment I have been obliged to inflict; tremble at that which I may be compelled to administer to that unfortunate young man: so sure as he marries you, madam, he dies.”
“I do not recognise,” said the widow, “the least right you have to give the law to the Countess of Lyndon: I do not in the least understand your threats, or heed them. What has passed between me and an Irish adventurer that should authorise this impertinent intrusion?”
“*These* have passed, madam,” said I﻿—“Calista’s letters to Eugenio. They may have been very innocent; but will the world believe it? You may have only intended to play with the heart of the poor artless Irish gentleman who adored and confided in you. But who will believe the stories of your innocence, against the irrefragable testimony of your own handwriting? Who will believe that you could write these letters in the mere wantonness of coquetry, and not under the influence of affection?”
“Villain!” cried my Lady Lyndon, “could you dare to construe out of those idle letters of mine any other meaning than that which they really bear?”

===Chinese===

就找尤金尼奥安慰自己吧！”轿子抬走时，她吓得连叫都叫不出。轿子在她家门口落下了；诸位放心，我又照旧候在轿门口，捾她下轿。
“可怕的人！”她说，“请你走开。”
“夫人，那就违背我的誓言了。”我答，“想想尤金尼奥寄给卡莉丝塔的那篇誓词。”
“你再不离开，我就叫仆人把你轰出门。”
“什么！我口袋里揣着我卡莉丝塔的信，兴许正是来还她的，也轰么？哄得住夫人您，吓不住雷德蒙·巴里。”
“您究竟要我怎么样，先生？”寡妇相当慌乱。
“容我上楼，我一五一十奉告。”我答；她竟屈尊把手给我，许我从轿前扶进她的客厅。
单独相对时，我把心迹光明磊落地向她摊开。
“最亲爱的夫人，”我说，“别叫您的狠心，逼一个绝望的奴才下毒手。我崇拜您。从前您许我对您低诉衷情、不加拦阻；如今您把我拒之门外，信也不回，另择他人。我的血肉受不住这样的待遇。瞧瞧我不得不施的惩罚；再抖一抖我兴许还要加在那不幸年轻人身上的：夫人，他娶不娶您？娶，他死。”
“我不认，”寡妇说，“您对林登伯爵夫人发号施令有半分权利；您的恫吓我全不懂，也不理。在我同一个爱尔兰冒险家之间，过去的事，哪一件容得下这般无礼的闯犯？”
“过去的是这些，夫人，”我说，“卡莉丝塔写给尤金尼奥的信。它们兴许清白得很；可世人肯信么？您兴许只想拿那颗崇拜您、信托您的天真爱尔兰绅士的心玩玩。可谁会信您清白的分辩，敌得过您亲笔字这铁证？谁会信您写这些信，只是卖弄风情，而不是出于情爱？”
“恶棍！”我的林登夫人喊，“我那些闲信，您竟敢从里头读出它们本意之外的半点意思？”

===Original===

“I will construe anything out of them,” said I; “such is the passion which animates me towards you. I have sworn it﻿—you must and shall be mine! Did you ever know me promise to accomplish a thing and fail? Which will you prefer to have from me﻿—a love such as woman never knew from man before, or a hatred to which there exists no parallel?”
“A woman of my rank, sir, can fear nothing from the hatred of an adventurer like yourself,” replied the lady, drawing up stately.
“Look at your Poynings﻿—was *he* of your rank? You are the cause of that young man’s wound, madam; and, but that the instrument of your savage cruelty relented, would have been the author of his murder﻿—yes, of his murder; for, if a wife is faithless, does not she arm the husband who punishes the seducer! And I look upon you, Honoria Lyndon, as my wife.”
“Husband? wife, sir!” cried the widow, quite astonished.
“Yes, wife! husband! I am not one of those poor souls with whom coquettes can play, and who may afterwards throw them aside. You would forget what passed between us at Spa: Calista would forget Eugenio; but I will not let you forget me. You thought to trifle with my heart, did you? When once moved, Honoria, it is moved forever. I love you﻿—love as passionately now as I did when my passion was hopeless; and, now that I can win you, do you think I will forego you? Cruel cruel Calista! you little know the power of your own charms if you think their effect is so easily obliterated﻿—you little know the constancy of this pure and noble heart if you think that, having once loved, it can ever cease to adore you. No! I swear by your cruelty that I will revenge it; by your wonderful beauty that I will win it, and be worthy to win it. Lovely, fascinating, fickle, cruel woman! you shall be mine﻿—I swear it! Your wealth may be great; but am I not of a generous nature enough to use it worthily? Your rank is lofty; but not so lofty as my ambition. You threw yourself away once on a cold and spiritless debauchee: give yourself now, Honoria, to a *man*; and one who, however lofty your rank may be, will enhance it and become it!”

===Chinese===

“我什么都读得出来。”我说，“这便是我对您的如火的心肠。我发过誓——您必须、也必将归我！我应下的事，几时没办到过？您要哪一样——一份女人从男人那里从不知道过的爱，还是一份天下没有第二份的恨？”
“我这样身份的女人，先生，您这样的冒险家的恨，吓不着我什么。”夫人凛然一挺，答道。
“看看您的波因宁斯——他够您的身份么？那位年轻人的伤，夫人，是您一手造成；若非您那野蛮残忍的工具手下留情，您已是他的凶手的帮手——对，凶手；因为妻子若负心，岂不正是她把惩罚诱奸者的剑，递到丈夫手里！而我把您，霍诺丽亚·林登，当我的妻子看待。”
“丈夫？妻子，先生！”寡妇惊得呆了。
“对，妻子！丈夫！我不是那种可怜虫，由着风流娘儿们耍，耍完了一脚踢开。您想忘掉我们斯帕的旧情：卡莉丝塔想忘掉尤金尼奥；我可不许您忘掉我。您当我这颗心好消遣，是么？霍诺丽亚，这颗心一旦动了，就永远动到底。我爱您——如今爱得同当年绝望时一样烈；如今既赢得到您，您想我会撒手？狠心的、狠心的卡莉丝塔！您若以为您魅力的效验这样容易抹掉，那是太小看它了——您若以为这颗高贵纯洁的心一旦爱过还会停止崇拜，那是太小看它了。不！我凭您的残忍起誓：我要报它；凭您的绝色起誓：我要赢它，也配得上赢它。可爱、迷人、善变、残忍的女人！您必将归我——我起誓！您的财富或大；可我的天性还不够慷慨，配不上善用它么？您的门第或高；高不过我的抱负。您从前把自己抛给过一个冷冰冰、没血性的酒色鬼：如今把您自己给一个男子汉吧，霍诺丽亚；一个任凭您门第多高、也抬得高它、也配得上它的人！”

===Original===

As I poured words to this effect out on the astonished widow, I stood over her, and fascinated her with the glance of my eye; saw her turn red and pale with fear and wonder; saw that my praise of her charms and the exposition of my passion were not unwelcome to her, and witnessed with triumphant composure the mastery I was gaining over her. Terror, be sure of that, is not a bad ingredient of love. A man who wills fiercely to win the heart of a weak and vapourish woman *must* succeed, if he have opportunity enough.
“Terrible man!” said Lady Lyndon, shrinking from me as soon as I had done speaking (indeed, I was at a loss for words, and thinking of another speech to make to her)﻿—“terrible man! leave me.”
I saw that I had made an impression on her, from those very words. “If she lets me into the house tomorrow,” said I, “she is mine.”
As I went downstairs I put ten guineas into the hand of the hall-porter, who looked quite astonished at such a gift.
“It is to repay you for the trouble of opening the door to me,” said I; “you will have to do so often.”

===Chinese===

我朝惊呆的寡妇倾泻着这样的话，居高临下，用我的眼光摄住她：眼看她骇惧交并、红一阵白一阵；眼看我对她姿色的颂扬、我热情的剖白，并非不受她欢迎；又以得胜的镇定，亲见我在她身上一步步占到上风。恐怖，信我，是爱情里不坏的一味。一个立意要赢弱质多愁女人之心的男人，只要机会够多，必成。
“可怕的人！”林登夫人的话一完，就朝后缩（说真的，我也正词穷，在想下一篇说辞）——“可怕的人！走吧。”
就从这几个字，我看出我给她留下了印子。“她明天若放我进门，”我自忖，“她就是我的了。”
下楼时，我塞给门房十枚几尼；那汉子瞧着这份赏赐，愣住了。
“这是谢您为我开门的辛苦，”我说，“往后这样的事还多着呢。”

## XVI I Provide Nobly for My Family / 我体面地提携我的家族
===Original===

The next day when I went back, my fears were realised: the door was refused to me﻿—my Lady was not at home. This I knew to be false: I had watched the door the whole morning from a lodging I took at a house opposite.
“Your lady is not out,” said I: “she has denied me, and I can’t, of course, force my way to her. But listen: you are an Englishman?”
“That I am,” said the fellow, with an air of the utmost superiority. “Your honour could tell that by my *haccent*.”
I knew he was, and might therefore offer him a bribe. An Irish family servant in rags, and though his wages were never paid him, would probably fling the money in your face.
“Listen, then,” said I. “Your lady’s letters pass through your hands, don’t they? A crown for every one that you bring me to read. There is a whisky-shop in the next street; bring them there when you go to drink, and call for me by the name of Dermot.”
“I recollect your honour at *spar*,” says the fellow, grinning: “seven’s the main, hey?” and being exceedingly proud of this reminiscence, I bade my inferior adieu.
I do not defend this practice of letter-opening in private life, except in cases of the most urgent necessity: when we must follow the examples of our betters, the statesmen of all Europe, and, for the sake of a great good, infringe a little matter of ceremony. My Lady Lyndon’s letters were none the worse for being opened, and a great deal the better; the knowledge obtained from the perusal of some of her multifarious epistles enabling me to become intimate with her character in a hundred ways, and obtain a power over her by which I was not slow to profit. By the aid of the letters and of my English friend, whom I always regaled with the best of liquor, and satisfied with presents of money still more agreeable (I used to put on a livery in order to meet him, and a red wig, in which it was impossible to know the dashing and elegant Redmond Barry), I got such an insight into the widow’s movements as astonished her. I knew beforehand to what public places she would go; they were, on account of her widowhood, but few: and wherever she appeared, at church or in the park, I was always ready to offer her her book, or to canter on horseback by the side of her chariot.
Many of her Ladyship’s letters were the most whimsical rodomontades that ever bluestocking penned.

===Chinese===

第二天我再上门，我的担心成了事实：门房拒我于门外——说我那位夫人“不在家”。我明知这是假话：我在对门一户人家租了住处，整整一个上午盯着那扇门。
“你家夫人并没有出门，”我说：“她拒不见我，我当然不能硬闯。可你听着：你是英格兰人？”
“正是，”那家伙答道，一副无上优越的神气。“您老一听俺这*口音*就听得出来。”
我早知他是，因此不妨行贿。爱尔兰的世家仆人，哪怕衣衫褴褛、工钱从不发给他，多半会把你的钱摔回你脸上。
“那好，听着，”我说。“你家夫人的信件都打你手上过，对不对？每给我送一封来看，赏你一克朗。隔壁街上有一家威士忌酒铺，你去喝酒时把信带到那儿，叫我德莫特就行。”
“俺记得老爷您玩*骰子*来着，”那家伙咧嘴笑道，“七点开庄，嘿？”——他对这段回忆得意非凡，我便向我的下等人道了别。
私生活里拆看他人信件这桩行径，我并不辩护，除非情势万不得已：那时我们只好效法我们的上等人——全欧洲的政治家们——为了大大的好处，小小地违背一点礼数。林登伯爵夫人的信，拆过了丝毫不见糟，反倒好得多：她那些五花八门的尺牍，我通读若干之后得到的知识，让我从一百个方面摸透了她的性情，并借此得了一种驾驭她的力量，而我利用它也毫不迟疑。靠这些信件，加上我那位英格兰朋友——我总拿顶好的酒款待他，赏钱的馈赠更叫他称心（我同他会面惯常穿一身号衣、戴一顶红色假发，在那副装束里，谁也认不出这位倜傥风雅的雷德蒙·巴里）——我把寡妇的行止打探得清清楚楚，叫她大吃一惊。她要去哪些公共场所，我事先便知；因居孀守制，那去处本也寥寥：而无论她现身何处，教堂也罢，园圃也罢，我总候在一旁，随时给她递上祈祷书，或骑马缓步跟在她的车旁。
夫人的许多封信，是女学究笔下最古怪夸张的大言炎炎之词。

===Original===

She was a woman who took up and threw off a greater number of dear friends than anyone I ever knew. To some of these female darlings she began presently to write about my unworthy self, and it was with a sentiment of extreme satisfaction I found at length that the widow was growing dreadfully afraid of me; calling me her bête noire, her dark spirit, her murderous adorer, and a thousand other names indicative of her extreme disquietude and terror. It was: “The wretch has been dogging my chariot through the park,” or, “my fate pursued me at church,” and “my inevitable adorer handed me out of my chair at the mercer’s,” or whatnot. My wish was to increase this sentiment of awe in her bosom, and to make her believe that I was a person from whom escape was impossible.
To this end I bribed a fortune-teller, whom she consulted along with a number of the most foolish and distinguished people of Dublin, in those days; and who, although she went dressed like one of her waiting-women, did not fail to recognise her real rank, and to describe as her future husband her persevering adorer Redmond Barry, Esquire. This incident disturbed her very much. She wrote about it in terms of great wonder and terror to her female correspondents. “Can this monster,” she wrote, “indeed do as he boasts, and bend even Fate to his will?﻿—can he make me marry him though I cordially detest him, and bring me a slave to his feet? The horrid look of his black serpent-like eyes fascinates and frightens me: it seems to follow me everywhere, and even when I close my own eyes, the dreadful gaze penetrates the lids, and is still upon me.”
When a woman begins to talk of a man in this way, he is an ass who does not win her; and, for my part, I used to follow her about, and put myself in an attitude opposite her, “and fascinate her with my glance,” as she said, most assiduously. Lord George Poynings, her former admirer, was meanwhile keeping his room with his wound, and seemed determined to give up all claims to her favour; for he denied her admittance when she called, sent no answer to her multiplied correspondence, and contented himself by saying generally, that the surgeon had forbidden him to receive visitors or to answer letters.

===Chinese===

她这位女子，结交又抛开的心肝朋友，比我平生识得的任何人都多。她对其中几位闺中宝贝不久便写起我这个不才之辈来，而我终于怀着极大的满足发现：寡妇怕我怕得要死；她唤我做她的克星、她的黑煞、她那杀气腾腾的拜倒者，还有千百种名目，无一不表明她极度的惶恐畏惧。动辄便是：“那恶棍一路尾随我的马车穿过了园子，”或是“我的命星星在教堂里也追着我不放”，“我那甩不脱的拜倒者在绸缎庄门口扶我下了轿”，诸如此类。我的愿望便是叫这份敬畏在她胸中滋长，叫她相信我是她万难逃脱的人。
为达到这个目的，我买通了一位算命婆——那年月，她同都柏林一班最愚蠢也最显贵的人物一样，常去请教这位神婆；那婆子虽然见她穿着同贴身侍女一样，仍旧一眼认出她的真实身份，并且把那位锲而不舍的拜倒者、雷德蒙·巴里绅士说成了她命定的丈夫。这件事把她搅得心神大乱。她写信给几位通信的女友，言辞间又是惊骇又是恐怖。“这个怪物，”她写道，“当真能如他夸口的那样，连命运都屈从他的意志么？——他当真能叫我嫁给他，哪怕我打心底憎恶他，把我当成奴隶带到他脚下么？他那双黑蛇似的眼睛射出的可怕目光，迷得住我也吓得了我：它仿佛到处跟着我，哪怕我闭上双眼，那骇人的凝视也穿透眼皮，仍旧落在我身上。”
一个女人既这样议论起一个男人来，那男人若还赢不下她，便是蠢驴；至于我，便常常跟随她左右，在她对面摆好架势，“用我的目光迷惑她”——用她自己的话说——而且勤勉之至。她旧日的爱慕者乔治·波伊宁斯勋爵，这时正带伤闭门养息，似乎铁了心放弃对她的所有权利：她登门，他拒不见；她接二连三去信，他一概不复，只笼统推说外科医生不许他见客，也不许他复信。

===Original===

Thus, while he went into the background, I came forward, and took good care that no other rivals should present themselves with any chance of success; for, as soon as I heard of one, I had a quarrel fastened on him, and, in this way, pinked two more, besides my first victim Lord George. I always took another pretext for quarrelling with them than the real one of attention to Lady Lyndon, so that no scandal or hurt to her Ladyship’s feelings might arise in consequence; but she very well knew what was the meaning of these duels; and the young fellows of Dublin, too, by laying two and two together, began to perceive that there was a certain dragon in watch for the wealthy heiress, and that the dragon must be subdued first before they could get at the lady. I warrant that, after the first three, not many champions were found to address the lady; and have often laughed (in my sleeve) to see many of the young Dublin beaux riding by the side of her carriage scamper off as soon as my bay-mare and green liveries made their appearance.
I wanted to impress her with some great and awful instance of my power, and to this end had determined to confer a great benefit upon my honest cousin Ulick, and carry off for him the fair object of his affections, Miss Kiljoy, under the very eyes of her guardian and friend, Lady Lyndon; and in the teeth of the squires, the young lady’s brothers, who passed the season at Dublin, and made as much swagger and to-do about their sister’s £10,000 Irish, as if she had had a plum to her fortune. The girl was by no means averse to Mr. Brady; and it only shows how faint-spirited some men are, and how a superior genius can instantly overcome difficulties which to common minds seem insuperable, that he never had thought of running off with her: as I at once and boldly did. Miss Kiljoy had been a ward in Chancery until she attained her majority (before which period it would have been a dangerous matter for me to put in execution the scheme I meditated concerning her); but, though now free to marry whom she liked, she was a young lady of timid disposition, and as much under fear of her brothers and relatives as though she had not been independent of them.

===Chinese===

就这样，他退到了幕后，我走到台前，并且格外当心，不再放任何一个有分胜算的情敌上前；因为一有风声，我便寻衅同那人干上一场；头一个牺牲品乔治勋爵之外，这样又戳翻了两个。寻衅的由头我从不取那真正的由头——倾心于林登伯爵夫人——免得招来闲话、伤了她夫人的心；可这些决斗是什么意思，她心里雪亮；都柏林的公子哥儿们也是二上加二，渐渐看出有一位恶龙看守着这位豪富的女继承人，不先降龙，休想近得美人。我担保，头三个之后，敢向这位夫人献殷勤的勇士就不多了；多少回，我瞧见都柏林的时髦少年们正骑行在她车侧，一望见我的栗色牝马和绿号衣出现，登时拨马飞逃，我常（暗地里）好笑。
我想拿一桩又大又吓人的事例，让她见识见识我的神通，为此决定给我那位老实堂弟尤里克行个大方便：当着她的监护人和密友林登伯爵夫人的面，也当着那位小姐的几位乡绅兄长的面，把他心上那位俏丽人儿基尔乔伊小姐劫走。她那几位兄长正在都柏林度季，为他们妹妹那10,000爱尔兰镑吹嘘摆谱、大惊小怪，仿佛她名下真有一笔十万镑的巨富。这位小姐对布雷迪先生并非无意；只可惜有些人天生胆薄，而天才人物转眼便能跨过庸人眼里的天堑——他压根儿没想到同她私奔，我却当场大胆地干了出来。基尔乔伊小姐在成年之前一直是衡平法院的受监护未成年人（在那以前，我要对她施行心中盘算的计谋，是桩危险的事）；如今她虽可随意嫁人，天性却懦怯，对兄长亲眷的惧怕，同她不能自主时并无二致。

===Original===

They had some friend of their own in view for the young lady, and had scornfully rejected the proposal of Ulick Brady, the ruined gentleman; who was quite unworthy, as these rustic bucks thought, of the hand of such a prodigiously wealthy heiress as their sister.
Finding herself lonely in her great house in Dublin, the Countess of Lyndon invited her friend Miss Amelia to pass the season with her at Dublin; and, in a fit of maternal fondness, also sent for her son the little Bullingdon, and my old acquaintance his governor, to come to the capital and bear her company. A family coach brought the boy, the heiress, and the tutor from Castle Lyndon; and I determined to take the first opportunity of putting my plan in execution.
For this chance I had not very long to wait. I have said, in a former chapter of my biography, that the kingdom of Ireland was at this period ravaged by various parties of banditti; who, under the name of Whiteboys, Oakboys, Steelboys, with captains at their head, killed proctors, fired stacks, houghed and maimed cattle, and took the law into their own hands. One of these bands, or several of them for what I know, was commanded by a mysterious personage called Captain Thunder; whose business seemed to be that of marrying people with or without their own consent, or that of their parents. The *Dublin Gazettes* and *Mercuries* of that period (the year 1772) teem with proclamations from the Lord Lieutenant, offering rewards for the apprehension of this dreadful Captain Thunder and his gang, and describing at length various exploits of the savage aide-de-camp of Hymen. I determined to make use, if not of the services, at any rate of the name of Captain Thunder, and put my cousin Ulick in possession of his lady and her ten thousand pounds. She was no great beauty, and, I presume, it was the money he loved rather than the owner of it.
On account of her widowhood, Lady Lyndon could not as yet frequent the balls and routs which the hospitable nobility of Dublin were in the custom of giving; but her friend Miss Kiljoy had no such cause for retirement, and was glad to attend any parties to which she might be invited. I made Ulick Brady a present of a couple of handsome suits of velvet, and by my influence procured him an invitation to many of the most elegant of these assemblies.

===Chinese===

兄长们属意把小姐嫁给自家的某个朋友，对破了产的绅士尤里克·布雷迪的提亲嗤之以鼻；在这班乡下阔少看来，他哪配攀他们妹妹这样豪富绝伦的女继承人的手。
林登伯爵夫人在都柏林的大宅里形单影只，便邀请好友阿梅莉亚小姐来都柏林同她一起过季；又在一阵母爱发作之下，派人去接她的小儿子布灵登和孩子的塾师——我的老相识——进京作伴。家用的马车把少爷、女继承人和塾师从林登堡接了来；我决计一有机便乘，实行我的计划。
这机会没有让我久等。我在传记前文说过，那一时期爱尔兰王国正被各股匪帮蹂躏；他们打着白衣帮、橡实帮、铁衣帮的名号，各有头目率领，杀害代收租赋的管事，焚烧谷垛，砍伤残害牲口，私设刑章。其中一股——据我所知或许不止一股——归一位神秘人物统辖，人称“雷霆队长”；他的营生，似乎就是不管当事人本人愿意不愿意、父母应允不应允，专替人成亲。那一时期的《都柏林公报》和《都柏林信使》（1772年）上，满是总督的悬赏布告，缉拿这位可怕的雷霆队长及其党羽，并不厌其详地描述这位婚姻之神麾下野蛮副官的种种功业。我决计用不上雷霆队长其人，至少也要借重他的名号，让我堂弟尤里克把他的心上人连同她的一万镑一并到手。她算不得什么大美人，我揣度，尤里克爱的与其说是人，不如说是钱。
林登伯爵夫人居孀未满，还不便出入都柏林好客的贵族们惯常举办的舞会堂会；她的朋友基尔乔伊小姐却无此顾忌，凡有邀请的聚会都欣然赴约。我送了尤里克·布雷迪两身漂亮的天鹅绒衣裳，又凭我的门路，替他弄到了好几处最风雅集会的请帖。

===Original===

But he had not had my advantages or experience of the manners of Court; was as shy with ladies as a young colt, and could no more dance a minuet than a donkey. He made very little way in the polite world or in his mistress’s heart: in fact, I could see that she preferred several other young gentlemen to him, who were more at home in the ballroom than poor Ulick; he had made his first impression upon the heiress, and felt his first flame for her, in her father’s house of Ballykiljoy, where he used to hunt and get drunk with the old gentleman.
“I could do *thim* two well enough, anyhow,” Ulick would say, heaving a sigh; “and if it’s drinking or riding across country would do it, there’s no man in Ireland would have a better chance with Amalia.”
“Never fear, Ulick,” was my reply; “you shall have your Amalia, or my name is not Redmond Barry.”
My Lord Charlemont﻿—who was one of the most elegant and accomplished noblemen in Ireland in those days, a fine scholar and wit, a gentleman who had travelled much abroad, where I had the honour of knowing him﻿—gave a magnificent masquerade at his house of Marino, some few miles from Dublin, on the Dunleary road. And it was at this entertainment that I was determined that Ulick should be made happy for life. Miss Kiljoy was invited to the masquerade, and the little Lord Bullingdon, who longed to witness such a scene; and it was agreed that he was to go under the guardianship of his governor, my old friend the Reverend Mr. Runt. I learned what was the equipage in which the party were to be conveyed to the ball, and took my measures accordingly.
Ulick Brady was not present: his fortune and quality were not sufficient to procure him an invitation to so distinguished a place, and I had it given out three days previous that he had been arrested for debt: a rumour which surprised nobody who knew him.
I appeared that night in a character with which I was very familiar, that of a private soldier in the King of Prussia’s guard. I had a grotesque mask made, with an immense nose and moustaches, talked a jumble of broken English and German, in which the latter greatly predominated; and had crowds round me laughing at my droll accent, and whose curiosity was increased by a knowledge of my previous history.

===Chinese===

可惜他不曾有过我这样的际遇，也不曾见过宫廷的规矩；见了太太小姐腼腆得像匹小马驹，小步舞跳得比驴子好不了多少。他在上流社会里、在他情人的心上都进展甚微：说实话，我看得出，她属意的几位别的少年郎君都胜过可怜的尤里克——他们进了舞场如鱼得水，尤里克却不然。他同这位女继承人结识、并对她初燃情焰，是在她父亲巴利基尔乔伊的宅子里；他从前常陪着老绅士打猎，喝得酩酊大醉。
“反正那*两样*俺还是满在行的，”尤里克常叹一口气说；“要是喝酒或是纵马越野能讨得欢心，全爱尔兰没有谁比俺更有指望赢得阿梅莉亚。”
“放心，尤里克，”我答道，“你准能得到你的阿梅莉亚，不然我就不叫雷德蒙·巴里。”
查尔蒙特勋爵——他是当年爱尔兰最风雅多才的贵族之一，出色的学者和才子，又是一位遍游外洋的绅士，我在国外有幸与他相识——在他在马里诺的宅邸大设化装舞会；那宅子离都柏林几英里，在去邓利里的路上。我打定的主意，就是要在这一场欢会上了却尤里克的终身之喜。基尔乔伊小姐收到了舞会请帖，渴望见识这等场面的小布灵登勋爵也收到了；并讲定他由塾师、我的老朋友伦特牧师先生照管同行。我打听到这一行人赴舞会要乘的车辆，便相应地做了布置。
尤里克·布雷迪没有到场：以他的财力和身份，弄不到这般显赫场合的请帖；而且三天前我就放出风声，说他因债务被捕了——这条谣言，认识他的人谁也不觉意外。
那晚我扮的是一个我极熟的角色：普鲁士国王近卫军的一名列兵。我定做了一副奇形怪状的面具，大鼻子大髭须，满口磕磕绊绊的英语德语大杂烩，德语居多；围拢来的人们听我滑稽的腔调直笑，又因风闻了我先前的经历，好奇心愈发大了。

===Original===

Miss Kiljoy was attired as an antique princess, with little Bullingdon as a page of the times of chivalry; his hair was in powder, his doublet rose-colour, and pea-green and silver, and he looked very handsome and saucy as he strutted about with my sword by his side. As for Mr. Runt, he walked about very demurely in a domino, and perpetually paid his respects to the buffet, and ate enough cold chicken and drank enough punch and champagne to satisfy a company of grenadiers.
The Lord Lieutenant came and went in state﻿—the ball was magnificent. Miss Kiljoy had partners in plenty, among whom was myself, who walked a minuet with her (if the clumsy waddling of the Irish heiress may be called by such a name); and I took occasion to plead my passion for Lady Lyndon in the most pathetic terms, and to beg her friend’s interference in my favour.
It was three hours past midnight when the party for Lyndon House went away. Little Bullingdon had long since been asleep in one of Lady Charlemont’s china closets. Mr. Runt was exceedingly husky in talk, and unsteady in gait. A young lady of the present day would be alarmed to see a gentleman in such a condition; but it was a common sight in those jolly old times, when a gentleman was thought a milksop unless he was occasionally tipsy. I saw Miss Kiljoy to her carriage, with several other gentlemen: and, peering through the crowd of ragged linkboys, drivers, beggars, drunken men and women, who used invariably to wait round great men’s doors when festivities were going on, saw the carriage drive off, with a hurrah from the mob; then came back presently to the supper-room, where I talked German, favoured the three or four topers still there with a High-Dutch chorus, and attacked the dishes and wine with great resolution.
“How can you drink aisy with that big nose on?” said one gentleman.
“Go an be hangt!” said I, in the true accent, applying myself again to the wine; with which the others laughed, and I pursued my supper in silence.
There was a gentleman present who had seen the Lyndon party go off, with whom I had made a bet, which I lost; and the next morning I called upon him and paid it him. All which particulars the reader will be surprised at hearing enumerated; but the fact is, that it was not I who went back to the party, but my late German valet, who was of my size, and, dressed in my mask, could perfectly pass for me.

===Chinese===

基尔乔伊小姐扮成一位古时的公主，小布灵登扮成骑士时代的小侍童；他扑了发粉，紧身上衣是玫瑰色、豆绿夹银，腰间挂着我的佩剑，昂首阔步，神气十足，煞是俊俏淘气。伦特先生则披一件化装斗篷，规规矩矩地踱来踱去，频频向冷餐台致敬，冷鸡吃得、潘趣酒和香槟喝得足以飨足一连掷弹兵。
总督摆着全副仪仗驾临，又摆着全副仪仗离去——舞会极尽豪奢。基尔乔伊小姐的舞伴多的是，其中便有我；我同她走了一支小步舞（倘若这位爱尔兰女继承人笨拙的摇摆也配叫这个名字的话）；我趁机动起我的一片痴情，用最凄恻的言辞诉说我对林登伯爵夫人的爱慕，恳求她的朋友替我玉成。
夜过三更，回林登宅的一行人才散去。小布灵登早已在查尔蒙特夫人的一间瓷器房里睡熟了。伦特先生嗓音含混得厉害，脚下也站立不稳。如今的小姐见了一位绅士这副模样，怕要吓着；可在那个快活的老年头，这是寻常光景——绅士们不时常醉上一醉，还要被讥作娘娘腔呢。我同另外几位绅士一起送基尔乔伊小姐上车；透过那一群衣衫褴褛的火把郎、车夫、乞丐、醉醺醺的男男女女——每逢喜庆，这班人照例守在豪门门外——目送马车在一阵乌拉的喊声里驶去；随后不久便回到晚餐厅，在那里说德语，给还在场的三四位酒友献上一支高地德语的合唱，又奋不顾身地向菜肴和美酒进攻。
“长着那么个大鼻子，您怎么喝得安稳哪？”一位先生问道。
“去上吊吧您！”我用道地的土腔答道，重又埋头对付那杯酒；众人听了大笑，我便闷头继续用我的晚餐。
在场有位先生，亲眼看着林登一行离去，我先前同他打过赌，赌输了；第二天上午我便去拜访他，把赌债付了。读者听我罗列这些细节定会诧异；可实情是：回到舞会上去的并不是我，而是我从前的德国贴身仆人。他同我一般身量，戴上我的面具，完全可以冒充我。

===Original===

We changed clothes in a hackney-coach that stood near Lady Lyndon’s chariot, and driving after it, speedily overtook it.
The fated vehicle which bore the lovely object of Ulick Brady’s affections had not advanced very far, when, in the midst of a deep rut in the road, it came suddenly to with a jolt; the footman, springing off the back, cried “Stop!” to the coachman, warning him that a wheel was off, and that it would be dangerous to proceed with only three. Wheel-caps had not been invented in those days, as they have since been by the ingenious builders of Long Acre. And how the linchpin of the wheel had come out I do not pretend to say; but it possibly may have been extracted by some rogues among the crowd before Lord Charlemont’s gate.
Miss Kiljoy thrust her head out of the window, screaming as ladies do; Mr. Runt the chaplain woke up from his boozy slumbers; and little Bullingdon, starting up and drawing his little sword, said, “Don’t be afraid, Miss Amelia: if it’s footpads, I am armed.” The young rascal had the spirit of a lion, that’s the truth; as I must acknowledge, in spite of all my after quarrels with him.
The hackney-coach which had been following Lady Lyndon’s chariot by this time came up, and the coachman seeing the disaster, stepped down from his box, and politely requested her Ladyship’s honour to enter his vehicle; which was as clean and elegant as any person of tip-top quality might desire. This invitation was, after a minute or two, accepted by the passengers of the chariot: the hackney-coachman promising to drive them to Dublin “in a hurry.” Thady, the valet, proposed to accompany his young master and the young lady; and the coachman, who had a friend seemingly drunk by his side on the box, with a grin told Thady to get up behind. However, as the footboard there was covered with spikes, as a defence against the street-boys, who love a ride gratis, Thady’s fidelity would not induce him to brave these; and he was persuaded to remain by the wounded chariot, for which he and the coachman manufactured a linchpin out of a neighbouring hedge.
Meanwhile, although the hackney-coachman drove on rapidly, yet the party within seemed to consider it was a long distance from Dublin; and what was Miss Kiljoy’s astonishment, on looking out of the window at length, to see around her a lonely heath, with no signs of buildings or city.

===Chinese===

我们在一辆出租马车里换了衣裳——那车就停在林登伯爵夫人的自备马车近旁——随后一路追赶，不多时便追上了。
载着尤里克·布雷迪心上俏人儿的那辆命定的马车，没走出多远，行到路当中一道深深的车辙里，猛地一颠，停住了；跟车的仆人从车后一跃而下，朝车夫喊“停！”，提醒他一只轮子掉了，只剩三只轮子再往前走可就险了。那年月还没有挡轮的螺盖，那是后来朗埃克的能工巧匠们才发明出来的。车轮的轴销怎么会脱出来的，我不敢妄言；不过很可能是在查尔蒙特勋爵大门外的人群里，叫哪几个歹徒顺手拔了去。
基尔乔伊小姐把头探出车窗，照太太小姐的做派尖叫起来；随行牧师伦特先生从醉梦里惊醒；小布灵登一骨碌跳起，抽出他的小剑，说道：“别怕，阿梅莉亚小姐：若是剪径的强盗，我带着家伙呢。”这小泼皮有一头雄狮的胆气，这是实话；尽管我后来同他龃龉不断，这一点我必须承认。
一直缀在林登伯爵夫人马车后面的那辆出租马车此时赶了上来；车夫见出了事故，跳下驭座，彬彬有礼地请夫人贵人们移乘他的车——那车干净体面，头等身份的人乘坐也无可挑剔。过了一两分钟，马车里的乘客接受了这番好意：出租马车夫应承“飞快”把他们送到都柏林。贴身仆人萨迪提出要陪同小主人和小姐一起走；车夫——他身旁的驭座上还坐着个看似烂醉的朋友——咧嘴一笑，叫萨迪到车后头踩踏板去。可是那踏板上密密麻麻钉满了尖钉，专为防备爱搭白车的街童，萨迪的忠心还不足以叫他去闯那钉板；众人劝他留在抛锚的马车旁，他同出租马车夫便从近旁的篱笆上现造了一枚轴销。
其间，出租马车夫虽是快马加鞭，车中诸位却似乎觉得离都柏林越走越远；基尔乔伊小姐终于往窗外一望，惊得呆了：四下里是一片荒凉的旷野，不见半点房舍城镇的影子。

===Original===

She began forthwith to scream out to the coachman to stop; but the man only whipped the horses the faster for her noise, and bade her Ladyship “hould on﻿—’twas a shortcut he was taking.”
Miss Kiljoy continued screaming, the coachman flogging, the horses galloping, until two or three men appeared suddenly from a hedge, to whom the fair one cried for assistance; and the young Bullingdon opening the coach-door, jumped valiantly out, toppling over head and heels as he fell; but jumping up in an instant, he drew his little sword, and, running towards the carriage, exclaimed, “This way, gentlemen! stop the rascal!”
“Stop!” cried the men; at which the coachman pulled up with extraordinary obedience. Runt all the while lay tipsy in the carriage, having only a dreamy half-consciousness of all that was going on.
The newly arrived champions of female distress now held a consultation, in which they looked at the young lord and laughed considerably.
“Do not be alarmed,” said the leader, coming up to the door; “one of my people shall mount the box by the side of that treacherous rascal, and, with your Ladyship’s leave, I and my companions will get in and see you home. We are well armed, and can defend you in case of danger.”
With this, and without more ado, he jumped into the carriage, his companion following him.
“Know your place, fellow!” cried out little Bullingdon indignantly: “and give place to the Lord Viscount Bullingdon!” and put himself before the huge person of the newcomer, who was about to enter the hackney-coach.
“Get out of that, my Lord,” said the man, in a broad brogue, and shoving him aside. On which the boy, crying “Thieves! thieves!” drew out his little hanger, and ran at the man, and would have wounded him (for a small sword will wound as well as a great one); but his opponent, who was armed with a long stick, struck the weapon luckily out of the lad’s hands: it went flying over his head, and left him aghast and mortified at his discomfiture.
He then pulled off his hat, making his Lordship a low bow, and entered the carriage; the door of which was shut upon him by his confederate, who was to mount the box.

===Chinese===

她登时尖叫着喊车夫停车；可她越喊，那人越把马抽得飞快，还请她夫人“坐稳——俺这是抄近道呢”。
基尔乔伊小姐不住地尖叫，车夫不住地扬鞭，马蹄翻飞，直到篱笆后头猛地闪出两三条汉子；美人儿向他们呼救；小布灵登拉开车门，奋勇跳下车，摔了个倒栽葱；可他一骨碌爬起，抽出小剑，朝马车奔去，口中喊道：“这边，诸位先生！拦住那恶棍！”
“停！”那几条汉子喝道；车夫闻声，异常顺从地勒住了马。伦特自始至终醉卧在车里，对眼前种种只有一场迷梦似的半知觉。
新到的这几位搭救落难女子的好汉当下碰了个头，一面朝小勋爵打量，一面笑个不住。
“不必惊慌，”为首的走到车门前说；“我派一个人到驭座上盯着那奸诈的恶棍；夫人若肯赏准，我和同伴们上车，护送夫人回府。我们家伙齐全，遇有凶险，足可保夫人无虞。”
说罢不由分说，他跳上车，他的同伴跟了进去。
“放规矩点，小子！”小布灵登义愤填膺地喝道：“给布灵登子爵大人让路！”说着抢身挡在那大块头新客的面前——那人正要钻进出租马车。
“让开，俺的大人，”那汉子操着满口土腔说，一把将他搡到旁边。孩子便喊：“有贼！有贼！”拔出小短剑直扑过去，险些伤着他（小剑同大剑一样能伤人）；亏得对手持一根长棍，一棍把那武器磕出孩子手中：剑飞过他头顶，落了空的孩子目瞪口呆，又羞又恼。
那人随后脱帽，朝小勋爵深深一躬，钻进了车厢；车门由他的同伙替他关上——那同伙便去登上驭座。

===Original===

Miss Kiljoy might have screamed; but I presume her shrieks were stopped by the sight of an enormous horse-pistol which one of her champions produced, who said, “No harm is intended you, ma’am, but if you cry out, we must gag you;” on which she suddenly became as mute as a fish.
All these events took place in an exceedingly short space of time; and when the three invaders had taken possession of the carriage, the poor little Bullingdon being left bewildered and astonished on the heath, one of them putting his head out of the window, said﻿—
“My Lord, a word with you.”
“What is it?” said the boy, beginning to whimper: he was but eleven years old, and his courage had been excellent hitherto.
“You are only two miles from Marino. Walk back till you come to a big stone, there turn to the right, and keep on straight till you get to the high road, when you will easily find your way back. **Captain Thunder’s** compliments, and say Miss Amelia Kiljoy is going to be married.”
“O heavens!” sighed out that young lady.
The carriage drove swiftly on, and the poor little nobleman was left alone on the heath, just as the morning began to break. He was fairly frightened; and no wonder. He thought of running after the coach; but his courage and his little legs failed him: so he sat down upon a stone and cried for vexation.
It was in this way that Ulick Brady made what I call a Sabine marriage. When he halted with his two groomsmen at the cottage where the ceremony was to be performed, Mr. Runt, the chaplain, at first declined to perform it. But a pistol was held at the head of that unfortunate preceptor, and he was told, with dreadful oaths, that his miserable brains would be blown out; when he consented to read the service. The lovely Amelia had, very likely, a similar inducement held out to her, but of that I know nothing; for I drove back to town with the coachman as soon as we had set the bridal party down, and had the satisfaction of finding Fritz, my German, arrived before me: he had come back in my carriage in my dress, having left the masquerade undiscovered, and done everything there according to my orders.

===Chinese===

基尔乔伊小姐本待尖叫；可我猜，她的尖叫被她的一位护救者亮出的一杆大号马枪堵了回去；那人说道：“俺们无意伤害小姐，可小姐要是喊叫，俺们只好堵嘴了；”她闻言顿时哑口如鱼。
这一切都在极短的工夫里办完；三条不速之客占了车厢，可怜的小布灵登被撇在荒野上，又是茫然又是惊愕；其中一人把头探出车窗，说道——
“大人，同您说句话。”
“什么事？”孩子说，开始抽喳起来：他才十一岁，而方才的胆气本是好生了得。
“这里离马里诺只有两英里。往回走，走到一块大石头跟前，向右转，一直走下去便上了大路，那时节您就不难寻路回家了。**雷霆队长**向您致意，并请您传话：阿梅莉亚·基尔乔伊小姐就要成亲了。”
“天哪！”那位年轻小姐长叹一声。
马车飞驰而去，可怜的小贵族被独自撇在荒野上，天色正要破晓。他当真心慌了；这也难怪。他想拔腿去追马车；无奈胆气和两条小腿都不听使唤：只得在一块石头上坐下，气得哭了起来。
尤里克·布雷迪就是这样成就了一桩我所谓的萨宾式婚姻。他同两位侯相在行礼的村舍门前停稳之后，随行牧师伦特先生起初不肯主礼。可一支手枪顶住了那位倒霉塾师的脑门，又赌下好不吓人的重咒，说他那点可怜的脑浆就要开花；他便应允诵经行礼了。可爱的阿梅莉亚多半也受了同样的劝诱，个中详情我却一概不知；因为婚庆一行人一下车，我便同马车夫赶回城里，而且心满意足地发现我的德国人弗里茨已先我而至：他穿着我的衣裳乘我的马车回来了，离开化装舞会时无人识破，在场上事事都遵照我的吩咐办妥。

===Original===

Poor Runt came back the next day in a piteous plight, keeping silence as to his share in the occurrences of the evening, and with a dismal story of having been drunk, of having been waylaid and bound, of having been left on the road and picked up by a Wicklow cart, which was coming in with provisions to Dublin, and found him helpless on the road. There was no possible means of fixing any share of the conspiracy upon him. Little Bullingdon, who, too, found his way home, was unable in any way to identify me. But Lady Lyndon knew that I was concerned in the plot, for I met her hurrying the next day to the Castle; all the town being up about the enlevement. And I saluted her with a smile so diabolical, that I knew she was aware that I had been concerned in the daring and ingenious scheme.
Thus it was that I repaid Ulick Brady’s kindness to me in early days; and had the satisfaction of restoring the fallen fortunes of a deserving branch of my family. He took his bride into Wicklow, where he lived with her in the strictest seclusion until the affair was blown over; the Kiljoys striving everywhere in vain to discover his retreat. They did not for a while even know who was the lucky man who had carried off the heiress; nor was it until she wrote a letter some weeks afterwards, signed Amelia Brady, and expressing perfect happiness in her new condition, and stating that she had been married by Lady Lyndon’s chaplain Mr. Runt, that the truth was known, and my worthy friend confessed his share of the transaction. As his good-natured mistress did not dismiss him from his post in consequence, everybody persisted in supposing that poor Lady Lyndon was privy to the plot; and the story of her Ladyship’s passionate attachment for me gained more and more credit.
I was not slow, you may be sure, in profiting by these rumours. Everyone thought I had a share in the Brady marriage; though no one could prove it. Everyone thought I was well with the widowed Countess; though no one could show that I said so. But there is a way of proving a thing even while you contradict it, and I used to laugh and joke so apropos that all men began to wish me joy of my great fortune, and look up to me as the affianced husband of the greatest heiress in the kingdom. The papers took up the matter; the female friends of Lady Lyndon remonstrated with her and cried “Fie!”

===Chinese===

可怜的伦特第二天回来时好不凄惨，对自己那一晚的干系绝口不提，只编了一篇惨兮兮的说辞：说自己喝醉了，遭了埋伏、被捆了，被丢在路旁，亏得一辆进都柏林送粮草的威克洛乡下大车把他这个动弹不得的人拾了回去。要想把这桩阴谋的哪怕一丝一毫安到他头上，全无办法。自己寻路回家的小布灵登，也怎么指认不出我来。可林登伯爵夫人晓得我脱不了干系；因为第二天满城都在议论这场劫婚，我遇见她正急匆匆赶往城堡。我朝她行礼，脸上那笑容凶煞得可以；我立刻明白：她知道这件大胆而巧妙的计谋里也有我一份。
我早年落难时尤里克·布雷迪待我不薄，我便这样报答了他；眼见家族中这枝有出息的旁支重振了败落的家运，我心中十分满足。他带着新娘遁入威克洛山中，同她深藏不出，直到风波平息；基尔乔伊家四处打探他的藏身之处，一无所获。有一阵子，他们连劫走女继承人的幸运儿是谁都不知道；直到几个星期后她写来一封信，署名阿梅莉亚·布雷迪，自言新生活美满得很，又说婚礼系由林登伯爵夫人的牧师伦特先生主持，真相方才大白，我那位可敬的朋友也招认了自己在其中的干系。他的好脾气的女主人并未因此革他的职，于是人人咬定可怜的林登伯爵夫人早知情；她夫人对我一片痴情的话头，也越传越有鼻子有眼。
你可以相信，我利用这些风传毫不迟疑。人人都道布雷迪家这桩婚事有我一份；尽管谁也拿不出凭据。人人都道我同这位居孀的伯爵夫人有交情；尽管谁也拿不出我说过此话的把柄。可有一样本事：一件事，你越否认越能坐实；我惯会恰到好处地又说又笑，于是人人都开始恭贺我交了大运，把我当作王国最大女继承人的未婚夫婿仰望着。报馆抓住这事大做文章；林登伯爵夫人的太太朋友们纷纷劝谏她，齐呼“不害臊！”

===Original===

Even the English journals and magazines, which in those days were very scandalous, talked of the matter; and whispered that a beautiful and accomplished widow, with a title and the largest possessions in the two kingdoms, was about to bestow her hand upon a young gentleman of high birth and fashion, who had distinguished himself in the service of His M﻿—﻿—﻿y the K﻿—﻿— of Pr﻿—﻿—. I won’t say who was the author of these paragraphs; or how two pictures, one representing myself under the title of “The Prussian Irishman,” and the other Lady Lyndon as “The Countess of Ephesus,” actually appeared in the *Town and Country Magazine*, published at London, and containing the fashionable tittle-tattle of the day.
