Lady Lyndon was so perplexed and terrified by this continual hold upon her, that she determined to leave the country. Well, she did; and who was the first to receive her on landing at Holyhead? Your humble servant, Redmond Barry, Esquire. And, to crown all, the *Dublin Mercury*, which announced her Ladyship’s departure, announced mine *the day before*. There was not a soul but thought she had followed me to England; whereas she was only flying me. Vain hope!﻿—a man of my resolution was not thus to be balked in pursuit. Had she fled to the antipodes, I would have been there: ay, and would have followed her as far as Orpheus did Eurydice!
Her Ladyship had a house in Berkeley Square, London, more splendid than that which she possessed in Dublin; and, knowing that she would come thither, I preceded her to the English capital, and took handsome apartments in Hill Street, hard by. I had the same intelligence in her London house which I had procured in Dublin. The same faithful porter was there to give me all the information I required. I promised to treble his wages as soon as a certain event should happen. I won over Lady Lyndon’s companion by a present of a hundred guineas down, and a promise of two thousand when I should be married, and gained the favours of her favourite lady’s-maid by a bribe of similar magnitude. My reputation had so far preceded me in London that, on my arrival, numbers of the genteel were eager to receive me at their routs.

===Chinese===

连英格兰的报刊杂志——那年月这些刊物最喜搬弄是非——也议论起这件事来；还绘声绘影地说，一位有爵位、坐拥两王国最大产业的美貌多才的寡妇，即将把她的手许给一位出身高贵、风流时髦的年轻绅士，此君曾在普——士的国——陛下麾下效命，屡建殊勋。这些段落出自谁的手笔，我不便说；还有两幅画，一幅画的是我，题为“普鲁士爱尔兰人”，另一幅画的是林登伯爵夫人，题为“以弗所伯爵夫人”，居然真登在伦敦出版、专载时髦闲话的《城乡杂志》上——它们怎么会上去的，我也不便说。
林登伯爵夫人被这只攥住她不放的手搅得惶惶不可终日，便决意离开爱尔兰。好，她走了；可在霍利黑德登岸时，头一个迎接她的是谁？正是鄙人，雷德蒙·巴里绅士。更妙的是，登载她夫人启程消息的《都柏林信使》，登我动身的消息还要*早一天*。人人都以为她是追着我到的英格兰；其实她不过是躲我。妄想！——像我这样有决断的人，岂肯在追途上就此却步。她便是逃到对跖天涯之地，我也一样跟到：是啊，俄耳甫斯追欧律狄刻追了多远，我就追她多远！
夫人在伦敦伯克利广场有一处宅邸，比她都柏林的还气派；我知道她早晚要来，便先她一步到了英格兰京城，在近旁的希尔街赁下一套漂亮的寓所。在她伦敦的宅子里，我安插了同都柏林一样的耳目。还是那位忠实的门房，替我递送我需要的一切消息。我许他：只等某件事情一发生，工钱加三倍。林登伯爵夫人的女伴，我送上一份100几尼的现礼、外加事成之后2,000的期许，便把她收买了；夫人的心腹侍女，也教我一份不相上下的贿礼笼络了过来。我的名声早我一步到了伦敦，我一到，上流社会中便有许多人争相请我去赴他们的堂会。

===Original===

We have no idea in this humdrum age what a gay and splendid place London was then: what a passion for play there was among young and old, male and female; what thousands were lost and won in a night; what beauties there were﻿—how brilliant, gay, and dashing! Everybody was delightfully wicked: the Royal Dukes of Gloucester and Cumberland set the example; the nobles followed close behind. Running away was the fashion. Ah! it was a pleasant time; and lucky was he who had fire, and youth, and money, and could live in it! I had all these; and the old frequenters of White’s, Wattier’s, and Goosetree’s could tell stories of the gallantry, spirit, and high fashion of Captain Barry.
The progress of a love-story is tedious to all those who are not concerned, and I leave such themes to the hack novel-writers, and the young boarding-school misses for whom they write. It is not my intention to follow, step by step, the incidents of my courtship, or to narrate all the difficulties I had to contend with, and my triumphant manner of surmounting them. Suffice it to say, I *did* overcome these difficulties. I am of opinion, with my friend the late ingenious Mr. Wilkes, that such impediments are nothing in the way of a man of spirit; and that he can convert indifference and aversion into love, if he have perseverance and cleverness sufficient. By the time the Countess’s widowhood was expired, I had found means to be received into her house; I had her women perpetually talking in my favour, vaunting my powers, expatiating upon my reputation, and boasting of my success and popularity in the fashionable world.
Also, the best friends I had in the prosecution of my tender suit were the Countess’s noble relatives; who were far from knowing the service that they did me, and to whom I beg leave to tender my heartfelt thanks for the abuse with which they then loaded me! and to whom I fling my utter contempt for the calumny and hatred with which they have subsequently pursued me.
The chief of these amiable persons was the Marchioness of Tiptoff, mother of the young gentleman whose audacity I had punished at Dublin.

===Chinese===

在这个古板乏味的年头，我们想象不出当年伦敦是何等花团锦簇的去处：男女老少嗜赌成狂；一夜之间几千上万的输赢；美人儿何其多——何其明艳、风流、俴傥！人人放浪得可爱：格洛斯特和坎伯兰两位王室公爵带头作榜样，贵族们紧随其后。私奔是时髦。啊！那是快活的年月；有火气、有青春、有钱财、活得其间的人，真是幸运儿！这些我样样都有；怀特俱乐部、瓦蒂埃俱乐部和古斯垂俱乐部的老主顾们，还能讲出巴里上尉的风流、胆气与摩登的种种故事来。
恋爱故事的进展，在局外人听来最是乏味，这类题目我留给拙劣的小说家，同为他们写书的女塾小姐们。我无意一步一步追述我求爱的经过，也不想细数我须得对付的重重难关，以及我克敌制胜的种种得意手段。只消说一句：这些难关我*确实*一一克服了。我同亡友、那位伶俐的威尔克斯先生所见相同：这点阻碍，在有胆气的男子面前算不得什么；只要有足够的坚忍和机敏，冷漠与嫌恶皆可化为爱情。到伯爵夫人孀居期满之时，我已有门路登堂入室；她宅里的女眷们无时无刻不替我说话：夸我的本事，铺陈我的名声，吹嘘我在上流社会的走运和得人缘。
再者，在我这场温柔攻势里帮衬最力的几位好友，便是伯爵夫人的贵族亲眷们；他们对自己帮了我多大的忙毫不知情，对于他们当时堆在我头上的辱骂，请容我在此致以衷心的感谢！对于他们后来追着我不放的诽谤与仇视，我也在此回敬以十足的轻蔑。
这班可爱人物中的头一位，是蒂普托夫侯爵夫人——我在都柏林惩戒过其胆大妄为的那位少年的母亲。

===Original===

This old harridan, on the Countess’s first arrival in London, waited upon her, and favoured her with such a storm of abuse for her encouragement of me, that I do believe she advanced my cause more than six months’ courtship could have done, or the pinking of a half-dozen of rivals. It was in vain that poor Lady Lyndon pleaded her entire innocence and vowed she had never encouraged me. “Never encouraged him!” screamed out the old fury; “didn’t you encourage the wretch at Spa, during Sir Charles’s own life? Didn’t you marry a dependant of yours to one of this profligate’s bankrupt cousins? When he set off for England, didn’t you follow him like a mad woman the very next day? Didn’t he take lodgings at your very door almost﻿—and do you call this no encouragement? For shame, madam, shame! You might have married my son﻿—my dear and noble George; but that he did not choose to interfere with your shameful passion for the beggarly upstart whom you caused to assassinate him; and the only counsel I have to give your Ladyship is this, to legitimatise the ties which you have contracted with this shameless adventurer; to make that connection legal which, real as it is now, is against both decency and religion; and to spare your family and your son the shame of your present line of life.”
With this the old fury of a marchioness left the room, and Lady Lyndon in tears: I had the whole particulars of the conversation from her Ladyship’s companion, and augured the best result from it in my favour.
Thus, by the sage influence of my Lady Tiptoff, the Countess of Lyndon’s natural friends and family were kept from her society. Even when Lady Lyndon went to Court the most august lady in the realm received her with such marked coldness, that the unfortunate widow came home and took to her bed with vexation. And thus I may say that Royalty itself became an agent in advancing my suit, and helping the plans of the poor Irish soldier of fortune. So it is that Fate works with agents, great and small; and by means over which they have no control the destinies of men and women are accomplished.
I shall always consider the conduct of Mrs.

===Chinese===

伯爵夫人初到伦敦，这老泼妇便登门“拜望”，为着她“勾引”我这件事，劈头盖脸赏了她一顿臭骂；我深信，这一场骂给我帮的忙，胜过半年的殷勤，或戳翻六个情敌。可怜的林登伯爵夫人百般分辩自己清白无辜，赌咒说她从不曾纵容过我，全属徒然。“从不曾纵容他！”老悍妇尖声嚷道：“查尔斯爵士尸骨未寒，你不是在斯帕纵容过这恶棍么？你不是把你家一个依附人嫁给了这浪子的一个破了产的堂兄弟么？他动身来英格兰，你不是第二天就像疯婆子一样跟来了么？他不是几乎就赁居在你家门边上么？——你管这个叫不曾纵容？不知羞，夫人，不知羞！你本可以嫁给我儿子——我那亲爱的、高贵的乔治；只怪他不肯碍你那不要脸的情焰，害你唆人对他下了毒手。我给夫人的劝告只有一句：把你同这个无耻冒险家结下的孽缘扶正；把这层如今既成事实、却于礼于教两不相容的关系变作名正言顺的婚配；也免得你家和你儿子再为你如今的行径蒙羞。”
侯爵老悍妇骂完，摔门而去，林登伯爵夫人泪流满面：这场谈话的种种细节，我从她夫人的女伴那里尽知详情，并预感到此事于我大为有利。
就这样，仗着蒂普托夫夫人的贤明影响，林登伯爵夫人的至亲和家人都同她断了往来。连林登伯爵夫人进宫朝觐时，王国最尊贵的娘娘接见她的那份冷淡都溢于言表，害这位不幸的寡妇回家便气恼得卧床不起。于是我可以说，王权本身也成了推进我婚事的帮手，成全了这个爱尔兰穷冒险家的谋划。命运便是这样驱遣着大大小小的工具行事的；男女的终身大事，就借着这些工具们自己全然做不了主的手段，一一成就。
我将永远把布丽奇太太

===Original===

Bridget (Lady Lyndon’s favourite maid at this juncture) as a masterpiece of ingenuity: and, indeed, had such an opinion of her diplomatic skill, that the very instant I became master of the Lyndon estates, and paid her the promised sum﻿—I am a man of honour, and rather than not keep my word with the woman, I raised the money of the Jews, at an exorbitant interest﻿—as soon, I say, as I achieved my triumph, I took Mrs. Bridget by the hand, and said, “Madam, you have shown such unexampled fidelity in my service that I am glad to reward you, according to my promise; but you have given proofs of such extraordinary cleverness and dissimulation, that I must decline keeping you in Lady Lyndon’s establishment, and beg you will leave it this very day:” which she did, and went over to the Tiptoff faction, and has abused me ever since.
But I must tell you what she did which was so clever. Why, it was the simplest thing in the world, as all masterstrokes are. When Lady Lyndon lamented her fate and my﻿—as she was pleased to call it﻿—shameful treatment of her, Mrs. Bridget said, “Why should not your Ladyship write this young gentleman word of the evil which he is causing you? Appeal to his feelings (which, I have heard say, are very good indeed﻿—the whole town is ringing with accounts of his spirit and generosity), and beg him to desist from a pursuit which causes the best of ladies so much pain? Do, my Lady, write: I know your style is so elegant that I, for my part, have many a time burst into tears in reading your charming letters, and I have no doubt Mr. Barry will sacrifice anything rather than hurt your feelings.” And, of course, the abigail swore to the fact.
“Do you think so, Bridget?” said her Ladyship. And my mistress forthwith penned me a letter, in her most fascinating and winning manner:﻿—

===Chinese===

（布丽奇太太时为林登伯爵夫人的心腹侍女）——的行事看作机巧的杰作：而且说实话，我对她那外交手段推许之至，以至于我一做了林登家产业的主人，便立刻付清许给她的那笔钱——我是个讲荣誉的人，宁肯找犹太人借印子钱、吃那惊人的利息，也不肯对一个女人失信——我是说，我大功一成，便执起布丽奇太太的手，说道：“夫人，您为我效力，忠心耿耿，世所罕见，我很高兴依约酬谢您；只是您的聪明与遮掩功夫也着实登峰造极，我不敢再把您留在林登伯爵夫人的宅里了，恳请您今日便离开：”她当真走了，投到了蒂普托夫一党门下，从此骂我不绝于口。
可她干的到底是一件什么聪明事，我必须讲给你听。啖，那是天底下最简单不过的一招，一切妙手莫不如此。林登伯爵夫人自叹命苦、抱怨我对她的——照她的说法——无耻作践时，布丽奇太太便说：“夫人何不写信知会这位少年绅士一声，说他给夫人带来了多少苦楚？何不诉一诉他的心肠（听说他那副心肠实在好得很——满城都在传他的义气和大方呢），求他别再纠缠，害天下最好的夫人这样伤心？写吧，夫人，写吧：我知道夫人的文笔何等风雅，就说我吧，读夫人那些妙信，不知落过多少回泪；巴里先生宁可牺牲一切，也断不肯伤夫人的心，这个我毫不怀疑。”这位贴身侍女自然又赌咒发誓，句句属实。
“你当真这样想么，布丽奇？”她夫人说。于是我这位女主人即刻提笔，用她最撩人、最婉转的笔致给我写了封信：——

===Original===

> “Why, sir,” wrote she, “will you pursue me? why environ me in a web of intrigue so frightful that my spirit sinks under it, seeing escape is hopeless from your frightful, your diabolical art? They say you are generous to others﻿—be so to me. I know your bravery but too well: exercise it on men who can meet your sword, not on a poor feeble woman, who cannot resist you. Remember the friendship you once professed for me. And now, I beseech you, I implore you, to give a proof of it. Contradict the calumnies which you have spread against me, and repair, if you can, and if you have a spark of honour left, the miseries which you have caused to the heartbroken
> “H. Lyndon.”

===Chinese===

> “唉，先生，”她写道，“您为什么偏要追逼我？为什么用一张如此可怕的阴谋罗网把我围住，叫我斗志全消——明知要逃出您那可怕的那魔鬼般的手段，全无指望？人家说您待人慷慨——对我也不妨慷慨一回。您的骁勇我再清楚不过：把它使在敢同您的剑相见的男人身上吧，别使在一个可怜的、无力抗拒您的柔弱女子身上。还记得您从前自称对我怀有的情谊么。如今我恳求您，哀告您，拿出一个凭证来。收回您散布的关于我的那些诽谤吧；倘若您能够，倘若您心头还剩一星荣誉的火种，请您补救您加诸这颗碎了心的
> “霍·林登。”

===Original===

What was this letter magnificent for but that I should answer it in person? My excellent ally told me where I should meet Lady Lyndon, and accordingly I followed, and found her at the Pantheon. I repeated the scene at Dublin over again; showed her how prodigious my power was, humble as I was, and that my energy was still untired. “But,” I added, “I am as great in good as I am in evil; as fond and faithful as a friend as I am terrible as an enemy. I will do everything,” I said, “which you ask of me, except when you bid me not to love you. That is beyond my power; and while my heart has a pulse I must follow you. It is *my* fate; your fate. Cease to battle against it, and be mine. Loveliest of your sex! with life alone can end my passion for you; and, indeed, it is only by dying at your command that I can be brought to obey you. Do you wish me to die?”
She said, laughing (for she was a woman of a lively, humorous turn), that she did not wish me to commit self-murder; and I felt from that moment that she was mine.
---
A year from that day, on the 15th of May, in the year 1773, I had the honour and happiness to lead to the altar Honoria, Countess of Lyndon, widow of the late Right Honourable Sir Charles Lyndon, K.B. The ceremony was performed at St. George’s, Hanover Square, by the Reverend Samuel Runt, her Ladyship’s chaplain. A magnificent supper and ball was given at our house in Berkeley Square, and the next morning I had a duke, four earls, three generals, and a crowd of the most distinguished people in London at my levée. Walpole made a lampoon about the marriage, and Selwyn cut jokes at the Cocoa-Tree. Old Lady Tiptoff, although she had recommended it, was ready to bite off her fingers with vexation; and as for young Bullingdon, who was grown a tall lad of fourteen, when called upon by the Countess to embrace his papa, he shook his fist in my face and said, “*He* my father! I would as soon call one of your Ladyship’s footmen Papa!”
But I could afford to laugh at the rage of the boy and the old woman, and at the jokes of the wits of St. James’s.

===Chinese===

这封信写得花团锦簇，不正是要我亲身作答的么？我那位出色的同盟告诉我何处可遇林登伯爵夫人，我便依言跟去，在万神殿遇见了她。我把都柏林那一幕重演了一遍；让她看看我虽出身寒微，神通却何等广大，我的元气丝毫未衰。“可是，”我补充道，“我行善同作恶一样在行；做朋友时的多情忠心，一如做仇敌时的可怕。您要我做的事，”我说，“我无有不从，只除了一件：您不许我爱您。那超出了我的能力；只要我心口还有一息脉搏，我就必须追随您。这是*我的*命运；也是您的命运。别再同它抗争了，做我的人吧。您那性别中的第一美人！唯有生命终结，我对您的热情才会终结；说真的，只有奉您的旨意去死，才算我听了您一回话。您可要我死么？”
她说着笑了（她本是位生性活泼诙谐的女子），说她并不要我自戕；从那一刻起，我便觉出她是我的人了。
---
自那一日起过了一年，1773年5月15日，我有幸且有福，牵着霍诺丽亚——林登伯爵夫人、已故的、非常尊敬的查尔斯·林登爵士（K.B.）的遗孀——走上了圣坛。婚礼在汉诺威广场的圣乔治教堂举行，主礼的是她夫人的牧师塞缪尔·伦特牧师。我们在伯克利广场的宅邸大张盛宴和舞会；第二天早晨，来我晨晤的有位公爵、四位伯爵、三位将军，还有伦敦满城的显贵。沃波尔为这桩婚事编了打油诗，塞尔温在可可树俱乐部拿它打趣。老蒂普托夫夫人虽曾亲自劝成此事，这会儿懊恼得恨不得把手指头咬下来；至于小布灵登，他已长成十四岁的高挑少年，伯爵夫人叫他来拥抱爸爸，他却对着我挥拳，说道：“*他*算我父亲！我宁可管您夫人跟前哪个跟班叫爸爸！”
不过，孩子同老太婆的火气、圣詹姆斯那班才子们的俏皮话，我都尽可以一笑置之。

===Original===

I sent off a flaming account of our nuptials to my mother and my uncle the good Chevalier; and now, arrived at the pitch of prosperity, and having, at thirty years of age, by my own merits and energy, raised myself to one of the highest social positions that any man in England could occupy, I determined to enjoy myself as became a man of quality for the remainder of my life.
After we had received the congratulations of our friends in London﻿—for in those days people were not ashamed of being married, as they seem to be now﻿—I and Honoria (who was all complacency, and a most handsome, sprightly, and agreeable companion) set off to visit our estates in the West of England, where I had never as yet set foot. We left London in three chariots, each with four horses; and my uncle would have been pleased could he have seen painted on their panels the Irish crown and the ancient coat of the Barrys beside the Countess’s coronet and the noble cognisance of the noble family of Lyndon.
Before quitting London, I procured His Majesty’s gracious permission to add the name of my lovely lady to my own; and henceforward assumed the style and title of **Barry Lyndon**, as I have written it in this autobiography.

===Chinese===

我给我母亲和我那好叔叔骑士先生发去一封大吹大擂的婚讯；如今我登上了昌盛的极顶，年方三十，全凭自己的才干和气力，挣到了英格兰人所能占据的最高的社会位置之一，我决计在下半辈子，像个有身份的人那样，好好享我的福。
在伦敦受完了朋友们的道贺——那年月人们还不以成婚为耻，不像如今似的——我和霍诺丽亚（她百依百顺，是位极标致、极活泼、极可人的伴侣）便动身去巡视我们在英格兰西部的产业，那里我还从未涉足。我们乘三辆四驾马车离开伦敦；车门外板上，伯爵夫人的宝冠纹同林登世家的尊贵徽记旁边，绘着爱尔兰王冠同巴里家的古纹章——叔叔若能看见，定然欢喜。
离开伦敦之前，我奏蒙陛下恩准，把我那可爱的夫人的姓氏加在我自己姓名之后；从此便以**巴里·林登**的名衔自称，正如我在这部自传里所署的那样。

## XIV I Return to Ireland, and Exhibit My Splendour and Generosity in That Kingdom / 十四 我重回爱尔兰，在该王国一显我的豪阔与慷慨
===Original===

How were times changed with me now! I had left my country a poor penniless boy﻿—a private soldier in a miserable marching regiment. I returned an accomplished man, with property to the amount of five thousand guineas in my possession, with a splendid wardrobe and jewel-case worth two thousand more; having mingled in all the scenes of life a not undistinguished actor in them; having shared in war and in love; having by my own genius and energy won my way from poverty and obscurity to competence and splendour. As I looked out from my chariot windows as it rolled along over the bleak bare roads, by the miserable cabins of the peasantry, who came out in their rags to stare as the splendid equipage passed, and huzza’d for his Lordship’s honour as they saw the magnificent stranger in the superb gilded vehicle, my huge body-servant Fritz lolling behind with curling moustaches and long queue, his green livery barred with silver lace, I could not help thinking of myself with considerable complacency, and thanking my stars that had endowed me with so many good qualities. But for my own merits I should have been a raw Irish squireen such as those I saw swaggering about the wretched towns through which my chariot passed on its road to Dublin. I might have married Nora Brady (and though, thank Heaven, I did not, I have never thought of that girl but with kindness, and even remember the bitterness of losing her more clearly at this moment than any other incident of my life); I might have been the father of ten children by this time, or a farmer on my own account, or an agent to a squire, or a gauger, or an attorney; and here I was one of the most famous gentlemen of Europe! I bade my fellow get a bag of copper money and throw it among the crowd as we changed horses; and I warrant me there was as much shouting set up in praise of my honour as if my Lord Townshend, the Lord Lieutenant himself, had been passing.
My second day’s journey﻿—for the Irish roads were rough in those days, and the progress of a gentleman’s chariot terribly slow﻿—brought me to Carlow, where I put up at the very inn which I had used eleven years back, when flying from home after the supposed murder of Quin in the duel. How well I remember every moment of the scene!

===Chinese===

如今我的时运是怎样翻了个身啊！我离开祖国时，是个身无分文的穷小子——一支可怜的行军团里的列兵。归来时是个全才的人，手头五千几尼的财产，加上价值再两千的华丽行头和首饰匣；人生的各色场面里都当过不算暗淡的角色；分沾过战争与爱情；凭自己的天分和精力，从贫贱无闻一路挣到温饱与煊赫。马车一路驶过荒凉的白土路，驶过庄稼人可怜的木屋——他们穿着破衣出来，呆望这煊赫的车乘驶过，看清那辆镀金巨车里气派的外乡人，便为“勋爵大人”喝起彩来，我的大块头贴身仆人弗里茨歪在车后座上，小胡子卷卷的，长辫子垂着，一身缀银绦的绿号衣——从车窗望出去，我不由得颇为自得地想起自己，感谢命运赋予我这么多好处。若非我自己的才具，我不过是我马车赴都柏林（Dublin）途中那些破镇上大摇大摆的爱尔兰小乡绅一流。我兴许娶了诺拉·布雷迪（感谢老天没娶；可我想起那姑娘从没有不带善意的，此刻回想失去她的苦味，比平生任何一件事都清楚）；我兴许如今已是十个孩子的父亲，或自耕自种的农户，或乡绅的经纪人，或税务官，或律师；而我，是欧洲数得着的绅士之一！我吩咐仆从拿一袋铜钱，换马时撒向人群；我担保，那阵颂扬大人恩德的欢呼，不亚于总督汤森勋爵本人驾到。
第二天的路程——那年月爱尔兰的路粗得很，绅士的马车走得慢得怕人——把我带到卡洛，我落脚的正是十一年前那家客栈：同奎因决斗、只当闹出了人命之后，我从家里逃出来时住过的。那一幕的每一刻我记得多清楚！

===Original===

The old landlord was gone who had served me; the inn that I then thought so comfortable looked wretched and dismantled; but the claret was as good as in the old days, and I had the host to partake of a jug of it and hear the news of the country.
He was as communicative as hosts usually are: the crops and the markets, the price of beasts at last Castle Dermot fair, the last story about the vicar, and the last joke of Father Hogan the priest; how the Whiteboys had burned Squire Scanlan’s ricks, and the highwaymen had been beaten off in their attack upon Sir Thomas’s house; who was to hunt the Kilkenny hounds next season, and the wonderful run entirely they had last March; what troops were in the town, and how Miss Biddy Toole had run off with Ensign Mullins: all the news of sport, assize, and quarter-sessions were detailed by this worthy chronicler of small-beer, who wondered that my honour hadn’t heard of them in England, or in foreign parts, where he seemed to think the world was as interested as he was about the doings of Kilkenny and Carlow. I listened to these tales with, I own, a considerable pleasure; for every now and then a name would come up in the conversation which I remembered in old days, and bring with it a hundred associations connected with them.
I had received many letters from my mother, which informed me of the doings of the Brady’s Town family. My uncle was dead, and Mick, his eldest son, had followed him too to the grave. The Brady girls had separated from their paternal roof as soon as their elder brother came to rule over it. Some were married, some gone to settle with their odious old mother in out-of-the-way watering-places. Ulick, though he had succeeded to the estate, had come in for a bankrupt property, and Castle Brady was now inhabited only by the bats and owls, and the old gamekeeper. My mother, Mrs. Harry Barry, had gone to live at Bray, to sit under Mr. Jowls, her favourite preacher, who had a chapel there; and, finally, the landlord told me, that Mrs. Barry’s son had gone to foreign parts, enlisted in the Prussian service, and had been shot there as a deserter.
I don’t care to own that I hired a stout nag from the landlord’s stable after dinner, and rode back at nightfall twenty miles to my old home. My heart beat to see it.

===Chinese===

当年伺候过我的老店主死了；我那时以为顶舒服的客栈，看着破败荒凉；可红葡萄酒还同旧日一样好，我叫掌柜的共饮一壶，听听乡下的新闻。
他同掌柜们素来一样健谈：庄稼同行情，上回卡斯尔德莫特集市牲口的价，牧师的新故事，还有霍根神父的新笑话；白衫党怎样烧了斯坎兰乡绅的草垛，强盗们攻打托马斯爵士的宅子怎样被击退；下季度基尔肯尼的猎犬归谁带，以及上三月那一场“了不得”的大追猎；镇上驻着什么队伍，比蒂·图尔小姐怎样跟穆林斯旗手私奔了：这位淡啤酒的值年史家，把猎讯、巡回法庭、季度法庭的新闻一样样数来，还纳闷大人您在英国、在外洋怎么没听说——他似乎当全世界同他一样，一心惦着基尔肯尼和卡洛的动静。我听着这些，认也不认，相当乐意：因为话里不时冒出一个旧日记得的名字，随身带来一百样牵连。
我接了母亲许多信，得知布雷迪镇一家的近况。舅舅故去了，长子米克也随他进了土。布雷迪家几位姑娘，大哥一当家便各奔东西：有的嫁了，有的跟着她们那位可憎的老母亲到偏僻的水边疗养地度日去了。尤利克虽继承了产业，接手的却是一份破产的家当，布雷迪堡如今只住着蝙蝠、猫头鹰和那个老猎场看守。我母亲哈里·巴里太太搬去布雷住，坐在她心爱的布道人焦尔斯先生座下——他在那里有个礼拜堂；末了，店主告诉我：巴里太太的独子上外洋投了普鲁士军，当逃兵在那边枪毙了。
我不怕认：晚饭后，我从店主马厩里雇了一匹壮马，趁夜色往回赶了二十英里，到我老家。见着它，我的心直跳。

===Original===

Barryville had got a pestle and mortar over the door, and was called “The Esculapian Repository,” by Doctor Macshane; a redheaded lad was spreading a plaster in the old parlour; the little window of my room, once so neat and bright, was cracked in many places, and stuffed with rags here and there; the flowers had disappeared from the trim garden-beds which my good orderly mother tended. In the churchyard there were two more names put into the stone over the family vault of the Bradys: they were those of my cousin, for whom my regard was small, and my uncle, whom I had always loved. I asked my old companion the blacksmith, who had beaten me so often in old days, to give my horse a feed and a litter: he was a worn weary-looking man now, with a dozen dirty ragged children paddling about his smithy, and had no recollection of the fine gentleman who stood before him. I did not seek to recall myself to his memory till the next day, when I put ten guineas into his hand, and bade him drink the health of English Redmond.
As for Castle Brady, the gates of the park were still there; but the old trees were cut down in the avenue, a black stump jutting out here and there, and casting long shadows as I passed in the moonlight over the worn grass-grown old road. A few cows were at pasture there. The garden-gate was gone, and the place a tangled wilderness. I sat down on the old bench, where I had sat on the day when Nora jilted me; and I do believe my feelings were as strong then as they had been when I was a boy, eleven years before; and I caught myself almost crying again, to think that Nora Brady had deserted me. I believe a man forgets nothing. I’ve seen a flower, or heard some trivial word or two, which have awakened recollections that somehow had lain dormant for scores of years; and when I entered the house in Clarges Street, where I was born (it was used as a gambling-house when I first visited London), all of a sudden the memory of my childhood came back to me﻿—of my actual infancy: I recollected my father in green and gold, holding me up to look at a gilt coach which stood at the door, and my mother in a flowered sack, with patches on her face. Some day, I wonder, will everything we have seen and thought and done come and flash across our minds in this way? I had rather not. I felt so as I sat upon the bench at Castle Brady, and thought of the bygone times.

===Chinese===

巴里维尔门楣上挂了药铺的杵臼招牌，唤作“埃斯库拉皮乌斯药房”，主人的是麦克沙恩医生；一个红头发小子正在旧客厅里摊膏药；我那间小屋的窗——从前那样齐整洁净——裂了多处，四处塞着破布；我那有条不紊的好母亲侍弄的整齐花畦，花已绝迹。教堂墓地里，布雷迪家墓窖的石碑上添了两个名字：一个是我那位表兄，我对他敬意寥寥；一个是我舅舅，我一向爱他。我请我旧日的伙伴、当年常揍我的铁匠，给我的马加一槽料、垫一捆草：他如今是个形容枯槁的人，十来个脏兮兮的破衣孩子绕着他的铁匠铺蹚水玩，全然认不出眼前这位漂亮绅士是谁。我没有自报家门，直到第二天，我把十枚几尼塞进他手里，请他喝一杯，祝“英国的雷德蒙”健康。
至于布雷迪堡，园子的大门还在；林荫道上的老树砍了，处处戳着黑桩，月光下在我脚底那荒草没胫的旧路上投出长长的影。几头牛在那里放牧。花园的门没了，园子成了一片纠结的荒野。我在那条旧长凳上坐下——诺拉甩了我的那天，我就坐在它上面；我相信那一刻我的感情，同十一年前做少年时一样强烈；想起诺拉·布雷迪抛弃了我，我发觉自己险些又哭。我相信人什么都不忘。我见过一朵花，或听见一两个闲字，唤醒的记忆竟沉睡了几十年；走进克拉格斯街我出生的那所房子（我初到伦敦时它开着赌场），童年的记忆突然全回来了——回到真正的婴孩时候：我记得一身绿底金衣的父亲，把我举起来看门口停着的一辆镀金马车；记得穿花缎袍、脸上贴着绸膏的母亲。我在想：将来某一天，我们见过、想过、做过的一切，会不会就这样统统闪回心头？我不情愿那样。我坐在布雷迪堡的长凳上想着逝去的时日，就是这感觉。

===Original===

The hall-door was open﻿—it was always so at that house; the moon was flaring in at the long old windows, and throwing ghastly chequers upon the floors; and the stars were looking in on the other side, in the blue of the yawning window over the great stair: from it you could see the old stable-clock, with the letters glistening on it still. There had been jolly horses in those stables once; and I could see my uncle’s honest face, and hear him talking to his dogs as they came jumping and whining and barking round about him of a gay winter morning. We used to mount there; and the girls looked out at us from the hall-window, where I stood and looked at the sad, mouldy, lonely old place. There was a red light shining through the crevices of a door at one corner of the building, and a dog presently came out baying loudly, and a limping man followed with a fowling-piece.
“Who’s there?” said the old man.
“**Phil Purcell**, don’t you know me?” shouted I; “it’s Redmond Barry.”
I thought the old man would have fired his piece at me at first, for he pointed it at the window; but I called to him to hold his hand, and came down and embraced him.﻿ ﻿… Psha! I don’t care to tell the rest: Phil and I had a long night, and talked over a thousand foolish old things that have no interest for any soul alive now: for what soul is there alive that cares for Barry Lyndon?
I settled a hundred guineas on the old man when I got to Dublin, and made him an annuity which enabled him to pass his old days in comfort.
Poor Phil Purcell was amusing himself at a game of exceedingly dirty cards with an old acquaintance of mine; no other than Tim, who was called my “valet” in the days of yore, and whom the reader may remember as clad in my father’s old liveries. They used to hang about him in those times, and lap over his wrists and down to his heels; but Tim, though he protested he had nigh killed himself with grief when I went away, had managed to grow enormously fat in my absence, and would have fitted almost into Daniel Lambert’s coat, or that of the vicar of Castle Brady, whom he served in the capacity of clerk.

===Chinese===

正门敞着——那所宅子向来如此；月光从长长的旧窗里照进来，在地板上投下惨白的方格；星星从另一边大楼梯顶上那扇黑洞洞的窗口望进来：从那里望得见旧马厩的钟，钟上的字还亮着。那些马厩里从前有过神气的骏马；我看得见舅舅诚实的脸，听得见他同围着他蹦跳、呜咽、吠叫的狗说话——那是冬日爽朗的早晨。我们惯在那里上马；姑娘们从大厅的窗口望我们——如今我站着，望着这凄凉、发霉、孤寂的旧地方。楼角一扇门的缝里透出红光，一条狗随即嚥着冲出来，一个瘸腿的人提着鸟枪跟在后头。
“谁？”老人说。
“菲尔·珀塞尔（Phil Purcell），认不得我了？”我喊，“我是雷德蒙·巴里。”
我起初只当老人要朝我开枪，他的枪口直指窗户；我叫他住手，走下来搂住了他。…呸！往下的事我不愿讲了：菲尔同我守了一整夜，把一千件痴赖的旧事翻了个遍——如今活在世上的哪颗心都不再关心它们了：活着的心里，还有哪一颗关心巴里·林登（Barry Lyndon）呢？
到都柏林后，我给老人安置了一百几尼，又给他立了年金，叫他晚年过得出舒坦。
可怜的菲尔·珀塞尔正同人打一副脏得厉害的牌；对手也是我的旧相识——正是蒂姆，当年人称我“贴身仆人”的那位，读者或许记得他穿着我父亲的旧号衣。那些年号衣拖着他：袖口盖过手腕，下摆扫到脚跟；可蒂姆——他赌咒说我走时他差点悲伤死——趁我不在，养得肥肥大大，快塞得进丹尼尔·兰伯特的外套，也塞得进布雷迪堡牧师的——他在牧师手下当文书。

===Original===

I would have engaged the fellow in my service but for his monstrous size, which rendered him quite unfit to be the attendant of any gentleman of condition; and so I presented him with a handsome gratuity, and promised to stand godfather to his next child: the eleventh since my absence. There is no country in the world where the work of multiplying is carried on so prosperously as in my native island. Mr. Tim had married the girls’ waiting-maid, who had been a kind friend of mine in the early times; and I had to go salute poor Molly next day, and found her a slatternly wench in a mud hut, surrounded by a brood of children almost as ragged as those of my friend the blacksmith.
From Tim and Phil Purcell, thus met fortuitously together, I got the very last news respecting my family. My mother was well.
“ ’Faith sir,” says Tim, “and you’re come in time, mayhap, for preventing an addition to your family.”
“Sir!” exclaimed I, in a fit of indignation.
“In the shape of father-in-law, I mane, sir,” says Tim: “the misthress is going to take on with Mister Jowls the praacher.”
Poor Nora, he added, had made many additions to the illustrious race of Quin; and my cousin Ulick was in Dublin, coming to little good, both my informants feared, and having managed to run through the small available remains of property which my good old uncle had left behind him.
I saw I should have no small family to provide for; and then, to conclude the evening, Phil, Tim, and I, had a bottle of usquebaugh, the taste of which I had remembered for eleven good years, and did not part except with the warmest terms of fellowship, and until the sun had been some time in the sky. I am exceedingly affable; that has always been one of my characteristics. I have no false pride, as many men of high lineage like my own have, and, in default of better company, will hob and nob with a ploughboy or a private soldier just as readily as with the first noble in the land.
I went back to the village in the morning, and found a pretext for visiting Barryville under a device of purchasing drugs.

===Chinese===

若不是他块头大得出格，当不了任何体面绅士的随从，我本想雇他进我的班底；于是我送了他一份可观的赏钱，又许他：他下个孩子我做教父——那是我走后添的第十一个。世界上再没有哪个国度，把传种接代这门功课办得比我可爱的岛上更兴旺。蒂姆先生娶了姑娘们的贴身丫头，早年间她待我有情有义；次日我只得去探望可怜的莫莉，见她已是泥屋里一个懒婆娘，围着一窝几乎同我那铁匠朋友的孩子一样破烂的娃。
从蒂姆和菲尔·珀塞尔——就这样偶然凑在一处——我得了家里的最新消息。母亲安好。
“说真的，先生，”蒂姆说，“您来得兴许正是时候，赶上拦一拦您家里添人口。”
“什么！”我火了。
“是添个后爹的意思，先生，”蒂姆说，“太太要招焦尔斯牧师先生进门啦。”
他补充说，可怜的诺拉给奎因那个煊赫的门第添了好几口人；我表兄尤利克在都柏林，没干出什么好事——两位报信人都怕——把我好老舅身后那点残余家当也踢腾光了。
我明白了：要养活的家口不小。当晚收场，菲尔、蒂姆同我干了一瓶威士忌——那口味我记了足足十一年——彼此以顶热乎的交情话别，直喝到太阳上了好一阵天。我这个人极其和气；这向来是我的品性之一。我没有假傲气——许多同我一样高门第的人有——没有更好的同伴时，我同庄稼汉、同列兵碰杯，同同国内第一号贵族碰杯一样爽快。
早晨我回村里，借口买药，寻了个由头去看巴里维尔。

===Original===

The hooks were still in the wall where my silver-hiked sword used to hang; a blister was lying on the windowsill, where my mother’s *Whole Duty of Man* had its place; and the odious Doctor Macshane had found out who I was (my countrymen find out everything, and a great deal more besides), and sniggering, asked me how I left the King of Prussia, and whether my friend the Emperor Joseph was as much liked as the Empress Maria Theresa had been. The bell-ringers would have had a ring of bells for me, but there was but one, Tim, who was too fat to pull; and I rode off before the vicar, Doctor Bolter (who had succeeded old Mr. Texter, who had the living in my time), had time to come out to compliment me; but the rapscallions of the beggarly village had assembled in a dirty army to welcome me, and cheered “Hurrah for Masther Redmond!” as I rode away.
My people were not a little anxious regarding me, by the time I returned to Carlow, and the landlord was very much afraid, he said, that the highwaymen had gotten hold of me. There, too, my name and station had been learned from my servant Fritz: who had not spared his praises of his master, and had invented some magnificent histories concerning me. He said it was the truth that I was intimate with half the sovereigns of Europe, and the prime favourite with most of them. Indeed I had made my uncle’s order of the Spur hereditary, and travelled under the name of the Chevalier Barry, chamberlain to the Duke of Hohenzollern-Sigmaringen.
They gave me the best horses the stable possessed to carry me on my road to Dublin, and the strongest ropes for harness; and we got on pretty well, and there was no rencontre between the highwaymen and the pistols with which Fritz and I were provided. We lay that night at Kilcullen, and the next day I made my entry into the city of Dublin, with four horses to my carriage, five thousand guineas in my purse, and one of the most brilliant reputations in Europe, having quitted the city a beggarly boy, eleven years before.
The citizens of Dublin have as great and laudable a desire for knowing their neighbours’ concerns as the country people have; and it is impossible for a gentleman, however modest his desires may be (and such mine have notoriously been through life), to enter the capital without having his name printed in every newspaper and mentioned in a number of societies.

===Chinese===

墙上钉子还在，从前挂我银柄剑的地方；窗台上摊着一枚发泡膏，那是我母亲那本《人的天职》的位置；可憎的麦克沙恩医生已打听到了我是谁（我的同胞什么都打听得到，还外加许多），嗤笑着问我普鲁士国王可好，我那位朋友约瑟夫皇帝是不是同玛丽亚·特蕾莎女皇一样得人心。敲钟人本想为我敲一通钟，可敲钟的只剩蒂姆一个，肥得拉不动；牧师博尔特博士（接的我那时节老特克斯特先生的俸）还没来得及出门迎我，我先骑马走了；可那村子里的那群叫花子无赖，已经结成一支脏兮兮的大军欢迎我，我一路走，一路欢呼“雷蒙德少爷万岁！”
等我回到卡洛，我的人们急得不行；店主说，他真怕强盗把我掳了去。在那儿，我的姓名和身份也从仆人弗里茨嘴里传开了：他把主人没夸够，还给我编了几段神乎其神的历史。他说：我同欧洲半数君主交好，是实情；多数君主跟前的头号红人。说真的，我把伯父那件马刺勋位作家传世袭，顶着“霍亨索伦-西格马林根公爵宫务官巴里骑士”的名号旅行。
店主拿出马厩里最好的马送我赴都柏林，套上最结实的绳索当挽具；一路顺利，弗里茨同我各带的手枪，同强盗没有照面。当夜宿基尔卡伦，第二天我进都柏林城：车驾四马，钱袋里五千几尼，一身欧洲数一数二煊赫的名声——十一年前，我是叫花子一样出城的。
都柏林的市民，打探邻家事务的兴致同乡下人一样高尚热烈；一位绅士，无论他多谦抑（我一生的谦抑是有名的），一进都城，名字必定上报、传遍各会。

===Original===

My name and titles were all over the town the day after my arrival. A great number of polite persons did me the honour to call at my lodgings, when I selected them; and this was a point very necessarily of immediate care, for the hotels in the town were but vulgar holes, unfit for a nobleman of my fashion and elegance. I had been informed of the fact by travellers on the Continent; and determining to fix on a lodging at once, I bade the drivers go slowly up and down the streets with my chariot, until I had selected a place suitable to my rank. This proceeding, and the uncouth questions and behaviour of my German Fritz, who was instructed to make inquiries at the different houses until convenient apartments could be lighted upon, brought an immense mob round my coach; and by the time the rooms were chosen you might have supposed I was the new General of the Forces, so great was the multitude following us.
I fixed at length upon a handsome suite of apartments in Capel Street, paid the ragged postilions who had driven me a splendid gratuity, and establishing myself in the rooms with my baggage and Fritz, desired the landlord to engage me a second fellow to wear my liveries, a couple of stout reputable chairmen and their machine, and a coachman who had handsome job-horses to hire for my chariot, and serviceable riding-horses to sell. I gave him a handsome sum in advance; and I promise you the effect of my advertisement was such, that next day I had a regular levée in my antechamber: grooms, valets, and *maîtres-d’hôtel* offered themselves without number; I had proposals for the purchase of horses sufficient to mount a regiment, both from dealers and gentlemen of the first fashion. Sir Lawler Gawler came to propose to me the most elegant bay-mare ever stepped; my Lord Dundoodle had a team of four that wouldn’t disgrace my friend the Emperor; and the Marquess of Ballyragget sent his gentleman and his compliments, stating that if I would step up to his stables, or do him the honour of breakfasting with him previously, he would show me the two finest greys in Europe. I determined to accept the invitations of Dundoodle and Ballyragget, but to purchase my horses from the dealers. It is always the best way.

===Chinese===

到后第二天，我的名号头衔传遍全城。一大批客气的人物赏脸到我的寓所来拜——寓所须先选定；这是一件刻不容缓的大事，因为城里的客栈不过是些粗陋的窟窿，配不上我这样风雅的贵族。这在大陆上就有旅人告诉过我；我决意立刻定下住处，吩咐车夫驾着我的马车沿街慢慢地来回走，等我看中一处配我身份的地方。这一番做派，加上我那德国人弗里茨粗声粗气的问话和做派——我吩咐他挨家去问，直到寻着合适的公寓——给我的车后聚起了一大群人；等到房间选定，人家准当我是新任全军总司令：跟的人有那么多。
末了我在卡佩尔街选定一套漂亮的公寓，给了那几个衣衫褴褛的后骑手一笔可观的犒赏，带着行李和弗里茨安顿下来，吩咐房东替我再雇一名穿我号衣的跟班，两名声誉好的壮实轿夫连同轿子，一个有漂亮雇用马的车夫——供我的马车，还有实用的坐骑出卖。我先付了一大笔；我担保，我那广告的效验是：第二天我的前厅开了正式的接见：马夫、跟班、管事的家仆，应者云集；买马的出价，足够装备一个团，贩马的有，头等时兴的绅士也有。劳勒·高勒爵士来向我兜售世上步子最优雅的枣红母马；邓杜德尔勋爵有一驾四马车，不辱我那位皇帝朋友；巴利拉格特侯爵差他的绅士送来敬意，说：若肯赏光先同他共进早餐，或劳步他的马厩，他给我看全欧洲顶好的两匹灰马。我决计应邓杜德尔和巴利拉格特之邀，马却向贩子们买。这永远是上策。

===Original===

Besides, in those days, in Ireland, if a gentleman warranted his horse, and it was not sound, or a dispute arose, the remedy you had was the offer of a bullet in your waistcoat. I had played at the bullet game too much in earnest to make use of it heedlessly: and I may say, proudly for myself, that I never engaged in a duel unless I had a real, available, and prudent reason for it.
There was a simplicity about this Irish gentry which amused and made me wonder. If they tell more fibs than their downright neighbours across the water, on the other hand they believe more; and I made myself in a single week such a reputation in Dublin as would take a man ten years and a mint of money to acquire in London. I had won five hundred thousand pounds at play; I was the favourite of the Empress Catherine of Russia; the confidential agent of Frederick of Prussia; it was I won the battle of Hochkirchen; I was the cousin of Madame Du Barry, the French King’s favourite, and a thousand things beside. Indeed, to tell the truth, I hinted a number of these stories to my kind friends Ballyragget and Gawler; and they were not slow to improve the hints I gave them.
After having witnessed the splendours of civilised life abroad, the sight of Dublin in the year 1771, when I returned thither, struck me with anything but respect. It was as savage as Warsaw almost, without the regal grandeur of the latter city. The people looked more ragged than any race I have ever seen, except the gipsy hordes along the banks of the Danube. There was, as I have said, not an inn in the town fit for a gentleman of condition to dwell in. Those luckless fellows who could not keep a carriage, and walked the streets at night, ran imminent risks of the knives of the women and ruffians who lay in wait there﻿—of a set of ragged savage villains, who neither knew the use of shoe nor razor; and as a gentleman entered his chair or his chariot, to be carried to his evening rout, or the play, the flambeaux of the footmen would light up such a set of wild gibbering Milesian faces as would frighten a genteel person of average nerves. I was luckily endowed with strong ones; besides, had seen my amiable countrymen before.
I know this description of them will excite anger among some Irish patriots, who don’t like to have the nakedness of our land abused, and are angry if the whole truth be told concerning it. But bah!

===Chinese===

再说，那些年在爱尔兰（Ireland），绅士若给马打了保票，马却不结实，或起了争执，你能得到的救济，是请你的背心吃一颗子弹。子弹这种游戏我玩过太多次真的，不肯轻率再用；我可以骄傲地说：我从不曾为决斗而决斗，除非有真实、可行、慎重的理由。
这班爱尔兰缙绅身上有股天真，叫我好笑又惊奇。他们若比海峡对岸直爽的邻居多撒几条谎，却也多信几条；我在都柏林一个礼拜挣下的名声，在伦敦得花十年外加一座造币厂。我赌钱赢过五十万镑；我是俄国女皇叶卡捷琳娜的宠臣；普鲁士腓特烈的机密代表；霍希基兴那一仗是我打赢的；我是法王宠妃杜巴利夫人的表亲——外加一千件。说真的，这些故事多半是我自己向我那两位好朋友巴利拉格特和高勒漏的口风；他们把我给的暗示发扬起来，毫不迟疑。
看惯了国外文明生活的煊赫，1771年我回到都柏林时，眼前的光景唤不起我半分敬意。它几乎同华沙一样野蛮，还没有华沙的王家气派。百姓的褴褛，除多瑙河岸那些吉普赛大群，我见过的种族里没有超过的。如前所说，城里没有一家客栈配体面绅士下榻。那些雇不起车、夜里步行街头的倒霉蛋，时时刻刻悬在刀口上——埋伏在那儿的婆娘和恶棍，一伙破衣烂衫的野贼，既不知鞋的用处，也不知剃刀的用处；绅士登轿登车上晚宴或看戏，仆人的火把一照，照出那样一张张野腔野调的米利西亚人脸，中等神经的文雅人要吓一跳。所幸我天赋神经坚强；何况我那些可爱的同胞，从前也领教过。
我知道这段描写要惹恼几位爱尔兰爱国志士——他们不爱听人揭我国土的裸相，谁讲全乎的实话就同谁急。可是呸！

===Original===

it was a poor provincial place, Dublin, in the old days of which I speak; and many a tenth-rate German residency is more genteel. There were, it is true, near three hundred resident Peers at the period; and a House of Commons; and my Lord Mayor and his corporation; and a roystering noisy University, whereof the students made no small disturbances nightly, patronised the roundhouse, ducked obnoxious printers and tradesmen, and gave the law at the Crow Street Theatre. But I had seen too much of the first society of Europe to be much tempted by the society of these noisy gentry, and was a little too much of a gentleman to mingle with the disputes and politics of my Lord Mayor and his Aldermen. In the House of Commons there were some dozen of right pleasant fellows. I never heard in the English Parliament better speeches than from Flood, and Daly, of Galway. Dick Sheridan, though not a well-bred person, was as amusing and ingenious a table-companion as ever I met; and though during Mr. Edmund Burke’s interminable speeches in the English House I used always to go to sleep, I yet have heard from well-informed parties that Mr. Burke was a person of considerable abilities, and even reputed to be eloquent in his more favourable moments.
I soon began to enjoy to the full extent the pleasures that the wretched place affords, and which were within a gentleman’s reach: Ranelagh and the Ridotto; Mr. Mossop, at Crow Street; my Lord Lieutenant’s parties, where there was a great deal too much boozing, and too little play, to suit a person of my elegant and refined habits. Daly’s Coffeehouse, and the houses of the nobility, were soon open to me; and I remarked with astonishment in the higher circles, what I had experienced in the lower on my first unhappy visit to Dublin, an extraordinary want of money, and a preposterous deal of promissory notes flying about, for which I was quite unwilling to stake my guineas. The ladies, too, were mad for play; but exceeding unwilling to pay when they lost. Thus, when the old Countess of Trumpington lost ten pieces to me at quadrille, she gave me, instead of the money, her Ladyship’s note of hand on her agent in Galway; which I put, with a great deal of politeness, into the candle.

===Chinese===

在我讲的那些年头，都柏林不过是个外省的穷地方；许多德意志三流驻节城都比它体面。诚然，那时本地住着近三百位上议院贵族；有下议院；有市长大人同他的自治体；还有一所吵吵闹闹的大学——学生们每夜闹出不小的乱子，把拘留所当靠山，把招怨的印刷商、买卖人按进水里，在克劳街戏院发号施令。可我见惯了欧洲的头等社会，这班闹哄哄的人物诱惑不了我；我又太是个绅士，不肯揽进市长大人和参事老爷们的争执和政治。下议院里倒有十来位顶可亲的。英国议会里我听过更好的演说的，只有高尔韦的弗勒德和戴利。迪克·谢里丹，虽说教养差些，却是我遇见过的最有趣最巧的席间伙伴；至于埃德蒙·伯克先生在英国下院那没完没了的演说——我一贯用来催眠；可我也听消息灵通的人说，伯克先生才具相当可观，顺风的时候还有雄辩之称。
不久我就把那可怜地方供给绅士的乐趣尽情享了起来：拉内拉和里多托；克劳街的莫索普先生；总督府的晚会——灌酒太多，赌得太少，不合我这样雅驯口味的人。戴利咖啡馆和贵族的府第随即为我敞开；我在上等圈子里惊异地发现——同我头一回倒霉来都柏林时在下等圈子里发现的一样——钱出奇地少，期票出奇地满天飞，而我的几尼，我不情愿拿去垫。太太小姐们也赌得发疯；输了又死不肯付。例如老特朗普顿伯爵夫人，四人对牌输我十枚，不给钱，给我一张她高尔韦经纪人的亲笔期票；我客客气气，把它就着烛火点了。

===Original===

But when the Countess made me a second proposition to play, I said that as soon as her Ladyship’s remittances were arrived, I would be the readiest person to meet her; but till then was her very humble servant. And I maintained this resolution and singular character throughout the Dublin society: giving out at Daly’s that I was ready to play any man, for any sum, at any game; or to fence with him, or to ride with him (regard being had to our weight), or to shoot flying, or at a mark; and in this latter accomplishment, especially if the mark be a live one, Irish gentlemen of that day had no ordinary skill.
Of course I despatched a courier in my liveries to Castle Lyndon with a private letter for Runt, demanding from him full particulars of the Countess of Lyndon’s state of health and mind; and a touching and eloquent letter to her Ladyship, in which I bade her remember ancient days, which I tied up with a single hair from the lock which I had purchased from her woman, and in which I told her that Sylvander remembered his oath, and could never forget his Calista. The answer I received from her was exceedingly unsatisfactory and inexplicit; that from Mr. Runt explicit enough, but not at all pleasant in its contents. My Lord George Poynings, the Marquess of Tiptoff’s younger son, was paying very marked addresses to the widow; being a kinsman of the family, and having been called to Ireland relative to the will of the deceased Sir Charles Lyndon.
Now, there was a sort of rough-and-ready law in Ireland in those days, which was of great convenience to persons desirous of expeditious justice; and of which the newspapers of the time contain a hundred proofs. Fellows with the nicknames of Captain Fireball, Lieutenant Buffcoat, and Ensign Steele, were repeatedly sending warning letters to landlords, and murdering them if the notes were unattended to. The celebrated Captain Thunder ruled in the southern counties, and his business seemed to be to procure wives for gentlemen who had not sufficient means to please the parents of the young ladies; or, perhaps, had not time for a long and intricate courtship.

===Chinese===

可等伯爵夫人第二次约我入局，我说：等夫人的汇款到了，我第一个奉陪；在那之前，谨为夫人卑贱的仆人。这条章程、这副独特的人格，我在都柏林社交界贯穿到底：在戴利放出话，任何人、任何注、任何牌局我都奉陪；或同他击剑，或同他赛马（体重量力而行），或打飞禽，或打靶；后一门功夫，尤其当靶子是活的时候，那个年头爱尔兰绅士的手段非同小可。
自然，我即刻打发一名穿我号衣的信使去林登堡（Castle Lyndon），带一封给伦特的私信，要他详报林登伯爵夫人身心两方面的近况；又给夫人带一封缠绵恳切的长信，唤她记起旧日——信用一根头发封缄，头发正是我从她侍女那里买来的那一绺里的——信里说，西尔万德记着他的誓，永远忘不了他的卡莉丝塔。她的回信含糊暖昧，全不着实；伦特先生的信倒着实，内容却全不讨喜。蒂普托夫侯爵的次子乔治·波因宁斯勋爵，正对寡妇大献殷勤；他是府上的亲戚，为故查尔斯·林登爵士的遗嘱事被召来爱尔兰。
再说，那些年爱尔兰有一种现成的法律，于求快的人极其便利；当日报纸足有一百个凭证。有些译名“火球上尉”“厚呢中尉”“钢条旗手”的家伙，屡屡给地主老爷们下警告信；信若不理，便取他们性命。赫赫的“霹雷上尉”雄踞南部数郡，他的营业仿佛是替绅士们办太太：哪位先生钱财不足以讨好小姐的父母，或没工夫谈一场旷日持久的曲折恋爱，都找他。

===Original===

I had found my cousin Ulick at Dublin, grown very fat, and very poor; hunted up by Jews and creditors: dwelling in all sorts of queer corners, from which he issued at nightfall to the Castle, or to his card-party at his tavern; but he was always the courageous fellow: and I hinted to him the state of my affections regarding Lady Lyndon.
“The Countess of Lyndon!” said poor Ulick; “well, that *is* a wonder. I myself have been mightily sweet upon a young lady, one of the Kiljoys of Ballyhack, who has ten thousand pounds to her fortune, and to whom her Ladyship is guardian; but how is a poor fellow without a coat to his back to get on with an heiress in such company as that? I might as well propose for the Countess myself.”
“You had better not,” said I, laughing; “the man who tries runs a chance of going out of the world first.” And I explained to him my own intention regarding Lady Lyndon. Honest Ulick, whose respect for me was prodigious when he saw how splendid my appearance was, and heard how wonderful my adventures and great my experience of fashionable life had been, was lost in admiration of my daring and energy, when I confided to him my intention of marrying the greatest heiress in England.
I bade Ulick go out of town on any pretext he chose, and put a letter into a post-office near Castle Lyndon, which I prepared in a feigned hand, and in which I gave a solemn warning to Lord George Poynings to quit the country; saying that the great prize was never meant for the likes of him, and that there were heiresses enough in England, without coming to rob them out of the domains of Captain Fireball. The letter was written on a dirty piece of paper, in the worst of spelling: it came to my Lord by the post-conveyance, and, being a high-spirited young man, he of course laughed at it.
As ill-luck would have it for him, he appeared in Dublin a very short time afterwards; was introduced to the Chevalier Redmond Barry, at the Lord Lieutenant’s table; adjourned with him and several other gentlemen to the club at Daly’s, and there, in a dispute about the pedigree of a horse, in which everybody said I was in the right, words arose, and a meeting was the consequence. I had had no affair in Dublin since my arrival, and people were anxious to see whether I was equal to my reputation.

===Chinese===

我在都柏林寻着了我表兄尤利克：养得极肥，穷得极狠；叫犹太人和债主撵得满城钻；栖身各色古怪的角落，天一黑就钻出来赴堡里的局，或去他那个客栈的牌局；可他勇气还是从前那个勇气；我把我对林登夫人（Lady Lyndon）的心事向他漏了底。
“林登伯爵夫人！”可怜的尤利克说，“嗬，这可是一桩奇迹。我自家也正痴着一位小姐，巴利哈克基尔乔伊家的一位，家私一万镑，监护人正是那位夫人；可一个背上没有外套的穷小子，在那样一群人里，拿什么去够一位继承女？我不如自己去求那位伯爵夫人。”
“你最好别，”我笑道，“去试的人，有机会先出这个世界。”随后我把自己对林登夫人的打算向他说明白。诚实的尤利克，见我这排场何等煊赫，听我历险何等惊人、时兴世故何等老到，对我的敬畏大极了；等我说出要娶英格兰头号继承女的心思，他对我的胆魄佩服得五体投地。
我吩咐尤利克随意寻个由头出城，往林登堡近处一家邮局投一封信——信我以假手写成，郑重警告乔治·波因宁斯勋爵离境：说那份头奖从没打算赏他这号人；英格兰的继承女有的是，犯不着上“火球上尉”的地界来抢。信写在一张脏纸上，错字连篇；驿路送到勋爵手里；他年轻气盛，自然一笑置之。
他倒霉就倒霉在：不出几日他到了都柏林；在总督的席上，被人引见给雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）骑士；饭后又同我和另外几位绅士转场到戴利俱乐部；在那里，为一匹马的谱系起了争执——人人说理在我——言语失和，于是约期相见了。我到都柏林以来还没开过张，人人急着看我配不配我的名声。

===Original===

I make no boast about these matters, but always do them when the time comes; and poor Lord George, who had a neat hand and a quick eye enough, but was bred in the clumsy English school, only stood before my point until I had determined where I should hit him.
My sword went in under his guard, and came out at his back. When he fell, he good-naturedly extended his hand to me, and said, “*Mr. Barry, I was wrong!*” I felt not very well at ease when the poor fellow made this confession: for the dispute had been of my making, and, to tell the truth, I had never intended it should end in any other way than a meeting.
He lay on his bed for four months with the effects of that wound; and the same post which conveyed to Lady Lyndon the news of the duel, carried her a message from Captain Fireball to say, “This is **number one**!”
“You, Ulick,” said I, “shall be *number two*.”
“ ’Faith,” said my cousin, “one’s enough:” But I had my plan regarding him, and determined at once to benefit this honest fellow, and to forward my own designs upon the widow.

===Chinese===

这些事我不吹，时候一到我照办不误；可怜的乔治勋爵，出手利落、眼也快，可受的是英国那路笨拳脚的教养，只是立在我的剑尖前，等我自己决定捾他哪儿。
我的剑从他护手底下进去，从背后出来。他倒下时，好性子地朝我伸出手，说：“巴里先生，是我错了！”可怜的家伙这一认输，我心里并不自在：这场口角本是我挑的，说句实话，我打一开始就没打算它有别的收场，只有见面一见。
他带着这一剑在床上躺了四个月；给林登夫人送去决斗消息的同一班邮路，也给她掠去了“火球上尉”的一句话：“这是第一号！”
“你，尤利克，”我说，“当第二号。”
“说真的，”我表兄说，“一个就尽够了。”可我心里自有关于他的算盘，当即打定主意：既拉扯这个诚实人一把，也把我在寡妇身上的计策推下去。

## II I Show Myself to Be a Man of Spirit / 二 我显出自己是个有血性的男儿
===Original===

During this dispute, my cousin Nora did the only thing that a lady, under such circumstances, could do, and fainted in due form. I was in hot altercation with Mick at the time, or I should have, of course, flown to her assistance, but Captain Fagan (a dry sort of fellow this Fagan was) prevented me, saying, “I advise you to leave the young lady to herself, Master Redmond, and be sure she will come to.” And so indeed, after a while, she did, which has shown me since that Fagan knew the world pretty well, for many’s the lady I’ve seen in after times recover in a similar manner. Quin did not offer to help her, you may be sure, for, in the midst of the diversion, caused by her screaming, the faithless bully stole away.
“Which of us is Captain Quin to engage?” said I to Mick; for it was my first affair, and I was as proud of it as of a suit of laced velvet. “Is it you or I, Cousin Mick, that is to have the honour of chastising this insolent Englishman?” And I held out my hand as I spoke, for my heart melted towards my cousin under the triumph of the moment.
But he rejected the proffered offer of friendship. “You﻿—you!” said he, in a towering passion; “hang you for a meddling brat: your hand is in everybody’s pie. What business had you to come brawling and quarrelling here, with a gentleman who has fifteen hundred a year?”
“Oh,” gasped Nora, from the stone bench, “I shall die: I know I shall. I shall never leave this spot.”
“The Captain’s not gone yet,” whispered Fagan; on which Nora, giving him an indignant look, jumped up and walked towards the house.
“Meanwhile,” Mick continued, “what business have you, you meddling rascal, to interfere with a daughter of this house?”
“Rascal yourself!” roared I: “call me another such name, Mick Brady, and I’ll drive my hanger into your weasand. Recollect, I stood to you when I was eleven years old. I’m your match now, and, by Jove, provoke me, and I’ll beat you like﻿—like your younger brother always did.” That was a home-cut, and I saw Mick turn blue with fury.
“This is a pretty way to recommend yourself to the family,” said Fagan, in a soothing tone.
“The girl’s old enough to be his mother,” growled Mick.
“Old or not,” I replied: “you listen to this, Mick Brady” (and I swore a tremendous oath, that need not be put down here): “the man that marries Nora Brady must first kill me﻿—do you mind that?”

===Chinese===

这场口角里，我表姐诺拉做了太太小姐在此情形下唯一可做的事：按部就班地晕了过去。当时我正同米克吵得火热，不然自然飞身去救；可费根上尉（费根是个干巴巴的家伙）拦住我，说：“我劝你随这位小姐去，雷德蒙少爷，她保准缓得过来。”果然，过了一阵她缓过来了。这叫我后来明白，费根颇懂世故：此后我见过多少太太小姐，都是以同样的方式缓过来的。奎因可没有伸手扶她——诸位想得到——趁她尖叫引起的忙乱，那负心的莽汉溜了。
“奎因上尉到底应战我们哪个？”我对米克说；这是我的头一遭，我得意得同穿了一身绦绒新衣一样。“是你还是我，米克表哥，来领这份教训无礼英国佬的荣幸？”我说着伸出手去：胜利的当口，我对表哥的心软了。
可他不受这份交情。“你——你！”他勃然大怒，“绞死你个多事崽子：哪锅汤都有你的爪子。谁叫你上这儿来，同一个一年一千五百镑的绅士大吵大闹？”
“哦，”诺拉在石凳上喘着气说，“我要死了：我知道我要死了。我一步也离不开这块地方了。”
“上尉还没走呢。”费根低声说；诺拉狠狠瞪了他一眼，跳起身，朝宅子走去。
“再说，”米克接着道，“你这个多事的流氓，谁叫你来管这家里小姐的事？”
“流氓是你自己！”我吼道，“再叫一声这个名目，米克·布雷迪，我把短刀捅进你的喉咙。记得么，我十一岁就同你对过阵。如今我同你旗鼓相当了；惹火了我，我揍你——像——像你弟弟一贯那样揍。”这一刀正扎在心窝上：我见米克气得脸色发青。
“好一个讨好这门亲的法子。”费根打圆场说。
“那丫头大得做他娘。”米克喃哝。
“大不大随它，”我答，“你听着，米克·布雷迪”（我起了一个天大的咒，此处不必录），“娶诺拉·布雷迪的男人，先得杀了我——记下这一条！”

===Original===

“Pooh, sir,” said Mick, turning away, “kill you﻿—flog you, you mean! I’ll send for Nick the huntsman to do it;” and so he went off.
Captain Fagan now came up, and taking me kindly by the hand, said I was a gallant lad, and he liked my spirit. “But what Brady says is true,” continued he; “it’s a hard thing to give a lad counsel who is in such a far-gone state as you; but, believe me, I know the world, and if you will but follow my advice, you won’t regret having taken it. Nora Brady has not a penny; you are not a whit richer. You are but fifteen, and she’s four-and-twenty. In ten years, when you’re old enough to marry, she will be an old woman; and, my poor boy, don’t you see﻿—though it’s a hard matter to see﻿—that she’s a flirt, and does not care a pin for you or Quin either?”
But who in love (or in any other point, for the matter of that) listens to advice? I never did, and I told Captain Fagan fairly, that Nora might love me or not as she liked, but that Quin should fight me before he married her﻿—that I swore.
“ ’Faith,” says Fagan, “I think you are a lad that’s likely to keep your word;” and, looking hard at me for a second or two, he walked away likewise, humming a tune: and I saw he looked back at me as he went through the old gate out of the garden. When he was gone, and I was quite alone, I flung myself down on the bench where Nora had made believe to faint, and had left her handkerchief; and, taking it up, hid my face in it, and burst into such a passion of tears as I would then have had nobody see for the world. The crumpled riband which I had flung at Quin lay in the walk, and I sat there for hours, as wretched as any man in Ireland, I believe, for the time being. But it’s a changeable world! When we consider how great our sorrows *seem*, and how small they *are*; how we think we shall die of grief, and how quickly we forget, I think we ought to be ashamed of ourselves and our fickle-heartedness. For, after all, what business has time to bring us consolation? I have not, perhaps, in the course of my multifarious adventures and experience, hit upon the right woman; and have forgotten, after a little, every single creature I adored; but I think, if I could but have lighted on the right one, I would have loved her for *ever*.

===Chinese===

“呸，先生，”米克掉头就走，“杀你——是抽你！我打发猎户尼克来动手。”说罢他走了。
费根上尉走上前来，亲热地执我的手，说我是个有胆的小子，他喜欢我的血性。“可布雷迪说的也是实情，”他接着说，“给你这样病入膏肓的小子出主意，是件难事；可相信我，我懂世界；你只要依我的话，不会后悔。诺拉·布雷迪身无一文；你也不比她富一分。你才十五，她二十四。等十年，到你娶亲的年纪，她已是老太婆；我可怜的孩子，你难道看不出——虽则这层难看清——她是个卖弄风情的，你也好奎因也好，她半根大头针也不放在心上？”
可热恋中的人（旁的事也一样）谁听劝？我从不听。我干脆告诉费根上尉：诺拉爱不爱我随她的便，可奎因要娶她，先得同我打过——这话我赌了咒。
“说真的，”费根说，“我看你是个说话算话的小子。”他定定看了我一两秒，也走开了，一路哼着曲子：我看见他出了园子的旧门，还回头望我。他一走，只剩我独自一个，我扑倒在诺拉装晕的那条长凳上——她把手绢落下了；我拾起来，把脸埋进去，一阵热泪涌出，那时节叫谁看见我都情愿没有。我朝奎因摔的那条皱缎带躺在小径上；我在那儿坐了几个钟头，一时信比爱尔兰任何人都要凄惨。可这世界多变！想想我们的悲苦看起来多大、实际多小；想想我们自以为要痛死、又忘得多快——我们真该为自己的薄情害臊。再说，时间有什么资格来安慰我们？我在千般历险与阅历里，兴许始终没碰上对的那个女人；我倾心过的每一个人，过不多久全忘了；可我想，我只要碰上了对的那一个，我会爱她到永远。

===Original===

I must have sat for some hours bemoaning myself on the garden bench, for it was morning when I came to Castle Brady, and the dinner-bell clanged as usual at three o’clock, which wakened me up from my reverie. Presently I gathered up the handkerchief, and once more took the riband. As I passed through the offices, I saw the Captain’s saddle was still hanging up at the stable-door, and saw his odious red-coated brute of a servant swaggering with the scullion-girls and kitchen-people. “The Englishman’s still there, Master Redmond,” said one of the maids to me (a sentimental black-eyed girl, who waited on the young ladies). “He’s there in the parlour, with the sweetest fillet of vale; go in, and don’t let him browbeat you, Master Redmond.”
And in I went, and took my place at the bottom of the big table, as usual, and my friend the butler speedily brought me a cover.
“Hallo, Reddy my boy!” said my uncle, “up and well?﻿—that’s right.”
“He’d better be home with his mother,” growled my aunt.
“Don’t mind her,” says Uncle Brady; “it’s the cold goose she ate at breakfast didn’t agree with her. Take a glass of spirits, Mrs. Brady, to Redmond’s health.” It was evident he did not know of what had happened; but Mick, who was at dinner too, and Ulick, and almost all the girls, looked exceedingly black, and the Captain foolish; and Miss Nora, who was again by his side, ready to cry. Captain Fagan sat smiling; and I looked on as cold as a stone. I thought the dinner would choke me: but I was determined to put a good face on it, and when the cloth was drawn, filled my glass with the rest; and we drank the King and the Church, as gentlemen should. My uncle was in high good-humour, and especially always joking with Nora and the Captain. It was, “Nora, divide that merry-thought with the Captain! see who’ll be married first.” “Jack Quin, my dear boy, never mind a clean glass for the claret, we’re short of crystal at Castle Brady; take Nora’s and the wine will taste none the worse;” and so on. He was in the highest glee﻿—I did not know why. Had there been a reconciliation between the faithless girl and her lover since they had come into the house?
I learned the truth very soon. At the third toast, it was always the custom for the ladies to withdraw; but my uncle stopped them this time, in spite of the remonstrances of Nora, who said, “Oh, pa! do let us go!” and said, “No, Mrs.

===Chinese===

我在园中长凳上自伤自怜，一定坐了几个钟头：早晨到布雷迪堡，三点钟开饭的钟照旧当当地敲，把我从沉思里敲醒。我拾起手绢，又拾起缎带。穿过下房时，我看见上尉的鞍子还挂在马厩门口，他那可憎的红衣粗仆正在同洗碗丫头和厨房的人瞎晃。“英国佬还在，雷德蒙少爷，”一个使女对我说（一个多愁善感的黑眼睛姑娘，伺候小姐们的）。“他在客厅呢，配着最嫩的里脊；进去吧，别叫他压着你，雷德蒙少爷。”
我进去了，照旧在大桌子末尾坐了我的位子，我的朋友管家很快替我摆上餐具。
“嗬，雷迪我的小子！”我舅舅说，“起来啦、好啦？——这才对。”
“他顶好回他娘那儿去。”我舅妈喃哝。
“别理她，”布雷迪舅舅说，“是早点吃的那只冷鹅跟她不对付。喝杯酒，布雷迪太太，祝雷德蒙健康。”他分明还不知道出了事；可同桌的米克、尤利克和几乎全体姑娘们脸色阴得吓人，上尉傻乎乎的；诺拉小姐又坐回他身边，随时要哭。费根上尉含笑坐着；我冷得像块石头看着。我只当这顿饭要把我喳死；可我打定主意撑住体面，撤席布时，也同大家一样斟满一杯：我们照绅士的规矩，祝了国王和教会。舅舅兴致极高，专拿诺拉和上尉打趣。一会儿是：“诺拉，同上尉分这条叉骨！看谁先结婚。”一会儿是：“杰克·奎因，亲爱的伙计，喝红葡萄酒别讲究干净杯子了，布雷迪堡水晶紧；就用诺拉的，酒味不会差半分。”等等。他乐到了顶点——我不知道为的什么。难道那负心的姑娘同她情人进屋之后又言归于好了？
真相我很快就知道了。第三道祝酒，照例是女眷退席的时辰；可这回舅舅拦住了，不顾诺拉的抗议——她说：“哦，爸！让我们走吧！”——“不行，布雷迪太太和各位女士，对不住；这一杯酒，我家喝的次数太少了，请各位满饮尽欢。敬约翰·奎因上尉和太太，祝他们长寿。亲她一口，杰克，你这坏家伙：说真的，你得着宝贝了！”

===Original===

Brady and ladies, if you plaise; this is a sort of toast that is drunk a great dale too seldom in my family, and you’ll plaise to receive it with all the honours. Here’s **Captain and Mrs. John Quin**, and long life to them. Kiss her, Jack, you rogue: for ’faith you’ve got a treasure!”
“He has already﻿—” I screeched out, springing up.
“Hold your tongue, you fool﻿—hold your tongue!” said big Ulick, who sat by me; but I wouldn’t hear.
“He has already,” I screamed, “been slapped in the face this morning, Captain John Quin; he’s already been called coward, Captain John Quin; and this is the way I’ll drink his health. Here’s your health, Captain John Quin!” And I flung a glass of claret into his face. I don’t know how he looked after it, for the next moment I myself was under the table, tripped up by Ulick, who hit me a violent cuff on the head as I went down; and I had hardly leisure to hear the general screaming and skurrying that was taking place above me, being so fully occupied with kicks, and thumps, and curses, with which Ulick was belabouring me. “You fool!” roared he﻿—“you great blundering marplot﻿—you silly beggarly brat” (a thump at each), “hold your tongue!” These blows from Ulick, of course, I did not care for, for he had always been my friend, and had been in the habit of thrashing me all my life.
When I got up from under the table all the ladies were gone; and I had the satisfaction of seeing the Captain’s nose was bleeding, as mine was﻿—*his* was cut across the bridge, and his beauty spoiled forever. Ulick shook himself, sat down quietly, filled a bumper, and pushed the bottle to me. “There, you young donkey,” said he, “sup that; and let’s hear no more of your braying.”
“In Heaven’s name, what does all the row mean?” says my uncle. “Is the boy in the fever again?”
“It’s all your fault,” said Mick sulkily: “yours and those who brought him here.”
“Hold your noise, Mick!” says Ulick, turning on him; “speak civil of my father and me, and don’t let me be called upon to teach you manners.”
“It *is* your fault,” repeated Mick. “What business has the vagabond here? If I had my will, I’d have him flogged and turned out.”
“And so he should be,” said Captain Quin.

===Chinese===

“他已经——”我尖叫着跳起来。
“住口，你这蠢货——住口！”坐在我旁边的大个子尤利克说；可我不听。
“他已经，”我喊，“今天早晨挨过一记耳光，约翰·奎因上尉；已经被人叫过懦夫，约翰·奎因上尉；他的健康我这样祝。”说罢一杯红葡萄酒泼在他脸上。泼完他什么样我没看见，因为下一刻我自己已到了桌子底下——尤利克勾倒了我，我倒下去时他朝我头上狠来了一记；上头尖叫声、奔逃声响成一片，我几乎没有工夫听：尤利克的脚踢、拳捶、咒骂忙得我应接不暇。“你这蠢货！”他吼——“你这大个子成事不足败事有余的——你这要饭的傻崽子”（一下一个），“住口！”尤利克这些打，我自然不计较：他一向是我的朋友，平生打我惯了。
我从桌子底下爬起来，女眷全走光了；我满意地看见上尉的鼻子在流血，同我的一样——他的鼻梁开了口子，他的相貌毁了终身。尤利克抖了抖身子，安然坐下，斟满一大杯，把酒瓶推给我。“喏，小毛驴，”他说，“灌下去；别再叫唤了。”
“老天爷，这场闹到底为什么？”我舅舅说，“这孩子热病又犯了？”
“全怪你，”米克阴沉沉地说，“怪你，怪把他领来的人。”
“少聒噪，米克！”尤利克转身对他，“对我父亲和我放尊重些，别等我教你规矩。”
“就是怪你们，”米克重复，“这流氓在这儿有什么名堂？依我的主意，抽他一顿鞭子撵出去。”
“正该如此。”奎因上尉说。

===Original===

“You’d best not try it, Quin,” said Ulick, who was always my champion; and turning to his father, “The fact is, sir, that the young monkey has fallen in love with Nora, and finding her and the Captain mighty sweet in the garden today, he was for murdering Jack Quin.”
“Gad, he’s beginning young,” said my uncle, quite good-humouredly. “ ’Faith, Fagan, that boy’s a Brady, every inch of him.”
“And I’ll tell you what, Mr. B.,” cried Quin, bristling up: “I’ve been insulted grossly in this *’ouse*. I ain’t at all satisfied with these here ways of going on. I’m an Englishman I am, and a man of property; and I﻿—I﻿—”
“If you’re insulted, and not satisfied, remember there’s two of us, Quin,” said Ulick gruffly. On which the Captain fell to washing his nose in water, and answered never a word.
“Mr. Quin,” said I, in the most dignified tone I could assume, “may also have satisfaction any time he pleases, by calling on Redmond Barry, Esquire, of Barryville.” At which speech my uncle burst out a-laughing (as he did at everything); and in this laugh, Captain Fagan, much to my mortification, joined. I turned rather smartly upon him, however, and bade him to understand that as for my cousin Ulick, who had been my best friend through life, I could put up with rough treatment from him; yet, though I was a boy, even that sort of treatment I would bear from him no longer; and any other person who ventured on the like would find me a man, to their cost. “Mr. Quin,” I added, “knows that fact very well; and if *he’s* a man, he’ll know where to find me.”
My uncle now observed that it was getting late, and that my mother would be anxious about me. “One of you had better go home with him,” said he, turning to his sons, “or the lad may be playing more pranks.” But Ulick said, with a nod to his brother, “Both of us ride home with Quin here.”
“I’m not afraid of Freny’s people,” said the Captain, with a faint attempt at a laugh; “my man is armed, and so am I.”
“You know the use of arms very well, Quin,” said Ulick; “and no one can doubt your courage; but Mick and I will see you home for all that.”
“Why, you’ll not be home till morning, boys. Kilwangan’s a good ten mile from here.”
“We’ll sleep at Quin’s quarters,” replied Ulick: “*We’re going to stop a week there.*”
“Thank you,” says Quin, very faint; “it’s very kind of you.”
“You’ll be lonely, you know, without us.”

“你顶好别试，奎因。”尤利克说——他向来是我的保护人；随后转向他父亲：“实情是，爸爸，这小猴子爱上了诺拉；今天在园子里撞见她同上尉亲热，就要谋杀杰克·奎因。”
“嗬，起步倒早。”舅舅说，全无恼意，“说真的，费根，这小子浑身上下，一寸寸是布雷迪。”
“我也告诉您，布雷迪先生，”奎因炸了毛，“我在这个家里受了粗野的侮辱。这一套做派我万难满意。我是英国人，是有产业的人；我——我——”
“你既受了侮辱、不满意，记得敝方还有两个人，奎因。”尤利克粗声说。上尉闻言，低头洗起鼻子来，一个字不答。
“奎因先生，”我用我能摆出的最尊严的声调说，“也可以随时取他的满足，只消到巴里维尔寻巴里维尔的雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）绅士。”这话引得我舅舅放声大笑（他样样事都笑）；叫我不痛快的是，费根上尉也跟着笑。我相当利落地回敬他：告诉他，至于我表哥尤利克——我平生最好的朋友——他对我怎样粗手粗脚，我受得；可我虽是个孩子，从今往后连他那样的对待也不再受了；旁人敢照样来的，叫他领教领教我是个男子汉——有他好看的。“奎因先生，”我补一句，“对这一条清楚得很；他若是个男人，自知道上哪儿找我。”
我舅舅这时说：天色不早了，我母亲要挂心了。“你们哪个送他回去，”他转向儿子们，“免得这小子再闯祸。”可尤利克朝他兄弟一点头：“我们俩一道，送奎因回家。”
“我不怕弗雷尼那伙人，”上尉勉强笑笑，“我仆人带家伙，我也带。”
“兵器的事你是行家，奎因，”尤利克说，“你的胆量没人疑心；可米克和我还是送你到家。”
“嗦，孩子们，你们到家得早晨了。基尔万根离这儿整十里。”
“我们睡奎因的住处，”尤利克答，“我们打算在那儿住一礼拜。”
“谢谢，”奎因有气无力地说，“二位真客气。”
“没有我们，你冷清啊，你知道。”

===Original===

“Oh yes, very lonely!” says Quin.
“And in *another week*, my boy,” says Ulick (and here he whispered something in the Captain’s ear, in which I thought I caught the words “marriage,” “parson,” and felt all my fury returning again).
“As you please,” whined out the Captain; and the horses were quickly brought round, and the three gentlemen rode away.
Fagan stopped, and, at my uncle’s injunction, walked across the old treeless park with me. He said that after the quarrel at dinner, he thought I would scarcely want to see the ladies that night, in which opinion I concurred entirely; and so we went off without an adieu.
“A pretty day’s work of it you have made, Master Redmond,” said he. “What! you a friend to the Bradys, and knowing your uncle to be distressed for money, try and break off a match which will bring fifteen hundred a year into the family? Quin has promised to pay off the four thousand pounds which is bothering your uncle so. He takes a girl without a penny﻿—a girl with no more beauty than yonder bullock. Well, well, don’t look furious; let’s say she *is* handsome﻿—there’s no accounting for tastes﻿—a girl that has been flinging herself at the head of every man in these parts these ten years past, and *missing* them all. And you, as poor as herself, a boy of fifteen﻿—well, sixteen, if you insist﻿—and a boy who ought to be attached to your uncle as to your father﻿—”
“And so I am,” said I.
“And this is the return you make him for his kindness! Didn’t he harbour you in his house when you were an orphan, and hasn’t he given you rent-free your fine mansion of Barryville yonder? And now, when his affairs can be put into order, and a chance offers for his old age to be made comfortable, who flings himself in the way of him and competence?﻿—You, of all others; the man in the world most obliged to him. It’s wicked, ungrateful, unnatural. From a lad of such spirit as you are, I expect a truer courage.”
“I am not afraid of any man alive,” exclaimed I (for this latter part of the Captain’s argument had rather staggered me, and I wished, of course, to turn it﻿—as one always should when the enemy’s too strong); “and it’s *I* am the injured man, Captain Fagan. No man was ever, since the world began, treated so. Look here﻿—look at this riband. I’ve worn it in my heart for six months. I’ve had it there all the time of the fever.

===Chinese===

“哦是，冷清得很！”奎因说。
“再过一个礼拜，我的伙计，”尤利克说——说着凑到上尉耳边喃咕了一句什么，我像听见了“婚事”“牧师”几个字，只觉怒火又全涌了上来。
“随你们便。”上尉哭丧着说；马很快牵了来，三位绅士骑马走了。
费根留了下来，奉我舅舅之命，陪我步行穿过那片没有树的老园子。他说，饭桌上那场争吵之后，今晚我想必不愿见女眷们，这层我完全同意；于是我们没有道别就走了。
“你这一天的活儿干得漂亮，雷德蒙少爷。”他说。“怎么！你算布雷迪家的朋友，明知你舅舅等着钱用，还想揽黄一门一年给家里带进一千五百镑的亲事？奎因应承替他清掉那四千镑的债——那正是缠着你舅舅的。他娶一个身无分文的姑娘——一个美貌不比那边那头小牛多一分的姑娘。行行行，别瞪眼；就算她标致——各有所好——一个十年来朝这一带每个男人头上扑、回回扑空的姑娘。而你，同她一样穷，一个十五岁的小子——好吧，十六，随你坚持——一个本该把舅舅当父亲一样亲的孩子——”
“我是亲的。”我说。
“你对他的一番好意就报答成这样！你做孤儿时谁收留的你？那边巴里维尔那所好宅子，谁免租给你娘住的？如今他的事眼看可以理顺，老年的安顿有了指望，谁横在路上挡他的道？——偏偏是你；天下人里最欠他情分的人。这狠心、忘恩、悖逆。像你这样有血性的少年，我指望的是一份更真的勇气。”
“活人我谁也不怕。”我喊（这后半篇道理把我顶得有些晃，我自然想把它岔开——敌人太强时谁都该这样），“受屈的正是我，费根上尉。开天辟地，没人受过我这样的对待。瞧这个——瞧这条缎带。我把它贴着心口戴了六个月。发热病那些天，它一直贴在我心口。

===Original===

Didn’t Nora take it out of her own bosom and give it me? Didn’t she kiss me when she gave it me, and call me her darling Redmond?”
“She was *practising*,” replied Mr. Fagan, with a sneer. “I know women, sir. Give them time, and let nobody else come to the house, and they’ll fall in love with a chimney-sweep. There was a young lady in Fermoy﻿—”
“A young lady in flames,” roared I (but I used a still hotter word). “Mark this; come what will of it, I swear I’ll fight the man who pretends to the hand of Nora Brady. I’ll follow him, if it’s into the church, and meet him there. I’ll have his blood, or he shall have mine; and this riband shall be found dyed in it. Yes, and if I kill him, I’ll pin it on his breast, and then she may go and take back her token.” This I said because I was very much excited at the time, and because I had not read novels and romantic plays for nothing.
“Well,” says Fagan after a pause, “if it must be, it must. For a young fellow, you are the most bloodthirsty I ever saw. Quin’s a determined fellow, too.”
“Will you take my message to him?” said I, quite eagerly.
“Hush!” said Fagan: “your mother may be on the lookout. Here we are, close to Barryville.”
“Mind! not a word to my mother,” I said; and went into the house swelling with pride and exultation to think that I should have a chance against the Englishman I hated so.
Tim, my servant, had come up from Barryville on my mother’s return from church; for the good lady was rather alarmed at my absence, and anxious for my return. But he had seen me go in to dinner, at the invitation of the sentimental lady’s-maid; and when he had had his own share of the good things in the kitchen, which was always better furnished than ours at home, had walked back again to inform his mistress where I was, and, no doubt, to tell her, in his own fashion, of all the events that had happened at Castle Brady. In spite of my precautions to secrecy, then, I half suspected that my mother knew all, from the manner in which she embraced me on my arrival, and received our guest, Captain Fagan. The poor soul looked a little anxious and flushed, and every now and then gazed very hard in the Captain’s face; but she said not a word about the quarrel, for she had a noble spirit, and would as lief have seen any one of her kindred hanged as shirking from the field of honour.

===Chinese===

不是诺拉亲手从她胸口的衣襟里取出来给我的么？她给我时不是亲过我、唤我做她亲爱的雷德蒙么？”
“她那是练手。”费根先生冷笑答，“女人我懂，先生。给她们工夫，别放别的男人进门，她们同扫烟囱的也能恋爱。费莫伊有过一位小姐——”
“小姐着了火。”我吼道（用的字眼比这更辣）。“记下：不管落个什么下场，我赌咒：谁敢打诺拉·布雷迪的主意，我就同谁决斗。他进教堂我也跟进去，就在教堂里会他。不是他的血，就是我的血；这条缎带到时候叫它在血里染色。对，我若杀了他，就把它别在他胸口；她要讨回她的信物，自己去取。”这些话，一半因为我当时激动得厉害，一半因为我的小说和浪漫戏没有白读。
“好吧，”费根隔了一阵说，“非如此不可，那就如此。小小年纪，你是我见过最嗜血的一个。奎因也是个狠角色。”
“肯替我传话么？”我急切地说。
“嘘！”费根说，“你娘兴许在望。到巴里维尔了。”
“记着！对我娘只字不提。”我说；进屋时胸膛挺得满满的，又骄又喜：想着就要同我痛恨的英国佬有机会碰一碰。
我的仆人蒂姆，随我母亲从教堂回来就上布雷迪堡来了：好太太见我不在，又是急，又是盼。可他受那位多情小姐的使女之邀，看见我进去吃饭了；他在厨房里分到了一份好菜——那里的伙食素来比我们家的强——随后便走回去禀报女主人在哪儿；多半还照他自己的路数，把布雷迪堡这一天的桩桩件件全讲了。所以，尽管我千防万防，从母亲迎我进门、接待费根上尉的神气里，我已有几分疑心她全知道了。可怜的人儿神色有点焦急、发红，时不时死死盯住上尉的脸；可对那场争吵，她一字不提：她有一副高贵的心肠，宁可看自家骨肉任何一个上绞架，也不看他逃避荣誉的战场。

===Original===

What has become of those gallant feelings nowadays? Sixty years ago a man was a *man*, in old Ireland, and the sword that was worn by his side was at the service of any gentleman’s gizzard, upon the slightest difference. But the good old times and usages are fast fading away. One scarcely every hears of a fair meeting now, and the use of those cowardly pistols, in place of the honourable and manly weapon of gentlemen, has introduced a deal of knavery into the practice of duelling, that cannot be sufficiently deplored.
When I arrived at home I felt that I was a man in earnest, and welcoming Captain Fagan to Barryville, and introducing him to my mother, in a majestic and dignified way, said the Captain must be thirsty after his walk, and called upon Tim to bring up a bottle of the yellow-sealed Bordeaux, and cakes and glasses, immediately.
Tim looked at the mistress in great wonderment: and the fact is, that six hours previous I would as soon have thought of burning the house down as calling for a bottle of claret on my own account; but I felt I was a man now, and had a right to command; and my mother felt this too, for she turned to the fellow and said, sharply, “Don’t you hear, you rascal, what *your master* says! Go, get the wine, and the cakes and glasses, directly.” Then (for you may be sure she did not give Tim the keys of our little cellar) she went and got the liquor herself; and Tim brought it in, on the silver tray, in due form. My dear mother poured out the wine, and drank the Captain welcome; but I observed her hand shook very much as she performed this courteous duty, and the bottle went clink, clink, against the glass. When she had tasted her glass, she said she had a headache, and would go to bed; and so I asked her blessing, as becomes a dutiful son﻿—(the modern *bloods* have given up the respectful ceremonies which distinguished a gentleman in my time)﻿—and she left me and Captain Fagan to talk over our important business.
“Indeed,” said the Captain, “I see now no other way out of the scrape than a meeting. The fact is, there was a talk of it at Castle Brady, after your attack upon Quin this afternoon, and he vowed that he would cut you in pieces: but the tears and supplications of Miss Honoria induced him, though very unwillingly, to relent. Now, however, matters have gone too far.

===Chinese===

如今那些堂堂的心气都到哪里去了？六十年前，在古老的爱尔兰，男人才是男人；腰里挎的剑，为一点小小过节，随时听候任何绅士的肝胆差遣。可那好时光、好风气正在飞快退去。如今难得听说一场公道的相会；那班怯懦的手枪顶替了绅士光荣而丈夫气的武器，给决斗的行当里掺进了太多的奸诈——怎么叹惜也不为过。
到家时我自觉是个真正的男人了；我把费根上尉迎进巴里维尔，把他引见给我母亲，态度庄严郑重，说上尉走这一趟必定渴了，吩咐蒂姆即刻端上一瓶黄封的波尔多，连同糕点和杯子。
蒂姆惊愕地看看主母：实情是，六个钟头之前，叫我自作主张要一瓶红葡萄酒，同放把火把房子烧了差不多；可我如今自觉是个男人，有发号施令的权利；母亲也觉出了这个，只见她转向那家伙，厉声说：“没听见你主人吩咐么，你这恶棍！去，取酒，取糕点和杯子，即刻。”随后（诸位放心，我们小酒窖的钥匙她是不会交给蒂姆的）她亲自去取了酒；蒂姆用银托盘端进来，规规矩矩。我亲爱的母亲斟了酒，敬上尉一杯接风；可我看见她尽这份客礼时手抖得厉害，酒瓶碰着杯子，叮叮直响。她咂了一口，说头痛，要睡了；于是我讨了她的祝福，像孝顺儿子该做的那样——（如今的纨绔们，把我那个时代区别于绅士的那些恭敬礼数全丢了）——她留下我同费根上尉谈我们的大事。
“说真的，”上尉说，“如今除了见面，我看没有别的出路了。实情是：今天下午你打了奎因之后，布雷迪堡里就议过一场；他赌咒要把你剁成几段：是霍诺丽亚小姐的眼泪和哀告，才叫他极不情愿地软了下来。可如今，事情闹得太过了。

===Original===

No officer, bearing His Majesty’s commission, can receive a glass of wine on his nose﻿—this claret of yours is very good, by the way, and by your leave we’ll ring for another bottle﻿—without resenting the affront. Fight you must; and Quin is a huge strong fellow.”
“He’ll give the better mark,” said I. “I am not afraid of him.”
“In faith,” said the Captain, “I believe you are not; for a lad, I never saw more game in my life.”
“Look at that sword, sir,” says I, pointing to an elegant silver-mounted one, in a white shagreen case, that hung on the mantelpiece, under the picture of my father, Harry Barry. “It was with that sword, sir, that my father pinked Mohawk O’Driscol, in Dublin, in the year 1740; with that sword, sir, he met Sir Huddlestone Fuddlestone, the Hampshire baronet, and ran him through the neck. They met on horseback, with sword and pistol, on Hounslow Heath, as I dare say you have heard tell of, and those are the pistols” (they hung on each side of the picture) “which the gallant Barry used. He was quite in the wrong, having insulted Lady Fuddlestone, when in liquor, at the Brentford assembly. But, like a gentleman, he scorned to apologise, and Sir Huddlestone received a ball through his hat, before they engaged with the sword. I am Harry Barry’s son, sir, and will act as becomes my name and my quality.”
“Give me a kiss, my dear boy,” said Fagan, with tears in his eyes. “You’re after my own soul. As long as Jack Fagan lives you shall never want a friend or a second.”
Poor fellow! he was shot six months afterwards, carrying orders to my Lord George Sackville, at Minden, and I lost thereby a kind friend. But we don’t know what is in store for us, and that night was a merry one at least. We had a second bottle, and a third too (I could hear the poor mother going downstairs for each, but she never came into the parlour with them, and sent them in by the butler, Mr. Tim): and we parted at length, he engaging to arrange matters with Mr. Quin’s second that night, and to bring me news in the morning as to the place where the meeting should take place. I have often thought since, how different my fate might have been, had I not fallen in love with Nora at that early age; and had I not flung the wine in Quin’s face, and so brought on the duel.

===Chinese===

扛着王上任状的军官，断不能让人把一杯酒泼上鼻子——顺便说，你这红葡萄酒很好，容我摇铃再来一瓶——而不讨这场侮辱。这一仗你非打不可；奎因可是个大块头的壮汉。”
“他目标也大。”我说，“我不怕他。”
“真的，”上尉说，“我看你是不怕；小小年纪，平生我没见过这么足的胆气。”
“看那口剑，先生。”我说，指着一口精致的银装剑，白鲨皮匣，挂在壁炉架上、我父亲哈里·巴里的画像底下。“先生，我父亲就是用这口剑，1740年在都柏林捅穿了莫霍克·奥德里斯科尔；先生，也是用这口剑，他会见了汉普郡的从男爵哈德尔斯通·法德尔斯通爵士，一剑穿了他的脖子。他们是骑马相会，剑枪并用，在亨斯洛荒原上——您想必听人讲过；这几支手枪（挂在画像两边），就是巴里勇士当年用的。理亏全在他：他在布伦特福德的舞会上喝多了，冒犯了法德尔斯通夫人。可他像绅士那样，屑也不屑道歉；交剑之前，哈德尔斯通爵士的帽子上先吃了他一颗子弹。我是哈里·巴里的儿子，先生，行事要配得上我的姓氏和身份。”
“让我亲一口，亲爱的孩子。”费根眼里含着泪说，“你对我的脾性。只要杰克·费根活着，你永远不缺朋友和副手。”
可怜的人！不出半年，他在明登给乔治·萨克维尔勋爵送军令，中了弹，我从此失了一位好朋友。可谁又知道前头等着我们什么；那一夜总算快活。我们又开了第二瓶，第三瓶也开了（每一瓶我都听得见可怜的母亲下楼去取，可她从不亲手端进客厅，都打发管家蒂姆先生送进来）；末了我们分手，他应承当夜就去同奎因先生的副手安排，明早把会面的地点带给我。此后我常想：倘若我不曾那样小小年纪爱上诺拉；倘若我不曾把酒泼上奎因的脸、惹出那场决斗，我的命运会是多么不同。

===Original===

I might have settled down in Ireland but for that (for Miss Quinlan was an heiress, within twenty miles of us, and Peter Burke, of Kilwangan, left his daughter Judy £700 a year, and I might have had either of them, had I waited a few years). But it was in my fate to be a wanderer, and that battle with Quin sent me on my travels at a very early age: as you shall hear anon.
I never slept sounder in my life, though I woke a little earlier than usual; and you may be sure my first thought was of the event of the day, for which I was fully prepared. I had ink and pen in my room﻿—had I not been writing those verses to Nora but the day previous, like a poor fond fool as I was? And now I sat down and wrote a couple of letters more: they might be the last, thought I, that I ever should write in my life. The first was to my mother:﻿—

> “**Honoured Madam**”﻿—I wrote﻿—“This will not be given you unless I fall by the hand of Captain Quin, whom I meet this day in the field of honour, with sword and pistol. If I die, it is as a good Christian and a gentleman﻿—how should I be otherwise when educated by such a mother as you? I forgive all my enemies﻿—I beg your blessing as a dutiful son. I desire that my mare Nora, which my uncle gave me, and which I called after the most faithless of her sex, may be returned to Castle Brady, and beg you will give my silver-hiked hanger to Phil Purcell, the gamekeeper. Present my duty to my uncle and Ulick, and all the girls of *my* party there. And I remain your dutiful son,
> “Redmond Barry.”

To Nora I wrote:﻿—

> “This letter will be found in my bosom along with the token you gave me. It will be dyed in my blood (unless I have Captain Quin’s, whom I hate, but forgive), and will be a pretty ornament for you on your marriage-day. Wear it, and think of the poor boy to whom you gave it, and who died (as he was always ready to do) for your sake.
> “Redmond.”

===Chinese===

若非如此，我本可以在爱尔兰安家（昆兰小姐就是一位继承女，离我们不到二十英里；基尔万根的彼得·伯克给他女儿朱迪留了一年七百镑——我若等上几年，两人随便哪个都是我的）。可我命里注定做个漂泊的人：同奎因那一仗，叫我小小年纪就上了路：诸位随后就听见。
平生我没睡得那样死过，只是醒得比平常早些；头一个念头自然是白天那件大事，我已整装待发。屋里笔墨都有——前一天我不还像个痴心的傻瓜，给诺拉写着诗么？此刻我坐下又写了两封信：兴许是我此生最后两封，我想。第一封给我母亲：

> “敬爱的太太”——我写道——“此信除非我倒在奎因上尉手下，不会交到您手里：今日我在荣誉的战场上同他相见，剑与枪并用。我若死，是作为基督徒和绅士而死——受您这样的母亲教养，还能是别的样么？我饶恕我所有的仇敌——我以孝顺儿子的身份求您的祝福。我的母马诺拉——舅舅给的，我拿她那性别里最负心的一个命了名——请送回布雷迪堡；请把我的银柄短刀赠给猎场看守菲尔·珀塞尔。向舅舅、尤利克，还有那边我这边的姑娘们，代我致意。我是您孝顺的儿子，
> 雷德蒙·巴里”

给诺拉我写道：

> “此信将在我怀里同你给我的信物一道寻见。它将在我的血里浸染（除非先浸了奎因上尉的——我恨他，但饶恕他），在你婚嫁那天，是给你的一件漂亮饰物。戴上它，想想那个收了你信物的可怜少年吧：他为你死去了（这是他一向准备好了去做的）。
> 雷德蒙”

===Original===

These letters being written, and sealed with my father’s great silver seal of the Barry arms, I went down to breakfast; where my mother was waiting for me, you may be sure. We did not say a single word about what was taking place: on the contrary, we talked of anything but that; about who was at church the day before, and about my wanting new clothes now I was grown so tall. She said I must have a suit against winter, if﻿—if﻿—she could afford it. She winced rather at the “if,” Heaven bless her! I knew what was in her mind. And then she fell to telling me about the black pig that must be killed, and that she had found the speckled hen’s nest that morning, whose eggs I liked so, and other such trifling talk. Some of these eggs were for breakfast, and I ate them with a good appetite; but in helping myself to salt I spilled it, on which she started up with a scream. “*Thank God*,” said she, “*It’s fallen towards me.*” And then, her heart being too full, she left the room. Ah! they have their faults, those mothers; but are there any other women like them?
