When she was gone I went to take down the sword with which my father had vanquished the Hampshire baronet, and, would you believe it?﻿—the brave woman had tied *a new riband* to the hilt: for indeed she had the courage of a lioness and a Brady united. And then I took down the pistols, which were always kept bright and well oiled, and put some fresh flints I had into the locks, and got balls and powder ready against the Captain should come. There was claret and a cold fowl put ready for him on the sideboard, and a case-bottle of old brandy too, with a couple of little glasses on the silver tray with the Barry arms emblazoned. In after life, and in the midst of my fortune and splendour, I paid thirty-five guineas, and almost as much more interest, to the London goldsmith who supplied my father with that very tray. A scoundrel pawnbroker would only give me sixteen for it afterwards; so little can we trust the honour of rascally tradesmen!
At eleven o’clock Captain Fagan arrived, on horseback, with a mounted dragoon after him. He paid his compliments to the collation which my mother’s care had provided for him, and then said, “Look ye, Redmond my boy; this is a silly business. The girl will marry Quin, mark my words; and as sure as she does you’ll forget her. You are but a boy.

===Chinese===

两封信写完，用我父亲那枚巴里家纹的大银印封好，我下楼吃早饭；母亲自是等着我。对将办的事我们一字不提；相反，谈的都是别的：昨天教堂里都有谁；我个子蹿高了，该添新衣裳了。她说入冬前一定得给我做一套——如果——如果——她做得起。说到那个“如果”，她心里一缩，老天保佑她！我懂她心里的意思。随后她讲起那口该宰的黑猪；讲她早晨寻着了花斑母鸡的窝——那些蛋我最爱；还有旁的闲话。早饭就有这蛋，我胃口很好地吃了；可取盐时我把盐撒了，她跳起来一声尖叫。“感谢上帝，”她说，“盐朝我这边撒的。”随后她心里太满，出了屋子。啊！母亲们自有她们的短处；可天下还有别的女人比得上她们么？
她一走，我去摘我父亲赢过汉普郡从男爵的那口剑——您道怎么着？那位勇敢的妇人在剑柄上系了一条新缎带：她实在兼有母狮和布雷迪家的胆气。随后我取下手枪——向来擦得雪亮、油润——给枪机换上新燧石，备好弹丸火药，单等上尉来。餐具柜上为他备下了红葡萄酒和一只冷鸡，还有一瓶陈白兰地，两只小酒杯摆在錾着巴里家纹的银托盘上。后来的岁月里，在我富贵煊赫的顶点，我付了三十五几尼——外加几乎同样多的利息——给当年供我父亲那只托盘的伦敦金匠。后来一个当铺恶棍只肯给我十六几尼；市侩奸商的信义，就这样信不得！
十一点钟，费根上尉骑马到了，身后跟着一名骑马的龙骑兵。他先恭维了母亲替他备的点心酒馔，随后说：“听着，雷德蒙我的小子；这是件蠢事。那姑娘要嫁奎因，记住我的话；她嫁定了，你也就把她忘了。你不过是个孩子。

===Original===

Quin is willing to consider you as such. Dublin’s a fine place, and if you have a mind to take a ride thither and see the town for a month, here are twenty guineas at your service. Make Quin an apology, and be off.”
“A man of honour, Mr. Fagan,” says I, “dies, but never apologises. I’ll see the Captain hanged before I apologise.”
“Then there’s nothing for it but a meeting.”
“My mare is saddled and ready,” says I; “where’s the meeting, and who’s the Captain’s second?”
“Your cousins go out with him,” answered Mr. Fagan.
“I’ll ring for my groom to bring my mare round,” I said, “as soon as you have rested yourself.” Tim was accordingly despatched for Nora, and I rode away, but I didn’t take leave of Mrs. Barry. The curtains of her bedroom windows were down, and they didn’t move as we mounted and trotted off﻿ ﻿… *but two hours afterwards*, you should have seen her as she came tottering downstairs, and heard the scream which she gave as she hugged her boy to her heart, quite unharmed and without a wound in his body.
What had taken place I may as well tell here. When we got to the ground, Ulick, Mick, and the Captain were already there: Quin, flaming in red regimentals, as big a monster as ever led a grenadier company. The party were laughing together at some joke of one or the other: and I must say I thought this laughter very unbecoming in my cousins, who were met, perhaps, to see the death of one of their kindred.
“I hope to spoil this sport,” says I to Captain Fagan, in a great rage, “and trust to see this sword of mine in yonder big bully’s body.”
“Oh! it’s with pistols we fight,” replied Mr. Fagan. “You are no match for Quin with the sword.”
“I’ll match any man with the sword,” said I.
“But swords are today impossible; Captain Quin is﻿—is lame. He knocked his knee against the swinging park-gate last night, as he was riding home, and can scarce move it now.”
“Not against Castle Brady gate,” says I: “that has been off the hinges these ten years.” On which Fagan said it must have been some other gate, and repeated what he had said to Mr. Quin and my cousins, when, on alighting from our horses, we joined and saluted those gentlemen.
“Oh yes! dead lame,” said Ulick, coming to shake me by the hand, while Captain Quin took off his hat and turned extremely red.

===Chinese===

奎因也愿意拿你当孩子看。都柏林（Dublin）是个好地方；你若有兴致骑马去逛一个月，这儿是二十几尼，听凭你用。向奎因赔个不是，走人吧。”
“荣誉之士，费根先生，”我说，“宁死不赔不是。叫我赔不是，我宁愿看上尉上绞架。”
“那就只剩见面一条路了。”
“我的母马已备好鞍，”我说，“在哪儿会？上尉的副手是谁？”
“你的表哥 们陪他去。”费根先生答。
“我这就摇铃叫马夫牵马，”我说，“等您歇好了。”于是蒂姆去牵诺拉，我上马走了，没有向巴里太太辞行。她卧室的窗帘垂着，我们上马、小跑离开时，窗帘一动没动……可两个钟头之后，您该看看她跌跌撞撞奔下楼的样子，听听她把完好无损、身上没有一处伤的儿子搂进怀里时那一声喊。
出了什么事，我不妨就在这里交代。到场时，尤利克、米克同上尉已先到了：奎因一身火红的军服，还是那个率领掷弹兵连的大怪物。那几位正为谁的什么笑话笑作一团：我得说，我觉得我表哥们的笑很不成体统——他们来，说不定是看一个亲人送死的。
“我指望揽了这场兴致。”我怒冲冲地对费根上尉说，“指望看见我这口剑，扎进那边那个大恶棍的身体。”
“哦！我们用枪打。”费根先生答，“论剑你不是奎因的对手。”
“论剑我同谁都配。”我说。
“可今天用剑万万不能；奎因上尉——腿瘸了。昨夜骑马回家，膝盖撞上了园子的吊门，如今动弹不得。”
“撞的可不是布雷迪堡的门，”我说，“那扇门掉合页都有十年了。”费根说那必是别的什么门，又把他先前对奎因先生和表哥们说的话重了一遍；这时我们下了马，同那几位先生会合见礼。
“哦是的！瘸得厉害。”尤利克过来同我握手说；奎因上尉摘了帽，脸涨得通红。

===Original===

“And very lucky for you, Redmond my boy,” continued Ulick; “you were a dead man else; for he is a devil of a fellow﻿—isn’t he, Fagan?”
“A regular Turk,” answered Fagan; adding, “I never yet knew the man who stood to Captain Quin.”
“Hang the business!” said Ulick; “I hate it. I’m ashamed of it. Say you’re sorry, Redmond: you can easily say that.”
“If the young *feller* will go to *Dubling*, as proposed”﻿—here interposed Mr. Quin.
“I am *not* sorry﻿—I’ll *not* apologise﻿—and I’ll as soon go to *Dubling* as to﻿—!” said I, with a stamp of my foot.
“There’s nothing else for it,” said Ulick with a laugh to Fagan. “Take your ground, Fagan﻿—twelve paces, I suppose?”
“Ten, sir,” said Mr. Quin, in a big voice; “and make them short ones, do you hear, Captain Fagan?”
“Don’t bully, Mr. Quin,” said Ulick surlily; “here are the pistols.” And he added, with some emotion, to me, “God bless you, my boy; and when I count three, fire.”
Mr. Fagan put my pistol into my hand﻿—that is, not one of mine (which were to serve, if need were, for the next round), but one of Ulick’s. “They are all right,” said he. “Never fear: and, Redmond, fire at his neck﻿—hit him there under the gorget. See how the fool shows himself open.” Mick, who had never spoken a word, Ulick, and the Captain retired to one side, and Ulick gave the signal. It was slowly given, and I had leisure to cover my man well. I saw him changing colour and trembling as the numbers were given. At “three,” both our pistols went off. I heard something whizz by me, and my antagonist, giving a most horrible groan, staggered backwards and fell.
“He’s down﻿—he’s down!” cried the seconds, running towards him. Ulick lifted him up﻿—Mick took his head.
“He’s hit here, in the neck,” said Mick; and laying open his coat, blood was seen gurgling from under his gorget, at the very spot at which I aimed.
“How is it with you?” said Ulick. “Is he really hit?” said he, looking hard at him. The unfortunate man did not answer, but when the support of Ulick’s arm was withdrawn from his back, groaned once more, and fell backwards.
“The young fellow has begun well,” said Mick, with a scowl. “You had better ride off, young sir, before the police are up. They had wind of the business before we left Kilwangan.”
“Is he quite dead?” said I.
“Quite dead,” answered Mick.

===Chinese===

“你也真走运，雷德蒙我的小子，”尤利克接着说，“不然你死定了；他是个魔鬼一样的家伙——是不是，费根？”
“一个地道的土耳其人。”费根答，又添一句，“我还从没见过哪个敢同奎因上尉对垒的。”
“这买卖该死！”尤利克说，“我恨它。我替它臊得慌。说你错了，雷德蒙：这个你容易说。”
“这小家伙肯照提议的去都柏林的话——”奎因先生插进来。
“我没错——我不赔不是——去都柏林，我宁可去——！”我跺着脚说。
“那就没有第二条路了。”尤利克笑着对费根说。“量地吧，费根——十二步，我猜？”
“十步，先生。”奎因先生粗声大气地说，“还要短步，听见没有，费根上尉？”
“少横，奎因先生。”尤利克没好气地说；“枪在这儿。”随后他带点感情地对我添道：“上帝保佑你，我的孩子；我数到三，开枪。”
费根先生把枪放进我手里——不是我那对（那对留着，万一还要第二回合），是尤利克的一支。“都收拾妥了，”他说，“别怕：雷德蒙，照他脖子打——往颈甲底下那儿打。看这蠢货把身子露得多开。”始终一言不发的米克、尤利克同上尉退到一边，尤利克发了口令。口令给得慢，我有充分的工夫瞄准。报数时我看见他变色、发抖。“三”字一出，两支枪同时响了。我听见什么东西嗖地掠过我，我的对手一声可怕的闷哼，踉跄着向后倒下。
“倒了——他倒了！”两个副手喊着奔过去。尤利克扶起他——米克托住他的头。
“他这儿中了，脖子上。”米克说；扒开他的军服，血正从颈甲底下、正是我瞄准的那一点咕咕地涌。
“你怎么样？”尤利克说。“真中了吗？”他死死看着他说。那不幸的人不答话；尤利克的手一从他背后撤开，他又哼了一声，向后倒了下去。
“这小子开张开得好。”米克沉着脸说，“你顶好快走，小先生，趁警察还没上来。我们离开基尔万根前，他们已得了风声。”
“他死透了吗？”我问。
“死透了。”米克答。

===Original===

“Then the world’s rid of *a coward*,” said Captain Fagan, giving the huge prostrate body a scornful kick with his foot. “It’s all over with him, Reddy﻿—he doesn’t stir.”
“*We* are not cowards, Fagan,” said Ulick roughly, “whatever he was! Let’s get the boy off as quick as we may. Your man shall go for a cart, and take away the body of this unhappy gentleman. This has been a sad day’s work for our family, Redmond Barry: you have robbed us of £1,500 a year.”
“It was Nora did it,” said I; “not I.” And I took the riband she gave me out of my waistcoat, and the letter, and flung them down on the body of Captain Quin. “There!” says I﻿—“take her those ribands. She’ll know what they mean: and that’s all that’s left to her of two lovers she had and ruined.”
I did not feel any horror or fear, young as I was, in seeing my enemy prostrate before me; for I knew that I had met and conquered him honourably in the field, as became a man of my name and blood.
“And now, in Heaven’s name, get the youngster out of the way,” said Mick.
Ulick said he would ride with me, and off accordingly we galloped, never drawing bridle till we came to my mother’s door. When there, Ulick told Tim to feed my mare, as I would have far to ride that day; and I was in the poor mother’s arms in a minute.
I need not tell how great were her pride and exultation when she heard from Ulick’s lips the account of my behaviour at the duel. He urged, however, that I should go into hiding for a short time; and it was agreed between them that I should drop my name of Barry, and, taking that of Redmond, go to Dublin, and there wait until matters were blown over. This arrangement was not come to without some discussion; for why should I not be as safe at Barryville, she said, as my cousin and Ulick at Castle Brady?﻿—bailiffs and duns never got near *them*; why should constables be enabled to come upon me? But Ulick persisted in the necessity of my instant departure; in which argument, as I was anxious to see the world, I must confess, I sided with him; and my mother was brought to see that in our small house at Barryville, in the midst of the village, and with the guard but of a couple of servants, escape would be impossible. So the kind soul was forced to yield to my cousin’s entreaties, who promised her, however, that the affair would soon be arranged, and that I should be restored to her. Ah!

===Chinese===

“那世界除掉了一个懦夫。”费根上尉说着，朝那具庞然卧倒的身子轻蔑地踢了一脚。“全完了，雷迪——他不动了。”
“不管他是什么，我们可不是懦夫，费根。”尤利克粗声说，“快把这孩子打发走。你的人去雇一辆车，把这位不幸先生运走。今天这一场，是我们家晦气的一天，雷德蒙·巴里：你抢了我们一年一千五百镑。”
“是诺拉干的，不是我。”我说着，从背心取出她给我的缎带和信，摔在奎因上尉的尸身上。“喏！”我说——“把缎带给她带去。她自会明白那是什么意思：那是她有过又毁掉的两个情人，留给她的全部。”
尽管年轻，眼见仇人横在我面前，我不觉得恐怖，也不害怕：我知道我在战场上光明正大地会了他、赢了他，配得上我的姓氏和血统。
“看在老天份上，快把小家伙弄走。”米克说。
尤利克说他陪我走；于是我们放马飞驰，直到我母亲门前才收缰。到了那儿，尤利克吩咐蒂姆喂我的母马——我那天还有远路；一转眼，我已在可怜母亲的怀抱里了。
她听尤利克亲口讲了我决斗时的做派，那份骄傲和欢喜，不必我说。可他力劝我躲一个时期；两人议定：我从此不用巴里的姓，单用雷德蒙这个名字，去都柏林，等风头过去。这一安排不是没有争执的：她说，我为什么在巴里维尔就不如我表哥和尤利克在布雷迪堡稳当？——法警和讨债的从没近过他们的身；官差凭什么就能拿我？可尤利克坚持我必须即刻动身；这场争执里——我巴望见世面，我得认——我站在他一边；母亲也终于明白：巴里维尔那所小屋在村子正中，手下只有两个仆人，逃是逃不掉的。好心的太太只得依了表哥的恳求；他却向她担保：事情很快会了结，我很快会回到她身边。唉！

===Original===

how little did he know what fortune was in store for me!
My dear mother had some forebodings, I think, that our separation was to be a long one; for she told me that all night long she had been consulting the cards regarding my fate in the duel: and that all the signs betokened a separation; then, taking out a stocking from her escritoire, the kind soul put twenty guineas in a purse for me (she had herself but twenty-five), and made up a little valise, to be placed at the back of my mare, in which were my clothes, linen, and a silver dressing-case of my father’s. She bade me, too, to keep the sword and the pistols I had known to use so like a man. She hurried my departure now (though her heart, I know, was full), and almost in half-an-hour after my arrival at home I was once more on the road again, with the wide world as it were before me. I need not tell how Tim and the cook cried at my departure: and, mayhap, I had a tear or two myself in my eyes; but no lad of sixteen is *very* sad who has liberty for the first time, and twenty guineas in his pocket: and I rode away, thinking, I confess, not so much of the kind mother left alone, and of the home behind me, as of tomorrow, and all the wonders it would bring.

===Chinese===

他哪里知道等着我的命运是什么！
我想，我亲爱的母亲早有预感，我们这一别要长：她告诉我，她整夜拿纸牌占我决斗的命：种种征象都主离别。随后她从写字柜里取出一只长袜，好心的人从里面给我凑了二十几尼装进钱袋（她自己统共只有二十五），又收拾了一只小行囊，捆在母马后鞍上：里面是我的衣裳、衬衣，还有我父亲的一只银梳妆匣。她还嘱咐我，把那口剑和那对手枪带走——我使唤它们已使得像个男人。这时她催我快走（我固然知道她心已满）：到家不出半个钟头，我重又上了路，眼前仿佛豁然展开整个世界。蒂姆同厨娘送我走时怎样哭，不必说了：兴许我自己的眼里也有一两滴；可一个十六岁的少年，头一回有了自由、口袋里有二十几尼，是愁不起来的；我骑马走时，想着的——我认——不是掠下的慈母和身后的家，而是明天，和明天将带来的一切新奇。

## IV In Which Barry Takes a Near View of Military Glory / 巴里近距离见识了战争的荣光
===Original===

I never had a taste for anything but genteel company, and hate all descriptions of low life. Hence my account of the society in which I at present found myself must of necessity be short; and, indeed, the recollection of it is profoundly disagreeable to me. Pah! the reminiscences of the horrid black-hole of a place in which we soldiers were confined; of the wretched creatures with whom I was now forced to keep company; of the ploughmen, poachers, pickpockets, who had taken refuge from poverty, or the law (as, in truth, I had done myself), is enough to make me ashamed even now, and it calls the blush into my old cheeks to think I was ever forced to keep such company. I should have fallen into despair, but that, luckily, events occurred to rouse my spirits, and in some measure to console me for my misfortunes.
The first of these consolations I had was a good quarrel, which took place on the day after my entrance into the transport-ship, with a huge red-haired monster of a fellow﻿—a chairman, who had enlisted to fly from a vixen of a wife, who, boxer as he was, had been more than a match for him. As soon as this fellow﻿—Toole, I remember, was his name﻿—got away from the arms of the washerwoman his lady, his natural courage and ferocity returned, and he became the tyrant of all round about him. All recruits, especially, were the object of the brute’s insult and ill-treatment.
I had no money, as I said, and was sitting very disconsolately over a platter of rancid bacon and mouldy biscuit, which was served to us at mess, when it came to my turn to be helped to drink, and I was served, like the rest, with a dirty tin noggin, containing somewhat more than half a pint of rum-and-water. The beaker was so greasy and filthy that I could not help turning round to the messman and saying, “Fellow, get me a glass!” At which all the wretches round about me burst into a roar of laughter, the very loudest among them being, of course, Mr. Toole. “Get the gentleman a towel for his hands, and serve him a basin of turtle-soup,” roared the monster, who was sitting, or rather squatting, on the deck opposite me; and as he spoke he suddenly seized my beaker of grog and emptied it, in the midst of another burst of applause.

===Chinese===

我生平只爱体面人的交游，厌恶各色下等生活。因此，对于我此刻混迹其间的这个社会，我的记述势必简短；说实话，回想起来就叫我浑身不自在。呸！想起我们大兵被塞在里面的那个黑洞洞的鬼地方；想起我如今被迫为伍的那群可怜虫——那些逃避穷困或刑章的庄稼汉、偷猎贼、扒手（老实说，我自己也是逃难的）；这至今仍叫我羞惭，一想到自己竟被迫同这等人厮混，我这一把老脸还要发烫。若不是幸而有几桩事发生，振奋了我的精神，多少排遣了我的厄运，我几乎要陷入绝望了。
头一桩安慰，是一场痛快的口角，发生在我上运输船的第二天，对头是个红头发的彪形大汉——他本是抬轿的行当，投军为的是躲开他那母老虎般的老婆；别看他是个拳师，老婆却比他还强三分。这家伙——我记得他名叫图尔——一脱开他那位洗衣妇太太的臂膀，天生的胆量和凶性便回来了，转眼成了周遭一切人的霸王。新兵尤其成了这畜生凌辱虐待的对象。
我说过，我一个钱也没有，正蔫头蔫脑地坐在那里，对着一盘发哈喇的咸肉和长毛的硬饼干——这是开饭时分给我们的；轮到给我斟酒时，同旁人一样，递到我手里的是一只油腻的锡缸子，盛着多半品脱兑水的朗姆酒。那缸子油腻肮脏得可以，我忍不住扭头对伙夫说：“伙计，给我拿只玻璃杯来！”此言一出，周围的可怜虫们哄堂大笑，笑得最响的自然是图尔先生。“给这位老爷备一条擦手毛巾，给他上盆甲鱼汤，”那畜生吼道；他正坐——不如说正蹲——在我对面的甲板上；话音未落，他突然一把抢过我的酒缸子，在又一阵喝彩声中一饮而尽。

===Original===

“If you want to vex him, ax him about his wife the washerwoman, who *bates* him,” here whispered in my ear another worthy, a retired linkboy, who, disgusted with his profession, had adopted the military life.
“Is it a towel of your wife’s washing, Mr. Toole?” said I. “I’m told she wiped your face often with one.”
“Ax him why he wouldn’t see her yesterday, when she came to the ship,” continued the linkboy. And so I put to him some other foolish jokes about soapsuds, henpecking, and flatirons, which set the man into a fury, and succeeded in raising a quarrel between us. We should have fallen to at once, but a couple of grinning marines, who kept watch at the door, for fear we should repent of our bargain and have a fancy to escape, came forward and interposed between us with fixed bayonets; but the sergeant coming down the ladder, and hearing the dispute, condescended to say that we might fight it out like men with *fistes* if we chose, and that the foredeck should be free to us for that purpose. But the use of *fistes*, as the Englishman called them, was not then general in Ireland, and it was agreed that we should have a pair of cudgels; with one of which weapons I finished the fellow in four minutes, giving him a thump across his stupid sconce which laid him lifeless on the deck, and not receiving myself a single hurt of consequence.
This victory over the cock of the vile dunghill obtained me respect among the wretches of whom I formed part, and served to set up my spirits, which otherwise were flagging; and my position was speedily made more bearable by the arrival on board our ship of an old friend. This was no other than my second in the fatal duel which had sent me thus early out into the world, Captain Fagan. There was a young nobleman who had a company in our regiment (Gale’s foot), and who, preferring the delights of the Mall and the clubs to the dangers of a rough campaign, had given Fagan the opportunity of an exchange; which, as the latter had no fortune but his sword, he was glad to make.

===Chinese===

“你要惹恼他，就问他那个洗衣妇老婆的事，她*揍*他。”这时另一个宝贝在我耳边嘀咕；他是个改行的火把郎，厌了本行，投了军。
“怎么，图尔先生，是要你老婆洗出来的毛巾么？”我说。“听说她常拿一条替你抹脸呢。”
“问他昨天他老婆上船来，他为什么死活不肯见面，”火把郎接着说。我便又拿肥皂沫、惧内、熨斗之类，添了几句浑话，把那人惹得性起，一场架便叫我挑成了。我们本要立时动手，亏得守在门口的两个海军陆战队士兵——他们看住我们，是怕我们反悔了这买卖、动了逃走的念头——咧着嘴上前，端着上了刺刀的枪隔开了我们；可军士从舷梯上下来，听了这场争端，屈尊发话说，我们若愿意，尽可以像男子汉那样用*拳头*打出个高下，前甲板尽可腾给我们用。只是*拳头*——那位英格兰人是这么叫的——那年在爱尔兰还不时兴，于是讲定各执一根短棍；开打不过四分钟，我便结果了这家伙：朝他那颗蠢脑壳横里一记，打得他直挺挺躺在甲板上不省人事，我自己连一点像样的伤都没挂上。
这一仗，胜了这只粪堆上的霸王鸡，替我在那群可怜虫——我如今也算其中之一——中间挣得了敬意，也把我本已委顿的精神撑了起来；随后我的一位老朋友上了船，我的处境很快好过了些。此人不是别人，正是那场致命决斗里做我副手的费根上尉——就是那场决斗，害我这么早便流落天涯。我们团（盖尔步兵团）里有一位年轻贵族挂着连尉之职，他嫌荒野征战凶险，宁可去享受林荫道和俱乐部的快活，便给了费根一个对调的机会；费根除一柄剑外别无家私，自然乐得成交。

===Original===

The sergeant was putting us through our exercise on deck (the seamen and officers of the transport looking grinning on) when a boat came from the shore bringing our captain to the ship; and though I started and blushed red as he recognised me﻿—a descendant of the Barrys﻿—in this degrading posture, I promise you that the sight of Fagan’s face was most welcome to me, for it assured me that a friend was near me. Before that I was so melancholy that I would certainly have deserted had I found the means, and had not the inevitable marines kept a watch to prevent any such escapes. Fagan gave me a wink of recognition, but offered no public token of acquaintance; it was not until two days afterwards, and when we had bidden adieu to old Ireland and were standing out to sea, that he called me into his cabin, and then, shaking hands with me cordially, gave me news, which I much wanted, of my family. “I had news of you in Dublin,” he said. “ ’Faith you’ve begun early, like your father’s son; and I think you could not do better than as you have done. But why did you not write home to your poor mother? She has sent a half-dozen letters to you at Dublin.”
I said I had asked for letters at the post-office, but there were none for Mr. Redmond. I did not like to add that I had been ashamed, after the first week, to write to my mother.
“We must write to her by the pilot,” said he, “who will leave us in two hours; and you can tell her that you are safe, and married to Brown Bess.” I sighed when he talked about being married; on which he said with a laugh, “I see you are thinking of a certain young lady at Brady’s Town.”
“Is Miss Brady well?” said I; and indeed, could hardly utter it, for I certainly *was* thinking about her: for, though I had forgotten her in the gaieties of Dublin, I have always found adversity makes man very affectionate.
“There’s only seven Miss Bradys now,” answered Fagan, in a solemn voice. “Poor Nora﻿—”
“Good heavens! what of her?” I thought grief had killed her.
“She took on so at your going away that she was obliged to console herself with a husband. She’s now Mrs. John Quin.”
“Mrs. John Quin! Was there *another* Mr. John Quin?” asked I, quite wonder-stricken.
“No; the very same one, my boy. He recovered from his wound. The ball you hit him with was not likely to hurt him. It was only made of tow.

===Chinese===

军士正在甲板上操练我们（运输船上的水手和船官们笑嘻嘻地围观），一条小船从岸上送我们的上尉来了；他认出我这副屈辱姿势下的巴里家后人时，我又惊又臊，满脸通红；可我敢说，见到费根的面孔我是由衷高兴的，因为这等于告诉我，身边有了朋友。在那之前，我消沉得很，只要有法子，又没有那甩不掉的陆战队看着，我准已开了小差。费根认出我，眨了眨眼，却不曾当众显露我们相识；直到两天之后，我们辞别了老爱尔兰、船头调向大海，他才把我叫进舱房，热络地同我握手，给了我家中的消息——这正是我求之不得的。“我在都柏林听到过你的事，”他说。“真的，你出道出道得早，不愧是你父亲的种；我看你怎么做也超不过眼下的做法。可你为什么不写家信给你可怜的母亲？她往都柏林给你寄了六封信。”
我说，我去邮局问过信，可没有寄给雷德蒙先生的。我不愿再补一句：过了头一个星期，我便没脸给母亲写信了。
“我们得托领航员带信，”他说，“他两个钟头后就要离船；你可以告诉她你平安无事，已经同‘棕贝丝’成了亲。”他说到成亲，我叹了口气；他笑着接道：“我看你是在想布雷迪镇的某位年轻小姐吧。”
“布雷迪小姐可好？”我说；说这话时几乎张不开口，因为我*确实*在想她：虽说都柏林的花花世界早叫我把她忘了，可我一向发现，人一倒霉，便格外多情。
“布雷迪家如今只剩七位小姐了，”费根声音郑重地答道。“可怜的诺拉——”
“天哪！她怎么了？”我只当悲伤要了她的命。
“你这一走，她伤心得没法，只得寻个丈夫自遣。如今她是约翰·奎因太太了。”
“约翰·奎因太太！难道还有*第二个*约翰·奎因先生？”我问，惊得目瞪口呆。
“没有；正是原来那一位，我的孩子。他那伤养好了。你打中他的那颗子弹害不了他的命。那不过是一团麻絮做的。”

===Original===

Do you think the Bradys would let you kill fifteen hundred a year out of the family?” And then Fagan further told me that, in order to get me out of the way﻿—for the cowardly Englishman could never be brought to marry from fear of me﻿—the plan of the duel had been arranged. “But hit him you certainly did, Redmond, and with a fine thick plugget of tow; and the fellow was so frightened, that he was an hour in coming to. We told your mother the story afterwards, and a pretty scene she made; she despatched a half-score of letters to Dublin after you, but I suppose addressed them to you in your real name, by which you never thought to ask for them.”
“The coward!” said I (though, I confess, my mind was considerably relieved at the thoughts of not having killed him). “And did the Bradys of Castle Brady consent to admit a poltroon like that into one of the most ancient and honourable families in the world?”
“He has paid off your uncle’s mortgage,” said Fagan; “he gives Nora a coach-and-six; he is to sell out, and Lieutenant Ulick Brady of the Militia is to purchase his company. That coward of a fellow has been the making of your uncle’s family. ’Faith! the business was well done.” And then, laughing, he told me how Mick and Ulick had never let him out of their sight, although he was for deserting to England, until the marriage was completed and the happy couple off on their road to Dublin. “Are you in want of cash, my boy?” continued the good-natured Captain. “You may draw upon me, for I got a couple of hundred out of Master Quin for my share, and while they last you shall never want.”
And so he bade me sit down and write a letter to my mother, which I did forthwith in very sincere and repentant terms, stating that I had been guilty of extravagances, that I had not known until that moment under what a fatal error I had been labouring, and that I had embarked for Germany as a volunteer. The letter was scarcely finished when the pilot sang out that he was going on shore; and he departed, taking with him, from many an anxious fellow besides myself, our adieux to friends in old Ireland.

===Chinese===

“你想，布雷迪家肯让你把一年1,500镑从家里杀掉么？”接着费根又告诉我，为了把我调开——因为那个胆小的英格兰人怕我怕得死，怎么也不敢成亲——决斗的计谋才安排出来的。“可你的确打中了他，雷德蒙，打中他的是好大一颗麻絮弹；那家伙吓得魂飞魄散，足足一个钟头才缓过气来。事后我们把真相告诉了你母亲，她那一场好闹；她随后追着你往都柏林发了十来封信，只是我猜信封上写的是你的真名实姓，你压根儿没想到拿这个名字去讨信。”
“这懦夫！”我说（不过我招认，想到自己并没有杀过人，心里着实松了一大截）。“布雷迪堡的布雷迪家，竟肯把这样一个胆小鬼接纳进世上最古老、最荣耀的门第之一？”
“他还清了你叔叔的抵押债，”费根说；“他给诺拉备了六驾马车；他就要退役出缺，民团的尤里克·布雷迪中尉要买下他的连队。那胆小鬼竟成全了你叔叔一家。真的！这桩事办得漂亮。”说着他大笑起来，告诉我米克和尤里克如何寸步不离地盯着他——那家伙一路只想逃回英格兰——直到婚事办完，新婚夫妇登上去都柏林的大路。“手头紧么，孩子？”这位好心的上尉接着说。“尽管向我支取，奎因那桩事里我分到了二百来镑，钱一天不完，你一天不缺。”
于是他叫我坐下给母亲写信；我即刻提笔，言辞恳切、悔意深长，说我荒唐挥霍、闯下大祸，说直到此刻方知自己一直错得多重，又说我已作为志愿兵登船开赴德意志。信刚写完，领航员便喊着他要上岸了；他带走了我、也带走了船上许多颗惦念的心捎给老爱尔兰亲友的告别。

===Original===

Although I was called Captain Barry for many years of my life, and have been known as such by the first people of Europe, yet I may as well confess I had no more claim to the title than many a gentleman who assumes it, and never had a right to an epaulet, or to any military decoration higher than a corporal’s stripe of worsted. I was made corporal by Fagan during our voyage to the Elbe, and my rank was confirmed on terra firma. I was promised a halbert, too, and afterwards, perhaps, an ensigncy, if I distinguished myself; but Fate did not intend that I should remain long an English soldier: as shall appear presently. Meanwhile, our passage was very favourable; my adventures were told by Fagan to his brother officers, who treated me with kindness; and my victory over the big chairman procured me respect from my comrades of the foredeck. Encouraged and strongly exhorted by Fagan, I did my duty resolutely; but, though affable and good-humoured with the men, I never at first condescended to associate with such low fellows: and, indeed, was called generally amongst them “my Lord.” I believe it was the ex-linkboy, a facetious knave, who gave me the title; and I felt that I should become such a rank as well as any peer in the kingdom.
It would require a greater philosopher and historian than I am to explain the causes of the famous Seven Years’ War in which Europe was engaged; and, indeed, its origin has always appeared to me to be so complicated, and the books written about it so amazingly hard to understand, that I have seldom been much wiser at the end of a chapter than at the beginning, and so shall not trouble my reader with any personal disquisitions concerning the matter. All I know is, that after His Majesty’s love of his Hanoverian dominions had rendered him most unpopular in his English kingdom, with Mr. Pitt at the head of the anti-German war-party, all of a sudden, Mr. Pitt becoming Minister, the rest of the empire applauded the war as much as they had hated it before. The victories of Dettingen and Crefeld were in everybody’s mouths, and “the Protestant hero,” as we used to call the godless old Frederick of Prussia, was adored by us as a saint, a very short time after we had been about to make war against him in alliance with the Empress-queen.

===Chinese===

虽然大半辈子人都唤我巴里上尉，欧洲的头面人物也都知道我这个名号，可我不妨招认：我对这个头衔的权利，同许多僭用它的绅士一样稀薄；肩章我从来没资格挂，论军中职衔，我从没高过下士的一道绒线袖章。开赴易北河的航程中，费根提拔我做了下士，上岸之后又得了正式确认。人家还许我一支军士戟，往后兴许再升个掌旗官，只要我建功立业；可命运并无意叫我久做英格兰的兵：这层后文自见分晓。其间，我们的航程一路顺风；我的历险由费根讲给了同僚军官们听，他们待我都和气；我打翻那个大轿夫的一战，也替我在前甲板的伙伴中挣得了敬意。有费根打气督励，我当差当得果决；只是同那些兵虽然随和亲热，起初我却从不肯屈尊同这等下等人结交：说实在的，他们当中一般都管我叫“老爷”。我想这尊称是那位退职火把郎——一个促狭鬼——起的；而我自觉受用这等身份，受用得同王国任何一位贵族一样。
要解释那场搅动全欧洲的著名七年战争的缘由，得比我更高明的哲学家兼史学家才成；说实在的，那战争的起因在我看来繁难至极，写它的那些书又深奥得叫人瞠目，我常常读完一章同读开头一样懵懂，也就不想拿我个人的高论来烦扰读者了。我只知道：陛下钟爱他的汉诺威领地，害得他在英格兰王国极不得人心，皮特先生领头的反德意志战争党呐喊了一阵；忽然间皮特先生做了首相，帝国上下便把这场战争捧得同先前恨它一样起劲。德廷根和克雷菲尔德的胜利挂在了人人嘴上，“新教英雄”——我们惯常用这个称号称呼普鲁士那位不信神的老腓特烈——被我们当作圣人崇拜，而这前后不久，我们还几乎要伙同女皇去打他呢。

===Original===

Now, somehow, we were on Frederick’s side: the Empress, the French, the Swedes, and the Russians, were leagued against us; and I remember, when the news of the battle of Lissa came even to our remote quarter of Ireland, we considered it as a triumph for the cause of Protestantism, and illuminated and bonfired, and had a sermon at church, and kept the Prussian king’s birthday; on which my uncle would get drunk: as indeed on any other occasion. Most of the low fellows enlisted with myself were, of course, Papists (the English army was filled with such, out of that never-failing country of ours), and these, forsooth, were fighting the battles of Protestantism with Frederick; who was belabouring the Protestant Swedes and the Protestant Saxons, as well as the Russians of the Greek Church, and the Papist troops of the Emperor and the King of France. It was against these latter that the English auxiliaries were employed, and we know that, be the quarrel what it may, an Englishman and a Frenchman are pretty willing to make a fight of it.
We landed at Cuxhaven, and before I had been a month in the Electorate I was transformed into a tall and proper young soldier, and having a natural aptitude for military exercise, was soon as accomplished at the drill as the oldest sergeant in the regiment. It is well, however, to dream of glorious war in a snug armchair at home; ay, or to make it as an officer, surrounded by gentlemen, gorgeously dressed, and cheered by chances of promotion. But those chances do not shine on poor fellows in worsted lace: the rough texture of our red coats made me ashamed when I saw an officer go by; my soul used to shudder when, on going the rounds, I would hear their voices as they sat jovially over the mess-table; my pride revolted at being obliged to plaster my hair with flour and candle-grease, instead of using the proper pomatum for a gentleman. Yes, my tastes have always been high and fashionable, and I loathed the horrid company in which I was fallen. What chances had I of promotion? None of my relatives had money to buy me a commission, and I became soon so low-spirited, that I longed for a general action and a ball to finish me, and vowed that I would take some opportunity to desert.

===Chinese===

而如今，不知怎么，我们站到了腓特烈一边：女皇、法国人、瑞典人、俄国人结成一伙与我们为敌；我记得，利萨一战的消息传到我们爱尔兰那偏僻角落时，大家把它当作新教事业的凯旋，张灯结彩、燃起篝火，教堂里布了一场道，还庆贺了普鲁士国王的诞辰；每逢这个日子我叔叔必要一醉——其实逢着任何别的日子也一样。同我一起投军的那些下等人，多数自然是教皇派人（英格兰的军队里塞满了这种人，都出自我们那个取之不竭的国度）；这班人，你瞧瞧，正跟着腓特烈为新教的事业打仗；而腓特烈打的是新教的瑞典人、新教的萨克森人，也打希腊正教的俄国人、皇帝和法兰西国王的天主教军队。英格兰的援军对仗的正是后面这几家；而且我们知道，不管争端为何，英格兰人和法兰西人碰了头，总是很乐意干上一仗的。
我们在库克斯港登岸；入选帝侯邦不出一个月，我已出落成一个高大像样的年轻士兵，操练一道我天生上手，不多时便练得同团里最老的军士一样精熟。坐在家中安乐椅里梦想光荣的战争，自然美妙；做军官随军出征也美妙——周旋于绅士之间，衣冠灿然，晋升有望。可这些机运，照不到戴绒线镶边的穷小子身上：我们红呢军衣的粗针大线，叫我一见军官走过便自惭形秽；查哨路过餐室，听见他们围桌欢宴的谈笑，我的灵魂便一阵阵发抖；不得不拿面粉和烛油往头发上涂抹，而不能用绅士该用的润发膏，我的傲气便直往上撞。是的，我的趣味从来高雅时髦，我厌恶极了沦落其间的这帮可怕伙伴。我晋身的机会在哪里？亲眷中没有一个拿得出钱替我买个军职；不久我便消沉到这般田地：只盼来一场大会战、一颗子弹了结了我，还赌咒发誓要寻个机会开小差。

===Original===

When I think that I, the descendant of the kings of Ireland, was threatened with a caning by a young scoundrel who had just joined from Eton College﻿—when I think that he offered to make me his footman, and that I did not, on either occasion, murder him! On the first occasion I burst into tears (I do not care to own it) and had serious thoughts of committing suicide, so great was my mortification. But my kind friend Fagan came to my aid in the circumstance, with some very timely consolation. “My poor boy,” said he, “you must not take the matter to heart so. Caning is only a relative disgrace. Young Ensign Fakenham was flogged himself at Eton School only a month ago: I would lay a wager that his scars are not yet healed. You must cheer up, my boy; do your duty, be a gentleman, and no serious harm can fall on you.” And I heard afterwards that my champion had taken Mr. Fakenham very severely to task for this threat, and said to him that any such proceedings for the future he should consider as an insult to himself; whereon the young ensign was, for the moment, civil. As for the sergeants, I told one of them, that if any man struck me, no matter who he might be, or what the penalty, I would take his life. And, ’faith! there was an air of sincerity in my speech which convinced the whole bevy of them; and as long as I remained in the English service no rattan was ever laid on the shoulders of Redmond Barry. Indeed, I was in that savage moody state, that my mind was quite made up to the point, and I looked to hear my own dead march played as sure as I was alive. When I was made a corporal, some of my evils were lessened; I messed with the sergeants by special favour, and used to treat them to drink, and lose money to the rascals at play: with which cash my good friend Mr. Fagan punctually supplied me.
Our regiment, which was quartered about Stade and Luneburg, speedily got orders to march southwards towards the Rhine, for news came that our great General, Prince Ferdinand of Brunswick, had been defeated﻿—no, not defeated, but foiled in his attack upon the French under the Duke of Broglio, at Bergen, near Frankfort-on-the-Main, and had been obliged to fall back.

===Chinese===

想想吧，我，爱尔兰列王的后裔，竟被一个刚从伊顿公学来的小恶少威胁要吃藤条——再想想，他竟提出收我做他的跟班，而这两桩事上，我竟都没有宰了他！头一桩事上，我当场大哭起来（我也不怕承认），羞愤得认真想过自尽。亏得我那位好心的朋友费根及时赶来，给了我一番极合时宜的宽慰。“可怜的孩子，”他说，“这事别这么放在心上。吃藤条只是一种相对的耻辱。年轻的掌旗官法肯汉一个月前在伊顿不也挨过鞭子？我敢打赌，他的伤疤这会儿还没长好呢。振作起来，孩子；尽你的职分，做个绅士，天大的祸也落不到你头上。”后来我还听说，我的这位护主为此狠狠数落了法肯汉先生一场，说往后再有这等行径，他便视同对他本人的侮辱；小掌旗官当下果然客气了些。至于军士们，我对其中一个说过：谁敢动我一根指头，不管他是什么人、不管要受什么刑罚，我必取他的性命。真的！我那话里有一股斩钉截铁的气概，把那一整窝人都镇住了；我在英格兰军中一日，就没有一根藤条落在过雷德蒙·巴里的肩上。说实话，我当时正在那凶狠乖张的心绪里，这条心是横定了的，只等着听自己那支葬礼进行曲，像自己活着一样有把握。升了下士，我的苦楚减了几分：承蒙特恩，我同军士们同桌用饭，常请他们喝酒，赌钱时故意把钱输给那帮坏蛋：这宗开销，都亏我的好朋友费根先生按数接济。
我们团驻扎在施塔德和吕讷堡一带，不久便奉命南下开赴莱茵河，因为消息传来，说我们伟大的统帅、不伦瑞克的费迪南德亲王吃了败仗——不，不是败仗，是他在美因河畔法兰克福附近的贝尔根攻打布罗格里奥公爵麾下的法军，攻而不克，只得后撤。

===Original===

As the allies retreated the French rushed forward, and made a bold push for the Electorate of our gracious monarch in Hanover, threatening that they would occupy it; as they had done before, when D’Estrees beat the hero of Culloden, the gallant Duke of Cumberland, and caused him to sign the capitulation of Closter Zeven. An advance upon Hanover always caused a great agitation in the Royal bosom of the King of England; more troops were sent to join us, convoys of treasure were passed over to our forces, and to our ally’s the King of Prussia; and although, in spite of all assistance, the army under Prince Ferdinand was very much weaker than that of the invading enemy, yet we had the advantage of better supplies, one of the greatest Generals in the world: and, I was going to add, of British valour, but the less we say about *that* the better. My Lord George Sackville did not exactly cover himself with laurels at Minden; otherwise there might have been won there one of the greatest victories of modern times.
Throwing himself between the French and the interior of the Electorate, Prince Ferdinand wisely took possession of the free town of Bremen, which he made his storehouse and place of arms; and round which he gathered all his troops, making ready to fight the famous battle of Minden.
Were these Memoirs not characterised by truth, and did I deign to utter a single word for which my own personal experience did not give me the fullest authority, I might easily make myself the hero of some strange and popular adventures, and, after the fashion of novel-writers, introduce my reader to the great characters of this remarkable time. These persons (I mean the romance-writers), if they take a drummer or a dustman for a hero, somehow manage to bring him in contact with the greatest lords and most notorious personages of the empire; and I warrant me there’s not one of them but, in describing the battle of Minden, would manage to bring Prince Ferdinand, and my Lord George Sackville, and my Lord Granby, into presence. It would have been easy for me to have *said* I was present when the orders were brought to Lord George to charge with the cavalry and finish the rout of the Frenchmen, and when he refused to do so, and thereby spoiled the great victory.

===Chinese===

联军一退，法军便扑了上来，大胆进逼我们圣明君主在汉诺威的选帝侯邦，扬言要把它占领——从前他们就干过一回：德斯特雷打垮了卡洛登的英雄、英勇的坎伯兰公爵，逼他签署了克洛斯特-泽文降约。法军一进逼汉诺威，英格兰国王陛下那颗王家的心总要大大震颤一番；更多的部队派来并入我军，一船船财货银钱运给我们、也运给盟友普鲁士国王；尽管有这许多接济，费迪南德亲王麾下的军队比起入侵之敌仍弱得多；可我们占着粮饷充足的优势，占着世界数一数二的名将——我本想再添一条不列颠之勇，只是这话*少说为妙*。乔治·萨克维尔勋爵在明登可没能挂满月桂；不然的话，那里本可赢下近代史上最伟大的胜仗之一。
费迪南德亲王横身挡在法军同选帝侯邦腹地之间，明智地占据了自由市不来梅，以之为粮秣军械之所；他将全军聚拢在城下，预备打那场著名的明登会战。
倘若这部回忆录不是以真实为标格，倘若我肯屈尊说一句没有亲身经历担保的话，我尽可以轻而易举把自己写成几段离奇轰动的冒险的主人公，学小说家的做派，把读者引见给这个大时代的大人物。这班人（我是说传奇作家），哪怕挑个鼓手或清道夫当主人公，也有法子叫他同帝国最显赫的贵胄、最有名的角色攀上交道；我敢担保，他们笔下描写明登之战，没有一个不设法让费迪南德亲王、乔治·萨克维尔勋爵和格兰比勋爵一一登场亮相。我本可以*顺口一说*：命令送到乔治勋爵手上、命他率骑兵出击、了结法军溃局的那一刻，以及他拒不从命、就此毁掉这场大捷的那一刻，我都在场。

===Original===

But the fact is, I was two miles off from the cavalry when his Lordship’s fatal hesitation took place, and none of us soldiers of the line knew of what had occurred until we came to talk about the fight over our kettles in the evening, and repose after the labours of a hard-fought day. I saw no one of higher rank that day than my colonel and a couple of orderly officers riding by in the smoke﻿—no one on our side, that is. A poor corporal (as I then had the disgrace of being) is not generally invited into the company of commanders and the great; but, in revenge, I saw, I promise you, some very good company on the *French* part, for their regiments of Lorraine and Royal Cravate were charging us all day; and in *that* sort of melee high and low are pretty equally received. I hate bragging, but I cannot help saying that I made a very close acquaintance with the colonel of the Cravates; for I drove my bayonet into his body, and finished off a poor little ensign, so young, slender, and small, that a blow from my pigtail would have despatched him, I think, in place of the butt of my musket, with which I clubbed him down. I killed, besides, four more officers and men, and in the poor ensign’s pocket found a purse of fourteen louis-d’or, and a silver box of sugarplums; of which the former present was very agreeable to me. If people would tell their stories of battles in this simple way, I think the cause of truth would not suffer by it. All I know of this famous fight of Minden (except from books) is told here above. The ensign’s silver bonbon box and his purse of gold; the livid face of the poor fellow as he fell; the huzzas of the men of my company as I went out under a smart fire and rifled him; their shouts and curses as we came hand in hand with the Frenchmen﻿—these are, in truth, not very dignified recollections, and had best be passed over briefly. When my kind friend Fagan was shot, a brother captain, and his very good friend, turned to Lieutenant Rawson and said, “Fagan’s down; Rawson, there’s your company.” It was all the epitaph my brave patron got. “I should have left you a hundred guineas, Redmond,” were his last words to me, “but for a cursed run of ill luck last night at faro.” And he gave me a faint squeeze of the hand; then, as the word was given to advance, I left him.

===Chinese===

可实情是，勋爵大人那致命的迟疑发生时，我离骑兵队足有两英里；我们这些战列步兵，直到晚间围着饭锅、在苦战一天的劳乏之后歇下来谈起这一仗，才知道出了什么事。那一天，我见过的军衔比我上高的，只有我们上校和两三名在硝烟里驰过的传令官——我是说，我方没有一个再高的。一个可怜的下士（我那时正不体面地做着下士），照例没人请他去同统帅大人物们做伴；可是补回来的是，我敢说，我在*法国*那一头倒见识了几位极好的伙伴：他们的洛林团和“皇家克罗阿特”团整日向我们冲杀；而*那一*种短兵相接，是贵贱不分、一体接待的。我讨厌吹牛，可有一样不能不说：我同克罗阿特团团长结下了极深的交情——我的刺刀捅进了他的身体；我还打发了一个可怜的小掌旗官，那孩子又年轻又细弱又矮小，我看不用枪托，单凭我一条辫子抽过去也就结果了他——实际上我是倒转枪托，一记把他砸倒的。此外我还结果了军官士兵共四名；在那可怜掌旗官的口袋里，我摸到一只装着14枚金路易的钱袋、一只银糖果盒——前者这件礼物，我是十分中意的。人们若都这样老老实实讲他们的战史，我想真理的事业不会吃亏。这场明登名战，我所知的一切（书本上的除外）尽在于此。掌旗官的银糖盒和他的金荷包；他倒下时那张铁青的脸；我顶着弹雨窜出去搜他腰包时，我那一连弟兄的阵阵欢呼；我们同法国人白刃相接时他们的呐喊咒骂——说实话，这些回忆都不大有体面，还是从简略过为妙。我那位好心的朋友费根中弹时，他的一位同僚上尉、也是他的至交，扭头对罗森中尉说：“费根倒了；罗森，那个连队归你了。”我这位勇敢的恩主所得的墓志铭，便只有这一句。“我本该给你留下100几尼的，雷德蒙，”这是他对我说的最后的话，“无奈昨夜法罗牌的手气背透了。”他把手轻轻一握；前进的号令一下，我便离开了他。

===Original===

When we came back to our old ground, which we presently did, he was lying there still; but he was dead. Some of our people had already torn off his epaulets, and, no doubt, had rifled his purse. Such knaves and ruffians do men in war become! It is well for gentlemen to talk of the age of chivalry; but remember the starving brutes whom they lead﻿—men nursed in poverty, entirely ignorant, made to take a pride in deeds of blood﻿—men who can have no amusement but in drunkenness, debauch, and plunder. It is with these shocking instruments that your great warriors and kings have been doing their murderous work in the world; and while, for instance, we are at the present moment admiring the “Great Frederick,” as we call him, and his philosophy, and his liberality, and his military genius, I, who have served him, and been, as it were, behind the scenes of which that great spectacle is composed, can only look at it with horror. What a number of items of human crime, misery, slavery, go to form that sum-total of glory! I can recollect a certain day about three weeks after the battle of Minden, and a farmhouse in which some of us entered; and how the old woman and her daughters served us, trembling, to wine; and how we got drunk over the wine, and the house was in a flame, presently; and woe betide the wretched fellow afterwards who came home to look for his house and his children!

===Chinese===

我们很快回到原先的阵地上，他还躺在那里；可人已经死了。肩章叫我们自己人扯掉了，钱袋不用说也被搜掠一空。战争把人变成了这等的骗子和恶棍！绅士们尽可以高谈骑士时代；可别忘了他们驱使的那群饿殍般的畜生——生于贫穷、目不识丁、被教得以杀人为荣的汉子——除了酗酒、纵欲、劫掠再无别的消遣的人。你那些伟大的武士和国王，在世上干的杀人营生，靠的正是这等骇人的工具；时至今日，我们一面叹赏“腓特烈大帝”——我们这样称呼他——叹赏他的哲学、他的宽仁、他的军事天才；而我，这个替他当过差、可以说在这台大戏后台待过的人，只能满怀恐怖地看着这一切。多少笔人类的罪恶、苦难、奴役，才凑成那“光荣”的总额啊！我记得明登之战后约莫三个星期的某一天，记得我们几个人闯进去的一座农舍；老妇人和她的女儿们怎样哆哆嗦嗦地侍酒；我们怎样喝得烂醉，那房子怎样转眼便腾起火苗；至于那可怜人后来回家寻找他的房屋和儿女时遭了什么——愿他自求多福吧！

## XI In Which the Luck Goes Against Barry / 十一 巴里的手风转背
===Original===

My hopes of obtaining the hand of one of the richest heiresses in Germany were now, as far as all human probability went, and as far as my own merits and prudence could secure my fortune, pretty certain of completion. I was admitted whenever I presented myself at the Princess’s apartments, and had as frequent opportunities as I desired of seeing the Countess Ida there. I cannot say that she received me with any particular favour; the silly young creature’s affections were, as I have said, engaged ignobly elsewhere; and, however captivating my own person and manners may have been, it was not to be expected that she should all of a sudden forget her lover for the sake of the young Irish gentleman who was paying his addresses to her. But such little rebuffs as I got were far from discouraging me. I had very powerful friends, who were to aid me in my undertaking; and knew that, sooner or later, the victory must be mine. In fact, I only waited my time to press my suit. Who could tell the dreadful stroke of fortune which was impending over my illustrious protectress, and which was to involve me partially in her ruin?
All things seemed for a while quite prosperous to my wishes; and in spite of the Countess Ida’s disinclination, it was much easier to bring her to her senses than, perhaps, may be supposed in a silly constitutional country like England, where people are not brought up with those wholesome sentiments of obedience to Royalty which were customary in Europe at the time when I was a young man.
I have stated how, through Magny, I had the Princess, as it were, at my feet. Her Highness had only to press the match upon the old Duke, over whom her influence was unbounded, and to secure the goodwill of the Countess of Liliengarten, (which was the romantic title of his Highness’s morganatic spouse), and the easy old man would give an order for the marriage: which his ward would perforce obey. Madame de Liliengarten was, too, from her position, extremely anxious to oblige the Princess Olivia; who might be called upon any day to occupy the throne. The old Duke was tottering, apoplectic, and exceedingly fond of good living. When he was gone, his relict would find the patronage of the Duchess Olivia most necessary to her.

===Chinese===

如今，凭一切人事的或然、凭我自身才具与审慎所能保全的限度，我要德意志最富继承女之一的手，眼看十拿九稳了。我一到公主的公寓递名求见，无有不放进的；在那里见伊达伯爵夫人的机会，要多频有多频。不能说她待我有什么特别的青眼；这痴心小姑娘的情愫，如前所述，卑贱地另有所属；我的人品风度再迷人，也难指望她为着一个正献殷勤的爱尔兰青年，忽然忘掉她的情人。可这一点点小小的回绝，远不足以叫我气馁。我有强硬的朋友襄助这件大事；我知道胜利迟早是我的。我只等时机成熟递上求婚。谁能料到，一场可怕的横祸正悬在我那位显赫的保护人头顶，还要把我卷进她的覆灭里去一分？
有一阵子，事事都顺遂我的心愿；任凭伊达伯爵夫人一百个不愿意，叫她回心转意，也比人们想象的容易得多——在英格兰这样的宪政痴国，人们自小不敬王室，我年轻那时节，欧洲可不是这样的风气。
我已交代过：靠着马尼，我等于把公主攦在了手心。殿下只消把这门亲事压给老公爵——她对他的影响无边——再买通利利恩加滕伯爵夫人（这是殿下那位贵贱配夫人的风流名号）的好感，那好说话的老人自会下一道成婚的敕令：被监护人只有服从的份。况且利利恩加滕太太的地位摆在那里，正急欲讨好奥莉维亚公主：公主哪一天都可能登基。老公爵风中残烛，中风体胖，贪口腹。他一去，那位遗孀要仰仗公爵夫人奥莉维亚的庇护，就太要紧了。

===Original===

Hence there was a close mutual understanding between the two ladies; and the world said that the Hereditary Princess was already indebted to the favourite for help on various occasions. Her Highness had obtained, through the Countess, several large grants of money for the payment of her multifarious debts; and she was now good enough to exert her gracious influence over Madame de Liliengarten in order to obtain for me the object so near my heart. It is not to be supposed that my end was to be obtained without continual unwillingness and refusals on Magny’s part; but I pushed my point resolutely, and had means in my hands of overcoming the stubbornness of that feeble young gentleman. Also, I may say, without vanity, that if the high and mighty Princess detested me, the Countess (though she was of extremely low origin, it is said) had better taste and admired me. She often did us the honour to go partners with us in one of our faro-banks, and declared that I was the handsomest man in the duchy. All I was required to prove was my nobility, and I got at Vienna such a pedigree as would satisfy the most greedy in that way. In fact, what had a man descended from the Barrys and the Bradys to fear before any *von* in Germany? By way of making assurance doubly sure, I promised Madame de Liliengarten ten thousand louis on the day of my marriage, and she knew that as a play-man I had never failed in my word: and I vow, that had I paid fifty percent for it, I would have got the money.
Thus by my talents, honesty, and acuteness, I had, considering I was a poor patronless outcast, raised for myself very powerful protectors. Even his Highness the Duke Victor was favourably inclined to me; for, his favourite charger falling ill of the staggers, I gave him a ball such as my uncle Brady used to administer, and cured the horse; after which his Highness was pleased to notice me frequently. He invited me to his hunting and shooting parties, where I showed myself to be a good sportsman; and once or twice he condescended to talk to me about my prospects in life, lamenting that I had taken to gambling, and that I had not adopted a more regular means of advancement. “Sir,” said I, “if you will allow me to speak frankly to your Highness, play with me is only a means to an end. Where should I have been without it? A private still in King Frederick’s grenadiers.

===Chinese===

于是两位夫人之间有一份紧密的默契；外间说，太子妃早欠着这位宠姬好几回的人情了。殿下通过伯爵夫人的手，弄到过好几笔巨款，还她那说不清的债；如今她承情对利利恩加滕太太施恩影响，替我谋我心中头一件大事。不必说，马尼那头少不了推三阻四；可我步步紧逼，手里有的是治这位软骨头少爷的招。再者，我可以不自夸地说：高高在上的公主固然厌我，伯爵夫人（据说出身极低）倒有好品味，欣赏我。她常赏脸同我们合伙开一庄法罗，还宣称我是全公国第一美男子。要我证的只剩门第；我在维也纳弄到一份谱牒，贪此道者谁也挑不出刺。说真的，巴里（Barry）家和布雷迪家的后人，站在德意志任何一个“冯”字面前，怕过什么？为把保险再加一道，我许给利利恩加滕太太：成婚之日奉上十万路易；她知道我这赌人向来说话算话：我起誓，哪怕出五分利去借，这笔钱我也拿得出来。
就这样，凭我的才具、诚实和精明，我一个无依无靠的穷落难人，给自己挣来了强硬的保护人。连公爵维克多殿下也对我另眼相看：他心爱的坐骑得了眩晕病，我照布雷迪舅舅的老方子给它服了一丸，把马治好了；此后殿下便时常赏脸垂顾。他邀我赴他的围猎，我在场中显出一手上好身手；有一两回他还屈尊同我谈前程，叹我走上了赌的路，没有掠一条更正的进身之阶。“殿下，”我说，“恕我直言：赌于我只是达目的的一个手段。没有它，我如今在哪里？还在腓特烈国王的掷弹兵里当列兵。

===Original===

I come of a race which gave princes to my country; but persecutions have deprived them of their vast possessions. My uncle’s adherence to his ancient faith drove him from our country. I too resolved to seek advancement in the military service; but the insolence and ill-treatment which I received at the hands of the English were not bearable by a highborn gentleman, and I fled their service. It was only to fall into another bondage to all appearance still more hopeless; when my good star sent a preserver to me in my uncle, and my spirit and gallantry enabled me to take advantage of the means of escape afforded me. Since then we have lived, I do not disguise it, by play; but who can say I have done him a wrong? Yet, if I could find myself in an honourable post, and with an assured maintenance, I would never, except for amusement, such as every gentleman must have, touch a card again. I beseech your Highness to inquire of your resident at Berlin if I did not on every occasion act as a gallant soldier. I feel that I have talents of a higher order, and should be proud to have occasion to exert them; if, as I do not doubt, my fortune shall bring them into play.”
The candour of this statement struck his Highness greatly, and impressed him in my favour, and he was pleased to say that he believed me, and would be glad to stand my friend.
Having thus the two Dukes, the Duchess, and the reigning favourite enlisted on my side, the chances certainly were that I should carry off the great prize; and I ought, according to all common calculations, to have been a Prince of the Empire at this present writing, but that my ill luck pursued me in a matter in which I was not the least to blame﻿—the unhappy Duchess’s attachment to the weak, silly, cowardly Frenchman. The display of this love was painful to witness, as its end was frightful to think of. The Princess made no disguise of it. If Magny spoke a word to a lady of her household, she would be jealous, and attack with all the fury of her tongue the unlucky offender. She would send him a half-dozen of notes in the day: at his arrival to join her circle or the courts which she held, she would brighten up, so that all might perceive.

===Chinese===

我出身的种族，出过我们国家的君王；迫害夺去了他们的庞大产业。我伯父因固守古教，被逐出国门。我也立志从军上进；可英国人加于我的欺凌与虐待，是出身高贵的绅士受不住的，我逃了他们的军。谁知又落进另一重奴役，看着更无出路；幸亏我的福星差我伯父来救，凭我的胆识，抓住了脱身的机会。此后我们靠赌为生——我不讳言；可谁能说我害过他一分？然而，倘能觅得一个体面的职位、一份稳妥的衣食，除了每个绅士必得有的消遣，我再也不碰一张牌。恳请殿下问您驻柏林的使节：我在哪一回不是个勇敢的士兵。我自觉有更高一等的才具，只盼有机会施展；我的命运——这一点我不疑心——自会把它们派上用场。”
这番话的坦白大大打动了殿下，替我在他心里挣了分；他表示他信我，愿做我的朋友。
两位公爵、公爵夫人同当权的宠姬既都揽在我这一边，大彩头眼看是我的了；照一切寻常的盘算，此刻执笔的我本该是帝国的一位亲王——偏我的霉运追着我不放，追在一桩我半点罪过没有的事上：不幸的公爵夫人对一个软弱、愚蠢、怯懦的法国人的痴情。这场爱情的做派，看的人难受；它的下场，想的人可怕。公主毫不遮掩。马尼对她宫里哪位女官多说一句话，她就吃醋，用她那条毒舌把倒霉的犯事者骂个狗血淋头。她一天给他写五六张条子；他一到，进她的圈子或她开的朝会，她满面放光，人人看得见。

===Original===

It was a wonder that her husband had not long ere this been made aware of her faithlessness; but the Prince Victor was himself of so high and stern a nature that he could not believe in her stooping so far from her rank as to forget her virtue: and I have heard say, that when hints were given to him of the evident partiality which the Princess showed for the equerry, his answer was a stern command never more to be troubled on the subject. “The Princess is light-minded,” he said; “she was brought up at a frivolous Court; but her folly goes not beyond coquetry: crime is impossible; she has her birth, and my name, and her children, to defend her.” And he would ride off to his military inspections and be absent for weeks, or retire to his suite of apartments, and remain closeted there whole days; only appearing to make a bow at her Highness’s levée, or to give her his hand at the Court galas, where ceremony required that he should appear. He was a man of vulgar tastes, and I have seen him in the private garden, with his great ungainly figure, running races, or playing at ball with his little son and daughter, whom he would find a dozen pretexts daily for visiting. The serene children were brought to their mother every morning at her toilette; but she received them very indifferently: except on one occasion, when the young Duke Ludwig got his little uniform as colonel of hussars, being presented with a regiment by his godfather the Emperor Leopold. Then, for a day or two, the Duchess Olivia was charmed with the little boy; but she grew tired of him speedily, as a child does of a toy. I remember one day, in the morning circle, some of the Princess’s rouge came off on the arm of her son’s little white military jacket; on which she slapped the poor child’s face, and sent him sobbing away. Oh, the woes that have been worked by women in this world! the misery into which men have lightly stepped with smiling faces; often not even with the excuse of passion, but from mere foppery, vanity, and bravado! Men play with these dreadful two-edged tools, as if no harm could come to them. I, who have seen more of life than most men, if I had a son, would go on my knees to him and beg him to avoid woman, who is worse than poison.

===Chinese===

奇的是她丈夫竟一直没有察觉她的负心；维克多亲王自己气性那样高傲冷峻，不信她肯从她的品级跌下这样远、忘掉她的贞操：听说有人向他暗示公主对那位御马监的偏心，他的回答是一道冷冷的严令：此题永不许再扰。“公主轻浮，”他说，“她是在轻浮宫廷里长大的；可她的糊涂不越调情一步；犯罪不可能：她有她的血统、我的姓氏、她的孩子，替她把关。”随后他骑马巡视他的军队，一去数周；或退进自己的套房，整日闭门不出；只在殿下的接见会上露一面鞠个躬，或在宫廷盛会上，礼数要他到场时，把手指给殿下一指。他是个趣味庸俗的人；我见过他在私家花园里，那副笨大的身量，同他的小儿子小女儿赛跑、抛球——他每天能找出十来个由头去看那两个孩子。两个恬静的小人儿每天晨间梳妆时抱到母亲跟前；她待他们冷冷淡淡：只有一回例外——小公爵路德维希领到他那身骠骑兵上校的小军装，他的教父利奥波德皇帝赏了他一个团。那一两天，公爵夫人奥莉维亚把小男孩稀罕得了不得；可很快就腻了，像孩子腻一件玩具。记得有一天，晨会上，公主的胭脂蹭掉了些，蹭在儿子小小的白军装袖上；她一巴掌打在可怜孩子脸上，把他打发哭着走了。哦，这世上女人造下的孽！男人们满脸堆笑、轻轻巧巧踏进去的深渊！常常连热恋的由头都没有，只为纨绔、虚荣、逞强！男人们耍弄这可怕的双刃家伙，只当伤不着自身。我，比多数人见过更多世面的我，若有儿子，我肯跪下来求他：躲开女人——女人比毒药还毒。

===Original===

Once intrigue, and your whole life is endangered: you never know when the evil may fall upon you; and the woe of whole families, and the ruin of innocent people perfectly dear to you, may be caused by a moment of your folly.
When I saw how entirely lost the unlucky Monsieur de Magny seemed to be, in spite of all the claims I had against him, I urged him to fly. He had rooms in the palace, in the garrets over the Princess’s quarters (the building was a huge one, and accommodated almost a city of noble retainers of the family); but the infatuated young fool would not budge, although he had not even the excuse of love for staying. “How she squints,” he would say of the Princess, “and how crooked she is! She thinks no one can perceive her deformity. She writes me verses out of Gresset or Crebillon, and fancies I believe them to be original. Bah! they are no more her own than her hair is!” It was in this way that the wretched lad was dancing over the ruin that was yawning under him. I do believe that his chief pleasure in making love to the Princess was, that he might write about his victories to his friends of the *petites maisons* at Paris, where he longed to be considered as a wit and a *vainqueur de dames*.
Seeing the young man’s recklessness, and the danger of his position, I became very anxious that *my* little scheme should be brought to a satisfactory end, and pressed him warmly on the matter.
My solicitations with him were, I need not say, from the nature of the connection between us, generally pretty successful; and, in fact, the poor fellow could *refuse me nothing*: as I used often laughingly to say to him, very little to his liking. But I used more than threats, or the legitimate influence I had over him. I used delicacy and generosity; as a proof of which, I may mention that I promised to give back to the Princess the family emerald, which I mentioned in the last chapter that I had won from her unprincipled admirer at play.
This was done by my uncle’s consent, and was one of the usual acts of prudence and foresight which distinguish that clever man. “Press the matter now, Redmond my boy,” he would urge. “This affair between her Highness and Magny must end ill for both of them, and that soon; and where will be your chance to win the Countess then? Now is your time!

===Chinese===

风流一回，你一生就悬在刀口：你永不知道灾祸几时落到头上；你一分钟的糊涂，可叫整整几家哭，叫同你亲爱无干的清白人毁掉。
眼见马尼先生沉溺到那步田地，尽管我对他握着满把的把柄，我还是催他逃。他在宫里有住处，就在公主居所顶上的阁楼（那座宫殿大得很，几乎容得下全家的一城贵族仆从）；可这昏了头的傻小子寸步不动，留下来连“为爱”这个借口都没有。“瞧她那对斗鸡眼，”他说公主，“瞧她那副歪身板！她还当没人看得出她的残缺。她拿格雷塞和克雷比永的诗改改写给我，还当我信是她的原作。呸！那些同她的头发一样，都不是她自己的！”这可怜虫就这样，在脚下大张的毁灭之上跳舞。我深信，他同公主谈情的主要乐趣，是写信到巴黎他那些“小公馆”朋友那里夸功——他巴望那边把他当才子、当“妇女的征服者”。
看这年轻人这样没轻重、处境这样险，我着急要把我那一盘小棋早日收官，在这件事上把他催得火热。
我对他的请求——凭我们中间那层关系，不必说——向来十拿九稳；说真的，那可怜虫没有一件事拒绝得了我：这话我常笑着对他讲，他老大不乐意听。可我不只用威胁，或我名正言顺握着的影响。我也用体贴和大度：为证此言，不妨一提——我应承把那颗家传的绿宝石还给公主；前一章说过，那是她那位没原则的崇拜者在赌桌上输给我的。
这是征得伯父同意办的，正是那位精明人惯常的深谋远虑。“趁现在把事催成，雷德蒙我的小子，”他催我，“殿下同马尼这段孽缘，必是两败的收场，且就在眼前；到那时你还哪里去赢伯爵夫人？时机就是现在！

===Original===

win her and wear her before the month is over, and we will give up the punting business, and go live like noblemen at our castle in Swabia. Get rid of that emerald, too,” he added: “should an accident happen, it will be an ugly deposit found in our hand.” This it was that made me agree to forego the possession of the trinket; which, I must confess, I was loth to part with. It was lucky for us both that I did: as you shall presently hear.
Meanwhile, then, I urged Magny: I myself spoke strongly to the Countess of Liliengarten, who promised formally to back my claim with his Highness the reigning Duke; and Monsieur de Magny was instructed to induce the Princess Olivia to make a similar application to the old sovereign in my behalf. It was done. The two ladies urged the Prince; his Highness (at a supper of oysters and champagne) was brought to consent, and her Highness the Hereditary Princess did me the honour of notifying personally to the Countess Ida that it was the Prince’s will that she should marry the young Irish nobleman, the Chevalier Redmond de Balibari. The notification was made in my presence; and though the young Countess said “Never!” and fell down in a swoon at her lady’s feet, I was, you may be sure, entirely unconcerned at this little display of mawkish sensibility, and felt, indeed, now that my prize was secure.
That evening I gave the Chevalier de Magny the emerald, which he promised to restore to the Princess; and now the only difficulty in my way lay with the Hereditary Prince, of whom his father, his wife, and the favourite, were alike afraid. He might not be disposed to allow the richest heiress in his duchy to be carried off by a noble, though not a wealthy foreigner. Time was necessary in order to break the matter to Prince Victor. The Princess must find him at some moment of good-humour. He had days of infatuation still, when he could refuse his wife nothing; and our plan was to wait for one of these, or for any other chance which might occur.
But it was destined that the Princess should never see her husband at her feet, as often as he had been. Fate was preparing a terrible ending to her follies, and my own hope. In spite of his solemn promises to me, Magny never restored the emerald to the Princess Olivia.
He had heard, in casual intercourse with me, that my uncle and I had been beholden to Mr.

===Chinese===

不出一个月，赢她、娶她；咱们收了坐庄的生意，上士瓦本咱们的城堡，过贵族的日子。还有，把那颗绿宝石脱手，”他补一句，“万一出事，那东西搜出来，是我们手里一件难看的赃。”正是这一条，叫我把那件小珍宝割舍了——我认，割舍得肉疼。我们两个都算走运，幸而我割了：诸位随后就听见。
于是，一面我催马尼；一面我亲自向利利恩加滕伯爵夫人重重托付，她正式应承在当朝公爵殿下面前替我说话；马尼先生奉命去劝奥莉维亚公主，叫她也替我向老君王递同样的话。办成了。两位夫人劝亲王；殿下（一顿牡蛎香槟晚宴上）点头允了；太子妃殿下赏脸，亲自向伊达伯爵夫人宣示：亲王之命，着她嫁那位年轻的爱尔兰贵族，雷德蒙·德·巴利巴里骑士。宣示时我在座；年轻的伯爵夫人虽说了一声“决不！”，在她女主人脚边晕倒，诸位放心，对这点黏糊糊的小儿女态我全不理会；我自觉彩头已稳稳到手。
当晚我把绿宝石交还马尼骑士，他应承物归公主；如今我道上唯一的障碍，只剩太子亲王一人——他父亲、他妻子、那位宠姬，个个性惮他。他兴许不肯让全国最富的继承女，叫一个虽贵而不富的外国人娶走。向维克多亲王开这个口，须得时间。公主得挑他兴头好的日子。他还有昏头的时候，老婆求什么都不驳回；我们的算盘就是等这样一天，或等任何别的机会。
可命运注定，公主再也见不到丈夫跪在她脚下了——他从前常跪的。命运正给她的痴愚、给我的指望，安排一个可怕的收梢。马尼虽对我赌过咒，始终没有把绿宝石还给奥莉维亚公主。

===Original===

Moses Löwe, the banker of Heidelberg, who had given us a good price for our valuables; and the infatuated young man took a pretext to go thither, and offered the jewel for pawn. Moses Löwe recognised the emerald at once, gave Magny the sum the latter demanded, which the Chevalier lost presently at play: never, you may be sure, acquainting us with the means by which he had made himself master of so much capital. We, for our parts, supposed that he had been supplied by his usual banker, the Princess: and many rouleaux of his gold pieces found their way into our treasury, when at the Court galas, at our own lodgings, or at the apartments of Madame de Liliengarten (who on these occasions did us the honour to go halves with us) we held our bank of faro.
Thus Magny’s money was very soon gone. But though the Jew held his jewel, of thrice the value no doubt of the sums he had lent upon it, that was not all the profit which he intended to have from his unhappy creditor; over whom he began speedily to exercise his authority. His Hebrew connections at X﻿—﻿—, money-brokers, bankers, horse-dealers, about the Court there, must have told their Heidelberg brother what Magny’s relations with the Princess were; and the rascal determined to take advantage of these, and to press to the utmost both victims. My uncle and I were, meanwhile, swimming upon the high tide of fortune, prospering with our cards, and with the still greater matrimonial game which we were playing; and we were quite unaware of the mine under our feet.
Before a month was passed, the Jew began to pester Magny. He presented himself at X﻿—﻿—, and asked for further interest-hush-money; otherwise he must sell the emerald. Magny got money for him; the Princess again befriended her dastardly lover. The success of the first demand only rendered the second more exorbitant. I know not how much money was extorted and paid on this unluckly emerald: but it was the cause of the ruin of us all.
One night we were keeping our table as usual at the Countess of Liliengarten’s, and Magny being in cash somehow, kept drawing out rouleau after rouleau, and playing with his common ill success.

===Chinese===

他同我闲谈中听说，我伯父和我受过海德堡银行家摩西·勒韦的好处——我们变卖珠宝，他给的价公道；这昏头青年寻了个由头跑到那儿，把宝石押了出去。摩西·勒韦一眼认出宝石，照马尼要的数付了钱；骑士转眼输在了赌桌上——这笔本钱怎么到手的，他自然一个字不告诉我们。我们呢，只当他照旧由他那家银行——公主——垫付；他的金币一卷一卷，流进我们的金库：宫廷盛会、我们自己寓所、或利利恩加滕太太的公寓（这种场合她总赏脸同我们对半分）开法罗庄的时候。
马尼的钱就这样很快光了。可犹太人虽攦着那颗宝石——抵他借出的数目绰绰有余——他打那倒霉债主身上要榨的，还不止这一点；很快便行使起权威来。他在X——的那些希伯来关系——钱贩、银行家、马贩，围着宫廷转的——必定早把马尼同公主的关系报给了海德堡的同门；那无赖拿定主意要加以利用，两头往死里挤。这期间，我同伯父正驾着鸿运的高潮：牌上得意，婚姻这盘更大的牌更得意；脚下埋着地雷，我们全然不知。
不出一月，犹太人开始缠马尼了。他跑到X——，讨追加的“封口利钱”；不给，就卖宝石。马尼替他弄到了钱；公主又一次帮了她那无赖情人。头一讨得手，第二讨愈发狮子开口。这颗倒霉的绿宝石上，榨出去、付出去的钱共有多少，我说不清：可它毁了我们全体。
一天夜里，我们照常在利利恩加滕伯爵夫人府上开庄；马尼手头不知怎么又有了钱，一卷接一卷地往外掏，手气照旧背。

===Original===

In the middle of the play a note was brought into him, which he read, and turned very pale on perusing; but the luck was against him, and looking up rather anxiously at the clock, he waited for a few more turns of the cards, when having, I suppose, lost his last rouleau, he got up with a wild oath that scared some of the polite company assembled, and left the room. A great trampling of horses was heard without; but we were too much engaged with our business to heed the noise, and continued our play.
Presently someone came into the playroom and said to the Countess, “Here is a strange story! A Jew has been murdered in the Kaiserwald. Magny was arrested when he went out of the room.” All the party broke up on hearing this strange news, and we shut up our bank for the night. Magny had been sitting by me during the play (my uncle dealt and I paid and took the money), and, looking under the chair, there was a crumpled paper, which I took up and read. It was that which had been delivered to him, and ran thus:﻿—

> “If you have done it, take the orderly’s horse who brings this. It is the best of my stable. There are a hundred louis in each holster, and the pistols are loaded. Either course lies open to you if you know what I mean. In a quarter of an hour I shall know our fate﻿—whether I am to be dishonoured and survive you, whether you are guilty and a coward, or whether you are still worthy of the name of
> “M.”

===Chinese===

牌局中段，有人递给他一张字条；他读了，脸白得吓人；可手风背，他焦虑地抬眼看看钟，又坐等了几轮；我猜是输掉了最后一卷，他霍地起身，一声狂咒，把满座体面人都吓了一跳，冲出屋去。门外一阵大乱的马蹄声；我们忙着正事，没理会，继续开牌。
不一会儿有人进屋对伯爵夫人说：“怪事！凯泽瓦尔德出了凶案，一个犹太人被杀了。马尼刚出屋就给拿下了。”满座闻讯而散；我们当夜收了庄。牌局里马尼一直坐在我旁边（伯父发牌，我收付），我朝椅子底下一看，有一张揉皱的纸；拾起来读了。正是递给他的那张，写道：

> “事已至此，牵马来者系我马厩中最好一匹，即骑去。两只枪套里各有一百路易，手枪已上膛。何去何从，你自掂量。一刻钟内我将知道我们的命运——是我受辱而你苟活，是你有罪且是懦夫，还是你仍配得上
> “M.”

===Original===

This was in the handwriting of the old General de Magny; and my uncle and I, as we walked home at night, having made and divided with the Countess Liliengarten no inconsiderable profits that night, felt our triumphs greatly dashed by the perusal of the letter. “Has Magny,” we asked, “robbed the Jew, or has his intrigue been discovered?” In either case, my claims on the Countess Ida were likely to meet with serious drawbacks: and I began to feel that my “great card” was played and perhaps lost.
Well, it *was* lost: though I say, to this day, it was well and gallantly played. After supper (which we never for fear of consequences took during play) I became so agitated in my mind as to what was occurring that I determined to sally out about midnight into the town, and inquire what was the real motive of Magny’s apprehension. A sentry was at the door, and signified to me that I and my uncle were under arrest.
We were left in our quarters for six weeks, so closely watched that escape was impossible, had we desired it; but, as innocent men, we had nothing to fear. Our course of life was open to all, and we desired and courted inquiry. Great and tragical events happened during those six weeks; of which, though we heard the outline, as all Europe did, when we were released from our captivity, we were yet far from understanding all the particulars, which were not much known to me for many years after. Here they are, as they were told me by the lady, who of all the world perhaps was most likely to know them. But the narrative had best form the contents of another chapter.

===Chinese===

这是老马尼将军的亲笔。夜里回家的路上，我同伯父——那一晚同利利恩加滕夫人分完了为数可观的红利——读了这封信，得胜的欢畅给浇了个透心凉。“马尼，”我们问，“是抢了犹太人，还是奸情败露了？”两样无论哪样，我在伊达伯爵夫人身上的权利，眼看要大打折扣：我开始觉得，我那张“大王”已经打出去了，多半还输了。
可不是输了：虽然到今天我仍说，那张牌打得漂亮，打得堂堂。宵夜之后（我们打牌时从不宵夜，怕坏了手风），我心里的焦灼压不住，决意午夜摸进城去，探一探拿办马尼的真由头。门口一名哨兵向我示意：我同伯父，都已被软禁。
我们在寓所里被看管了六个星期，看守严密，想逃也逃不成；可我们是清白人，无所畏惧。我们的营生人人看得见，我们求之不得地候查。那六个星期里发生了几桩惨烈的大事：轮廓，我们同全欧洲一道听到了——获释的时候；细节，我们远没有摸清，许多年后我才略知。如今把它们摆在这里，照那位夫人亲口讲的那样——全世界数她最知情。只是这段叙述，最好另起一章。

## X More Runs of Luck / 十 再几局好运
===Original===

I am not going to entertain my readers with an account of my professional career as a gamester, any more than I did with anecdotes of my life as a military man. I might fill volumes with tales of this kind were I so minded; but at this rate, my recital would not be brought to a conclusion for years, and who knows how soon I may be called upon to stop? I have gout, rheumatism, gravel, and a disordered liver. I have two or three wounds in my body, which break out every now and then, and give me intolerable pain, and a hundred more signs of breaking up. Such are the effects of time, illness, and free-living, upon one of the strongest constitutions and finest forms the world ever saw. Ah! I suffered from none of these ills in the year ’66, when there was no man in Europe more gay in spirits, more splendid in personal accomplishments, than young Redmond Barry.
Before the treachery of the scoundrel Pippi, I had visited many of the best Courts of Europe; especially the smaller ones, where play was patronised, and the professors of that science always welcome. Among the ecclesiastical principalities of the Rhine we were particularly well received. I never knew finer or gayer Courts than those of the Electors of Treves and Cologne, where there was more splendour and gaiety than at Vienna; far more than in the wretched barrack-court of Berlin. The Court of the Archduchess-Governess of the Netherlands was, likewise, a royal place for us knights of the dice-box and gallant votaries of fortune; whereas in the stingy Dutch or the beggarly Swiss republics, it was impossible for a gentleman to gain a livelihood unmolested.
After our mishap at Mannheim, my uncle and I made for the Duchy of X﻿—﻿—. The reader may find out the place easily enough; but I do not choose to print at full the names of some illustrious persons in whose society I then fell, and among whom I was made the sharer in a very strange and tragical adventure.
There was no Court in Europe at which strangers were more welcome than at that of the noble Duke of X﻿—﻿—; none where pleasure was more eagerly sought after, and more splendidly enjoyed.

===Chinese===

我不打算拿赌客生涯的流水账消遣读者，正如不曾拿行伍生涯的逸闻去填篇幅。这样的故事，我若有意，能写满好几卷；可照这个写法，我的叙述几年也收不了尾——谁又知道几时就该搁笔？我有痛风、风湿、结石，肝也坏了。身上带着两三处旧伤，时不时溃痛难忍，外加一百样散架的征兆。时间、疾病、放浪的日子，就这样作践了世上少见的一副最结实的体格、最漂亮的身段。啊！六六年那会儿，这些病一样也不沾我；那时的欧洲，再没有谁比年轻的雷德蒙·巴里更兴致飞扬、更一身风流本事了。
恶棍皮皮出卖我们之前，我已跑遍欧洲许多最好的宫廷，尤其是那些小朝廷：赌在那里受保护，这门学问的教授处处受欢迎。莱茵河畔那些教会长国待我们尤厚。特里尔、科隆两位选帝侯的宫廷，我从没见过更漂亮更欢乐的；其煊赫超过维也纳，更远胜柏林那座寒碜的兵营宫廷。尼德兰的女大公总督宫廷，同样是我们这班骰盅骑士、命运的勇敢信徒的乐土；至于吝啬的荷兰共和国、乞丐似的瑞士各邦，绅士想安然混口饭吃，门儿也没有。
曼海姆栽了跟头之后，我同伯父投X——公国去了。那地方读者不难猜到；只是当年同我往来的几位显赫人物，我不愿把名字印全，正是在他们中间，我摊上了一桩离奇而悲惨的奇遇。
欧洲再没有哪国宫廷比X——公爵的更欢迎外客；再没有哪里，行乐寻得更急、享得更奢。

===Original===

The Prince did not inhabit his capital of S﻿—﻿—, but, imitating in every respect the ceremonial of the Court of Versailles, built himself a magnificent palace at a few leagues from his chief city, and round about his palace a superb aristocratic town, inhabited entirely by his nobles, and the officers of his sumptuous Court. The people were rather hardly pressed, to be sure, in order to keep up this splendour; for his Highness’s dominions were small, and so he wisely lived in a sort of awful retirement from them, seldom showing his face in his capital, or seeing any countenances but those of his faithful domestics and officers. His palace and gardens of Ludwigslust were exactly on the French model. Twice a week there were Court receptions, and grand Court galas twice a month. There was the finest opera out of France, and a ballet unrivalled in splendour; on which his Highness, a great lover of music and dancing, expended prodigious sums. It may be because I was then young, but I think I never saw such an assemblage of brilliant beauty as used to figure there on the stage of the Court theatre, in the grand mythological ballets which were then the mode, and in which you saw Mars in red-heeled pumps and a periwig, and Venus in patches and a hoop. They say the costume was incorrect, and have changed it since; but for my part, I have never seen a Venus more lovely than the Coralie, who was the chief dancer, and found no fault with the attendant nymphs, in their trains, and lappets, and powder. These operas used to take place twice a week, after which some great officer of the Court would have his evening, and his brilliant supper, and the dice-box rattled everywhere, and all the world played. I have seen seventy play-tables set out in the grand gallery of Ludwigslust, besides the faro-bank; where the Duke himself would graciously come and play, and win or lose with a truly royal splendour.
It was hither we came after the Mannheim misfortune. The nobility of the Court were pleased to say our reputation had preceded us, and the two Irish gentleman were made welcome. The very first night at Court we lost 740 of our 800 louis; the next evening, at the Court Marshal’s table, I won them back, with 1,300 more.

===Chinese===

亲王不驻在他的都城S——；他事事模仿凡尔赛的宫廷仪制，在离都城几里格的地方给自己盖了一座壮丽宫殿，宫殿四围又造起一座华贵的贵族新城，全住他的贵族和他那豪奢宫廷的官员。要维持这份煊赫，百姓自然被榨得不轻：殿下的领地小；于是他明智地深居简出，很少在都城露面，见来见去只有忠仆和官员的那些脸。他的路德维希卢斯特宫殿园林，全照法国的式样。每礼拜两次宫廷接见，每月两次大典。法国以外最好的歌剧院在这里，还有一班豪丽无双的芭蕾；殿下雅好音乐舞蹈，在这上头一掷千金。也许因为我那时年轻：可我从不曾见过那样一片亮丽的美人，在宫廷剧院的台上，在当年时兴的神话题材大芭蕾里——战神踩着红高跟、戴看假发，维纳斯贴着绸膏、撑着裙环。人说那样的行头不合考据，如今改掉了；可依我说，再没见过比首席舞女科拉莉更可爱的维纳斯；陪衬的那些宁芙仙子，拖着裙裾、飘着帽带、扑着粉，我也挑不出毛病。这样的歌剧每礼拜两场；散了戏，宫廷里某位大官便轮到开他的夜宴、摆他的华筵，骰盅处处响，人人上场。我见过路德维希卢斯特大画廊里一字排开七十张赌桌，还不算法罗庄——公爵亲自驾临下注，赢也赢得、输也输得一身真正的王者气派。
曼海姆的晦气之后，我们投奔的就是这里。宫廷的贵人们客气说，我们的名声先我们而至，两位爱尔兰绅士大受欢迎。进宫头一夜，我们八百路易输掉七百四；第二天晚上，在宫廷元帅的桌上，我连本带利赢了回来，还多掠一千三。

===Original===

You may be sure we allowed no one to know how near we were to ruin on the first evening; but, on the contrary, I endeared everyone to me by my gay manner of losing, and the Finance Minister himself cashed a note for 400 ducats, drawn by me upon my steward of Ballybarry Castle in the kingdom of Ireland; which very note I won from his Excellency the next day, along with a considerable sum in ready cash. In that noble Court everybody was a gambler. You would see the lackeys in the ducal anterooms at work with their dirty packs of cards; the coach and chair men playing in the court, while their masters were punting in the saloons above; the very cook-maids and scullions, I was told, had a bank, where one of them, an Italian confectioner, made a handsome fortune: he purchased afterwards a Roman marquisate, and his son has figured as one of the most fashionable of the illustrious foreigners in London. The poor devils of soldiers played away their pay when they got it, which was seldom; and I don’t believe there was an officer in any one of the guard regiments but had his cards in his pouch, and no more forgot his dice than his sword-knot. Among such fellows it was diamond cut diamond. What you call fair play would have been a folly. The gentlemen of Ballybarry would have been fools indeed to appear as pigeons in such a hawk’s nest. None but men of courage and genius could live and prosper in a society where everyone was bold and clever; and here my uncle and I held our own: ay, and more than our own.
His Highness the Duke was a widower, or rather, since the death of the reigning Duchess, had contracted a morganatic marriage with a lady whom he had ennobled, and who considered it a compliment (such was the morality of those days) to be called the Northern Dubarry. He had been married very young, and his son, the Hereditary Prince, may be said to have been the political sovereign of the State: for the reigning Duke was fonder of pleasure than of politics, and loved to talk a great deal more with his grand huntsman, or the director of his opera, than with ministers and ambassadors.
The Hereditary Prince, whom I shall call Prince Victor, was of a very different character from his august father.

===Chinese===

诸位放心，头天晚上我们离破产那样近，谁也没叫知道；相反，我输得那样潇洒，人人对我生了好感；财政大臣大人亲自替我兑现了一张四百达卡特的票据——出票人是我，付款人写的是我在爱尔兰王国的巴利巴里堡的管家；这张票，第二天我又从大人手里赢了回来，外加相当一笔现钱。在那座高贵的宫廷里，人人是赌客。公爵前厅里，你看得见跟班们拿着脏兮兮的纸牌开庄；院里车夫轿夫在赌，楼上他们的主子们在下注；听说连厨娘洗碗工都有一庄，其中一个意大利糖果师就此发了一笔：后来买了一个罗马侯爵领，他儿子如今是伦敦最时髦的外国名流之一。可怜的大兵们，饷一下来就输光——饷又难得发；我看各卫队团没有一个军官腰包里不带纸牌，骰子同剑穗一样忘不了。同这样一伙人在一起，是金刚钻对金刚钻。你们所谓的公平戏，在那儿是傻话。巴利巴里的绅士们要在那样的鹰巢里扮鸽子，才是真傻。在人人胆大、人人精明的社会里，只有胆大加天才的人立得住、发得开；就在这里，我同伯父不但立住，还压倒了众人。
公爵殿下是鳏夫；或者说，当朝公爵夫人死后，他行了一门贵贱婚，把那位夫人封了贵族；她认为“北方的杜巴利”是个恭维——那个时代的道德如此。他结婚极早；儿子太子亲王，可以说才是国家的实际元首：当朝公爵爱行乐胜过爱政治，同他的大猎监、他的剧院总监聊天，远比同大臣公使聊天来劲。
太子亲王——我姑且唤他维克多亲王——同他至尊的父亲全然两样。

===Original===

He had made the Wars of the Succession and Seven Years with great credit in the Empress’s service, was of a stern character, seldom appeared at Court, except when ceremony called him, but lived almost alone in his wing of the palace, where he devoted himself to the severest studies, being a great astronomer and chemist. He shared in the rage then common throughout Europe, of hunting for the philosopher’s stone; and my uncle often regretted that he had no smattering of chemistry, like Balsamo (who called himself Cagliostro), St. Germain, and other individuals, who had obtained very great sums from Duke Victor by aiding him in his search after the great secret. His amusements were hunting and reviewing the troops; but for him, and if his good-natured father had not had his aid, the army would have been playing at cards all day, and so it was well that the prudent prince was left to govern.
Duke Victor was fifty years of age, and his princess, the Princess Olivia, was scarce three-and-twenty. They had been married seven years, and in the first years of their union the Princess had borne him a son and a daughter. The stern morals and manners, the dark and ungainly appearance, of the husband, were little likely to please the brilliant and fascinating young woman, who had been educated in the south (she was connected with the ducal house of S﻿—﻿—), who had passed two years at Paris under the guardianship of Mesdames the daughters of His Most Christian Majesty, and who was the life and soul of the Court of X﻿—﻿—, the gayest of the gay, the idol of her august father-in-law, and, indeed, of the whole Court. She was not beautiful, but charming; not witty, but charming, too, in her conversation as in her person. She was extravagant beyond all measure; so false, that you could not trust her; but her very weaknesses were more winning than the virtues of other women, her selfishness more delightful than others’ generosity. I never knew a woman whose faults made her so attractive. She used to ruin people, and yet they all loved her. My old uncle has seen her cheating at ombre, and let her win 400 louis without resisting in the least. Her caprices with the officers and ladies of her household were ceaseless: but they adored her. She was the only one of the reigning family whom the people worshipped.

===Chinese===

他在女皇麾下打过王位继承战争和七年战争，声名极佳；性情冷峻，除非礼数召唤，绝少进宫，几乎独居在宫殿他自己那一翼，埋头最严酷的学问：他是大天文学家兼化学家。他也染上了当时弥漫全欧的点金石狂热；我伯父常常懊悔自己不通化学——不像巴尔萨莫（自称卡廖斯特罗）、圣热尔曼那几号人物，靠替维克多公爵寻那大秘密，从他手里弄到大笔大笔的钱。他的消遣是行猎和检阅部队；倘若没有他，倘若他那好脾气的父亲没有他帮衬，军队怕要整天打牌了；所以务实谨慎的亲王当家，是件幸事。
维克多公爵五十岁；他的王妃奥莉维亚公主才二十三岁不到。成婚七年，头几年里公主为他生了一子一女。丈夫的严峻道学与做派、黝黑而笨拙的外表，难讨这位明媚迷人的年轻妇人的欢心：她生在南边（同S——公爵家沾亲），在法兰西至圣陛下的几位公主监护下在巴黎住过两年，是X——宫廷的生命与灵魂，快活人里最快活的，是她那至尊公公的偶像，实在也是全宫廷的偶像。她不美，却迷人；不俏皮，谈吐却同人品一样迷人。她挥霍无度；她虚假得叫你没法信她；可她的短处偏偏比别的女人的美德还惹人爱，她的自私比旁人的慷慨还叫人快活。我从没见过哪个女人的缺点这样动人。她惯常毁人，人还个个爱她。我老伯父亲眼见她在翁布尔牌上作弊，由她赢了四百路易，半点不拦。她对宫里的官员女官使性子，没个完；他们却崇拜她。王室里唯有她一个，百姓肯拜。

===Original===

She never went abroad but they followed her carriage with shouts of acclamation: and, to be generous to them, she would borrow the last penny from one of her poor maids of honour, whom she would never pay. In the early days her husband was as much fascinated by her as all the rest of the world was; but her caprices had caused frightful outbreaks of temper on his part, and an estrangement which, though interrupted by almost mad returns of love, was still general. I speak of her Royal Highness with perfect candour and admiration, although I might be pardoned for judging her more severely, considering her opinion of myself. She said the elder Monsieur de Balibari was a finished old gentleman, and the younger one had the manners of a courier. The world has given a different opinion, and I can afford to chronicle this almost single sentence against me. Besides, she had a reason for her dislike to me, which you shall hear.
Five years in the army, long experience of the world, had ere now dispelled any of those romantic notions regarding love with which I commenced life; and I had determined, as is proper with gentlemen (it is only your low people who marry for mere affection), to consolidate my fortunes by marriage. In the course of our peregrinations, my uncle and I had made several attempts to carry this object into effect; but numerous disappointments had occurred which are not worth mentioning here, and had prevented me hitherto from making such a match as I thought was worthy of a man of my birth, abilities, and personal appearance. Ladies are not in the habit of running away on the Continent, as is the custom in England (a custom whereby many honourable gentlemen of my country have much benefited!); guardians, and ceremonies, and difficulties of all kinds intervene; true love is not allowed to have its course, and poor women cannot give away their honest hearts to the gallant fellows who have won them.

===Chinese===

她一出宫，百姓便追着车驾欢呼；为对他们慷慨，她可以向她那些永远赖账的穷女官借走最后一个铜板。起初，她丈夫同全世界一样为她着迷；可她的反复无常惹得他几回狂怒，两人渐渐疏远——中间虽也有近乎发疯的旧情复燃，疏远到底是常态。我说殿下，句句出自坦白的倾慕，虽然念着她对我的评语，我判她更严些也情有可原。她说，老巴利巴里先生是位登峰造极的老绅士，小的那位，一副信差的做派。世人另有公论；这一句对我少有的贬词，我记录得下。何况她厌我有她的缘由——诸位听罢便知。
五年行伍，多年涉世，早把我起步人生时那点关于爱情的浪漫念头驱散尽了；我拿定主意——这也是绅士的本分（只有下层人才为纯粹的恩爱结婚）——借婚姻巩固家业。漂泊途中，我同伯父几次三番想办成这件大事；只是挫折连连，不值一叙，害我至今没能攖上一门配得上我的门第、才具、仪表的亲事。大陆上的小姐不兴私奔——英格兰才兴那一套（我国多少体面绅士受惠于这套风俗！）；监护人、礼法、重重难关横在中间；真正的爱情不许通行，可怜的女人们没法把一颗真心，交给赢得它的勇敢汉子。

===Original===

Now it was settlements that were asked for; now it was my pedigree and title-deeds that were not satisfactory: though I had a plan and rent-roll of the Ballybarry estates, and the genealogy of the family up to King Brian Boru, or Barry, most handsomely designed on paper; now it was a young lady who was whisked off to a convent just as she was ready to fall into my arms; on another occasion, when a rich widow of the Low Countries was about to make me lord of a noble estate in Flanders, comes an order of the police which drives me out of Brussels at an hour’s notice, and consigns my mourner to her château. But at X﻿—﻿— I had an opportunity of playing a great game: and had won it too, but for the dreadful catastrophe which upset my fortune.
In the household of the Hereditary Princess there was a lady nineteen years of age, and possessor of the greatest fortune in the whole duchy. The Countess Ida, such was her name, was daughter of a late Minister and favourite of his Highness the Duke of X﻿—﻿— and his Duchess, who had done her the honour to be her sponsors at birth, and who, at the father’s death, had taken her under their august guardianship and protection.
