At sixteen she was brought from her castle, where, up to that period, she had been permitted to reside, and had been placed with the Princess Olivia, as one of her Highness’s maids of honour.
The aunt of the Countess Ida, who presided over her house during her minority, had foolishly allowed her to contract an attachment for her cousin-german, a penniless sublieutenant in one of the Duke’s foot regiments, who had flattered himself to be able to carry off this rich prize; and if he had not been a blundering silly idiot indeed, with the advantage of seeing her constantly, of having no rival near him, and the intimacy attendant upon close kinsmanship, might easily, by a private marriage, have secured the young Countess and her possessions. But he managed matters so foolishly, that he allowed her to leave her retirement, to come to Court for a year, and take her place in the Princess Olivia’s household; and then what does my young gentleman do, but appear at the Duke’s levée one day, in his tarnished epaulet and threadbare coat, and make an application in due form to his Highness, as the young lady’s guardian, for the hand of the richest heiress in his dominions!

===Chinese===

这一回要的是财产授予；那一回嫌我的谱牒地契不过硬——尽管我有巴利巴里产业的规划图和租册，有直溯布赖恩·博鲁王、或叫巴里王的家谱，纸上画得堂堂皇皇；这一回是位小姐，眼看要落进我怀里，被人一把送进了修道院；另一回，低地国家一位富孀正要让我做弗兰德斯一处豪产的主人，警察一纸命令，限一点钟把我逐出布鲁塞尔，把我的哭丧人儿打发回了她的城堡。可在X——，我得了打一场大牌的机会：也赢了——若不是那场倾覆我家业的大祸。
太子妃宫里有一位十九岁的小姐，全公国头一份的家私。伊达伯爵夫人——这是她的名字——是先大臣的女儿，X——公爵同公爵夫人的宠儿；夫妇俩赏脸做了她受洗的教父母，父亲死时又把她收在至尊的监护与庇护之下。十六岁上，她从获准久居的城堡被接进宫，安在奥莉维亚公主名下做女官。
伊达伯爵夫人的姑母，在她未成年时主持她的家事，糊涂地容她同她的嫡表哥生出了情愫——公爵步兵团里一个身无分文的少尉；这家伙自以为能捾走这份肥彩。他若不是个笨透的蠢材——既同她朝夕相见，身边又没有情敌，加上至亲的亲密——凭一场私婚，原可稳稳把年轻伯爵夫人连同产业一起到手。可他把事情办得那样蠢：竟放她出了深闺，进宫一年，在奥莉维亚公主宫中当了女官；随后我这年轻的爷干了什么呢？他戴看发乌的肩章、穿着磨破的军衣，上了公爵的接见会，一板一眼向殿下——小姐的监护人——求娶全国最富的继承女！

===Original===

The weakness of the good-natured Prince was such that, as the Countess Ida herself was quite as eager for the match as her silly cousin, his Highness might have been induced to allow the match, had not the Princess Olivia been induced to interpose, and to procure from the Duke a peremptory veto to the hopes of the young man. The cause of this refusal was as yet unknown; no other suitor for the young lady’s hand was mentioned, and the lovers continued to correspond, hoping that time might effect a change in his Highness’s resolutions; when, of a sudden, the lieutenant was drafted into one of the regiments which the Prince was in the habit of selling to the great powers then at war (this military commerce was a principal part of his Highness’s and other princes’ revenues in those days), and their connection was thus abruptly broken off.
It was strange that the Princess Olivia should have taken this part against a young lady who had been her favourite; for, at first, with those romantic and sentimental notions which almost every woman has, she had somewhat encouraged the Countess Ida and her penniless lover, but now suddenly turned against them; and, from loving the Countess, as she previously had done, pursued her with every manner of hatred which a woman knows how to inflict: there was no end to the ingenuity of her tortures, the venom of her tongue, the bitterness of her sarcasm and scorn. When I first came to Court at X﻿—﻿—, the young fellows there had nicknamed the young lady the Dumme Grafinn, the stupid Countess. She was generally silent, handsome, but pale, stolid-looking, and awkward; taking no interest in the amusements of the place, and appearing in the midst of the feasts as glum as the death’s-head which, they say, the Romans used to have at their tables.
It was rumoured that a young gentleman of French extraction, the Chevalier de Magny, equerry to the Hereditary Prince, and present at Paris when the Princess Olivia was married to him by proxy there, was the intended of the rich Countess Ida; but no official declaration of the kind was yet made, and there were whispers of a dark intrigue: which, subsequently, received frightful confirmation.
This Chevalier de Magny was the grandson of an old general officer in the Duke’s service, the Baron de Magny.

===Chinese===

好脾气的亲王耳根那样软，伊达伯爵夫人自己又同她那傻表哥一样巴望这门亲，殿下本多半会点头；亏得奥莉维亚公主出面拦下，从公爵手里讨来一道断然的驳斥，绝了年轻人的念想。驳回的缘由当时无人知晓；小姐门前也不见别的求婚人；一对恋人继续通信，指望时间改变殿下的圣意；不想一纸调令，把少尉调进了亲王惯于卖给交战列强的团队之一（那年月，这桩兵马买卖是殿下同众亲王进项的大宗），两人的情缘就此生生掠断。
奇怪的是，奥莉维亚公主竟会对她素来宠爱的小姐下这样的手；起初，怀着女人人皆有之的那点浪漫痴念，她还着实撮合过伊达伯爵夫人同她那穷情人，如今忽然反戈；由爱转恨，把女人懂得的种种恨法，一样不落全用在这位伯爵夫人身上：那些折磨的花样无穷无尽，舌头的毒，讥诣轻蔑的苦。我初到X——宫廷时，那里的年轻人给小姐起了个译名，叫“傻伯爵”。她素来沉默，标致，可苍白、呆板、笨拙；对当地的行乐毫无兴致，宴席当中坐着，阴沉得像——据说罗马人席上摆的——那颗髅髅。
风传一位法国血统的青年绅士、太子亲王的御马监马尼骑士——公主在巴黎代理成婚时他在场——是富有的伊达伯爵夫人的未婚夫；可这样的事并无正式宣布，暗地里另有流言：说是一桩暖昧的勾当——后来这流言得了可怕的证实。
这位马尼骑士，是公爵麾下老将军马尼男爵的孙子。

===Original===

The Baron’s father had quitted France at the expulsion of Protestants after the revocation of the edict of Nantes, and taken service in X﻿—﻿—, where he died. The son succeeded him, and, quite unlike most French gentlemen of birth whom I have known, was a stern and cold Calvinist, rigid in the performance of his duty, retiring in his manners, mingling little with the Court, and a close friend and favourite of Duke Victor; whom he resembled in disposition.
The Chevalier his grandson was a true Frenchman; he had been born in France, where his father held a diplomatic appointment in the Duke’s service. He had mingled in the gay society of the most brilliant Court in the world, and had endless stories to tell us of the pleasures of the petites maisons, of the secrets of the Parc aux Cerfs, and of the wild gaieties of Richelieu and his companions. He had been almost ruined at play, as his father had been before him; for, out of the reach of the stern old Baron in Germany, both son and grandson had led the most reckless of lives. He came back from Paris soon after the embassy which had been despatched thither on the occasion of the marriage of the Princess, was received sternly by his old grandfather; who, however, paid his debts once more, and procured him the post in the Duke’s household. The Chevalier de Magny rendered himself a great favourite of his august master; he brought with him the modes and the gaieties of Paris; he was the deviser of all the masquerades and balls, the recruiter of the ballet-dancers, and by far the most brilliant and splendid young gentleman of the Court.
After we had been a few weeks at Ludwigslust, the old Baron de Magny endeavoured to have us dismissed from the duchy; but his voice was not strong enough to overcome that of the general public, and the Chevalier de Magny especially stood our friend with his Highness when the question was debated before him. The Chevalier’s love of play had not deserted him. He was a regular frequenter of our bank, where he played for some time with pretty good luck; and where, when he began to lose, he paid with a regularity surprising to all those who knew the smallness of his means, and the splendour of his appearance.
Her Highness the Princess Olivia was also very fond of play. On half-a-dozen occasions when we held a bank at Court, I could see her passion for the game.

===Chinese===

男爵的父亲在南特敕令废止、新教徒被逐时离开法国，到X——投军，死在那里。儿子承了他的职；同我认得的大多法国世家绅士全然两样，是个冷峻的加尔文信徒，尽忠职守一丝不苟，举止深敛，少与宫廷往来，是维克多公爵的密友与宠臣——性情两人也相像。
他孙子那位骑士却是地道的法国人；生在法国，父亲在公爵麾下驻法任外交差事。他混过全世界最煊赫宫廷的快活社会，讲起小公馆的乐子、鹿苑的秘密、黎塞留一伙的荒唐，三天三夜讲不完。他叫赌桌毁得几乎精光，同他父亲从前一样；老男爵远在德国管不着，父子两代都过着最放浪的日子。公主成婚时公国派过使团赴巴黎，使团一回来他也跟着回来了，被老祖父冷脸收下；老爷子又一次替他还了债，又替他在公爵内廷谋了差。马尼骑士成了他至尊主人的大红人：巴黎的时式和欢乐由他带来；一切化装舞会和舞会出自他的擘画；芭蕾舞女由他去招罗；全宫廷最煊赫最华丽的年轻人，数他第一。
我们在路德维希卢斯特住了没几礼拜，老马尼男爵便设法要把我们逐出公国；可他的嗓门压不过众人的公论；尤其此事在殿下面前议起时，马尼骑士还替我们说了话。骑士爱赌的性没有改。他是我们赌庄的常客，手气有一阵还不错；等到输了，他照付不误——付得那样准时，认识他的人都惊：谁不知道他进项多薄、排场多大。
奥莉维亚公主殿下也好赌。宫廷里开庄的六七个场合，我看得出她对这门营生的痴迷。

===Original===

I could see﻿—that is, my cool-headed old uncle could see﻿—much more. There was an intelligence between Monsieur de Magny and this illustrious lady. “If her Highness be not in love with the little Frenchman,” my uncle said to me one night after play, “may I lose the sight of my last eye!”
“And what then, sir?” said I.
“What then?” said my uncle, looking me hard in the face. “Are you so green as not to know what then? Your fortune is to be made, if you choose to back it now; and we may have back the Barry estates in two years, my boy.”
“How is that?” asked I, still at a loss.
My uncle drily said, “Get Magny to play; never mind his paying: take his notes of hand. The more he owes the better; but, above all, make him play.”
“He can’t pay a shilling,” answered I. “The Jews will not discount his notes at cent percent.”
“So much the better. You shall see we will make use of them,” answered the old gentleman. And I must confess that the plan he laid was a gallant, clever, and fair one.
I was to make Magny play; in this there was no great difficulty. We had an intimacy together, for he was a good sportsman as well as myself, and we came to have a pretty considerable friendship for one another; if he saw a dice-box it was impossible to prevent him from handling it; but he took to it as natural as a child does to sweetmeats.
At first he won of me; then he began to lose; then I played him money against some jewels that he brought: family trinkets, he said, and indeed of considerable value. He begged me, however, not to dispose of them in the duchy, and I gave and kept my word to him to this effect. From jewels he got to playing upon promissory notes; and as they would not allow him to play at the Court tables and in public upon credit, he was very glad to have an opportunity of indulging his favourite passion in private. I have had him for hours at my pavilion (which I had fitted up in the Eastern manner, very splendid) rattling the dice till it became time to go to his service at Court, and we would spend day after day in this manner. He brought me more jewels﻿—a pearl necklace, an antique emerald breast ornament, and other trinkets, as a set-off against these losses: for I need not say that I should not have played with him all this time had he been winning; but, after about a week, the luck set in against him, and he became my debtor in a prodigious sum.

===Chinese===

我看得出——不如说，我那位头脑冷静的老伯父看得出——更多的东西。马尼先生同这位显贵的夫人之间，有一份默契。“殿下若不是爱上了这小法国人，”一夜赌罢，伯父对我说，“叫我瞎掉最后这只眼！”
“那么怎样呢，先生？”我说。
“怎样？”伯父死死盯着我的脸，“你绿到连‘怎样’都不知道？你的家业眼看成——只消你现在下注；不出两年，咱们能把巴里家的产业赎回来，我的小子。”
“怎么讲？”我问，还是不明白。
伯父干巴巴地说：“哄马尼上桌；先别管他付账：收他的期票。他欠得越多越好；顶要紧的，叫他赌。”
“他一个先令也付不出，”我答，“犹太人打对折都不肯贴他的票。”
“那就更好。你瞧着我们拿它们派用场。”老先生答。我得说，他布下的局，漂亮、聪明、公道。
我去哄马尼上桌；这不为难。我们本就相熟：同我一样，他也是个好猎手，渐渐彼此有了相当的情分；他见了骰盅，不叫他摸一摸是不可能的；他上手的馋，同孩子见了甜食一样。
起初他赢我；随后开始输；随后我借钱给他，赢他拿来的几件珠宝：家传的小饰物，他说——也确实值不少。他求我别在公国里脱手，我应了，也守了约。由珠宝赌到期票；宫里的公开赌桌不许记账，他正巴不得有个私下的机会过瘾。他常在我那座凉亭里（我按东方样式布置的，豪华之至）摇骰子，一摇摇到他该进宫当值的时辰；我们就这样一天连一天地过。他又拿来更多珠宝——一挂珠链、一件古式的绿宝石胸饰，还有别的小件，抵那些输账——不必说，他若一直赢，我不会同他这样打下去；可约莫一个礼拜后，手风转背，他欠下我一大笔。

===Original===

I do not care to mention the extent of it; it was such as I never thought the young man could pay.
Why, then, did I play for it? Why waste days in private play with a mere bankrupt, when business seemingly much more profitable was to be done elsewhere? My reason I boldly confess. I wanted to win from Monsieur de Magny, not his money, but his intended wife, the Countess Ida. Who can say that I had not a right to use *any* stratagem in this matter of love? Or, why say love? I wanted the wealth of the lady: I loved her quite as much as Magny did; I loved her quite as much as yonder blushing virgin of seventeen does who marries an old lord of seventy. I followed the practice of the world in this; having resolved that marriage should achieve my fortune.
I used to make Magny, after his losses, give me a friendly letter of acknowledgment to some such effect as this﻿—

> “**My dear Monsieur de Balibari**﻿—I acknowledge to have lost to you this day at lansquenet [or picquet, or hazard, as the case may be: I was master of him at any game that is played] the sum of three hundred ducats, and shall hold it as a great kindness on your part if you will allow the debt to stand over until a future day, when you shall receive payment from your very grateful humble servant.”

===Chinese===

数目我不愿提；总之我从不信这年轻人还得起。
那我为什么还同他打？放着别处明显更赚钱的买卖不做，为什么同一个准破产户关起门来日夜磨？我的理由，我坦白承认：我要从马尼先生手里赢的，不是钱，是他的未婚妻，伊达伯爵夫人。情场之上，谁敢说我无权使任何计谋？何况何必叫情？我要的是小姐的家产：我爱她，绝不亚于马尼爱她；也不亚于那边红着脸的十七岁处女嫁给七十岁老勋爵的那份爱。我在这上头行的，正是世人的通行做法：既拿定借婚姻成家业，便照章办事。
我惯在马尼输过之后，叫他给我一封认账的友好短简，大意如下——

> “亲爱的巴利巴里先生——兹确认本日于兰斯克内特牌局（或皮克，或哈杂德，视情形而定：凡天下有的牌戏，我无不叫他服帖）输与阁下三百达卡特；倘蒙阁下容此账暂悬，容异日奉还，阁下恭顺感恩之仆不胜感荷。”

===Original===

With the jewels he brought me I also took the precaution (but this was my uncle’s idea, and a very good one) to have a sort of invoice, and a letter begging me to receive the trinkets as so much part payment of a sum of money he owed me.
When I had put him in such a position as I deemed favourable to my intentions, I spoke to him candidly, and without any reserve, as one man of the world should speak to another. “I will not, my dear fellow,” said I, “pay you so bad a compliment as to suppose that you expect we are to go on playing at this rate much longer, and that there is any satisfaction to me in possessing more or less sheets of paper bearing your signature, and a series of notes of hand which I know you never can pay. Don’t look fierce or angry, for you know Redmond Barry is your master at the sword; besides, I would not be such a fool as to fight a man who owes me so much money; but hear calmly what I have to propose.
“You have been very confidential to me during our intimacy of the last month; and I know all your personal affairs completely. You have given your word of honour to your grandfather never to play upon parole, and you know how you have kept it, and that he will disinherit you if he hears the truth. Nay, suppose he dies tomorrow, his estate is not sufficient to pay the sum in which you are indebted to me; and, were you to yield me up all, you would be a beggar, and a bankrupt too.
“Her Highness the Princess Olivia denies you nothing. I shall not ask why; but give me leave to say, I was aware of the fact when we began to play together.”
“Will you be made baron-chamberlain, with the grand cordon of the order?” gasped the poor fellow. “The Princess can do anything with the Duke.”
“I shall have no objection,” said I, “to the yellow riband and the gold key; though a gentleman of the house of Ballybarry cares little for the titles of the German nobility. But this is not what I want. My good Chevalier, you have hid no secrets from me. You have told me with what difficulty you have induced the Princess Olivia to consent to the project of your union with the Grafinn Ida, whom you don’t love. I know whom you love very well.”
“Monsieur de Balibari!” said the discomfited Chevalier; he could get out no more. The truth began to dawn upon him.
“You begin to understand,” continued I.

===Chinese===

他拿来的珠宝，我也留了个后手（这是我伯父的主意，好主意）：立一纸货物清单，附一信，请我把这些饰物充作他所欠款项的部分偿付。
等我把布好了自认为有利的局面，我便开诚布公同他谈，全无保留，如一个世界中人同另一个世界中人。“亲爱的伙计，”我说，“我不愿那样看轻你：以为你还指望我们照这个路子打下去，以为你签过字的纸多一些少些、你永远还不出的一叠期票，于我有什么可满足。别瞪眼、别动火：你知道雷德蒙·巴里（Redmond Barry）论剑是你师父；再说，同欠我这么多钱的人决斗，我还不至于那样傻。且平心静气，听我的提议。
“这一个月的交情里，你对我推心置腹；你私事我全知道。你对你祖父以名誉担保，再不记账打牌；你自己知道这誓守成什么样；他若听了实情，必定剥你的继承权。慢说这个：就算他明天死，他那点产业也不够还我这一笔；你便把全部家当指给我，也还是个叫花子兼破产人。
“奥莉维亚公主殿下对你有求必应。我不问为什么；只容我说一句：我们同桌开打时，我早知道这层。”
“您是要当宫务总监男爵、挂大绶带么？”可怜虫喘着问。“公主在公爵跟前说一不二。”
“黄绶带、金钥匙，”我说，“我不嫌；虽然巴利巴里府上的绅士，不大稀罕德国贵族的头衔。可我要的不是这个。我的好骑士：你对我没有瞒过秘密。你讲过，你说动奥莉维亚公主应允你同‘傻伯爵’伊达小姐的婚事，费了多大的劲——你并不爱她。你爱的是谁，我可清楚。”
“巴利巴里先生！”失魂落魄的骑士说；再吐不出一个字。真相开始在他头上亮了。
“你开始懂了。”我接着说。

===Original===

“Her Highness the Princess” (I said this in a sarcastic way) “will not be very angry, believe me, if you break off your connection with the stupid Countess. I am no more an admirer of that lady than you are; but I want her estate. I played you for that estate, and have won it; and I will give you your bills and five thousand ducats on the day I am married to it.”
“The day *I* am married to the Countess,” answered the Chevalier, thinking to have me, “I will be able to raise money to pay your claim ten times over” (this was true, for the Countess’s property may have been valued at near half a million of our money); “and then I will discharge my obligations to you. Meanwhile, if you annoy me by threats, or insult me again as you have done, I will use that influence, which, as you say, I possess, and have you turned out of the duchy, as you were out of the Netherlands last year.”
I rang the bell quite quietly. “Zamor,” said I to a tall negro fellow habited like a Turk, that used to wait upon me, “when you hear the bell ring a second time, you will take this packet to the Marshal of the Court, this to his Excellency the General de Magny, and this you will place in the hands of one of the equerries of his Highness the Hereditary Prince. Wait in the anteroom, and do not go with the parcels until I ring again.”
The black fellow having retired, I turned to Monsieur de Magny and said, “Chevalier, the first packet contains a letter from you to me, declaring your solvency, and solemnly promising payment of the sums you owe me; it is accompanied by a document from myself (for I expected some resistance on your part), stating that my honour has been called in question, and begging that the paper may be laid before your august master his Highness. The second packet is for your grandfather, enclosing the letter from you in which you state yourself to be his heir, and begging for a confirmation of the fact. The last parcel, for his Highness the Hereditary Duke,” added I, looking most sternly, “contains the Gustavus Adolphus emerald, which he gave to his princess, and which you pledged to me as a family jewel of your own. Your influence with her Highness must be great indeed,” I concluded, “when you could extort from her such a jewel as that, and when you could make her, in order to pay your play-debts, give up a secret upon which both your heads depend.”
“Villain!”

===Chinese===

“公主殿下（我说得带点讥诣）并不会怎样动怒，信我：你同那位傻伯爵断了，她恼不到哪儿去。我同你一样不是那位小姐的倾慕者；可我要她的产业。我同你打的就是那份产业，也赢了；到我同‘它’成婚那天，你的期票如数奉还，另加五千达卡特。”
“到‘我’同伯爵夫人成婚那天，”骑士答，想将我一军，“我筹钱还您十遍也筹得齐”（这是实情：伯爵夫人的产业照我们的钱折算，值近五十万）；“那时我自当清偿。这期间，您若再敢拿话吓我，或再像方才那样冒犯我，我就使出您说的那点影响，把您逐出公国——像去年逐出尼德兰那样。”
我静静地摇铃。“扎莫尔，”我对那惯伺候我的、土耳其装束的高个黑奴说，“铃响第二遍，你把这包送给宫廷元帅，这包送给马尼将军大人，这一包，放到太子亲王殿下一位御马监手里。在前厅候着，我不再摇铃不许动。”
黑人退下，我转向马尼先生：“骑士，头一包里，是你给我的信，声明你有清偿能力，郑重许诺归还欠款；随信附我的一纸文书（我料到你会抗拒），声明我的名誉受过质疑，请将此纸呈交你的至尊主人殿下。第二包给你祖父，内附你的信，信里你自称他的继承人，请他予以确认。末一包，给太子殿下，”我说得最严厉，“装的是古斯塔夫·阿道夫绿宝石——他赠给王妃的那颗，你当自家祖传押给我的。你同殿下的情分果然不深，”我收尾道，“深到能逼出这样一件宝贝；深到叫她为付你的赌债，舍得交出一桩秘密——你们两颗脑袋都拴在上面的秘密。”
“恶棍！”

===Original===

said the Frenchman, quite aghast with fury and terror, “would you implicate the Princess?”
“Monsieur de Magny,” I answered, with a sneer, “no: I will say *you stole* the jewel.” It was my belief he did, and that the unhappy and infatuated Princess was never privy to the theft until long after it had been committed. How we came to know the history of the emerald is simple enough. As we wanted money (for my occupation with Magny caused our bank to be much neglected), my uncle had carried Magny’s trinkets to Mannheim to pawn. The Jew who lent upon them knew the history of the stone in question; and when he asked how her Highness came to part with it, my uncle very cleverly took up the story where he found it, said that the Princess was very fond of play, that it was not always convenient to her to pay, and hence the emerald had come into our hands. He brought it wisely back with him to S﻿—﻿—; and, as regards the other jewels which the Chevalier pawned to us, they were of no particular mark: no inquiries have ever been made about them to this day; and I did not only not know then that they came from her Highness, but have only my conjectures upon the matter now.
The unfortunate young gentleman must have had a cowardly spirit, when I charged him with the theft, not to make use of my two pistols that were lying by chance before him, and to send out of the world his accuser and his own ruined self. With such imprudence and miserable recklessness on his part and that of the unhappy lady who had forgotten herself for this poor villain, he must have known that discovery was inevitable. But it was written that this dreadful destiny should be accomplished: instead of ending like a man, he now cowered before me quite spirit-broken, and, flinging himself down on the sofa, burst into tears, calling wildly upon all the saints to help him: as if they could be interested in the fate of such a wretch as he!
I saw that I had nothing to fear from him; and, calling back Zamor my black, said I would myself carry the parcels, which I returned to my escritoire; and, my point being thus gained, I acted, as I always do, generously towards him.

===Chinese===

法国人气得、吓得发怔，“你要把公主也攀进去？”
“马尼先生，”我冷笑答，“不：我只说是‘你偷的’。”我也真信是他偷的；那不幸而昏头的公主，直到事发许久之后，始终不是知情的人。这颗绿宝石的来历，我们怎么知道的，说来简单。我们要钱用（我缠住马尼，赌庄疏于照管），伯父把马尼的小件珠宝捎去曼海姆典押。放款的犹太人认得那颗宝石的来历；问殿下怎么舍得割爱时，伯父顺水推舟，接着那话头编：说公主嗜赌，又往往不便付账，绿宝石便这样到了我们手里。他精细，把原物带了回S——；至于骑士押给我们的其余珠宝，都无特别记认：至今无人查问；我不但当时不知它们出自殿下，如今也不过是有此猜想。
这不幸的年轻人必是天生怯懦：我指他偷窃时，面前恰巧摆着我的两支手枪，他竟不用来把原告连同他自己一并送出尘世，倒叫我诧异。凭他、也凭那位为这可怜恶棍忘了自己的不幸夫人那样昏聩胡来，事发分明只是迟早。可命中注定要这可怕的结局走完：他不曾像男人那样了断，此刻只在我面前筛糠，魂飞魄散，扑倒在长沙发上放声大哭，狂呼众圣徒搭救——仿佛圣徒肯管他这号货色的死活！
我看他吓破了胆，召回落奴扎莫尔，说包裹我自己送，把它们收回进了写字柜；目的既已到手，我对他就照我一贯的做派：宽厚。

===Original===

I said that, for security’s sake, I should send the emerald out of the country, but that I pledged my honour to restore it to the Duchess, without any pecuniary consideration, on the day when she should procure the sovereign’s consent to my union with the Countess Ida.
This will explain pretty clearly, I flatter myself, the game I was playing; and, though some rigid moralist may object to its propriety, I say that anything is fair in love, and that men so poor as myself can’t afford to be squeamish about their means of getting on in life. The great and rich are welcomed, smiling, up the grand staircase of the world; the poor but aspiring must clamber up the wall, or push and struggle up the back stair, or, *pardi*, crawl through any of the conduits of the house, never mind how foul and narrow, that lead to the top. The unambitious sluggard pretends that the eminence is not worth attaining, declines altogether the struggle, and calls himself a philosopher. I say he is a poor-spirited coward. What is life good for but for honour? and that is so indispensable, that we should attain it anyhow.
The manner to be adopted for Magny’s retreat was proposed by myself, and was arranged so as to consult the feelings of delicacy of both parties. I made Magny take the Countess Ida aside, and say to her, “Madam, though I have never declared myself your admirer, you and the Court have had sufficient proof of my regard for you; and my demand would, I know, have been backed by his Highness, your august guardian. I know the Duke’s gracious wish is, that my attentions should be received favourably; but, as time has not appeared to alter your attachment elsewhere, and as I have too much spirit to force a lady of your name and rank to be united to me against your will, the best plan is, that I should make you, for form’s sake, a proposal *un*authorised by his Highness: that you should reply, as I am sorry to think your heart dictates to you, in the negative: on which I also will formally withdraw from my pursuit of you, stating that, after a refusal, nothing, not even the Duke’s desire, should induce me to persist in my suit.”
The Countess Ida almost wept at hearing these words from Monsieur de Magny, and tears came into her eyes, he said, as she took his hand for the first time, and thanked him for the delicacy of the proposal.

===Chinese===

我说：为稳妥起见，绿宝石我要送出国去；但我以名誉担保：她促成君王允准我同伊达伯爵夫人成婚之日，宝石分文不取，壁还公爵夫人。
我这场牌是怎么打的，想来这一下交代得够清楚了；纵有方正的道学先生要挑礼，我说：情场之上无事不可为；穷得像我这样的人，在进身的手段上讲究不得。又富又大的人物，含笑由世界的大梯拾级而上；穷而有志的，只好攀墙上房，或从后楼梯挤上去，要不，妈的，从宅子随便哪条管子里爬上去——管它多脏多窄，通到顶就行。没出息的懒汉假装那高位不值一爬，干脆掠挑子，自称哲人。我说他是个没血性的懦夫。人生在世，不为名为什么？名是如此不可少，就该怎么到手怎么来。
马尼退场的做派由我拟定，安排得兼顾两方的体面。我叫马尼把伊达伯爵夫人拉到一旁，对她说：“夫人，我从不曾自命为您的倾慕者；可您和宫廷都见过了我对您的敬意；我知道，我若求婚，您的至尊监护人必定玉成。我知道公爵慈心，愿您青眼相待；然而岁月既不曾改变您别有所钟，我又岂有血性，强逼您这样名门的女士违心相就。上策是：由我未经殿下许可，向您作一次形式上的求婚；您依着——恕我直言——您心里的答复，回我一个‘不’字；我便随即正式收兵，声明既遭拒绝，天下再无一事，连公爵之命也不例外，能叫我再续此求。”
伊达伯爵夫人听马尼先生说完这番话，几乎垂泪；他说，她头一回伸手给他，眼里含着泪，谢他这提议的体贴。

===Original===

She little knew that the Frenchman was incapable of that sort of delicacy, and that the graceful manner in which he withdrew his addresses was of my invention.
As soon as he withdrew, it became my business to step forward; but cautiously and gently, so as not to alarm the lady, and yet firmly, so as to convince her of the hopelessness of her design of uniting herself with her shabby lover, the sublieutenant. The Princess Olivia was good enough to perform this necessary part of the plan in my favour, and solemnly to warn the Countess Ida, that, though Monsieur de Magny had retired from paying his addresses, his Highness her guardian would still marry her as he thought fit, and that she must forever forget her out-at-elbowed adorer. In fact, I can’t conceive how such a shabby rogue as that could ever have had the audacity to propose for her: his birth was certainly good; but what other qualifications had he?
When the Chevalier de Magny withdrew, numbers of other suitors, you may be sure, presented themselves; and amongst these your very humble servant, the cadet of Ballybarry. There was a carrousel, or tournament, held at this period, in imitation of the antique meetings of chivalry, in which the chevaliers tilted at each other, or at the ring; and on this occasion I was habited in a splendid Roman dress (viz., a silver helmet, a flowing periwig, a cuirass of gilt leather richly embroidered, a light blue velvet mantle, and crimson morocco half-boots): and in this habit I rode my bay horse Brian, carried off three rings, and won the prize over all the Duke’s gentry, and the nobility of surrounding countries who had come to the show. A wreath of gilded laurel was to be the prize of the victor, and it was to be awarded by the lady he selected. So I rode up to the gallery where the Countess Ida was seated behind the Hereditary Princess, and, calling her name loudly, yet gracefully, begged to be allowed to be crowned by her, and thus proclaimed myself to the face of all Germany, as it were, her suitor. She turned very pale, and the Princess red, I observed; but the Countess Ida ended by crowning me: after which, putting spurs into my horse, I galloped round the ring, saluting his Highness the Duke at the opposite end, and performing the most wonderful exercises with my bay.
My success did not, as you may imagine, increase my popularity with the young gentry.

===Chinese===

她哪里知道，那法国人天生没有那路体贴；他那退婚退得这样风雅的做派，全是我的手笔。
他一撤，就轮到我上：却要小心、要软，不惊了小姐；又要坚决，叫她死心：同她那寒酸的少尉情人结合，是休想。奥莉维亚公主赏脸，替我把这必要的一环办了：郑重警告伊达伯爵夫人——马尼先生虽已撤了求婚，殿下监护人仍要把她嫁给他中意的人；她必须永远忘掉她那捉襟见肘的情人。说真的，我想不出那样寒碜的一个流氓，怎么竟有脸向她求婚：门第固然好；可除门第之外，他还有什么？
马尼骑士一撤，其余的求婚人，诸位放心，纷纷登场；其中有您卑微的仆人，巴利巴里的小公子。那阵子办了一场骑士比武会，仿古时的骑士大会：骑士们互相比枪，或刺环。那天我一身华丽的罗马装束（即：银盔、飘洒的假发、绣工繁复的镀金皮胸甲、浅蓝天鹅绒披风、紫红摩洛哥半筒靴），骑着我的枣红马“布赖恩”，连挑三环，压倒公爵满朝的贵宦和四方来看会的邻国贵族，夺了头奖。胜者的彩头是一顶金桂冠，由他选中的女士颁授。于是我驰到看台前——伊达伯爵夫人坐在太子妃身后——朗声而不失风度地唤她的名字，请她准我为她加冕：等于当着全德意志的面，自认了她的求婚者。我看见她白了脸，公主红了脸；末了伊达伯爵夫人还是给我戴了冠；随后我一磕马刺，绕场疾驰，向对面看台的公爵殿下致礼，同我的枣红马耍出一套惊人把戏。
诸位想得到，这份得意并没叫我更受年轻绅士们欢迎。

===Original===

They called me adventurer, bully, dice-loader, impostor, and a hundred pretty names; but I had a way of silencing these gentry. I took the Count de Schmetterling, the richest and bravest of the young men who seemed to have a hankering for the Countess Ida, and publicly insulted him at the ridotto; flinging my cards into his face. The next day I rode thirty-five miles into the territory of the Elector of B﻿—﻿—, and met Monsieur de Schmetterling, and passed my sword twice through his body; then rode back with my second, the Chevalier de Magny, and presented myself at the Duchess’s whist that evening. Magny was very unwilling to accompany me at first; but I insisted upon his support, and that he should countenance my quarrel. Directly after paying my homage to her Highness, I went up to the Countess Ida, and made her a marked and low obeisance, gazing at her steadily in the face until she grew crimson red; and then staring round at every man who formed her circle, until, *ma foi*, I stared them all away. I instructed Magny to say, everywhere, that the Countess was madly in love with me; which commission, along with many others of mine, the poor devil was obliged to perform. He made rather a *sotte figure*, as the French say, acting the pioneer for me, praising me everywhere, accompanying me always! he who had been the pink of the *mode* until my arrival; he who thought his pedigree of beggarly Barons of Magny was superior to the race of great Irish kings from which I descended; who had sneered at me a hundred times as a *spadassin*, a deserter, and had called me a vulgar Irish upstart. Now I had my revenge of the gentleman, and took it too.
I used to call him, in the choicest societies, by his Christian name of Maxime. I would say, “*Bon jour, Maxime; comment vas-*tu*?*” in the Princess’s hearing, and could see him bite his lips for fury and vexation. But I had him under my thumb, and her Highness too﻿—I, poor private of Bülow’s regiment. And this is a proof of what genius and perseverance can do, and should act as a warning to great people never to have *secrets*﻿—if they can help it.
I knew the Princess hated me; but what did I care?

===Chinese===

他们骂我冒险家、恶霸、灌铅骰子、骗子，好听的名字一百个；可我自有叫这班人闭嘴的手段。我挑了施梅特林伯爵——对伊达伯爵夫人似有意思的年轻人里最富、最勇的一个——在化装舞会上当众羞辱：把牌摔在他脸上。次日我驰马三十五英里，进B——选帝侯地界，同施梅特林先生见了面，一剑两剑，两剑穿身；随后带着副手马尼骑士驰回，当晚照旧出现在公爵夫人的惠斯特桌上。马尼起初死活不肯陪我；我硬要他撑腰，要他给我的争执站台。向殿下致敬一毕，我便走到伊达伯爵夫人跟前，行了一个意味深长的深躬，直直盯住她的脸，盯到她绯红；再环眼扫她圈子里的每一个男人，直到，说真的，一个个全叫我瞪跑了。我吩咐马尼到各处放话，说伯爵夫人爱我爱得发疯；这差事，连同我许多别的差事，那可怜鬼都不得不办。他落的那个“傻相”——法国人的说法——替我当开路先锋，处处夸我，时时随行！他，在我来之前的风头少年；他，自以为他那寒酸马尼男爵的谱系，压得过我所出自的那支爱尔兰大王世系；他，一百回讥我“剑客”、骂我逃兵、骂我爱尔兰暴发户。如今轮到我报复这位爷了，我也照报不误。
我在头一等的风雅场合，直唤他的教名马克西姆。当着公主的面我说：“日安，马克西姆；好啊？”看得见他咬嘴唇，气得、恼得。可我把他攦在手心，连殿下也在我手心——我，比洛团的一个穷大兵。这证明天才加恒心能办到什么；也该给大人物们一个警告：秘密，能不沾就不沾。
我知道公主恨我；可我管它？

===Original===

She knew I knew all: and indeed, I believe, so strong was her prejudice against me, that she thought I was an indelicate villain, capable of betraying a lady, which I would scorn to do; so that she trembled before me as a child before its schoolmaster. She would, in her woman’s way, too, make all sorts of jokes and sneers at me on reception days; ask about my palace in Ireland, and the kings my ancestors, and whether, when I was a private in Bülow’s foot, my royal relatives had interposed to rescue me, and whether the cane was smartly administered there﻿—anything to mortify me. But, Heaven bless you! I can make allowances for people, and used to laugh in her face. Whilst her jibes and jeers were continuing, it was my pleasure to look at poor Magny and see how *he* bore them. The poor devil was trembling lest I should break out under the Princess’s sarcasm and tell all; but my revenge was, when the Princess attacked me, to say something bitter to *him*﻿—to pass it on, as boys do at school. And *that* was the thing which used to make her Highness feel. She would wince just as much when I attacked Magny as if I had been saying anything rude to herself. And, though she hated me, she used to beg my pardon in private; and though her pride would often get the better of her, yet her prudence obliged this magnificent princess to humble herself to the poor penniless Irish boy.
As soon as Magny had formally withdrawn from the Countess Ida, the Princess took the young lady into favour again, and pretended to be very fond of her. To do them justice, I don’t know which of the two disliked me most﻿—the Princess, who was all eagerness, and fire, and coquetry; or the Countess, who was all state and splendour. The latter, especially, pretended to be disgusted by me: and yet, after all, I have pleased her betters; was once one of the handsomest men in Europe, and would defy any *heyduc* of the Court to measure a chest or a leg with me: but I did not care for any of her silly prejudices, and determined to win her and wear her in spite of herself. Was it on account of her personal charms or qualities? No. She was quite white, thin, shortsighted, tall, and awkward, and my taste is quite the contrary; and as for her mind, no wonder that a poor creature who had a hankering after a wretched ragged ensign could never appreciate *me*.

===Chinese===

她知道我全知道；而且我相信，她对我的偏见那样深，深到把我当成肯出卖一位女士的下作恶棍——那是我屑也不屑的——于是她在我面前发抖，像学童见先生。她照她的女人路数，也在接见日拿我打趣挖苦：问起我在爱尔兰的宫殿、我的列祖列王；问我在比洛步兵团当列兵时，我的王亲国戚可曾出面捞我；问那里的鞭子抽得痛快不痛快——只要能臊我就行。可是老天保佑！我体谅人，惯当着她的面笑。她冷嘲热讽正酣时，我的乐趣是看可怜的马尼怎么受：那可怜鬼抖着，只怕我受不住公主的讥诣发作起来，和盘托出；可我的报复，是每逢公主攻我，就冲“他”来一句狠的——像学堂里孩子们那样，把条子传下去。这一下才真扎得着殿下：我戳马尼时，她抽的那个缩，同戳了她自己一般。她虽恨我，却常私下赔我的不是；傲气虽常占上风，审慎到底逼得这位煊赫的公主，向爱尔兰穷小子低头。
马尼从伊达伯爵夫人跟前正式一撤，公主重新把小姐宠了起来，装得十分疼她。凭良心说，两人谁更恨我，我断不清——是那位浑身烈火、卖弄、急性子的公主，还是那位一身架子的伯爵夫人。后者尤其装出厌恶我；可是说到底，比我强的人我也中过意；我也做过全欧洲数得着的美男子；论胸围腿围，宫廷里随便哪个黑衣卫同我比比看；她那点蠢偏见我不放在心上，拿定主意：她愿不愿，我都要赢她、娶她。为的是她的人品姿色么？不。她白白净净、瘦瘦长长、近视、笨手笨脚，同我的口味正相反；至于头脑，一个惦着破衣烂衫小旗手的可怜虫，赏识不了“我”，不足为奇。

===Original===

It was her estate I made love to; as for herself, it would be a reflection on my taste as a man of fashion to own that I liked her.

===Chinese===

我求爱的是她的产业；至于她本人，我这个时兴人物若认了喜欢她，倒是有失我的品味。

## XVIII My Good Fortune Begins to Waver / XVIII 我的好运开始动摇
===Original===

And now, if any people should be disposed to think my history immoral (for I have heard some assert that I was a man who never deserved that so much prosperity should fall to my share), I will beg those cavillers to do me the favour to read the conclusion of my adventures; when they will see it was no such great prize that I had won, and that wealth, splendour, thirty thousand per annum, and a seat in Parliament, are often purchased at too dear a rate, when one has to buy those enjoyments at the price of personal liberty, and saddled with the charge of a troublesome wife.
They are the deuce, these troublesome wives, and that is the truth. No man knows until he tries how wearisome and disheartening the burden of one of them is, and how the annoyance grows and strengthens from year to year, and the courage becomes weaker to bear it; so that that trouble which seemed light and trivial the first year, becomes intolerable ten years after. I have heard of one of the classical fellows in the dictionary who began by carrying a calf up a hill every day, and so continued until the animal grew to be a bull, which he still easily accommodated upon his shoulders; but take my word for it, young unmarried gentlemen, a wife is a very much harder pack to the back than the biggest heifer in Smithfield and, if I can prevent one of you from marrying, the *Memoirs of Barry Lyndon, Esq.* will not be written in vain. Not that my Lady was a scold or a shrew, as some wives are; I could have managed to have cured her of that; but she was of a cowardly, crying, melancholy, maudlin temper, which is to me still more odious: do what one would to please her, she would never be happy or in good-humour. I left her alone after a while; and because, as was natural in my case, where a disagreeable home obliged me to seek amusement and companions abroad, she added a mean detestable jealousy to all her other faults: I could not for some time pay the commonest attention to any other woman, but my Lady Lyndon must weep, and wring her hands, and threaten to commit suicide, and I know not what.

===Chinese===

如今，倘若有人觉得我这段往事不道德（我确实听人断言过，说我这人根本不配享有落到头上的这许多富贵），我倒要请那些挑剔的人行个好，把我这段奇遇的结局读完；到那时他们自会看到，我赢到手的原不是什么了不得的大奖；财富、排场、一年三万镑进项，外加一个议会席位，若是得拿人身自由作价去换，还外带倒贴一位难缠的太太，这笔买卖往往是吃了大亏的。
这些难缠的太太真是活见鬼，这是实在话。不亲身领教过，哪个男人也不知道背上这么一副担子有多磨人、多叫人丧气，这桩烦恼如何一年年滋长、加重，承受它的勇气却一年年减弱；于是头一年看着轻如鸿毛的麻烦，十年之后便教人忍无可忍。我听说字典里有位古典人物，起初每天扛一头牛犊上山，日日不辍，直到那畜生长成公牛，他照样能轻轻松松扛上肩头；可是请相信我，年轻的单身绅士们，一副太太的担子，比史密斯菲尔德市场上顶大的母牛还要难背得多；倘若我能拦下你们中间哪一位不去结婚，这部《巴里·林登绅士回忆录》（*Memoirs of Barry Lyndon, Esq.*）便不算白写。倒不是我那位夫人像有些太太那样撒泼耍横——那个毛病我自有法子治好她；她是那种胆小、爱哭、忧郁、动辄抹泪的性子，这在我眼里还要可厌得多：任凭你千方百计讨她欢心，她从来不肯快活，也不肯有个好脸色。过了一阵我就由她去了；于是——这在我那样的处境原是自然不过——家里无趣，逼得我出门寻欢觅伴，她又给自己的一切毛病添上一条卑鄙可憎的嫉妒：有一阵子，我哪怕对别的女人致以顶寻常的殷勤，林登伯爵夫人便必定落泪、绞手，扬言要寻短见，还有什么什么，不一而足。

===Original===

Her death would have been no comfort to me, as I leave any person of common prudence to imagine; for that scoundrel of a young Bullingdon (who was now growing up a tall, gawky, swarthy lad, and about to become my greatest plague and annoyance) would have inherited every penny of the property, and I should have been left considerably poorer even than when I married the widow: for I spent my personal fortune as well as the lady’s income in the keeping up of our rank, and was always too much a man of honour and spirit to save a penny of Lady Lyndon’s income. Let this be flung in the teeth of my detractors, who say I never could have so injured the Lyndon property had I not been making a private purse for myself; and who believe that, even in my present painful situation, I have hoards of gold laid by somewhere, and could come out as a Croesus when I choose. I never raised a shilling upon Lady Lyndon’s property but I spent it like a man of honour; besides incurring numberless personal obligations for money, which all went to the common stock. Independent of the Lyndon mortgages and incumbrances, I owe myself at least one hundred and twenty thousand pounds, which I spent while in occupancy of my wife’s estate; so that I may justly say that property is indebted to me in the above-mentioned sum.
Although I have described the utter disgust and distaste which speedily took possession of my breast as regarded Lady Lyndon; and although I took no particular pains (for I am all frankness and aboveboard) to disguise my feelings in general, yet she was of such a mean spirit, that she pursued me with her regard in spite of my indifference to her, and would kindle up at the smallest kind word I spoke to her. The fact is, between my respected reader and myself, that I was one of the handsomest and most dashing young men of England in those days, and my wife was violently in love with me; and though I say it who shouldn’t, as the phrase goes, my wife was not the only woman of rank in London who had a favourable opinion of the humble Irish adventurer. What a riddle these women are, I have often thought! I have seen the most elegant creatures at St. James’s grow wild for love of the coarsest and most vulgar of men; the cleverest women passionately admire the most illiterate of our sex, and so on.

===Chinese===

她若死了，于我也无半分安慰——这个道理，随便哪个有寻常头脑的人都想得到；因为那个小恶棍布林登（他如今已出落成个高挑笨拙、面色黝黑的小子，眼看要成为我头顶最大的祸害与冤家）就会把家产一便士不剩地承继去，我的境况会比当初娶这位寡妇时反倒还穷上一大截：因为撑持我们两家的门第排场，我把自己的私产连同太太的进项一齐花了个精光，何况我向来是个太讲荣誉、太有气性的男子汉，断不肯从林登伯爵夫人的进项里省下一个便士。请把这句话摔到那些诋毁我的人脸上去——他们说我若不是暗地里往自家腰包搂钱，绝败坏不了林登家的产业到这个地步；他们还认定，哪怕我落到眼下这步痛苦的田地，也还在什么地方窖着成堆的金子，只消我愿意，随时能像克洛伊索斯（Croesus）一般阔气登场。我在林登伯爵夫人的产业上每筹得一先令，无不以荣誉之士的做派花将出去；此外还以个人名义欠下数不清的银钱债务，笔笔归入公共家计。不算林登家的抵押与种种负担，我至少还欠着自己十二万镑——那是住着太太产业期间花出去的；因此我尽可以说：那份产业反倒欠下我上述这笔款子。
虽说我已写过，对林登伯爵夫人，我的胸中不久便生出了彻底的厌恶与腻烦；虽说我也没特意花功夫（我这人一向坦白，行事光明）掩饰自己的情绪，可她的气性竟卑屈到这般地步：任我待她冷淡，她偏拿一片痴情缠定我，我对她讲一句顶细小的温存话，她便顿时眉开眼笑。实情是——这话只在我敬爱的读者与我二人之间讲——当年在英格兰，我也算得最英俊、最神气的年轻人之一，我太太爱我爱得如痴如狂；俗语说“自夸者不齿”，可太太并不是伦敦城里唯一对这卑微的爱尔兰冒险家青眼有加的名门贵妇。这些女人真是难解的谜，我时常这样想！我见过圣詹姆斯最风雅的名媛，为了顶粗俗、顶下流的男人爱得发狂；顶聪明的女人，死心塌地倾慕我们男性中顶目不识丁的那一等，如此等等，不一而足。

===Original===

There is no end to the contrariety in the foolish creatures; and though I don’t mean to hint that *I* am vulgar or illiterate, as the persons mentioned above (I would cut the throat of any man who dared to whisper a word against my birth or my breeding), yet I have shown that Lady Lyndon had plenty of reason to dislike me if she chose: but, like the rest of her silly sex, she was governed by infatuation, not reason; and, up to the very last day of our being together, would be reconciled to me, and fondle me, if I addressed her a single kind word.
“Ah,” she would say, in these moments of tenderness﻿—“Ah, *Redmond*, if you would always be so!” And in these fits of love she was the most easy creature in the world to be persuaded, and would have signed away her whole property, had it been possible. And, I must confess, it was with very little attention on my part that I could bring her into good-humour. To walk with her on the Mall, or at Ranelagh, to attend her to church at St. James’s, to purchase any little present or trinket for her, was enough to coax her. Such is female inconsistency! The next day she would be calling me “Mr. Barry” probably, and be bemoaning her miserable fate that she ever should have been united to such a monster. So it was she was pleased to call one of the most brilliant men in His Majesty’s three kingdoms: and I warrant me *other* ladies had a much more flattering opinion of me.
Then she would threaten to leave me; but I had a hold of her in the person of her son, of whom she was passionately fond: I don’t know why, for she had always neglected Bullingdon her older son, and never bestowed a thought upon his health, his welfare, or his education.
It was our young boy, then, who formed the great bond of union between me and her Ladyship; and there was no plan of ambition I could propose in which she would not join for the poor lad’s behoof, and no expense she would not eagerly incur, if it might by any means be shown to tend to his advancement. I can tell you, bribes were administered, and in high places too﻿—so near the royal person of His Majesty, that you would be astonished were I to mention what great personages condescended to receive our loans.

===Chinese===

这些蠢物心里的别扭没有个了局；我倒并不打算暗示，*我*本人便像上文提到的那种人一样粗俗无学（谁敢对我的出身或教养嘀咕半个字，我就割谁的喉咙），况且我也已表明，林登伯爵夫人若要厌弃我，理由有的是：可她同她那性子的蠢货们一样，行事凭痴迷，不凭理智；直到我们俩相守的最后一天，只要我对她讲一句好话，她便肯同我和好，还要来温存我。
“唉，”逢着这种软语温存的时刻，她便说——“唉，*雷德蒙德*（Redmond），你要是永远这样多好！”在这种情热发作的当口，她是天下顶容易劝转的人，只消办得到，她肯把整份家产一纸签尽。而且我必须承认，我费不了多少殷勤，就能哄得她回嗔作喜。陪她在马尔步道（the Mall）散散步，或是去拉尼拉格（Ranelagh），陪她上圣詹姆斯的教堂做礼拜，替她买件小礼物、小玩意儿，就尽够哄她了。女人家的反复无常竟至于此！到了第二天，她说不定又“巴里先生”长“巴里先生”短地叫起来，哀叹自己命苦，怎么嫁了这么一个怪物。陛下三王国内数一数二的显赫人物，她就爱这样称呼；我敢担保，*旁的*太太们对我的品评，可要奉承得多。
她还会扬言要离开我；可我手里攥着一张牌——她的儿子，她对那孩子疼得要命：缘由我实在不明白，因为对她的大儿子布林登，她向来冷落，从没把他的身子、他的前程、他的教养放在心上过。
于是，正是我们的小儿子，成了维系我同伯爵夫人的大纽带；凡我提出的功名大计，没有一件她不肯为那可怜的孩子出力同谋；只要多少可以证明有助于孩子的高升，没有一笔开销她不踊跃承当。我可以告诉诸位，贿赂是送出去了，而且送进了高位——离陛下御体那么近，倘若我说出有哪些大人物屈尊收纳了我们的借贷，你们准要吃惊。

===Original===

I got from the English and Irish heralds a description and detailed pedigree of the Barony of Barryogue, and claimed respectfully to be reinstated in my ancestral titles, and also to be rewarded with the Viscounty of Ballybarry. “This head would become a coronet,” my Lady would sometimes say, in her fond moments, smoothing down my hair; and, indeed, there is many a puny whipster in their Lordships’ house who has neither my presence nor my courage, my pedigree, nor any of my merits.
The striving after this peerage I considered to have been one of the most unlucky of all my unlucky dealings at this period. I made unheard-of sacrifices to bring it about. I lavished money here and diamonds there. I bought lands at ten times their value; purchased pictures and articles of *vertu* at ruinous prices. I gave repeated entertainments to those friends to my claims who, being about the Royal person, were likely to advance it. I lost many a bet to the Royal Dukes His Majesty’s brothers; but let these matters be forgotten, and, because of my private injuries, let me not be deficient in loyalty to my Sovereign.
The only person in this transaction whom I shall mention openly, is that old scamp and swindler, Gustavus Adolphus, thirteenth Earl of Crabs. This nobleman was one of the gentlemen of His Majesty’s closet, and one with whom the revered monarch was on terms of considerable intimacy. A close regard had sprung up between them in the old King’s time; when His Royal Highness, playing at battledore and shuttlecock with the young lord on the landing-place of the great staircase at Kew, in some moment of irritation the Prince of Wales kicked the young Earl downstairs, who, falling, broke his leg. The Prince’s hearty repentance for his violence caused him to ally himself closely with the person whom he had injured; and when His Majesty came to the throne there was no man, it is said, of whom the Earl of Bute was so jealous as of my Lord Crabs. The latter was poor and extravagant, and Bute got him out of the way, by sending him on the Russian and other embassies; but on this favourite’s dismissal, Crabs sped back from the Continent, and was appointed almost immediately to a place about His Majesty’s person.

===Chinese===

我从英格兰和爱尔兰的纹章院弄到了巴里奥格男爵领的记述与详细谱牒，恭恭敬敬地申请恢复我祖上的封号，并求赏巴利巴里子爵之位。“这颗脑袋，正配一顶宝冕呢，”伯爵夫人情热的时候时常这样说，一面替我抚平头发；说实在的，他们上议院老爷们的座中，多少孱弱的小白脸，论仪表、论胆气、论谱牒，论我哪一样长处，没有一样拿得出来。
如今回想，为这爵位奔走一事，是我这一时期全部背运营生里顶背运的一桩。为促成此事，我作出的牺牲闻所未闻。我在这里撒钱，在那里撒钻石。我以十倍的价钱买地；以倾家荡产的价格购进画作与古玩（*vertu*）。我给那些支持我申领、又在御前当差、可能替我出力的朋友们，一而再、再而三地设宴；对陛下御弟那几位御封公爵，我故意输掉了多少赌注；只是这些且撇开——纵然我一身是伤，也断不能因此亏了对君王的忠心。
这桩交易里，我唯一肯公开点名的，是那个老无赖、老骗子——克拉布斯第十三代伯爵，古斯塔夫斯·阿道弗斯。这位贵人身列陛下御前近侍，同那位可敬的君王过从甚密。这份情分起于老王在位年间：当年殿下在邱园大楼梯的平台上同这位少年贵人打板羽球，一时性起，把年轻的伯爵一脚踹下楼去，摔折了一条腿。亲王对自家的粗暴衷心悔过，从此同这位被他伤害的人结得极紧；及至陛下登基，据说满朝上下，布特伯爵顶忌的便是克拉布斯勋爵。后者又穷又挥霍，布特便把他远远打发出去，派往俄国等国充当使节，眼不见为净；可等这位宠臣一失势，克拉布斯立刻从欧陆飞奔而回，几乎转眼之间，便又得了御前的差事。

===Original===

It was with this disreputable nobleman that I contracted an unluckly intimacy; when, fresh and unsuspecting, I first established myself in town, after my marriage with Lady Lyndon: and, as Crabs was really one of the most entertaining fellows in the world, I took a sincere pleasure in his company; besides the interesting desire I had in cultivating the society of a man who was so near the person of the highest personage in the realm.
To hear the fellow, you would fancy that there was scarce any appointment made in which he had not a share. He told me, for instance, of Charles Fox being turned out of his place a day before poor Charley himself was aware of the fact. He told me when the Howes were coming back from America, and who was to succeed to the command there. Not to multiply instances, it was upon this person that I fixed my chief reliance for the advancement of my claim to the Barony of Barryogue and the Viscounty which I proposed to get.
One of the main causes of expense which this ambition of mine entailed upon me was the fitting out and arming a company of infantry from the Castle Lyndon and Hackton estates in Ireland, which I offered to my gracious Sovereign for the campaign against the American rebels. These troops, superbly equipped and clothed, were embarked at Portsmouth in the year 1778; and the patriotism of the gentleman who had raised them was so acceptable at Court, that, on being presented by my Lord North, His Majesty condescended to notice me particularly, and said, “That’s right, Mr. Lyndon, raise another company; and go with them, too!” But this was by no means, as the reader may suppose, to my notions. A man with thirty thousand pounds per annum is a fool to risk his life like a common beggar: and on this account I have always admired the conduct of my friend Jack Bolter, who had been a most active and resolute cornet of horse, and, as such, engaged in every scrape and skirmish which could fall to his lot; but just before the battle of Minden he received news that his uncle, the great army contractor, was dead, and had left him five thousand per annum.

===Chinese===

同这位声名狼藉的贵人结下的这段倒霉交情，是在我初娶林登伯爵夫人、新新鲜鲜、毫不设防、刚在京城安顿下来的时候；况且克拉布斯实在是天下顶有趣的人物之一，与他做伴，我自有真心实意的快乐；更何况还别有一层妙处——结交一位离王国头号人物如此贴近的人物。
听这家伙讲话，你会以为天下几乎没有一件官职任命里没有他的份。比如查尔斯·福克斯（Charles Fox）被罢官，他头一天就漏了信，可怜的查利本人过了一日才知晓实情。豪氏兄弟几时从美洲回来，那边军权归谁接掌，他都先说与我听。实例不必多举——总之，为着我巴里奥格男爵的申领和我志在必得的那个子爵位，我主要的指望，就押在了这个人身上。
我的这份雄心带来的主要开销之一，便是从爱尔兰的林登城堡与哈克顿两处田庄招募、装备一连步兵，献与我仁慈的君王，用于征讨美洲叛军。这队兵马衣甲鲜明，装备精良，于1778年在朴茨茅斯登船出征；这位募兵绅士的一腔爱国热忱在宫中如此讨喜，以致经诺思勋爵引见时，陛下竟屈尊特地垂顾于我，说道：“好啊，林登先生，再募一连；索性自己也跟了去！”可是，读者可想而知，这正正不合我的主意。一年三万镑进项的人，犯不上像个寻常叫花子似的去搏命：为此我一向佩服我朋友杰克·博尔特（Jack Bolter）的行事——他原是骑兵里顶顶活跃、顶顶果决的旗手，但凡轮得上的乱子与小接仗，无有不赶；可就在明登（Minden）大战之前，他得了信：他的叔父、那位大军承包商死了，给他留下一年五千镑。

===Original===

Jack that instant applied for leave; and, as it was refused him on the eve of a general action, my gentleman took it, and never fired a pistol again: except against an officer who questioned his courage, and whom he winged in such a cool and determined manner, as showed all the world that it was from prudence and a desire of enjoying his money, not from cowardice, that he quitted the profession of arms.
When this Hackton company was raised, my stepson, who was now sixteen years of age, was most eager to be allowed to join it, and I would have gladly consented to have been rid of the young man; but his guardian, Lord Tiptoff, who thwarted me in everything, refused his permission, and the lad’s military inclinations were balked. If he could have gone on the expedition, and a rebel rifle had put an end to him, I believe, to tell the truth, I should not have been grieved overmuch; and I should have had the pleasure of seeing my other son the heir to the estate which his father had won with so much pains.
The education of this young nobleman had been, I confess, some of the loosest; and perhaps the truth is, I *did* neglect the brat. He was of so wild, savage, and insubordinate a nature, that I never had the least regard for him; and before me and his mother, at least, was so moody and dull, that I thought instruction thrown away upon him, and left him for the most part to shift for himself. For two whole years he remained in Ireland away from us; and when in England, we kept him mainly at Hackton, never caring to have the uncouth ungainly lad in the genteel company in the capital in which we naturally mingled. My own poor boy, on the contrary, was the most polite and engaging child ever seen: it was a pleasure to treat him with kindness and distinction; and before he was five years old, the little fellow was the pink of fashion, beauty, and good breeding.
In fact he could not have been otherwise, with the care both his parents bestowed upon him, and the attentions that were lavished upon him in every way. When he was four years old, I quarrelled with the English nurse who had attended upon him, and about whom my wife had been so jealous, and procured for him a French gouvernante, who had lived with families of the first quality in Paris; and who, of course, must set my Lady Lyndon jealous too.

===Chinese===

杰克当下便告假；大战前夕假没批下来，我这位爷干脆自作主张走了，从此再没开过一枪——只有一回例外：一位军官疑他的胆量，他冷静果决地一枪打中对方臂膀，叫全世界看清了：他脱离军旅，是出于审慎，是想安享他的钱财，绝非出于怯懦。
当初募集哈克顿连队时，我的继子——他那年十六岁——恳切求我准他入队，我巴不得甩脱这位年轻人，本想一口应承；可他的监护人蒂普托夫勋爵——此人凡事跟我作对——偏不点头，这少年的从军志向便落了空。说句实话，倘若他当真随军出征，叫一支叛军步枪结果了性命，我想我也不会怎样悲伤；我还能得一份快意，眼看我的另一个儿子，承继他父亲千辛万苦挣下的那份家业。
这位少年贵人的教养，我承认，荒疏得可以；或许实情就是：我*确实*怠慢了那小崽子。他的天性狂野、蛮横、不服管束，我对他从来没有过一丝怜爱；至少在我和他母亲面前，他永远那般阴沉呆滞，我以为教他也是白费，便多半由他自谋生路。他足足两年待在爱尔兰，不与我们同住；在英格兰时，我们大抵把他搁在哈克顿，从不想带这个粗手笨脚的小子进京城的上流圈子——那圈子我们自然混迹其中。反观我自己可怜的娃娃，那是世上顶懂礼、顶讨人欢喜的孩子：疼他、抬举他，本身就是一桩乐事；不到五岁，这小人儿已是时髦、俊美、好教养的头一份。
其实他也不可能是别的样子——双亲这样经心，方方面面又这样娇宠。他四岁那年，我同照看他的英国奶妈吵翻了——我太太为这奶妈吃过好一阵干醋——给他换了位法国家庭女教师，巴黎头等人家出身；这一位呢，不用说，又必定惹得林登伯爵夫人醋意大发。

===Original===

Under the care of this young woman my little rogue learned to chatter French most charmingly. It would have done your heart good to hear the dear rascal swear *Mort de ma vie!* and to see him stamp his little foot, and send the *manants* and canaille of the domestics to the *trente mille diables*. He was precocious in all things: at a very early age he would mimic everybody; at five, he would sit at table, and drink his glass of champagne with the best of us; and his nurse would teach him little French catches, and the last Parisian songs of Vadé and Collard﻿—pretty songs they were too; and would make such of his hearers as understood French burst with laughing, and, I promise you, scandalise some of the old dowagers who were admitted into the society of his mamma: not that there were many of them; for I did not encourage the visits of what you call respectable people to Lady Lyndon. They are sad spoilers of sport﻿—talebearers, envious narrow-minded people; making mischief between man and wife. Whenever any of these grave personages in hoops and high heels used to make their appearance at Hackton, or in Berkeley Square, it was my chief pleasure to frighten them off; and I would make my little Bryan dance, sing, and play the diable a quatre, and aid him myself, so as to scare the old frumps.
I never shall forget the solemn remonstrances of our old square-toes of a rector at Hackton, who made one or two vain attempts to teach little Bryan Latin, and with whose innumerable children I sometimes allowed the boy to associate. They learned some of Bryan’s French songs from him, which their mother, a poor soul who understood pickles and custards much better than French, used fondly to encourage them in singing; but which their father one day hearing, he sent Miss Sarah to her bedroom and bread and water for a week, and solemnly horsed Master Jacob in the presence of all his brothers and sisters, and of Bryan, to whom he hoped that flogging would act as a warning. But my little rogue kicked and plunged at the old parson’s shins until he was obliged to get his sexton to hold him down, and swore, *corbleu*, *morbleu*, *ventrebleu*, that his young friend Jacob should not be maltreated.

===Chinese===

在这位年轻女子的调教下，我的小淘气学会了一口顶讨人喜欢的法国话。听那亲爱的小捣蛋赌咒“Mort de ma vie!”，看他跺着小脚，把宅子里下人中的*manants*（贱骨头）、canaille（臭东西）一概打发到“trente mille diables”（三万魔鬼）那里去，真叫人心里舒坦。他样样早慧：年纪顶小就见谁学谁；五岁上便能入席，同我们这班好手一道干他的香槟；他的女教师教他些法国小曲，还有瓦代与科拉尔顶时新的巴黎歌谣——歌儿也真好听；听得懂法文的客人无不绝倒，我还可担保，这准要气煞几位蒙她母亲接纳的老太君——其实也没有几位；因为你们所谓的体面人物来拜望林登伯爵夫人，我从不鼓励。那种人是头一号败兴的行家——搬弄口舌，妒忌量窄，专在小两口中间搬是弄非。每逢这些裙撑高跟、道貌岸然的大人物在哈克顿或伯克利广场（Berkeley Square）露面，我顶大的乐趣便是把他们吓跑；我便让我家小布莱恩又唱又跳、大闹天宫，我亲自一旁助阵，好吓退那些老古板。

===Original===

After this scene, his reverence forbade Bryan the rectory-house; on which I swore that his eldest son, who was bringing up for the ministry, should never have the succession of the living of Hackton, which I had thoughts of bestowing on him; and his father said, with a canting hypocritical air, which I hate, that Heaven’s will must be done; that he would not have his children disobedient or corrupted for the sake of a bishopric, and wrote me a pompous and solemn letter, charged with Latin quotations, taking farewell of me and my house. “I do so with regret,” added the old gentleman, “for I have received so many kindnesses from the Hackton family that it goes to my heart to be disunited from them. My poor, I fear, may suffer in consequence of my separation from you, and my being henceforward unable to bring to your notice instances of distress and affliction; which, when they were known to you, I will do you the justice to say, your generosity was always prompt to relieve.”
There may have been some truth in this, for the old gentleman was perpetually pestering me with petitions, and I know for a certainty, from his own charities, was often without a shilling in his pocket; but I suspect the good dinners at Hackton had a considerable share in causing his regrets at the dissolution of our intimacy: and I know that his wife was quite sorry to forego the acquaintance of Bryan’s gouvernante, Mademoiselle Louison, who had all the newest French fashions at her fingers’ ends, and who never went to the rectory but you would see the girls of the family turn out in new sacks or mantles the Sunday after.
I used to punish the old rebel by snoring very loud in my pew on Sundays during sermon-time; and I got a governor presently for Bryan, and a chaplain of my own, when he became of age sufficient to be separated from the women’s society and guardianship. His English nurse I married to my head gardener, with a handsome portion; his French gouvernante I bestowed upon my faithful German Fritz, not forgetting the dowry in the latter instance; and they set up a French dining-house in Soho, and I believe at the time I write they are richer in the world’s goods than their generous and freehanded master.
For Bryan I now got a young gentleman from Oxford, the Rev.

===Chinese===

这场风波过后，牧师大人不许布莱恩再进牧师宅；我闻言便赌咒说，他那正攻神学、预备领圣职的长子，从此休想承继哈克顿的圣俸——这本是我有意赏他的。他父亲于是摆出一副假仁假义的嘴脸——我最恨这副腔调——说天意总要成全；说他断不肯为着一个主教区，教坏儿女、纵容忤逆；接着给我来了一封信，满纸拉丁引文，又堂皇、又庄严，同我、同我一家就此作别。“我是怀着遗憾这样做的，”老先生又添一句，“因为哈克顿府上待我的种种恩义，如今要断了往来，实在痛心。我手下的穷人们，恐怕也要因我与您分离而受累——往后再不能把种种困苦患难呈到您眼前；而凡经我知道呈到您眼前的，我必须凭良心说一句：您的慷慨，素来解囊最是不迟疑。”
这话或许有几分真——老先生确实不断拿请赈单来缠磨我，而且我确切知道，他自己行起善来，常常落得口袋里不剩一个先令；只是我疑心，哈克顿的精美晚餐，在他惋惜这番交情断绝一事里，占了不小的分量：我还知道，他的太太对于从此失掉布莱恩那位女教师路易松小姐（Mademoiselle Louison）的交情，是十二分伤心的——这位小姐满肚子顶时新的法国时样，她每回进牧师宅，不出礼拜天，必见那家的姑娘们一色新式长衣、新式斗篷穿将出来。
我惯于在礼拜天讲道的时辰，坐在自家包厢里高声打呼噜，以此惩罚这个老忤逆；不久我又给布莱恩请了位师保，还请了自家的专职教士——他年纪到了，可以从妇道人家的陪伴看管中分出来了。他的英国奶妈，我配给了我的大园丁，陪送了一份体面妆奁；他的法国家庭教师，我赏给我那位忠心的德国人弗里茨（Fritz），这一份陪嫁也照样没短；两人在索霍（Soho）开了一家法国餐馆，我想，在我落笔的此时，论世上的家当，他们怕是比他们那位慷慨疏财的主子还要阔绰了。
至于布莱恩，我这时又从牛津延请了一位年轻绅士、牧师——

===Original===

Edmund Lavender, who was commissioned to teach him Latin, when the boy was in the humour, and to ground him in history, grammar, and the other qualifications of a gentleman. Lavender was a precious addition to our society at Hackton. He was the means of making a deal of fun there. He was the butt of all our jokes, and bore them with the most admirable and martyrlike patience. He was one of that sort of men who would rather be kicked by a great man than not be noticed by him; and I have often put his wig into the fire in the face of the company, when he would laugh at the joke as well as any man there. It was a delight to put him on a high-mettled horse, and send him after the hounds﻿—pale, sweating, calling on us, for Heaven’s sake, to stop, and holding on for dear life by the mane and the crupper. How it happened that the fellow was never killed I know not; but I suppose hanging is the way in which *his* neck will be broke. He never met with any accident, to speak of, in our hunting-matches: but you were pretty sure to find him at dinner in his place at the bottom of the table making the punch, whence he would be carried off fuddled to bed before the night was over. Many a time have Bryan and I painted his face black on those occasions. We put him into a haunted room, and frightened his soul out of his body with ghosts; we let loose cargoes of rats upon his bed; we cried fire, and filled his boots with water; we cut the legs of his preaching-chair, and filled his sermon-book with snuff. Poor Lavender bore it all with patience; and at our parties, or when we came to London, was amply repaid by being allowed to sit with the gentlefolks, and to fancy himself in the society of men of fashion. It was good to hear the contempt with which he talked about our rector. “He has a son, sir, who is a servitor: and a servitor at a small college,” he would say. “How *could* you, my dear sir, think of giving the reversion of Hackton to such a lowbred creature?”
I should now speak of my other son, at least my Lady Lyndon’s: I mean the Viscount Bullingdon. I kept him in Ireland for some years, under the guardianship of my mother, whom I had installed at Castle Lyndon; and great, I promise you, was her state in that occupation, and prodigious the good soul’s splendour and haughty bearing.

===Chinese===

埃德蒙·拉文德（Edmund Lavender）。这位受聘在孩子有兴致的时辰教他拉丁文，并替他打好历史、文法以及绅士其他各项学问的底子。拉文德是我们哈克顿交际场中一件难得的活宝。有了他，笑话便少不了。他是我们全部戏谑的靶子，而承受这些，他的耐性好得可敬，赛过殉道者。他是那一类人：宁可挨大人物一脚，也不肯受大人物冷落。多少回，我当着满座宾客把他的假发丢进火里，他哈哈大笑，笑得同在场任何一位一样欢。把他扶上一匹烈马，撵他去追猎犬——看他面无人色、浑身大汗，口口声声求老天爷看在份上叫我们停下，两只手死命揪住马鬃、攥住鞍后韂——真是一大乐事。这家伙怎么竟没有摔死，我至今不解；我只料想，*他*那根脖颈子，将来的了局多半在绞架上。在我们的狩猎会上，倒也没出过什么说得上的事故：只是每到晚饭，你准能在桌尾他的老位子上找到他，正张罗着调潘趣酒；不等夜尽，便被人醉醺醺地扛上床去。逢这种场合，布莱恩和我没少把他的脸涂黑。我们把他关进一间闹鬼的屋子，拿鬼魂吓得他魂灵出窍；我们放开一舱一舱的老鼠，倾倒在他床上；我们喊失火，把他的靴子灌满水；我们锯短他讲道椅的腿，往他的讲道书里塞满鼻烟。可怜的拉文德忍受了这一切，耐心十足；而一到我们的宴会，或是随我们进京，准许他同老爷太太们同席，容他自命混在时髦人物中间，他便算得了丰厚的报偿。听他议论我们那位教区长时那股鄙夷的口气，真是有趣。“他有位公子，先生，在做侍读生——还是在一所不入流的小学院里做侍读生，”他常说。“亲爱的先生，您*怎么*能想着把哈克顿的圣俸传给这样一介下品？”
如今该讲讲我的另一个儿子了——至少是林登伯爵夫人的儿子：我说的便是布林登子爵。有几年我把他放在爱尔兰，托我母亲管教；我已把她老人家安置在林登城堡。我敢说，她老人家在那份任上好不威风，这位好心肠太太的排场之阔、架子之大，叹为观止。

===Original===

With all her oddities, the Castle Lyndon estate was the best managed of all our possessions; the rents were excellently paid, the charges of getting them in smaller than they would have been under the management of any steward. It was astonishing what small expenses the good widow incurred; although she kept up the dignity of the *two* families, as she would say. She had a set of domestics to attend upon the young lord; she never went out herself but in an old gilt coach and six; the house was kept clean and tight; the furniture and gardens in the best repair; and, in our occasional visits to Ireland, we never found any house we visited in such good condition as our own. There were a score of ready serving-lasses, and half as many trim men about the castle; and everything in as fine condition as the best housekeeper could make it. All this she did with scarcely any charges to us: for she fed sheep and cattle in the parks, and made a handsome profit of them at Ballinasloe; she supplied I don’t know how many towns with butter and bacon; and the fruit and vegetables from the gardens of Castle Lyndon got the highest prices in Dublin market. She had no waste in the kitchen, as there used to be in most of our Irish houses; and there was no consumption of liquor in the cellars, for the old lady drank water, and saw little or no company. All her society was a couple of the girls of my ancient flame Nora Brady, now Mrs. Quin; who with her husband had spent almost all their property, and who came to see me once in London, looking very old, fat, and slatternly, with two dirty children at her side. She wept very much when she saw me, called me “Sir,” and “Mr. Lyndon,” at which I was not sorry, and begged me to help her husband; which I did, getting him, through my friend Lord Crabs, a place in the excise in Ireland, and paying the passage of his family and himself to that country. I found him a dirty, cast-down, snivelling drunkard; and, looking at poor Nora, could not but wonder at the days when I had thought her a divinity. But if ever I have had a regard for a woman, I remain through life her constant friend, and could mention a thousand such instances of my generous and faithful disposition.
Young Bullingdon, however, was almost the only person with whom she was concerned that my mother could not keep in order.

===Chinese===

别看她有她的古怪，林登城堡的田产，在我们全部产业里经营得顶好；地租收得分文不爽，收租的花销比落到哪位管家手里都省。这位好寡妇自己破费之少，令人称奇；虽说——照她的说法——她撑的是*两*家的门面。她拨了一班仆人伺候小勋爵；自己出门，无非一辆旧镀金马车、驾六匹马；宅子收拾得窗明几净；家具、花园修缮一新；我们偶尔回爱尔兰，走访各处宅第，没见一家有自家这样齐整的。城堡里有二十个手脚麻利的使女，外加十来个齐齐整整的男仆；家里各样，都收拾得像顶讲究的管家妇亲手操持一般。而这一切，她几乎不向我们索分文：她在园子里放牧牛羊，赶到巴林纳斯洛（Ballinasloe）卖了好价钱；她家的黄油咸肉供应着不知多少个镇子；林登城堡园子的果菜，在都柏林市面上卖的总是头一等价。她厨房里没有浪费——我们爱尔兰的大多数宅子向来是浪费惯的；酒窖里的酒也不见消耗，因为老太太喝白水，又几乎不待客。她的全部交游，是我昔日的心上人诺拉·布雷迪（Nora Brady）——如今昆因太太（Mrs. Quin）——家的两个姑娘。她跟丈夫几乎荡尽了家私，有一回来伦敦看我，人老、发福、邋遢，身边还拖着两个脏孩子。她一见我便淌眼泪，口口声声“先生”、“林登先生”——我听了并不难过——又求我提携她丈夫；我也照办了：托我朋友克拉布斯勋爵的面子，替他在爱尔兰税务署谋了个差事，又替他携家带口出了盘缠。我见他是个邋遢、颓唐、淌眼抹泪的酒鬼；再看可怜的诺拉，不由纳罕：当年我竟把她当作天仙一般。可是，但凡我对一个女人动过一分真情，我便终生做她不变心的朋友；这类慷慨忠厚的例证，我一口气能举出一千桩来。
然而在她经手的人物里头，小布林登差不多是唯一她管束不住的。

===Original===

The accounts she sent me of him at first were such as gave my paternal heart considerable pain. He rejected all regularity and authority. He would absent himself for weeks from the house on sporting or other expeditions. He was when at home silent and queer, refusing to make my mother’s game at piquet of evenings, but plunging into all sorts of musty old books, with which he muddled his brains; more at ease laughing and chatting with the pipers and maids in the servants’ hall, than with the gentry in the drawing-room; always cutting jibes and jokes at Mrs. Barry, at which she (who was rather a slow woman at repartee) would chafe violently: in fact, leading a life of insubordination and scandal. And, to crown all, the young scapegrace took to frequenting the society of the Romish priest of the parish﻿—a threadbare rogue, from some Popish seminary in France or Spain﻿—rather than the company of the vicar of Castle Lyndon, a gentleman of Trinity, who kept his hounds and drank his two bottles a day.
Regard for the lad’s religion made me not hesitate then how I should act towards him. If I have any principle which has guided me through life, it has been respect for the Establishment, and a hearty scorn and abhorrence of all other forms of belief. I therefore sent my French body-servant, in the year 17﻿—, to Dublin with a commission to bring the young reprobate over; and the report brought to me was that he had passed the whole of the last night of his stay in Ireland with his Popish friend at the mass-house; that he and my mother had a violent quarrel on the very last day; that, on the contrary, he kissed Biddy and Dosy, her two nieces, who seemed very sorry that he should go; and that being pressed to go and visit the rector, he absolutely refused, saying he was a wicked old Pharisee, inside whose doors he would never set his foot. The doctor wrote me a letter, warning me against the deplorable errors of this young imp of perdition, as he called him; and I could see that there was no love lost between them. But it appeared that, if not agreeable to the gentry of the country, young Bullingdon had a huge popularity among the common people. There was a regular crowd weeping round the gate when his coach took its departure.

===Chinese===

起初她寄来的关于他的种种报告，桩桩件件都叫我这颗为父的心痛楚不堪。规矩与权威，他一概不认。他动辄外出打猎或另有营生，一走数星期不着家。人在家时闷声不响、古里古怪，晚间不肯陪我母亲凑皮克牌的局，一头扎进各样发霉的旧书里，把脑子都读糨了；宁可在下人大堂里同风笛手、女仆们有说有笑，不愿同客厅里的士绅周旋；句句话都在挖苦巴里太太——她回嘴的功夫又慢，回回气得七窍生烟：总而言之，过的是无法无天、招摇造孽的日子。顶顶不堪的是，这小坏种竟一味亲近教区的罗马教神父——一个法兰西或西班牙哪所天主教神学院出来的、衣衫褴褛的无赖——反倒不理林登城堡的代牧：那位三一学院出身的绅士，养着一群猎犬，每天两瓶酒喝得好不滋润。
顾念这孩子的宗教，我当时便毫不迟疑地拿定了主意。若说我此生还有一条一以贯之的原则，那便是敬重国教，并对其他一切信仰报以衷心的鄙夷与憎恶。于是我派了我的法国贴身男仆——时在17——年——前往都柏林，命他把这小孽障接了回来；带回的报告说：他在爱尔兰居留的最后一夜，是同他那教皇派朋友在望弥撒的堂口里过的；临行那天，他同我母亲大吵了一场；反过来，他吻别了她的两个侄女碧蒂和多茜，两个姑娘看他要走，都十分难过；众人劝他去拜望教区长，他断然不肯，说那是个伪善的老法利赛人，他的门坎，自己一辈子也不踏。博士先生随后给我来了一封信，叫我提防这个——照他的说法——“沉沦的孽童”的种种可悲谬误；我看得出来，他们两位之间也没什么情分。不过事情明摆着：小布林登虽不合当地士绅的心意，在平民百姓中间却极得人心。他的马车动身时，大门口围了一圈人哭哭啼啼，好不齐整。

===Original===

Scores of the ignorant savage wretches ran for miles along by the side of the chariot; and some went even so far as to steal away before his departure, and appear at the Pigeon-House at Dublin to bid him a last farewell. It was with considerable difficulty that some of these people could be kept from secreting themselves in the vessel, and accompanying their young lord to England.
To do the young scoundrel justice, when he came among us, he was a manly noble-looking lad, and everything in his bearing and appearance betokened the high blood from which he came. He was the very portrait of some of the dark cavaliers of the Lyndon race, whose pictures hung in the gallery at Hackton: where the lad was fond of spending the chief part of his time, occupied with the musty old books which he took out of the library, and which I hate to see a young man of spirit poring over. Always in my company he preserved the most rigid silence, and a haughty scornful demeanour; which was so much the more disagreeable because there was nothing in his behaviour I could actually take hold of to find fault with: although his whole conduct was insolent and supercilious to the highest degree. His mother was very much agitated at receiving him on his arrival; if he felt any such agitation he certainly did not show it. He made her a very low and formal bow when he kissed her hand; and, when I held out mine, put both his hands behind his back, stared me full in the face, and bent his head, saying, “Mr. Barry Lyndon, I believe;” turned on his heel, and began talking about the state of the weather to his mother, whom he always styled “Your Ladyship.” She was angry at this pert bearing, and, when they were alone, rebuked him sharply for not shaking hands with his father.
“My father, madam?” said he; “surely you mistake. My father was the Right Honourable Sir Charles Lyndon. *I* at least have not forgotten him, if others have.” It was a declaration of war to me, as I saw at once; though I declare I was willing enough to have received the boy well on his coming amongst us, and to have lived with him on terms of friendliness. But as men serve me I serve them. Who can blame me for my after-quarrels with this young reprobate, or lay upon my shoulders the evils which afterwards befell? Perhaps I lost my temper, and my subsequent treatment of him *was* hard.

===Chinese===

几十个愚昧粗野的可怜虫，顺着马车一路跟出去好几里；有几个甚至赶在他动身之前先溜了出去，跑到都柏林的鸽子坞（Pigeon-House），同他们的少年老爷诀别。费了好大的劲，才拦住这些人偷偷藏进船里、随他们的小勋爵一路去英格兰。
平心而论，这小恶棍来到我们中间时，倒是个有男子气概、相貌高贵的小伙子，举止仪表处处显出他血脉的高贵。他活脱是林登家族几位黑肤骑士的画像——那些画像就悬在哈克顿的长廊里；这小子顶爱把大半光阴消磨在那儿，抱着从书房里搬出的一堆发霉旧书——我最见不得一个有血性的年轻人埋在这种故纸堆里。有我在场，他永远缄口不言，神气倨傲轻蔑；这就加倍可恶，因为他的行止偏偏又叫我抓不着错处挑剔：虽说浑身上下无礼狂妄到了极处。他母亲接到他时激动得很；他心里若有半点激动，脸上可一丝不见。他吻她的手时，深深一躬，规矩周到；待我伸出手去，他却把双手往背后一背，直直盯着我的脸，微微颔首，道一声：“巴里·林登先生，我想是吧”；说罢脚跟一转，同他母亲攀谈起天气来——对她，他一律尊称“夫人”。她见这副轻狂样十分着恼，娘儿俩独处时，狠狠数落他不该不同父亲握手。
“我的父亲，夫人？”他说，“您怕是弄错了。我的父亲是极尊贵的查尔斯·林登爵士。*我*至少没有忘掉他，哪怕别人忘了。”我一看便知，这是对我下的战书；虽则我必须声明，他来到我们家时，我原本十分愿意好好待这孩子，同他和睦相处。可是人怎样待我，我便怎样待人。后来我同这小孽障的种种争斗，谁能怪我？后来发生的种种祸事，谁能栽到我头上？兴许我是动了火性，后来待他*确实*狠了些。

===Original===

But it was he began the quarrel, and not I; and the evil consequences which ensued were entirely of his creating.
As it is best to nip vice in the bud, and for a master of a family to exercise his authority in such a manner as that there may be no question about it, I took the earliest opportunity of coming to close quarters with Master Bullingdon; and the day after his arrival among us, upon his refusal to perform some duty which I requested of him, I had him conveyed to my study, and thrashed him soundly. This process, I confess, at first agitated me a good deal, for I had never laid a whip on a lord before; but I got speedily used to the practice, and his back and my whip became so well acquainted, that I warrant there was very little *ceremony* between us after a while.
If I were to repeat all the instances of the insubordination and brutal conduct of young Bullingdon, I should weary the reader. His perseverance in resistance was, I think, even greater than mine in correcting him: for a man, be he ever so much resolved to do his duty as a parent, can’t be flogging his children all day, or for every fault they commit: and though I got the character of being so cruel a stepfather to him, I pledge my word I spared him correction when he merited it many more times than I administered it. Besides, there were eight clear months in the year when he was quit of me, during the time of my presence in London, at my place in Parliament, and at the Court of my Sovereign.
At this period I made no difficulty to allow him to profit by the Latin and Greek of the old rector; who had christened him, and had a considerable influence over the wayward lad. After a scene or a quarrel between us, it was generally to the rectory-house that the young rebel would fly for refuge and counsel; and I must own that the parson was a pretty just umpire between us in our disputes. Once he led the boy back to Hackton by the hand, and actually brought him into my presence, although he had vowed never to enter the doors in my lifetime again, and said, “He had brought his Lordship to acknowledge his error, and submit to any punishment I might think proper to inflict.” Upon which I caned him in the presence of two or three friends of mine, with whom I was sitting drinking at the time; and to do him justice, he bore a pretty severe punishment without wincing or crying in the least.

===Chinese===

可挑起这场争端的是他，不是我；随之而来的一切恶果，全出自他一手炮制。
罪恶顶好扼杀在萌芽，一家之主行使权威，也顶好做得叫人无从置喙。于是我逮着顶早的机会同比林登少爷短兵相接：他到家第二天，我吩咐他办一桩差事，他抗命不从，我便叫人把他带到书房，结结实实抽了一顿。不瞒诸位，这套程序起初叫我心里跳了好一阵，毕竟我从没拿鞭子抽过勋爵；可我很快便下手惯了，他的脊背同我的鞭子厮混得烂熟，我敢担保，过不多时，我们俩之间就再谈不上什么*客气*了。
小布林登忤逆横行的种种事迹，若要我一一复述，读者必要生厌。他顽抗的韧劲，我想，比我省诫他的韧劲还要大些：一个人，纵然横下心要尽为父之责，也不能成天鞭打孩子，更不能孩子犯一样错便抽一样；况且我虽落了个毒继父的名声，我以名誉担保，他该领责罚而我不曾施与的回数，比施与的回数多得多。何况一年里他有整整八个月躲开了我——那阵子我在伦敦，坐我的议会席，在君王的宫廷里当差。
这个时期，我毫不介意让他去受用老教区长的拉丁文与希腊文——他的洗礼便是这位行的，对这倔强的少年颇有几分影响力。我们之间每闹过一场、吵过一架，这小反叛多半飞奔到牧师宅去讨庇护、讨主意；我也必须承认，在我们爷俩的争执里，那位牧师算得一员相当公道的裁判。有一回，他牵住孩子的手领回哈克顿，竟真把他带到了我跟前——虽说孩子发过誓，只要我活着，就再不进这道门——牧师说：“我已领着勋爵大人认了错，任凭您认为合宜的责罚，他都甘领。”于是我当着两三位正陪我饮酒的朋友的面，用手杖抽了他一顿；说句公道话，这顿责罚着实不轻，他硬是没皱一皱眉、没掉一滴泪。

===Original===

This will show that I was not too severe in my treatment of the lad, as I had the authority of the clergyman himself for inflicting the correction which I thought proper.
Twice or thrice, Lavender, Bryan’s governor, attempted to punish my Lord Bullingdon; but I promise you the rogue was too strong for *him*, and levelled the Oxford man to the ground with a chair: greatly to the delight of little Byran, who cried out, “Bravo, Bully! thump him, thump him!” And Bully certainly did, to the governor’s heart’s content; who never attempted personal chastisement afterwards; but contented himself by bringing the tales of his Lordship’s misdoings to me, his natural protector and guardian.
With the child, Bullingdon was, strange to say, pretty tractable. He took a liking for the little fellow﻿—as, indeed, everybody who saw that darling boy did﻿—liked him the more, he said, because he was “half a Lyndon.” And well he might like him, for many a time, at the dear angel’s intercession of “Papa, don’t flog Bully today!” I have held my hand, and saved him a horsing, which he richly deserved.
With his mother, at first, he would scarcely deign to have any communication. He said she was no longer one of the family. Why should he love her, as she had never been a mother to him? But it will give the reader an idea of the dogged obstinacy and surliness of the lad’s character, when I mention one trait regarding him. It has been made a matter of complaint against me, that I denied him the education befitting a gentleman, and never sent him to college or to school; but the fact is, it was of his own choice that he went to neither. He had the offer repeatedly from me (who wished to see as little of his impudence as possible), but he as repeatedly declined; and, for a long time, I could not make out what was the charm which kept him in a house where he must have been far from comfortable.
It came out, however, at last. There used to be very frequent disputes between my Lady Lyndon and myself, in which sometimes she was wrong, sometimes I was; and which, as neither of us had very angelical tempers, used to run very high. I was often in liquor; and when in that condition, what gentleman is master of himself? Perhaps I *did*, in this state, use my Lady rather roughly; fling a glass or two at her, and call her by a few names that were not complimentary.

===Chinese===

这足见我待这孩子并不算太狠：我认为合宜的责罚，是得了牧师本人亲自首肯才动的手。
有那么两三回，布莱恩的师保拉文德想惩戒布林登勋爵；可我向你担保，这位爷力气比*他*大，一把椅子便把牛津人撂倒在地：小布莱恩乐不可支，连声叫好：“好样的，布利！揍他，揍他！”布利也当真揍了，揍得师保十二分满足；从此他再不敢亲身动刑，只得满足于把勋爵大人的种种胡作非为，一桩桩报到我这里——报到他天生的保护人兼监护人这里。
对那娃娃，说来奇怪，布林登倒相当驯顺。他喜欢这小的——也难怪，谁见了那宝贝疙瘩不喜欢——他说他越发喜欢这孩子，因为他是“半个林登”。他尽可以喜欢：多少回，多亏那亲爱的小天使求情，说一句“爸爸，今天别打布利啦！”我便住了手，免了他一顿好打——那顿打他本是受之无愧。
对他母亲，起初他几乎不屑一顾。他说她已不算这家里的人了。她从来不曾做过他的母亲，凭什么要他爱她？不过待我说出他的一桩行状，读者便可想见这小子性格何等死倔、何等乖戾。有人拿这件事控诉我，说我剥夺了他绅士应受的教育，从不送他进大学、入学堂；可事实是，两处他都不去，全是他自己拿的主意。我不止一回向他提出——我也巴不得少看他那张无礼的脸——他却回回回绝；有很长一段时间，我猜不透究竟是什么魔法，把他拴在一个他住着断然不自在的宅子里。
谜底后来终于揭开。林登伯爵夫人同我之间，口角本是家常便饭，有时她错，有时我错；我们俩又都不是天使般的脾气，一吵便吵得天翻地覆。我时常喝得酩酊；到了那个份上，哪位绅士还管得住自己？兴许在那样的状态下，我*确实*亏待过夫人；朝她摔过一两只杯子，也叫过她几句不中听的称呼。

===Original===

I may have threatened her life (which it was obviously my interest not to take), and have frightened her, in a word, considerably.
After one of these disputes, in which she ran screaming through the galleries, and I, as tipsy as a lord, came staggering after, it appears Bullingdon was attracted out of his room by the noise; as I came up with her, the audacious rascal tripped up my heels, which were not very steady, and catching his fainting mother in his arms, took her into his own room; where he, upon her entreaty, swore he would never leave the house as long as she continued united with me. I knew nothing of the vow, or indeed of the tipsy frolic which was the occasion of it; I was taken up “glorious,” as the phrase is, by my servants, and put to bed, and, in the morning, had no more recollection of what had occurred any more than of what happened when I was a baby at the breast. Lady Lyndon told me of the circumstance years after; and I mention it here, as it enables me to plead honourably “not guilty” to one of the absurd charges of cruelty trumped up against me with respect to my stepson. Let my detractors apologise, if they dare, for the conduct of a graceless ruffian who trips up the heels of his own natural guardian and stepfather after dinner.
This circumstance served to unite mother and son for a little; but their characters were too different. I believe she was too fond of me ever to allow him to be sincerely reconciled to her. As he grew up to be a man, his hatred towards me assumed an intensity quite wicked to think of (and which I promise you I returned with interest): and it was at the age of sixteen, I think, that the impudent young hangdog, on my return from Parliament one summer, and on my proposing to cane him as usual, gave me to understand that he would submit to no farther chastisement from me, and said, grinding his teeth, that he would shoot me if I laid hands on him. I looked at him; he was grown, in fact, to be a tall young man, and I gave up that necessary part of his education.
It was about this time that I raised the company which was to serve in America; and my enemies in the country (and since my victory over the Tiptoffs I scarce need say I had many of them) began to propagate the most shameful reports regarding my conduct to that precious young scapegrace my stepson, and to insinuate that I actually wished to get rid of him.

===Chinese===

兴许我还威胁过她的性命（那性命明摆着留着她于我更有利），总而言之，一句话：把她吓得不轻。
有这么一场争吵过后，她尖叫着穿过长廊奔逃，我醉得像位王爷，跌跌撞撞追在后头；布林登闻声出了房门——事情便是这样；待我赶上她，那胆大包天的小子伸脚一绊，绊了我一个趔趄（我那双脚本就站不稳），又把他晕厥的母亲搂进怀里，抱回自己房中；经她百般央求，他起了誓：只要她同我夫妻一日不断，他便一日不离这座宅子。这道誓我毫不知情，连招出这道誓的那场醉后胡闹，我也一概不知；仆人们架起“荣归”的我——俗话是这般讲的——送上床去；第二天早晨，头天夜里的事我记得的，不比我吃奶时节的事多。林登伯爵夫人隔了多年才把这段情由讲给我听；我在此写明，正可叫我堂堂正正地喊一声“无罪”，驳掉那桩硬栽在我头上的虐待继子的荒唐罪名。让那些诋毁我的人——他们若敢——去替那下流坯的行径赔个不是吧：一个饭后果敢绊倒自己法定监护人兼继父的混账东西。
这桩事让母子俩贴心了一阵子；可两人的性子相差太远。我相信，她爱我太深，由不得他真心同她和解。他长大成人后，对我的仇恨烈到不堪设想的地步（我担保，我也连本带利奉还了）；记得就在他十六岁那年，我想是夏天，我自议会回乡，照例要动手杖教训他，那厚脸皮的小无赖却示意于我：他再不受我的责罚了；还咬着牙说，我再碰他一碰，他便开枪崩了我。我端详他：他确实已长成个高大的青年，于是他这门必修的功课，我便就此豁免。
大约就在这时，我募起那支开赴美洲的连队；我乡间的仇家们（自我胜了蒂普托夫家那场官司，不消说，我的仇家多了去了）便开始散播顶下流的流言，讲我如何苛待我继子——那位金贵的小祸害——还含沙射影，说我分明就想除掉他。

===Original===

Thus my loyalty to my Sovereign was actually construed into a horrid unnatural attempt on my part on Bullingdon’s life; and it was said that I had raised the American corps for the sole purpose of getting the young Viscount to command it, and so of getting rid of him. I am not sure that they had not fixed upon the name of the very man in the company who was ordered to despatch him at the first general action, and the bribe I was to give him for this delicate piece of service.
But the truth is, I was of opinion then (and though the fulfilment of my prophecy has been delayed, yet I make no doubt it will be brought to pass ere long), that my Lord Bullingdon needed none of *my* aid in sending him into the other world; but had a happy knack of finding the way thither himself, which he would be sure to pursue. In truth, he began upon this way early: of all the violent, daring, disobedient scapegraces that ever caused an affectionate parent pain, he was certainly the most incorrigible; there was no beating him, or coaxing him, or taming him.
For instance, with my little son, when his governor brought him into the room as we were over the bottle after dinner, my Lord would begin his violent and undutiful sarcasms at me.
“Dear child,” he would say, beginning to caress and fondle him, “what a pity it is I am not dead for thy sake! The Lyndons would then have a worthier representative, and enjoy all the benefit of the illustrious blood of the Barrys of Barryogue; would they not, Mr. Barry Lyndon?” He always chose the days when company, or the clergy or gentry of the neighbourhood, were present, to make these insolent speeches to me.
Another day (it was Bryan’s birthday) we were giving a grand ball and gala at Hackton, and it was time for my little Bryan to make his appearance among us, as he usually did in the smartest little court-suit you ever saw (ah me! but it brings tears into my old eyes now to think of the bright looks of that darling little face).
