There was a great crowding and tittering when the child came in, led by his half-brother, who walked into the dancing-room (would you believe it?) in his stocking-feet, leading little Bryan by the hand, paddling about in the great shoes of the elder! “Don’t you think he fits my shoes very well, Sir Richard Wargrave?”

===Chinese===

就这样，我对君王的一片忠心，竟被穿凿成一桩灭绝人性的毒计，要害布林登的性命；还说我募那支美洲部队，就是专为了骗小勋爵去统领，好借此甩脱他。他们兴许连人都替我点好了：连队里奉命在头一场大会战中了结他的那个具体人，还有我付给这件细密差使的贿金——我都不敢保他们没有议定。
可实情是，我当时的看法便是（虽说我的预言迟迟未验，我却毫不怀疑它早晚应验）：要送布林登勋爵上西天，*我*根本不必出力；他自己就有一手寻路的天才，而且必定循着这条路走到底。说真的，这条路他起步极早：古往今来惹慈父伤心的那些横暴、胆大、忤逆的小祸害里，他准是顶不可救药的一个；打不服，哄不转，驯不服。
举个例子：饭后我们正传杯递盏，小儿的师保把他领进屋来，勋爵大人便冲我发起他那套横暴不孝的冷嘲热讽。
“亲爱的孩子，”他会这样说，一面爱怜地摩挲他，“为了你，我要是死了多好！林登家便有一位更配得上的人做主人，尽享巴里奥格巴里家高贵血脉的一切好处——是不是啊，巴里·林登先生？”这类无礼话，他偏拣家里有客、或左近牧师士绅在座的日子，说给我听。
又有一天（那天正是布莱恩的生日），我们在哈克顿大开舞会、举行庆典，到了我家小布莱恩照例出场的时辰——他往常总穿一身你从未见过的顶神气的小宫廷礼服（唉！如今想起那张宝贝小脸上的光彩，我这双老眼还要落泪）。孩子进来了，领他的正是他同母异父的哥哥；满场拥挤、嗤笑；只见那位（你肯信么？）光着一双袜子走进舞厅，一手牵着小布莱恩——小的正趿拉着大的那双大鞋，啪嗒啪嗒地迈！“理查德·沃格雷夫爵士（Sir Richard Wargrave），您不觉得他穿我的鞋正合脚么？”

===Original===

says the young reprobate: upon which the company began to look at each other and to titter; and his mother, coming up to Lord Bullingdon with great dignity, seized the child to her breast, and said, “From the manner in which I love this child, my Lord, you ought to know how I would have loved his elder brother had he proved worthy of any mother’s affection!” and, bursting into tears, Lady Lyndon left the apartment, and the young lord rather discomfited for once.
At last, on one occasion, his behaviour to me was so outrageous (it was in the hunting-field and in a large public company), that I lost all patience, rode at the urchin straight, wrenched him out of his saddle with all my force, and, flinging him roughly to the ground, sprang down to it myself, and administered such a correction across the young caitiff’s head and shoulders with my horsewhip as might have ended in his death, had I not been restrained in time; for my passion was up, and I was in a state to do murder or any other crime. The lad was taken home and put to bed, where he lay for a day or two in a fever, as much from rage and vexation as from the chastisement I had given him; and three days afterwards, on sending to inquire at his chamber whether he would join the family at table, a note was found on his table, and his bed was empty and cold. The young villain had fled, and had the audacity to write in the following terms regarding me to my wife, his mother:﻿—

===Chinese===

那小恶棍说。一时间宾客们面面相觑、嗤嗤窃笑；他母亲端着十足的威仪走到布林登勋爵跟前，一把把孩子搂进怀里，说道：“勋爵，看我疼这孩子疼到什么地步，您就该明白：倘若他上面的哥哥争气、配得上一分母爱，我也会怎样地疼他！”说罢泪如泉涌，林登伯爵夫人离席而去；这一回，小勋爵倒是难得地讪讪然了。
末了有一回，他对我放肆到了极处（那是在猎场上，当着一大群外人），我按捺不住，拍马直取那小崽子，使出全身力气把他从鞍上拽下，狠狠掼倒在地，自己也翻身跃下，抄起马鞭，朝那小恶徒的头和肩膀一顿好抽——若不是众人及时拦住，险些当场要了他的性命；那时我怒火上头，杀人放火的事都做得出来。这小子被抬回家送上床，发了一两天的热——一半是气恼攻心，一半才是我给他的那顿责打；三天过后，家里派人到他房里问一声，可愿下楼同全家上桌，只见桌上一封信，床上空空、冰冷。小恶棍逃了，还胆敢给我太太、他母亲，写下这样一封数说我的信：——

===Original===

> “Madam,” he said, “I have borne as long as mortal could endure the ill-treatment of the insolent Irish upstart whom you have taken to your bed. It is not only the lowness of his birth and the general brutality of his manners which disgust me, and must make me hate him so long as I have the honour to bear the name of Lyndon, which he is unworthy of, but the shameful nature of his conduct towards your Ladyship; his brutal and ungentlemanlike behaviour, his open infidelity, his habits of extravagance, intoxication, his shameless robberies and swindling of my property and yours. It is these insults to you which shock and annoy me, more than the ruffian’s infamous conduct to myself. I would have stood by your Ladyship as I promised, but you seem to have taken latterly your husband’s part; and, as I cannot personally chastise this lowbred ruffian, who, to our shame be it spoken, is the husband of my mother; and as I cannot bear to witness his treatment of you, and loathe his horrible society as if it were the plague, I am determined to quit my native country: at least during his detested life, or during my own. I possess a small income from my father, of which I have no doubt Mr. Barry will cheat me if he can; but which, if your Ladyship has some feelings of a mother left, you will, perhaps, award to me. Messrs. Childs, the bankers, can have orders to pay it to me when due; if they receive no such orders, I shall be not in the least surprised, knowing you to be in the hands of a villain who would not scruple to rob on the highway; and shall try to find out some way in life for myself more honourable than that by which the penniless Irish adventurer has arrived to turn me out of my rights and home.”

===Chinese===

> “夫人，”他写道，“您引上床的那个狂妄的爱尔兰暴发户百般作践我；凡人所能忍受的，我都忍到了头。叫我厌恶的，不止是他出身的低贱、举止的一贯粗野——只要我还有幸姓一天林登，这姓氏他不配——我便恨他一天；更是他对待您本人的行径之无耻：他蛮横无礼、全无绅士体统，他公开负心，他挥霍无度、酗酒成癖，他对我的产业和您的产业明抢暗骗、恬不知耻。他加在您身上的这些羞辱，比这恶棍施到我头上的卑劣行径，更叫我心惊，叫我愤恨。我本要依着诺言护住您，可您近来似乎事事偏袒您的丈夫；我既不能亲手责罚这个下流坯——说来是我们全家的耻辱——我母亲招的那位丈夫；又眼见不得他这般的对待您，憎恶同他相处，如避瘟疫；于是我拿定主意，离开我的故土：至少在他那可憎的有生之年，或在我自己的余年。我承受父亲遗下的一小笔进项；巴里先生若骗得掉，我毫不怀疑他必骗；但倘若您心里还剩一点为娘的情分，也许肯把它裁划给我。奇尔德银行（Messrs. Childs）可以奉命到期付给我；若收不到这道命令，我一丝一毫也不奇怪——我深知您落在一个拦路打劫也不皱一皱眉的恶棍手里；往后我自会给自己寻一条生计，总比那个一文不名的爱尔兰冒险家发迹的门路更清白些——他就是打那条道儿上来，把我从我的名分和我的家里撵出去的。”

===Original===

This mad epistle was signed “Bullingdon,” and all the neighbours vowed that I had been privy to his flight, and would profit by it; though I declare on my honour my true and sincere desire, after reading the above infamous letter, was to have the author within a good arm’s length of me, that I might let him know my opinion regarding him. But there was no eradicating this idea from people’s minds, who insisted that I wanted to kill Bullingdon; whereas murder, as I have said, was never one of my evil qualities: and even had I wished to injure my young enemy ever so much, common prudence would have made my mind easy, as I knew he was going to ruin his own way.
It was long before we heard of the fate of the audacious young truant; but after some fifteen months had elapsed, I had the pleasure of being able to refute some of the murderous calumnies which had been uttered against me, by producing a bill with Bullingdon’s own signature, drawn from General Tarleton’s army in America, where my company was conducting itself with the greatest glory, and with which my Lord was serving as a volunteer. There were some of my kind friends who persisted still in attributing all sorts of wicked intentions to me. Lord Tiptoff would never believe that I would pay any bill, much more any bill of Lord Bullingdon’s; old Lady Betty Grimsby, his sister, persisted in declaring the bill was a forgery, and the poor dear lord dead; until there came a letter to her Ladyship from Lord Bullingdon himself, who had been at New York at headquarters, and who described at length the splendid festival given by the officers of the garrison to our distinguished chieftains, the two Howes.
In the meanwhile, if I *had* murdered my Lord, I could scarcely have been received with more shameful obloquy and slander than now followed me in town and country. “You will hear of the lad’s death, be sure,” exclaimed one of my friends. “And then his wife’s will follow,” added another. “He will marry Jenny Jones,” added a third; and so on. Lavender brought me the news of these scandals about me: the country was up against me.

===Chinese===

这封疯信署名“布林登”。左邻右舍众口一词，咬定他的出逃必是我同谋，我还要从中渔利；可是我以名誉声明：读完上面这封无耻的信，我真心实意所愿的，是叫写信人站到我一条手臂之内，好教他领教领教我对他高见如何品评。可这念头在世人脑子里生了根，拔也拔不去，一口咬定我要害死布林登；其实杀生这一条，如我前文交代过的，从来不在我的恶德之列：即便我真想重重收拾我这位年轻的冤家，寻常的审慎也尽够叫我安心——我早知道他自会一路奔向自己的毁灭。
这大胆的小逃亡者命运如何，我们久久不得而知；约莫过了十五个月，我有幸拿出一张布林登亲笔签名的票据，是从美洲塔莱顿将军（General Tarleton）军中开出的——我的连队正在那边挣得赫赫声名，勋爵大人以义勇兵之身随营——这才驳倒了几桩说我行凶的诽谤。可几位厚道的朋友，照旧往我头上安各式歹毒的居心。蒂普托夫勋爵死也不肯信我会兑付什么票据，更不必说是布林登勋爵的票据；他的姐姐贝蒂·格里姆斯比老夫人一口咬定票据是伪造、可怜亲爱的勋爵早已不在人世；直到后来勋爵大人本人来了信——他当时正在纽约的总部——信里详详细细描写了守军将官为两位杰出的统帅、豪氏兄弟，大张盛筵。
这期间，城乡上下追着我的那些无耻的辱骂与中伤，即便我真*害了*勋爵大人，怕也不过如此。“那孩子的死讯，你等着吧，笃定的。”我的一位朋友嚷道。“跟着便是他太太的死讯。”另一位补一句。“他要娶珍妮·琼斯啦。”第三位再补一句；如此等等。这些关于我的丑闻，是拉文德捎回来的：举国上下都起来同我作对了。

===Original===

The farmers on market-days used to touch their hats sulkily, and get out of my way; the gentlemen who followed my hunt now suddenly seceded from it, and left off my uniform; at the county ball, where I led out Lady Susan Capermore, and took my place third in the dance after the duke and the marquis, as was my wont, all the couples turned away as we came to them, and we were left to dance alone. Sukey Capermore has a love of dancing which would make her dance at a funeral if anybody asked her, and I had too much spirit to give in at this signal instance of insult towards me; so we danced with some of the very commonest low people at the bottom of the set﻿—your apothecaries, wine-merchants, attorneys, and such scum as are allowed to attend our public assemblies.
The bishop, my Lady Lyndon’s relative, neglected to invite us to the palace at the assizes; and, in a word, every indignity was put upon me which could by possibility be heaped upon an innocent and honourable gentleman.
My reception in London, whither I now carried my wife and family, was scarcely more cordial. On paying my respects to my Sovereign at St. James’s, His Majesty pointedly asked me when I had news of Lord Bullingdon. On which I replied, with no ordinary presence of mind, “Sir, my Lord Bullingdon is fighting the rebels against your Majesty’s crown in America. Does your Majesty desire that I should send another regiment to aid him?” On which the King turned on his heel, and I made my bow out of the presence-chamber. When Lady Lyndon kissed the Queen’s hand at the drawing-room, I found that precisely the same question had been put to her Ladyship; and she came home much agitated at the rebuke which had been administered to her. Thus it was that my loyalty was rewarded, and my sacrifice, in favour of my country, viewed! I took away my establishment abruptly to Paris, where I met with a very different reception: but my stay amidst the enchanting pleasures of that capital was extremely short; for the French Government, which had been long tampering with the American rebels, now openly acknowledged the independence of the United States. A declaration of war ensued: all we happy English were ordered away from Paris; and I think I left one or two fair ladies there inconsolable. It is the only place where a gentleman can live as he likes without being incommoded by his wife.

===Chinese===

集市的日子，庄稼汉们阴沉着脸碰一碰帽檐，便侧身让开我；从前跟随我行猎的绅士们，忽然全体散伙，脱下了我的号衣；郡里的舞会上，我照例挽出苏珊·凯珀莫尔小姐（Lady Susan Capermore）领舞，照例排在公爵、侯爵之后、舞列第三；可我们转到哪一对跟前，哪一对便转身让开，剩下我们两个独自跳。苏琪·凯珀莫尔（Sukey Capermore）爱跳舞爱到只要有人请，出殡她也肯跳；而我气性未许我在这一番登峰造极的羞辱面前低头；于是我们便同舞列末尾顶顶低贱的下等人跳了——药剂师、酒商、律师之流，还有那些获准入场公共舞会的渣滓。
林登伯爵夫人那位做主教的本家，巡回开庭时节也忘了请我们进主教府；总而言之，凡能想得到的羞辱，一样不落，全数堆到了我这个清白而高尚的绅士头上。
这之后我带着妻小进京，伦敦给我的接待，热络也有限。我到圣詹姆斯宫朝觐我君，陛下单刀直入，问我几时得到布林登勋爵的消息。我当下凭着不同寻常的镇定答道：“陛下，布林登勋爵正在美洲，为陛下的王冠讨伐叛军。陛下可是要臣再派一团人马去援助他？”陛下闻言脚跟一转，我便一躬到地，退出了觐见厅。林登伯爵夫人觐见时吻了王后的手，我发现她被问的恰是同一句话；她吃了这一顿抢白，回家激动了半日。瞧瞧吧：我的忠诚得了这般酬报，我舍家纾难的一片牺牲，得了这般看待！我索性把全家骤然迁往巴黎，在那儿受到的接待可大不相同：只是我流连于那座都城销魂荡魄的种种乐事，时日极短；因为久已同美洲叛军暗通款曲的法国政府，此时公然承认了美利坚合众国独立。战书随之而下：我们这班快乐的英国人奉命一律离开巴黎；我想，我撇下的一两位俏丽贵妇，从此怕是郁郁难安。巴黎是天下独一处，绅士在那里想怎么过活就怎么过活，没有太太来搅扰。
伯爵夫人和我客居期间，除凡尔赛宫、王后的牌桌这些大场合，几乎彼此不见面；我们亲爱的小布莱恩则百尺竿头，精进了一千样雅致的才艺，成了识得他的人人人心头的欢喜。

===Original===

The Countess and I, during our stay, scarcely saw each other except upon public occasions, at Versailles, or at the Queen’s play-table; and our dear little Bryan advanced in a thousand elegant accomplishments which rendered him the delight of all who knew him.
I must not forget to mention here my last interview with my good uncle, the Chevalier de Ballybarry, whom I left at Brussels with strong intentions of making his salut, as the phrase is, and who had gone into retirement at a convent there. Since then he had come into the world again, much to his annoyance and repentance; having fallen desperately in love in his old age with a French actress, who had done, as most ladies of her character do﻿—ruined him, left him, and laughed at him. His repentance was very edifying. Under the guidance of Messieurs of the Irish College, he once more turned his thoughts towards religion; and his only prayer to me when I saw him and asked in what I could relieve him, was to pay a handsome fee to the convent into which he proposed to enter.
This I could not, of course, do: my religious principles forbidding me to encourage superstition in any way; and the old gentleman and I parted rather coolly, in consequence of my refusal, as he said, to make his old days comfortable.
I was very poor at the time, that is the fact; and *entre nous*, the Rosemont of the French Opera, an indifferent dancer, but a charming figure and ankle, was ruining me in diamonds, equipages, and furniture bills, added to which I had a run of ill-luck at play, and was forced to meet my losses by the most shameful sacrifices to the moneylenders, by pawning part of Lady Lyndon’s diamonds (that graceless little Rosemont wheedled me out of some of them), and by a thousand other schemes for raising money. But when Honour is in the case, was I ever found backward at her call: and what man can say that Barry Lyndon lost a bet which he did not pay?
As for my ambitious hopes regarding the Irish peerage, I began, on my return, to find out that I had been led wildly astray by that rascal Lord Crabs; who liked to take my money, but had no more influence to get me a coronet than to procure for me the Pope’s tiara.

===Chinese===

在这里，我不可不提同我那位好叔父、巴利巴里骑士（Chevalier de Ballybarry）的最后一面。当年我把他留在布鲁塞尔，他正一门心思——照那边的说法——“救自己的灵魂”，进了当地一座修道院静修。打那以后他又还了俗，懊恼悔恨得不亦乐乎：偌大年纪，竟同比一位法国女戏子爱得死去活来；那一位也干出了她那一路太太们惯干的事——毁了他，甩了他，还嘲笑他。他的悔罪煞是感化人心。在爱尔兰学院诸位神父的引导下，他再一次把心思转回了宗教；我见他时，问有什么可以替他张罗，他唯一的祷求，便是我替他向那座他打算皈依的修道院，奉上一笔体面的香资。
这个我自然不能应承：我的宗教原则禁止我以任何方式助长迷信；于是老先生同我分手时相当冷淡，怪我——照他的说法——不肯叫他晚景安逸。
那时节我手头很紧，这是实情；而且——这话只你我关起门讲——法兰西歌剧院的罗丝蒙（Rosemont），舞跳得平平，身段与脚踝却动人，正在钻石、车马、家具账单上一寸一寸败光我；偏生我又赶上赌运背极的一轮，只好拿顶不体面的牺牲去填印子钱债主的口，拿典押林登伯爵夫人的一部分钻石去填赌亏——那一部分里，还有几件是那个没脸没臊的小罗丝蒙甜言蜜语哄了去的——再拿一千样别的弄钱法子去遮补。可是一旦关涉到荣誉，几时见我临阵退缩过？哪个人敢说，巴里·林登输过一笔没付的赌账？
至于我对爱尔兰爵位的那番青云之望，一回国我便渐渐看穿：我早叫克拉布斯勋爵那个恶棍牵着鼻子，疯跑进了一条岔道——他收我的钱倒是乐意；真要替我谋一顶宝冕，他的本事，不比替我谋来教皇的三重冠多一丝一毫。

===Original===

The Sovereign was not a whit more gracious to me on returning from the Continent than he had been before my departure; and I had it from one of the aides-de-camp of the Royal Dukes his brothers, that my conduct and amusements at Paris had been odiously misrepresented by some spies there, and had formed the subject of Royal comment; and that the King had, influenced by these calumnies, actually said I was the most disreputable man in the three kingdoms. I disreputable! I a dishonour to my name and country! When I heard these falsehoods, I was in such a rage that I went off to Lord North at once to remonstrate with the Minister; to insist upon being allowed to appear before His Majesty and clear myself of the imputations against me, to point out my services to the Government in voting with them, and to ask when the reward that had been promised to me﻿—viz., the title held by my ancestors﻿—was again to be revived in my person?
There was a sleepy coolness in that fat Lord North which was the most provoking thing that the Opposition had ever to encounter from him. He heard me with half-shut eyes. When I had finished a long violent speech﻿—which I made striding about his room in Downing Street, and gesticulating with all the energy of an Irishman﻿—he opened one eye, smiled, and asked me gently if I had done. On my replying in the affirmative, he said, “Well, Mr. Barry, I’ll answer you, point by point. The King is exceedingly averse to make peers, as you know. Your claims, as you call them, *have* been laid before him, and His Majesty’s gracious reply was, that you were the most impudent man in his dominions, and merited a halter rather than a coronet. As for withdrawing your support from us, you are perfectly welcome to carry yourself and your vote whithersoever you please. And now, as I have a great deal of occupation, perhaps you will do me the favour to retire.” So saying, he raised his hand lazily to the bell, and bowed me out; asking blandly if there was any other thing in the world in which he could oblige me.
I went home in a fury which can’t be described; and having Lord Crabs to dinner that day, assailed his Lordship by pulling his wig off his head, and smothering it in his face, and by attacking him in that part of the person where, according to report, he had been formerly assaulted by Majesty.

===Chinese===

从欧陆回来，君王待我的和悦不曾添半分，同我动身之前一般无二；御弟公爵们麾下的一位副官私下告诉我：我在巴黎的行止与取乐，被安插在那边的探子糟蹋得不成样子，还成了御前的谈资；君王受了这些谗言的影响，竟说我是不列颠三国之中最不名誉的人。我不名誉！我辱没了我的姓氏与邦国！一听这些谎话，我登时怒不可遏，径直奔诺思勋爵而去，同首相大人理论：坚决要求面见君王御前，洗清泼在我身上的种种指控；历数我投票襄助政府之功；再请问一句：当年许下给我的酬报——即我祖先传下的那个封号——几时才在敝人身上重见天日？
那位胖胖的诺思勋爵，有一副睡意惺忪的冷静，在野党在他手里吃过的瘪，数这个顶气人。他眯缝着眼听我讲。等我那篇长篇大论的激烈演说收了尾——那是在唐宁街他的屋里，我来回踱步，拿出爱尔兰人全部的劲头指天画地——他睁开一只眼，微微一笑，轻轻地问我：讲完了么？我答说讲完了，他便道：“好吧，巴里先生，我一条一条答你。你也知道，国王极不愿意册封爵位。你的所谓呈请，*确实*是呈到御前了；陛下仁慈的答复是：你是我王土之内最厚颜无耻之人，与其赏你宝冕，不如赏你绞索。至于收回你对我们的支持，悉听尊便——你这个人、你这一票，爱往哪儿去往哪儿去。现在嘛，我事务繁忙，就烦请大驾告退吧。”说着，他懒洋洋抬手按铃，一躬身把我送了出来；还和颜悦色地问道：世上可还有别的什么事，能替我效劳。

===Original===

The whole story was over the town the next day, and pictures of me were hanging in the clubs and print-shops performing the operation alluded to. All the town laughed at the picture of the lord and the Irishman, and, I need not say, recognised both. As for me, I was one of the most celebrated characters in London in those days: my dress, style, and equipage being as well known as those of any leader of the fashion; and my popularity, if not great in the highest quarters, was at least considerable elsewhere. The people cheered me in the Gordon rows, at the time they nearly killed my friend Jemmy Twitcher and burned Lord Mansfield’s house down. Indeed, I was known as a staunch Protestant, and after my quarrel with Lord North veered right round to the Opposition, and vexed him with all the means in my power.
These were not, unluckily, very great, for I was a bad speaker, and the House would not listen to me, and presently, in 1780, after the Gordon disturbance, was dissolved, when a general election took place. It came on me, as all my mishaps were in the habit of coming, at a most unlucky time. I was obliged to raise more money, at most ruinous rates, to face the confounded election, and had the Tiptoffs against me in the field more active and virulent than ever.
My blood boils even now when I think of the rascally conduct of my enemies in that scoundrelly election. I was held up as the Irish Bluebeard, and libels of me were printed, and gross caricatures drawn representing me flogging Lady Lyndon, whipping Lord Bullingdon, turning him out of doors in a storm, and I know not what. There were pictures of a pauper cabin in Ireland, from which it was pretended I came; others in which I was represented as a lackey and shoeblack. A flood of calumny was let loose upon me, in which any man of less spirit would have gone down.
But though I met my accusers boldly, though I lavished sums of money in the election, though I flung open Hackton Hall and kept champagne and Burgundy running there, and at all my inns in the town, as commonly as water, the election went against me.

===Chinese===

我回到家里，一腔怒火没法形容；那天正请克拉布斯勋爵吃饭，我便向勋爵大人大兴问罪之师：揪下他的假发，劈脸捂将过去，又朝据传当年王上亲自施过礼的那个部位，痛加挞伐。第二天，这故事传遍全城；各家俱乐部、版画铺子里，挂出了我行礼如仪的画像，画的正是上文影射的那桩手术。全伦敦看着“老爵爷与爱尔兰人”这幅画哄笑；不消说，画的是谁，人人认得。至于我，那些日子也是伦敦鼎鼎大名的人物之一：我的衣冠、做派、车马，同任何一位时髦领袖的一样无人不晓；我的人望，虽说在顶高的那几处不吃开，在别处至少也着实可观。戈登乱子那阵子，人们几乎打死我朋友吉米·特威彻（Jemmy Twitcher）、烧掉曼斯菲尔德勋爵的宅子，就在那时，众人为我喝过彩。的确，我素以坚定的新教徒闻名；同诺思勋爵闹翻之后，我兜个圈子，一转到了在野党一边，凡力所能及的手段，没有一样不拿来惹他老人家动气。
不巧，我的手段并不算大：我天生不善演说，议院又不肯听我；不久，1780年，戈登乱子过后，议院解散，举行大选。灾祸临头，照例专拣我最倒霉的时辰——我的一切不幸向来有这个习惯。为应付这场该死的大选，我只得拿顶毁人的利息再去筹款；蒂普托夫家的人在选场上同我作对，也比从前更加卖力、更加狠毒。
直到如今，一想起那场下流大选里仇家们的下流行径，我的血还要沸腾。我被摆出来示众，当作爱尔兰的蓝胡子；攻讦我的谤书印了出来；粗鄙的漫画一张张画出来：画我鞭打林登伯爵夫人、抽布林登勋爵、在暴风雨里把他扫地出门，还有些我不记得的名堂。有的画着爱尔兰一间叫花子窝棚，硬说我便是从那里面出来的；又有的把我画成听差、画成擦鞋童。一股诽谤的洪流朝我决开堤闸，换了气性小些的人，早淹死了。

===Original===

The rascally gentry had all turned upon me and joined the Tiptoff faction: it was even represented that I held my wife by force; and though I sent her into the town alone, wearing my colours, with Bryan in her lap, and made her visit the mayor’s lady and the chief women there, nothing would persuade the people but that she lived in fear and trembling of me; and the brutal mob had the insolence to ask her why she dared to go back, and how she liked horsewhip for supper.
I was thrown out of my election, and all the bills came down upon me together﻿—all the bills I had been contracting during the years of my marriage, which the creditors, with a rascally unanimity, sent in until they lay upon my table in heaps. I won’t cite their amount: it was frightful. My stewards and lawyers made matters worse. I was bound up in an inextricable toil of bills and debts, of mortgages and insurances, and all the horrible evils attendant upon them. Lawyers upon lawyers posted down from London; composition after composition was made, and Lady Lyndon’s income hampered almost irretrievably to satisfy these cormorants. To do her justice, she behaved with tolerable kindness at this season of trouble; for whenever I wanted money I had to coax her, and whenever I coaxed her I was sure of bringing this weak and light-minded woman to good-humour: who was of such a weak terrified nature, that to secure an easy week with me she would sign away a thousand a year. And when my troubles began at Hackton, and I determined on the only chance left, viz. to retire to Ireland and retrench, assigning over the best part of my income to the creditors until their demands were met, my Lady was quite cheerful at the idea of going, and said, if we would be quiet, she had no doubt all would be well; indeed, was glad to undergo the comparative poverty in which we must now live for the sake of the retirement and the chance of domestic quiet which she hoped to enjoy.
We went off to Bristol pretty suddenly, leaving the odious and ungrateful wretches at Hackton to vilify us, no doubt, in our absence. My stud and hounds were sold off immediately; the harpies would have been glad to pounce upon my person; but that was out of their power.

===Chinese===

可是，尽管我昂然迎击控我的人，尽管我在选举里大把撒钱，尽管我敞开哈克顿府的大门，香槟、勃艮第在那里、在城里我包下的各处客栈，放得同自来水一般贱——选举还是输了。那班无赖士绅齐齐倒戈，投了蒂普托夫一党：甚至有话传出来，说我把老婆强押在手里；尽管我打发她独自进城，佩着我的彩带，膝上坐着布莱恩，去拜会市长夫人和城中各位头面女眷，人们却咬定了她在我手底下活得战战兢兢；下流的暴民还斗胆当面问她：怎么敢回去？马鞭子就晚饭，滋味如何？
选举落了败，一应账单便齐齐压将下来——成婚这些年我赊下的全部账单，债主们步调一致、用心卑鄙，一古脑儿送到，摞在我桌上成了小山。数目我便不引了：骇人听闻。我的管事和律师把事情弄得更糟。我被裹进票据与债务、抵押与保险以及随之而来的一切可怕祸患织成的乱网，脱身不得。律师一批接一批从伦敦驰驿而下；和解契约订了一张又一张；为喂饱这些鸬鹚，林登伯爵夫人的进项几乎被捆缚得无可挽回。凭良心说，在这多事之秋，她的行事还算厚道：因为每逢我等钱使，少不得哄她，而每逢我哄她，这位耳软心活的女人准能叫我哄出好脾气来；她天性怯懦惊惶，为求同我安安稳稳过上一礼拜，一年一千镑也肯落笔让掉。及至哈克顿的祸事开了端，我拿定了仅剩的一条活路：退往爱尔兰，厉行省缩，把我收入的大头指派给债主，直到他们的索求了结——伯爵夫人一听要走，倒相当爽利，说只要我们肯安分，不怕一切都会好起来；为了退隐清居、为了她指望享到的一份家宅安宁，如今这相形见绌的清苦，她当真甘之如饴。

===Original===

I had raised, by cleverness and management, to the full as much on my mines and private estates as they were worth; so the scoundrels were disappointed in *this* instance; and as for the plate and property in the London house, they could not touch that, as it was the property of the heirs of the house of Lyndon.
I passed over to Ireland, then, and took up my abode at Castle Lyndon for a while; all the world imagining that I was an utterly ruined man, and that the famous and dashing Barry Lyndon would never again appear in the circles of which he had been an ornament. But it was not so. In the midst of my perplexities, Fortune reserved a great consolation for me still. Despatches came home from America announcing Lord Cornwallis’s defeat of General Gates in Carolina, and the death of Lord Bullingdon, who was present as a volunteer.
For my own desires to possess a paltry Irish title I cared little. My son was now heir to an English earldom, and I made him assume forthwith the title of Lord Viscount Castle Lyndon, the third of the family titles. My mother went almost mad with joy at saluting her grandson as “my Lord,” and I felt that all my sufferings and privations were repaid by seeing this darling child advanced to such a post of honour.

===Chinese===

我们相当仓猝地取道布里斯托尔走了，撇下哈克顿那群可憎的负义东西——他们这时多半正在我们背后败坏我们。我的马群与猎犬随即变卖一空；那些鹰鸷恨不能扑到我个人身上来；只是那是他们办不到的。凭着机变与手腕，我在矿场和私产上筹措的钱，早已足抵那些产业的全值；这一层上，恶棍们落了一场空；至于伦敦宅邸里的银器什物，他们更是碰也碰不得——那是林登家嗣子的产业。
于是我渡海到了爱尔兰，在林登城堡住了些时日；全世界都当我彻底破产，那位名噪一时、神气活现的巴里·林登，再不会回到他曾为之生色的那个圈子里去了。然而不然。在我困顿的当口，命运还替我藏着一份大大的安慰。军报从美洲传到：康沃利斯勋爵（Lord Cornwallis）在卡罗来纳击溃盖茨将军（General Gates）；布林登勋爵阵亡——他正以义勇兵之身在军中。
至于我自己想弄一顶小小爱尔兰爵位的念想，我已不甚放在心上。我儿子如今是英格兰伯爵爵位的继承人，我当即着他称林登城堡子爵勋爵（Lord Viscount Castle Lyndon）——家族封号中的第三个。我母亲口口声声“勋爵大人”、向孙儿道贺，欢喜得几乎发了狂；眼见这心肝宝贝擢升到这般荣耀的高位，我觉得自己吃的一切苦、受的一切穷，都得了补偿。

## I My Pedigree and Family﻿—Undergo the Influence of the Tender Passion / 我的家世与家族——如何受柔情支配
===Original===

Since the days of Adam, there has been hardly a mischief done in this world but a woman has been at the bottom of it. Ever since ours was a family (and that must be very *near* Adam’s time﻿—so old, noble, and illustrious are the Barrys, as everybody knows) women have played a mighty part with the destinies of our race.
I presume that there is no gentleman in Europe that has not heard of the house of Barry of Barryogue, of the kingdom of Ireland, than which a more famous name is not to be found in Gwillim or D’Hozier; and though, as a man of the world, I have learned to despise heartily the claims of some *pretenders* to high birth who have no more genealogy than the lackey who cleans my boots, and though I laugh to utter scorn the boasting of many of my countrymen, who are all for descending from kings of Ireland, and talk of a domain no bigger than would feed a pig as if it were a principality; yet truth compels me to assert that my family was the noblest of the island, and, perhaps, of the universal world; while their possessions, now insignificant and torn from us by war, by treachery, by the loss of time, by ancestral extravagance, by adhesion to the old faith and monarch, were formerly prodigious, and embraced many counties, at a time when Ireland was vastly more prosperous than now. I would assume the Irish crown over my coat-of-arms, but that there are so many silly pretenders to that distinction who bear it and render it common.
Who knows, but for the fault of a woman, I might have been wearing it now? You start with incredulity. I say, why not? Had there been a gallant chief to lead my countrymen, instead of puling knaves who bent the knee to King Richard II, they might have been freemen; had there been a resolute leader to meet the murderous ruffian Oliver Cromwell, we should have shaken off the English forever. But there was no Barry in the field against the usurper; on the contrary, my ancestor, Simon de Bary, came over with the first-named monarch, and married the daughter of the then King of Munster, whose sons in battle he pitilessly slew.
In Oliver’s time it was too late for a chief of the name of Barry to lift up his war-cry against that of the murderous brewer. We were princes of the land no longer; our unhappy race had lost its possessions a century previously, and by the most shameful treason.

===Chinese===

自亚当的时代以来，这世上几乎没有一桩祸事，背后没有一个女人。自从我们家成为一个家族起（那必定离亚当的时代很*近*——巴里家族有多么古老、高贵、显赫，众所周知），女人们便一直在我们家族的命运中扮演着举足轻重的角色。
我想，欧洲没有一位绅士不曾听说过爱尔兰王国巴里奥格的巴里世家——在吉利姆或德奥齐耶的谱牒里，再找不出比它更响亮的姓氏；虽说作为一个见过世面的人，我早已学会打心底里鄙夷某些*僭称*出身高贵之徒——他们的家谱不见得比替我擦靴子的跟班更长；虽说我对许多同胞的吹嘘嗤之以鼻——他们个个自称爱尔兰国王的后裔，把一块只够喂猪的田产说成一方公国——然而真理迫使我声明：我的家族是这座岛屿上最高贵的家族，或许也是普天之下最高贵的；如今我们那份微不足道的家产，虽已被战乱、背叛、时移世易、祖辈的挥霍、以及对旧信仰和旧君王的效忠撕扯而去，但在往昔——爱尔兰远比今日富庶的年代——它们曾广袤得惊人，囊括许多郡县。我本想在纹章上端端正正加上爱尔兰王冠，只可惜觊觎这殊荣的蠢货太多，戴的人一多，反倒使它俗气了。
谁说得准呢？若不是一个女人误事，我说不定如今正戴着它。你听了直摇头不信。我问：有何不可？当年若有一位豪迈的首领来带领我的同胞，而不是那班向理查二世屈膝的哭包无赖，他们早成了自由人；若有一位果决的领袖迎击那个杀人不眨眼的恶棍奥利弗·克伦威尔，我们早把英国人永远甩脱了。可惜战场上没有巴里家的人去讨伐那个篡位者；恰恰相反，我的祖先西蒙·德·巴里，是随头一位君王渡海而来的，还娶了当时芒斯特国王的女儿——她的几个兄弟，都在战场上被他无情地杀掉了。
到了奥利弗的时代，巴里家的首领再要向那个杀人如麻的酿酒匠发出战吼，为时已晚。我们已不再是这片土地上的亲王；我们不幸的家族早在百年之前便失去了领地——而且是毁于最无耻的背叛。

===Original===

This I know to be the fact, for my mother has often told me the story, and besides had worked it in a worsted pedigree which hung up in the yellow saloon at Barryville where we lived.
That very estate which the Lyndons now possess in Ireland was once the property of my race. Rory Barry of Barryogue owned it in Elizabeth’s time, and half Munster beside. The Barry was always in feud with the O’Mahonys in those times; and, as it happened, a certain English colonel passed through the former’s country with a body of men-at-arms, on the very day when the O’Mahonys had made an inroad upon our territories, and carried off a frightful plunder of our flocks and herds.
This young Englishman, whose name was Roger Lyndon, Linden, or Lyndaine, having been most hospitably received by the Barry, and finding him just on the point of carrying an inroad into the O’Mahonys’ land, offered the aid of himself and his lances, and behaved himself so well, as it appeared, that the O’Mahonys were entirely overcome, all the Barrys’ property restored, and with it, says the old chronicle, twice as much of the O’Mahonys’ goods and cattle.
It was the setting in of the winter season, and the young soldier was pressed by the Barry not to quit his house of Barryogue, and remained there during several months, his men being quartered with Barry’s own gallowglasses, man by man in the cottages round about. They conducted themselves, as is their wont, with the most intolerable insolence towards the Irish; so much so, that fights and murders continually ensued, and the people vowed to destroy them.
The Barry’s son (from whom I descend) was as hostile to the English as any other man on his domain; and, as they would not go when bidden, he and his friends consulted together and determined on destroying these English to a man.
But they had let a woman into their plot, and this was the Barry’s daughter. She was in love with the English Lyndon, and broke the whole secret to him; and the dastardly English prevented the just massacre of themselves by falling on the Irish, and destroying Phaudrig Barry, my ancestor, and many hundreds of his men. The cross at Barrycross near Carrignadihioul is the spot where the odious butchery took place.

===Chinese===

这事我知道确凿无疑，因为我母亲常对我讲这段故事，还用绒线把它绣成一幅家谱，挂在我们所住的巴里维尔那间黄色客厅里。
如今林登家族在爱尔兰占有的那份田产，正是我家族的旧业。伊丽莎白时代，巴里奥格的罗里·巴里拥有它，外加半个芒斯特。那时候，巴里家与奥马霍尼家世代结仇；恰巧某日，一位英国上校带着一队武装士兵路过巴里家的地界，而正是在那一天，奥马霍尼家侵入我们的领地，把我们的牛羊畜群掳掠了个痛快。
这位年轻的英国人名叫罗杰·林登——抑或林登、抑或林丹，拼法不一——他受到巴里家的殷勤款待，眼见主人正要杀入奥马霍尼家的地界，便主动提出携长枪队助战，而且——据称——表现得极为出色：奥马霍尼人被彻底击溃，巴里家被掠的财物尽数夺回，据老编年史记载，还额外缴获了两倍于失物的奥马霍尼家的财货牛羊。
时值初冬，巴里再三挽留这位年轻军人在巴里奥格宅中盘桓，他一住数月；他的部下与巴里家的武装随从一一分散，安置在四周的农舍里。这些人照他们惯常的脾气对待爱尔兰人，骄横得令人忍无可忍，斗殴凶案接连不断，当地百姓发誓要把他们斩尽杀绝。
巴里的儿子（我的直系先祖）对英国人的仇视，不亚于领地上的任何人；既然好言相劝他们不肯走，他便与友人们密议，决意把这批英国人一个不留地消灭掉。
可是他们让一个女人参与了密谋——她就是巴里的女儿。她爱上了英国人林登，把全部秘密和盘托出；于是那帮怯懦的英国人先发制人，扑向爱尔兰人，杀死了我的祖先福德里克·巴里和他数百名部众。卡里格纳迪霍尔附近巴里十字的那座十字架，便是那场可憎屠杀的发生地。

===Original===

Lyndon married the daughter of Roderick Barry, and claimed the estate which he left: and though the descendants of Phaudrig were alive, as indeed they are in my person,1 on appealing to the English courts, the estate was awarded to the Englishman, as has ever been the case where English and Irish were concerned.
Thus, had it not been for the weakness of a woman, I should have been born to the possession of those very estates which afterwards came to me by merit, as you shall hear. But to proceed with my family, history.
My father was well known to the best circles in this kingdom, as in that of Ireland, under the name of Roaring Harry Barry. He was bred like many other young sons of genteel families to the profession of the law, being articled to a celebrated attorney of Sackville Street in the city of Dublin; and, from his great genius and aptitude for learning, there is no doubt he would have made an eminent figure in his profession, had not his social qualities, love of field-sports, and extraordinary graces of manner, marked him out for a higher sphere. While he was attorney’s clerk he kept seven racehorses, and hunted regularly both with the Kildare and Wicklow hunts; and rode on his grey horse Endymion that famous match against Captain Punter, which is still remembered by lovers of the sport, and of which I caused a splendid picture to be made and hung over my dining-hall mantelpiece at Castle Lyndon. A year afterwards he had the honour of riding that very horse Endymion before his late Majesty King George II at Newmarket, and won the plate there and the attention of the august sovereign.
Although he was only the second son of our family, my dear father came naturally into the estate (now miserably reduced to £400 a year); for my grandfather’s eldest son Cornelius Barry (called the Chevalier Borgne, from a wound which he received in Germany) remained constant to the old religion in which our family was educated, and not only served abroad with credit, but against His Most Sacred Majesty George II in the unhappy Scotch disturbances in ’45. We shall hear more of the Chevalier hereafter.
For the conversion of my father I have to thank my dear mother, Miss Bell Brady, daughter of Ulysses Brady of Castle Brady, county Kerry, Esquire and J.P. She was the most beautiful woman of her day in Dublin, and universally called the Dasher there.

===Chinese===

林登娶了罗德里克·巴里的女儿，并索占了死者留下的田产：尽管福德里克的后裔还活着——事实上就活在我身上1——一告到英国法庭，田产还是判给了英国人；凡是英国人与爱尔兰人对簿公堂，向来都是这个结局。
可见，若不是那一个女人心志软弱，我本该生来就坐拥那些田产——后来它们凭我的本事重新落到我手中，此是后话。闲言少叙，接着讲我的家史。
家父在本王国乃至爱尔兰的上流圈子里都鼎鼎有名，人称“吼哈里·巴里”。他像许多体面人家的次子一样被送去学法律，在都柏林城萨克维尔街一位著名律师手下当学徒；以他过人的天资与向学之心，毫无疑问他本会在法律界出人头地，若非他的社交才能、对田野狩猎的热爱以及出众的风度举止，注定他要往更高的圈子里去。他当律师学徒时就养着七匹赛马，定期随基尔代尔和威克洛两处猎队出猎；还骑着他那匹灰色骏马恩底弥翁，同庞特上尉进行过那场著名的比赛——至今仍为赛马爱好者所铭记；我曾命人为这场赛事绘制一幅气势恢宏的画，挂在林登城堡我饭厅的壁炉上方。一年之后，他更有幸骑着这匹恩底弥翁，在纽马基特御前为已故君主乔治二世国王陛下献技，赢得了那儿的奖盘，也赢得了那位威严君王的垂青。
家父虽只是家中次子，却顺理成章地承继了家业（如今可怜地缩水到每年四百镑）；因为我祖父的长子科尼利厄斯·巴里（因在德国作战时受过一处伤，人称“独眼骑士”）始终坚守我们家族世代受教的旧教，不但以战功效力于海外，还在四五年苏格兰那场不幸的动乱中，同神圣的乔治二世陛下兵戎相见。这位骑士，后文还有交代。
家父的改宗，得归功于我亲爱的母亲——贝尔·布雷迪小姐，克里郡布雷迪城堡的乌利塞斯·布雷迪老爷兼治安官的千金。她是当年都柏林最美的女人，人人称她“风头贝儿”。

===Original===

Seeing her at the assembly, my father became passionately attached to her; but her soul was above marrying a Papist or an attorney’s clerk; and so, for the love of her, the good old laws being then in force, my dear father slipped into my uncle Cornelius’s shoes and took the family estate. Besides the force of my mother’s bright eyes, several persons, and of the genteelest society too, contributed to this happy change; and I have often heard my mother laughingly tell the story of my father’s recantation, which was solemnly pronounced at the tavern in the company of Sir Dick Ringwood, Lord Bagwig, Captain Punter, and two or three other young sparks of the town. Roaring Harry won 300 pieces that very night at faro, and laid the necessary information the next morning against his brother; but his conversion caused a coolness between him and my uncle Corney, who joined the rebels in consequence.
This great difficulty being settled, my Lord Bagwig lent my father his own yacht, then lying at the Pigeon House, and the handsome Bell Brady was induced to run away with him to England, although her parents were against the match, and her lovers (as I have heard her tell many thousands of times) were among the most numerous and the most wealthy in all the kingdom of Ireland. They were married at the Savoy, and my grandfather dying very soon, Harry Barry, Esquire, took possession of his paternal property and supported our illustrious name with credit in London. He pinked the famous Count Tiercelin behind Montague House, he was a member of White’s, and a frequenter of all the chocolate-houses; and my mother, likewise, made no small figure. At length, after his great day of triumph before His Sacred Majesty at Newmarket, Harry’s fortune was just on the point of being made, for the gracious monarch promised to provide for him. But alas! he was taken in charge by another monarch, whose will have no delay or denial﻿—by Death, namely, who seized upon my father at Chester races, leaving me a helpless orphan. Peace be to his ashes! He was not faultless, and dissipated all our princely family property; but he was as brave a fellow as ever tossed a bumper or called a main, and he drove his coach-and-six like a man of fashion.

===Chinese===

家父在舞会上见到她，便炽烈地爱上了她；可她的心气高，不肯嫁给一个天主教徒或律师学徒；于是，为了她的爱情——恰逢那些好古的刑法当时还有效——我亲爱的父亲便顺势顶了科尼利厄斯叔叔的位，把家业承继过来。除了我母亲明亮双眸的威力，还有几位人物——也都出自最上流的社交界——促成了这桩美事；我常听母亲笑着讲起家父当众宣布叛教的经过：那是在酒馆里，当着迪克·林伍德爵士、巴威格勋爵、庞特上尉和城里两三位时髦青年的面，郑重其事地宣布的。就在当天夜里，吼哈里在法罗牌桌上赢了三百枚金币，第二天一早便去官府告发了兄长；不过他这一改宗，使得他与科尼舅舅之间生了嫌隙，科尼舅舅一怒之下投奔了叛军。
这桩大难题解决之后，巴威格勋爵把自己停泊在鸽子房的游艇借给了家父，美丽的贝尔·布雷迪便被说动，跟他私奔到了英格兰——尽管她父母反对这门亲事，而她的追求者（这话我听她讲过不知几千遍）在整个爱尔兰王国里也算得上人数最多、家财最厚的一批。两人在萨伏伊成了婚；不久我祖父去世，哈里·巴里老爷便继承了家产，在伦敦为我们显赫的姓氏挣得了体面。他在蒙塔古府背后刺伤过著名的蒂尔瑟兰伯爵，他是怀特俱乐部的会员，各家巧克力屋的常客；我母亲也同样出尽了风头。终于，在纽马基特神圣陛下御前大出风头之后，哈里的前程眼看就要青云直上，因为仁慈的君王许诺要给他安排前程。可惜啊！他落入了另一位君王的掌控——那位君王的旨意容不得拖延和推却——那就是死神。死神在切斯特赛马场上攫走了我父亲，留下我成了无依无靠的孤儿。愿他的骨灰安息！他并非完人，把我们亲王般的家产挥霍得一干二净；但论胆气，举杯痛饮、押局叫阵的好汉里数得着他；他驾起六马大车，那派头俨然一位时髦人物。

===Original===

I do not know whether His gracious Majesty was much affected by this sudden demise of my father, though my mother says he shed some royal tears on the occasion. But they helped us to nothing: and all that was found in the house for the wife and creditors was a purse of ninety guineas, which my dear mother naturally took, with the family plate, and my father’s wardrobe and her own; and putting them into our great coach, drove off to Holyhead, whence she took shipping for Ireland. My father’s body accompanied us in the finest hearse and plumes money could buy; for though the husband and wife had quarrelled repeatedly in life, yet at my father’s death his high-spirited widow forgot all her differences, gave him the grandest funeral that had been seen for many a day, and erected a monument over his remains (for which I subsequently paid), which declared him to be the wisest, purest, and most affectionate of men.
In performing these sad duties over her deceased lord, the widow spent almost every guinea she had, and, indeed, would have spent a great deal more, had she discharged one-third of the demands which the ceremonies occasioned. But the people around our old house of Barryogue, although they did not like my father for his change of faith, yet stood by him at this moment, and were for exterminating the mutes sent by Mr. Plumer of London with the lamented remains. The monument and vault in the church were then, alas! all that remained of my vast possessions; for my father had sold every stick of the property to one Notley, an attorney, and we received but a cold welcome in his house﻿—a miserable old tumbledown place it was.2
The splendour of the funeral did not fail to increase the widow Barry’s reputation as a woman of spirit and fashion; and when she wrote to her brother Michael Brady, that worthy gentleman immediately rode across the country to fling himself in her arms, and to invite her in his wife’s name to Castle Brady.
Mick and Barry had quarrelled, as all men will, and very high words had passed between them during Barry’s courtship of Miss Bell.

===Chinese===

仁慈的陛下对我父亲的猝然离世是否深感悲痛，我不得而知，虽然我母亲说，陛下为此流过几滴王家的眼泪。可那眼泪于我们毫无用处：家里留给寡妻和债主们的全部家当，只有一袋九十枚几尼。我亲爱的母亲顺理成章地收下了它，连同家族的银器、我父亲的衣柜和她自己的衣物；把这一切装进我们的大马车，直奔霍利黑德，从那里乘船去了爱尔兰。我父亲的遗体随行在后，用的是金钱所能买到的最华丽的灵车和羽饰；生前这对夫妻虽屡有争吵，但丈夫一死，这位烈性的寡妇尽释前嫌，为他举办了多年来最盛大的葬礼，还在他遗骨上立了一块纪念碑（碑钱后来是我付的），称颂他是世人中最有智慧、最纯洁、最深情的男人。
为亡夫尽这些伤心的义务，寡妇几乎花光了手头每一枚几尼；说实话，倘若丧仪的各项索费她付足三分之一，花的还要多得多。不过，巴里奥格老宅四邻的乡亲们，虽然因家父叛教而不喜欢他，此刻却站在他这一边，只差没把伦敦的普卢默先生随灵柩派来的扶丧哑仆们撵尽杀绝。唉！教堂里的那块碑和那座墓穴，当时便是我庞大家产所余的一切了；因为家父早已把田产上的一草一木，统统卖给了一个名叫诺特利的律师，我们在他的宅子里受到的接待冷若冰霜——那是一座破败不堪的老房子。2
葬礼的排场果然进一步提升了巴里寡妇作为一位有气派、懂时尚的女人的名声；她写信给弟弟迈克尔·布雷迪，那位可敬的绅士立刻快马加鞭横穿乡野，扑进她的怀抱，并代表妻子邀请她到布雷迪城堡去住。米克同巴里从前吵过架——男人之间在所难免——当年巴里追求贝尔小姐时，两人之间可说过不少极难听的话。

===Original===

When he took her off, Brady swore he would never forgive Barry or Bell; but coming to London in the year ’46, he fell in once more with Roaring Harry, and lived in his fine house in Clarges Street, and lost a few pieces to him at play, and broke a watchman’s head or two in his company﻿—all of which reminiscences endeared Bell and her son very much to the good-hearted gentleman, and he received us both with open arms. Mrs. Barry did not, perhaps wisely, at first make known to her friends what was her condition; but arriving in a huge gilt coach with enormous armorial bearings, was taken by her sister-in-law and the rest of the county for a person of considerable property and distinction. For a time, then, and as was right and proper, Mrs. Barry gave the law at Castle Brady. She ordered the servants to and fro, and taught them, what indeed they much wanted, a little London neatness; and “English Redmond,” as I was called, was treated like a little lord, and had a maid and a footman to himself; and honest Mick paid their wages﻿—which was much more than he was used to do for his own domestics﻿—doing all in his power to make his sister decently comfortable under her afflictions. Mamma, in return, determined that, when her affairs were arranged, she would make her kind brother a handsome allowance for her son’s maintenance and her own; and promised to have her handsome furniture brought over from Clarges Street to adorn the somewhat dilapidated rooms of Castle Brady.
But it turned out that the rascally landlord seized upon every chair and table that ought by rights to have belonged to the widow. The estate to which I was heir was in the hands of rapacious creditors; and the only means of subsistence remaining to the widow and child was a rent-charge of £50 upon my Lord Bagwig’s property, who had many turf-dealings with the deceased. And so my dear mother’s liberal intentions towards her brother were of course never fulfilled.
It must be confessed, very much to the discredit of Mrs. Brady of Castle Brady, that when her sister-in-law’s poverty was thus made manifest, she forgot all the respect which she had been accustomed to pay her, instantly turned my maid and manservant out of doors, and told Mrs. Barry that she might follow them as soon as she chose. Mrs.

===Chinese===

当年巴里把贝尔拐走，布雷迪发誓永远不原谅巴里和贝尔；可到了四六年他来伦敦，又同吼哈里重归于好，住进克拉杰斯街那座漂亮宅邸，赌钱输给他几个金币，还跟着他敲破过一两个更夫的脑袋——凡此种种旧谊，都叫贝尔和她儿子在这位好心肠的绅士心里愈发可亲，于是他把我们母子俩张开双臂迎进了门。巴里太太起初并没有把自己的境况向亲友们和盘托出——这或许不算明智；她乘着一辆饰有巨大纹章的镀金大马车而来，被弟媳妇和全郡的人当成了一位家资巨万、身份贵显的人物。于是一时间——这也是合情合理的——布雷迪城堡里由巴里太太发号施令。她把仆人们差遣得团团转，教给他们一点伦敦式的利落——这的确是他们很欠缺的；而我，人称“英国雷德蒙”的那一位，被当作小老爷一样伺候，专属一个女仆、一个男仆；老实的米克替他们付工钱——这可比他对自己家里的仆人大方多了——竭尽所能让妹妹在落难之中过得体面舒适。妈妈呢，作为回报，打定主意等她的事务料理停当，就给好心的弟弟一笔可观的生活津贴，供母子二人花销；还许诺要把克拉杰斯街那些漂亮家具运过来，装点布雷迪城堡里略显破旧的房间。
谁知那无赖的房东把理应归寡妇所有的桌椅板凳，扣押了个干净。我名下继承的产业落在贪得无厌的债主们手里；寡妇和孩子仅剩的活路，是巴威格勋爵田产上每年五十镑的租金收益——勋爵生前同死者做过不少赛马生意。于是我亲爱的母亲对弟弟那番慷慨的心愿，自然也就永远没有兑现。
必须承认——这话大大有损布雷迪城堡布雷迪太太的名声——当她小姑子的穷相一旦暴露，她便把先前对她惯有的敬意忘了个一干二净，立刻把我的女仆和男仆撵出门去，还叫巴里太太愿意走就尽早跟着走。

===Original===

Mick was of a low family, and a sordid way of thinking; and after about a couple of years (during which she had saved almost all her little income) the widow complied with Madam Brady’s desire. At the same time, giving way to a just though prudently dissimulated resentment, she made a vow that she would never enter the gates of Castle Brady while the lady of the house remained alive within them.
She fitted up her new abode with much economy and considerable taste, and never, for all her poverty, abated a jot of the dignity which was her due and which all the neighbourhood awarded to her. How, indeed, could they refuse respect to a lady who had lived in London, frequented the most fashionable society there, and had been presented (as she solemnly declared) at Court? These advantages gave her a right which seems to be pretty unsparingly exercised in Ireland by those natives who have it﻿—the right of looking down with scorn upon all persons who have not had the opportunity of quitting the mother-country and inhabiting England for a while. Thus, whenever Madam Brady appeared abroad in a new dress, her sister-in-law would say, “Poor creature! how can it be expected that she should know anything of the fashion?” And though pleased to be called the handsome widow, as she was, Mrs. Barry was still better pleased to be called the English widow.
Mrs. Brady, for her part, was not slow to reply: she used to say that the defunct Barry was a bankrupt and a beggar; and as for the fashionable society which he saw, he saw it from my Lord Bagwig’s side-table, whose flatterer and hanger-on he was known to be. Regarding Mrs. Barry, the lady of Castle Brady would make insinuations still more painful. However, why should we allude to these charges, or rake up private scandal of a hundred years old? It was in the reign of George II that the above-named personages lived and quarrelled; good or bad, handsome or ugly, rich or poor, they are all equal now; and do not the Sunday papers and the courts of law supply us every week with more novel and interesting slander?
At any rate, it must be allowed that Mrs. Barry, after her husband’s death and her retirement, lived in such a way as to defy slander.

===Chinese===

布雷迪太太——米克·布雷迪的太太——出身低微，心思寒酸；约莫两年之后（这两年里寡妇几乎攒下了自己全部的些微收入），寡妇依了布雷迪太太的意思搬了出去。与此同时，她心中怀着一份义愤——虽说被她审慎地掩藏了起来——并起下誓言：只要这座宅子的女主人在世一日，她绝不踏进布雷迪城堡的大门。
她以极度的节俭和相当的眼光布置了新居；尽管穷困，她却从不曾减损一分一毫她应得的尊严——四邻八舍也都把这份尊严归于她。说实在的，一位在伦敦住过、出入过最时髦社交界、还（据她郑重宣称）进宫觐见过御驾的夫人，他们怎能不尊崇呢？这些优越之处给了她一种权利——在爱尔兰，凡有此经历的本地人行使起这种权利来似乎都毫不留情——即带着轻蔑俯视一切无福离开故土、去英格兰住上一阵的人的权利。因此，每当布雷迪太太穿了新衣裳出门，她那位小姑子就会说：“可怜的东西！还能指望她懂什么时髦吗？”巴里太太固然乐意被人称作俊俏的寡妇——她也确实当得起——但更乐意被人称作英国寡妇。
布雷迪太太这方面也不甘示弱地回敬：她常说，死去的巴里是个破产的叫花子；至于他见过的什么时髦社交界，那是从巴威格勋爵的偏席上看来的——谁都知道，他是勋爵的马屁精和跟班。说到巴里太太本人，布雷迪城堡的这位女主人的含沙射影就更刻毒了。罢了，何必再提这些指控，去翻一百年前的私家丑闻呢？上述这些人物，生活并争吵在乔治二世朝；是好是歹，是俊是丑，是贫是富，如今他们都一律平等了；何况每周的周日报纸和法庭讼案，供给我们新鲜有趣的诽谤还少吗？无论如何，总得承认：巴里太太自丈夫死后退居以来，其行止做派，足以令一切谗言却步。

===Original===

For whereas Bell Brady had been the gayest girl in the whole county of Wexford, with half the bachelors at her feet, and plenty of smiles and encouragement for every one of them, Bell Barry adopted a dignified reserve that almost amounted to pomposity, and was as starch as any Quakeress. Many a man renewed his offers to the widow, who had been smitten by the charms of the spinster; but Mrs. Barry refused all offers of marriage, declaring that she lived now for her son only, and for the memory of her departed saint.
“Saint forsooth!” said ill-natured Mrs. Brady.
“Harry Barry was as big a sinner as ever was known; and ’tis notorious that he and Bell hated each other. If she won’t marry now, depend on it, the artful woman has a husband in her eye for all that, and only waits until Lord Bagwig is a widower.”
And suppose she did, what then? Was not the widow of a Barry fit to marry with any lord of England? and was it not always said that a woman was to restore the fortunes of the Barry family? If my mother fancied that *she* was to be that woman, I think it was a perfectly justifiable notion on her part; for the Earl (my godfather) was always most attentive to her: I never knew how deeply this notion of advancing my interests in the world had taken possession of mamma’s mind, until his Lordship’s marriage in the year ’57 with Miss Goldmore, the Indian nabob’s rich daughter.
Meanwhile we continued to reside at Barryville, and, considering the smallness of our income, kept up a wonderful state. Of the half-dozen families that formed the congregation at Brady’s Town, there was not a single person whose appearance was so respectable as that of the widow, who, though she always dressed in mourning, in memory of her deceased husband, took care that her garments should be made so as to set off her handsome person to the greatest advantage; and, indeed, I think, spent six hours out of every day in the week in cutting, trimming, and altering them to the fashion. She had the largest of hoops and the handsomest of furbelows, and once a month (under my Lord Bagwig’s cover) would come a letter from London containing the newest accounts of the fashions there. Her complexion was so brilliant that she had no call to use rouge, as was the mode in those days.

===Chinese===

要知道，做姑娘时，贝尔·布雷迪曾是整个韦克斯福德郡最快乐的姑娘，半数的单身汉拜倒在她的裙下，她对人人都不吝微笑和鼓励；而成了巴里太太之后，她端起一种庄重的矜持，几乎到了摆架子的地步，古板得赛过贵格会女教徒。当年倾倒于姑娘魅力的许多男人，又纷纷向寡妇重申求婚；但巴里太太一概回绝，宣称她如今只为儿子而活，只为纪念她那位升天的圣人。
“圣人，亏她说得出口！”刻薄的布雷迪太太说。“哈里·巴里是有名的大罪人；而且谁都知道，他们两口子互相恨得要死。她如今不肯改嫁，你只管等着瞧，这个精明的女人心里早有了夫婿的人选，只等巴威格勋爵做了鳏夫呢。”
就算她真这么打算，又有什么不可以？巴里家的寡妇难道配不上英格兰任何一位勋爵？况且人家不是一直说，巴里家要靠一个女人重振家业吗？倘若我母亲自以为*她*便是那个女人，我认为这念头在她再合情理不过；因为伯爵（我的教父）对她一向殷勤备至：直到五七年勋爵同戈德莫尔小姐——那位印度大富豪的富有千金——成婚，我才知道“为我在世上谋前程”这个念头，在妈妈心里扎得有多深。
这期间我们继续住在巴里维尔，以我们的微薄收入而论，那份排场维持得实在惊人。布雷迪镇教堂会众中的那六七户人家，没有一个人的气派比得上这位寡妇；她虽为悼念亡夫常年身着丧服，却务必叫衣裳裁得最大限度衬托她美好的身段；说实话，我想她一周里每天都要花六个钟头裁剪、镶边、翻改，务求赶上时新样式。她有最大的裙撑、最漂亮的荷叶边；每月一次，伦敦会寄来一封信（借巴威格勋爵的名义转来），报告那边最新的时兴样式。她的肤色天生光彩照人，用不着像当时风尚那样搽胭脂。

===Original===

No, she left red and white, she said (and hence the reader may imagine how the two ladies hated each other) to Madam Brady, whose yellow complexion no plaster could alter. In a word, she was so accomplished a beauty, that all the women in the country took pattern by her, and the young fellows from ten miles round would ride over to Castle Brady church to have the sight of her.
But if (like every other woman that ever I saw or read of) she was proud of her beauty, to do her justice she was still more proud of her son, and has said a thousand times to me that I was the handsomest young fellow in the world. This is a matter of taste. A man of sixty may, however, say what he was at fourteen without much vanity, and I must say I think there was some cause for my mother’s opinion. The good soul’s pleasure was to dress me; and on Sundays and holidays I turned out in a velvet coat with a silver-hilted sword by my side and a gold garter at my knee, as fine as any lord in the land. My mother worked me several most splendid waistcoats, and I had plenty of lace for my ruffles, and a fresh riband to my hair, and as we walked to church on Sundays, even envious Mrs. Brady was found to allow that there was not a prettier pair in the kingdom.
Of course, too, the lady of Castle Brady used to sneer, because on these occasions a certain Tim, who used to be called my valet, followed me and my mother to church, carrying a huge prayerbook and a cane, and dressed in the livery of one of our own fine footmen from Clarges Street, which, as Tim was a bandy-shanked little fellow, did not exactly become him. But, though poor, we were gentlefolks, and not to be sneered out of these becoming appendages to our rank; and so would march up the aisle to our pew with as much state and gravity as the Lord Lieutenant’s lady and son might do. When there, my mother would give the responses and amens in a loud dignified voice that was delightful to hear, and, besides, had a fine loud voice for singing, which art she had perfected in London under a fashionable teacher; and she would exercise her talent in such a way that you would hardly hear any other voice of the little congregation which chose to join in the psalm. In fact, my mother had great gifts in every way, and believed herself to be one of the most beautiful, accomplished, and meritorious persons in the world.

===Chinese===

不错，红脂白粉，她说，都让给布雷迪太太用了（读者由此可以想见这两位夫人彼此何等仇视）——反正后者的黄脸盘，什么膏子也漂不白。总而言之，她是那样一位技艺精湛的美人，全国的女子都以她为榜样，方圆十英里的年轻人专程骑马赶到布雷迪城堡教堂，只为看她一眼。
不过，倘若她（同我见过或读说过的所有女人一样）为自己的美貌而骄傲，那么说句公道话，她更为她的儿子骄傲；她对我讲过一千遍，说我是世上最俊美的少年。这是个见仁见智的问题。然而，一个六十岁的人讲述自己十四岁时的模样，总归算不上多么自负；我必须说，我母亲的看法确有几分道理。这位好人的乐事就是给我打扮；每逢礼拜天和节日，我出门总穿着天鹅绒外衣，腰佩银柄佩剑，膝上扎着金袜带，气派不输国内任何一位爵爷。母亲亲手给我绣了好几件华丽无比的背心，我的袖口有的是花边，头发上系着簇新的缎带；礼拜天我们母子步行去教堂，连爱嫉妒的布雷迪太太也不得不承认，全王国再找不出比我们更漂亮的一对儿了。
当然，布雷迪城堡的女主人照例要讥笑几句，因为每逢这种场合，总有一个名叫蒂姆的、号称是我贴身男仆的家伙，捧着一部巨大的祈祷书和一根手杖，跟在我们母子身后上教堂；他穿着我们克拉杰斯街漂亮男仆的号衣——可惜蒂姆是个罗圈腿的小个子，这身行头实在不怎么合他的身。但是，我们虽穷，却是上等人，休想讥笑几声就把我们这等与身份相称的排场吓掉；于是我们沿着过道走向自家包座，那份威仪端庄，直比得上总督大人的夫人和公子。到了教堂里，我母亲总是用洪亮而庄重的嗓音应答经文、唱诵阿们，煞是悦耳；她还有一副唱圣歌的好嗓子，这门功夫她在伦敦师从一位时髦教师练得炉火纯青；她施展起来，小小会众里即便有人愿意同唱赞美诗，你也几乎听不见别的声音。事实上，我母亲各方面的禀赋都极为出众，而且深信自己是世上最美丽、最多才多艺、最有德行的人物之一。

===Original===

Often and often has she talked to me and the neighbours regarding her own humility and piety, pointing them out in such a way that I would defy the most obstinate to disbelieve her.
When we left Castle Brady we came to occupy a house in Brady’s town, which mamma christened Barryville. I confess it was but a small place, but, indeed, we made the most of it. I have mentioned the family pedigree which hung up in the drawingroom, which mamma called the yellow saloon, and my bedroom was called the pink bedroom, and hers the orange tawny apartment (how well I remember them all!); and at dinnertime Tim regularly rang a great bell, and we each had a silver tankard to drink from, and mother boasted with justice that I had as good a bottle of claret by my side as any squire of the land. So indeed I had, but I was not, of course, allowed at my tender years to drink any of the wine; which thus attained a considerable age, even in the decanter.
Uncle Brady (in spite of the family quarrel) found out the above fact one day by calling at Barryville at dinnertime, and unluckily tasting the liquor. You should have seen how he sputtered and made faces! But the honest gentleman was not particular about his wine, or the company in which he drank it. He would get drunk, indeed, with the parson or the priest indifferently; with the latter, much to my mother’s indignation, for, as a true blue Nassauite, she heartily despised all those of the old faith, and would scarcely sit down in the room with a benighted Papist. But the squire had no such scruples; he was, indeed, one of the easiest, idlest, and best-natured fellows that ever lived, and many an hour would he pass with the lonely widow when he was tired of Madam Brady at home. He liked me, he said, as much as one of his own sons, and at length, after the widow had held out for a couple of years, she agreed to allow me to return to the castle; though, for herself, she resolutely kept the oath which she had made with regard to her sister-in-law.
The very first day I returned to Castle Brady my trials may be said, in a manner, to have begun. My cousin, Master Mick, a huge monster of nineteen (who hated me, and I promise you I returned the compliment), insulted me at dinner about my mother’s poverty, and made all the girls of the family titter.

===Chinese===

她时常对我、也对邻居们谈起她自己的谦逊与虔诚，而且指点得那样明白，即便最顽固的人，我也敢保不敢不信。
我们离开布雷迪城堡后，搬进了布雷迪镇上的一座宅子，妈妈给它行了个命名礼，唤作“巴里维尔”。我承认那只是个小小的居所，但我们确实把它利用到了极致。我说过，客厅——妈妈管它叫黄色客厅——里挂着那幅家谱；我的卧室唤作粉色卧室，她的是橙褐色套房（这一切我都记得多清楚啊！）；每到开饭时分，蒂姆照例敲响一口大钟；我们各用一只银质大杯饮酒；母亲理直气壮地夸口说，我手边的红葡萄酒，同国内任何乡绅的相比都不逊色。确实不逊色——只是在我那稚嫩的年纪，自然一口也不许沾；于是那酒便陈上了相当的年头，连醒酒瓶里的都算数。
有一天，布雷迪舅舅（不顾两家吵过架）赶在饭点儿登门巴里维尔，不幸尝了尝那酒，这才发现了上述事实。真该看看他喷酒挤眼做怪相的那副模样！不过这位老实绅士对酒倒不挑剔，对同饮的伴儿也不挑剔。说真的，不论同牧师还是同神父，他都能喝个烂醉；同后者对饮，尤其令我母亲愤慨——因为她是个铁杆的奥兰治派，打心眼里蔑视一切旧教徒，连同蒙昧的天主教徒同室而坐都嫌勉强。但这位乡绅可没有这些顾忌；说实在的，他是天底下最随和、最懒惰、最好心的汉子之一；在家里受够了布雷迪太太，他便常常到孤居的寡妇这里消磨上好几个钟头。他说，他喜欢我不亚于喜欢自己的亲生儿子；终于，在寡妇坚持了两年之后，她同意让我回城堡去住；至于她自己，则坚决恪守她对着那位弟媳妇发下的誓言。
我回到布雷迪城堡的头一天，可以说，我的磨难便以某种方式开了场。我表哥米克少爷，一个十九岁的大块头（他恨我，我向您保证，这份情我如数奉还），在饭桌上拿我母亲的穷酸辱骂我，惹得全家的姑娘们咯咯直笑。

===Original===

So when we went to the stables, whither Mick always went for his pipe of tobacco after dinner, I told him a piece of my mind, and there was a fight for at least ten minutes, during which I stood to him like a man, and blacked his left eye, though I was myself only twelve years old at the time. Of course he beat me, but a beating makes only a small impression on a lad of that tender age, as I had proved many times in battles with the ragged Brady’s Town boys before, not one of whom, at my time of life, was my match. My uncle was very much pleased when he heard of my gallantry; my cousin Nora brought brown paper and vinegar for my nose, and I went home that night with a pint of claret under my girdle, not a little proud, let me tell you, at having held my own against Mick so long.
And though he persisted in his bad treatment of me, and used to cane me whenever I fell in his way, yet I was very happy now at Castle Brady with the company there, and my cousins, or some of them, and the kindness of my uncle, with whom I became a prodigious favourite. He bought a colt for me, and taught me to ride. He took me out coursing and fowling, and instructed me to shoot flying. And at length I was released from Mick’s persecution, for his brother, Master Ulick, returning from Trinity College, and hating his elder brother, as is mostly the way in families of fashion, took me under his protection; and from that time, as Ulick was a deal bigger and stronger than Mick, I, English Redmond, as I was called, was left alone; except when the former thought fit to thrash me, which he did whenever he thought proper.
Nor was my learning neglected in the ornamental parts, for I had an uncommon natural genius for many things, and soon topped in accomplishments most of the persons around me. I had a quick ear and a fine voice, which my mother cultivated to the best of her power, and she taught me to step a minuet gravely and gracefully, and thus laid the foundation of my future success in life. The common dances I learned (as, perhaps, I ought not to confess) in the servants’ hall, which, you may be sure, was never without a piper, and where I was considered unrivalled both at a hornpipe and a jig.

===Chinese===

于是等到了马厩——米克饭后总要到那儿抽斗烟——我把心里话一股脑倒给了他，接着便是一场足足十分钟的恶斗；其间我像条汉子似的同他对阵，把他左眼打青了——虽说当时我自己才十二岁。当然，他把我打败了；可一顿打，对那样嫩年纪的孩子印痕实在有限——这一点我早有验证：此前同布雷迪镇上一帮衣衫褴褛的顽童打过多少仗，在我那个岁数，没有一个孩子是我的对手。舅舅听说了我的英勇，非常高兴；表姐诺拉给我拿来了棕色纸和醋，敷我的鼻子；那天夜里我揣着“一品位脱红酒”回了家——行家都懂，挨打见血才叫喝红酒——但不瞒您说，能同米克周旋那么久，我还着实有些得意呢。
尽管他照旧虐待我，一碰见我就用藤条抽我，可我如今在布雷迪城堡过得很快活：有那儿的伙伴，有我的表兄弟姐妹们——至少其中几个——还有舅舅的疼爱，我成了他顶顶偏爱的孩子。他给我买了一匹小马，教我骑术；带我出去追猎野兔、打鸟，教我射击飞禽。终于，我算是从米克的迫害底下解脱了：他弟弟尤利克少爷从三一学院回来了，并且照时髦人家兄弟之间的惯例，恨透了兄长，把我置于他的羽翼之下；从此以后，因为尤利克比米克高大强壮得多，我这个“英国雷德蒙”便无人来惹了——除非前者觉得该揍我一顿，而他也确实想揍就揍。
说到才艺方面的教养，也不曾荒废：我对许多东西有一种非凡的天赋，很快就压过了身边大多数人。我耳朵灵，嗓子好，母亲竭尽所能加以栽培；她教我庄重而优雅地跳小步舞——由此奠下了我日后飞黄腾达的根基。至于普通的舞步，我（说出来也许不大体面）是在下人饭厅里学的——那里，您尽可放心，风笛手是从不缺的——而且在号管舞和吉格舞上，那儿的人公认我无人能敌。

===Original===

In the matter of book-learning, I had always an uncommon taste for reading plays and novels, as the best part of a gentleman’s polite education, and never let a pedlar pass the village, if I had a penny, without having a ballad or two from him. As for your dull grammar, and Greek and Latin and stuff, I have always hated them from my youth upwards, and said, very unmistakably, I would have none of them.
This I proved pretty clearly at the age of thirteen, when my aunt Biddy Brady’s legacy of £100 came in to mamma, who thought to employ the sum on my education, and sent me to Doctor Tobias Tickler’s famous academy at Ballywhacket﻿—Backwhacket, as my uncle used to call it. But six weeks after I had been consigned to his reverence, I suddenly made my appearance again at Castle Brady, having walked forty miles from the odious place, and left the Doctor in a state near upon apoplexy. The fact was, that at taw, prison-bars, or boxing, I was at the head of the school, but could not be brought to excel in the classics; and after having been flogged seven times, without its doing me the least good in my Latin, I refused to submit altogether (finding it useless) to an eighth application of the rod. “Try some other way, sir,” said I, when he was for horsing me once more; but he wouldn’t; whereon, and to defend myself, I flung a slate at him, and knocked down a Scotch usher with a leaden inkstand. All the lads huzza’d at this, and some or the servants wanted to stop me; but taking out a large clasp-knife that my cousin Nora had given me, I swore I would plunge it into the waistcoat of the first man who dared to balk me, and faith they let me pass on. I slept that night twenty miles off Ballywhacket, at the house of a cottier, who gave me potatoes and milk, and to whom I gave a hundred guineas after, when I came to visit Ireland in my days of greatness. I wish I had the money now. But what’s the use of regret? I have had many a harder bed than that I shall sleep on tonight, and many a scantier meal than honest Phil Murphy gave me on the evening I ran away from school. So six weeks’ was all the schooling I ever got.

===Chinese===

至于书本上的学问，我对读戏剧和小说向来有一种不寻常的嗜好——我以为那是一位绅士教养中最好的部分；只要兜里有一便士，我从不放一个小贩进村而不买他一两首歌谣。至于你们那套枯燥的语法，还有希腊文、拉丁文之类的劳什子，我打小就恨之入骨，而且把话说得明明白白：我一概不要。
十三岁那年，我把这一点证明得相当清楚：比迪·布雷迪姨妈留给妈妈的一百镑遗产到了手，妈妈想用这笔钱供我读书，把我送进了托比亚斯·蒂克勒博士设在巴利怀克特的著名学塾——舅舅总管它叫“巴利怀不开特”。可把我托付给那位可敬博士才六个星期，我突然又出现在布雷迪城堡——我从那个可憎的地方一路走了四十英里，把博士气得险些中风。实情是：论弹珠、牢栏棋还是拳击，我在全校都拔尖；可就是没法让我在古典学问上出类拔萃。挨了七顿鞭子，我的拉丁文毫未见长进，于是（发现挨打也无用）我断然拒绝再领教第八顿。“换个法子吧，先生，”他又要让我伏凳受笞时，我说道；他不肯；于是，出于自卫，我朝他扔了一块石板，又用一个铅墨水瓶砸倒了一个苏格兰助教。全体学生为这一手齐声欢呼；有几个仆人想拦我；我掏出表姐诺拉送我的一把大折刀，发誓谁敢拦路，我就把刀捅进谁的背心；说真的，他们就放我过去了。那天夜里我睡在离巴利怀克特二十英里的一个佃农家里；他给了我土豆和牛奶；后来我显赫之时回爱尔兰，送了他一百几尼。那笔钱如今要还在我手里就好了。可悔恨有什么用？我睡过的床，比今晚将要睡的硬得多的多的是；我吃过的饭，比老实的菲尔·墨菲在我逃学那晚给的那顿寒酸得多的也多的是。统共六个星期——这便是此生受过的全部学堂教育。

===Original===

And I say this to let parents know the value of it; for though I have met more learned bookworms in the world, especially a great hulking, clumsy, blear-eyed old doctor, whom they called Johnson, and who lived in a court off Fleet Street, in London, yet I pretty soon silenced him in an argument (at Button’s Coffeehouse); and in that, and in poetry, and what I call natural philosophy, or the science of life, and in riding, music, leaping, the small-sword, the knowledge of a horse, or a main of cocks, and the manners of an accomplished gentleman and a man of fashion, I may say for myself that Redmond Barry has seldom found his equal. “Sir,” said I to Mr. Johnson, on the occasion I allude to﻿—he was accompanied by a Mr. Buswell of Scotland, and I was presented to the club by a Mr. Goldsmith, a countryman of my own﻿—“Sir,” said I, in reply to the schoolmaster’s great thundering quotation in Greek, “you fancy you know a great deal more than me, because you quote your Aristotle and your Pluto; but can you tell me which horse will win at Epsom Downs next week?﻿—Can you run six miles without breathing?﻿—Can you shoot the ace of spades ten times without missing? If so, talk about Aristotle and Pluto to me.”
“D’ye knaw who ye’re speaking to?” roared out the Scotch gentleman, Mr. Boswell, at this.
“Hold your tongue, Mr. Boswell,” said the old schoolmaster. “I had no right to brag of my Greek to the gentleman, and he has answered me very well.”
“Doctor,” says I, looking waggishly at him, “do you know ever a rhyme for Aris*totle*?”
“Port, if you plaise,” says Mr. Goldsmith, laughing. And we had *six rhymes for Aristotle* before we left the coffeehouse that evening. It became a regular joke afterwards when I told the story, and at White’s or the Cocoa-tree you would hear the wags say, “Waiter, bring me one of Captain Barry’s rhymes for Aristotle.” Once, when I was in liquor at the latter place, young Dick Sheridan called me a great Staggerite, a joke which I could never understand. But I am wandering from my story, and must get back to home, and dear old Ireland again.

===Chinese===

我说这话，是要让天下的父母知道学堂的分量；因为虽说世上更有学问的书呆子我也见过——尤其是一位身材臃肿、笨手笨脚、老眼昏花的老博士，人家管他叫约翰逊，住在伦敦舰队街旁的一个院子里——可我（在巴顿咖啡馆）同他辩论，没几回合就叫他哑口无言；而论学问、论诗、论我所谓自然哲学（也就是人生的科学）、论骑术、音乐、跳高、短剑、相马、斗鸡，以及一位多才多艺的绅士和时髦人物的谈吐风度，我可以说，雷德蒙·巴里鲜逢敌手。
“先生，”在我所说的那个场合，我对约翰逊先生说道——他身边陪着一位苏格兰的布斯韦尔先生，我则由一位戈德史密斯先生、我的同乡引荐给俱乐部——“先生，”针对那位教书先生雷霆万钧的希腊语引文，我答道，“您自以为比我懂得多，因为您能引用您的亚里士多德和您的普鲁托；可您能告诉我下星期埃普索姆岗地哪匹马会赢吗？——您能一口气跑六英里吗？——您能十枪十中打中黑桃尖吗？若能，再来同我谈亚里士多德和普鲁托吧。”
“侬晓得侬在同啥人讲话吗？”那位苏格兰绅士博斯韦尔先生听了这话吼道。
“闭嘴，博斯韦尔先生，”老教书先生说，“我不该向这位先生夸耀我的希腊文，他回答得很好。”
“博士，”我说，狡黠地望着他，“您可知道‘亚里士*多*德’能押个什么韵儿？”
“波特酒，劳驾。”戈德史密斯先生笑着说。于是那天晚上，离开咖啡馆之前，我们为亚里士多德凑足了*六个韵脚*。后来我把这故事一讲，它成了一则固定的笑谈；在怀特俱乐部或可可树咖啡馆，你会听见那些促狭鬼喊：“侍者，给我来一杯巴里上尉的‘亚里士多德韵脚’。”有一回我在后一家馆子里喝多了，小迪克·谢里丹管我叫“伟大的蹒跚派”——这个笑话我从来没能明白。不过我的话扯远了，得回到家乡、回到亲爱的老爱尔兰来。

===Original===

I have made acquaintance with the best in the land since, and my manners are such, I have said, as to make me the equal of them all; and, perhaps, you will wonder how a country boy, as I was, educated amongst Irish squires, and their dependants of the stable and farm, should arrive at possessing such elegant manners as I was indisputably allowed to have. I had, the fact is, a very valuable instructor in the person of an old gamekeeper, who had served the French king at Fontenoy, and who taught me the dances and customs, and a smattering of the language of that country, with the use of the sword, both small and broad. Many and many a long mile I have trudged by his side as a lad, he telling me wonderful stories of the French king, and the Irish brigade, and Marshal Saxe, and the opera-dancers; he knew my uncle, too, the Chevalier Borgne, and indeed had a thousand accomplishments which he taught me in secret. I never knew a man like him for making or throwing a fly, for physicking a horse, or breaking, or choosing one; he taught me manly sports, from birds’-nesting upwards, and I always shall consider Phil Purcell as the very best tutor I could have had. His fault was drink, but for that I have always had a blind eye; and he hated my cousin Mick like poison; but I could excuse him that too.
With Phil, and at the age of fifteen, I was a more accomplished man than either of my cousins; and I think Nature had been also more bountiful to me in the matter of person. Some of the Castle Brady girls (as you shall hear presently) adored me. At fairs and races many of the prettiest lasses present said they would like to have me for their bachelor; and yet somehow, it must be confessed, I was not popular.
In the first place, everyone knew I was bitter poor; and I think, perhaps, it was my good mother’s fault that I was bitter proud too. I had a habit of boasting in company of my birth, and the splendour of my carriages, gardens, cellars, and domestics, and this before people who were perfectly aware of my real circumstances. If it was boys, and they ventured to sneer, I would beat them, or die for it; and many’s the time I’ve been brought home well-nigh killed by one or more of them, on what, when my mother asked me, I would say was “a family quarrel.”

===Chinese===

后来我结识过国内最上等的人物，而如前所说，我的风度足以同他们所有人平起平坐；你们或许要奇怪：一个像我这样的乡下孩子，成天同爱尔兰乡绅以及马厩田庄上的下人们混在一起受的教养，怎么竟修来了一副公认无可争辩的优雅举止。实情是，我有一位极为难得的良师——一个老猎场看守人；他曾在丰特努瓦为法国国王效力，教了我那个国度的舞步和风俗，一点皮毛的法语，还有剑术——短剑长剑都教。少年时，我在他身边走过的长路一程又一程；他给我讲法国国王、爱尔兰旅、萨克司元帅和歌剧院舞娘们的奇妙故事；他还认识我那位舅舅独眼骑士；的确，他身怀千般本事，都悄悄传授给了我。论绑蝇饵抛钓线、给马治病、驯马挑马，我从没见过比他更精的人；他教我男子的各种运动，从掏鸟窝往上数；我将永远认为，菲尔·珀塞尔是我所能遇到的最好的老师。他的毛病是贪杯，不过这一点我一向睁一只眼闭一只眼；他恨我表哥米克恨之入骨；可这一点我也能体谅。
有菲尔调教，到十五岁上，我已比两位表哥中任何一个都更有本事；而且我认为，就容貌身量而言，造化对我也更慷慨。布雷迪城堡的几位姑娘（下文您就会听到）对我崇拜得五体投地；集市和赛马场上，许多在场的漂亮姑娘都说愿意要我做她们的“情郎”；然而不知怎么的，必须承认，我并不受人欢迎。首先，谁都知道我穷得叮当响；而且我想，我那股穷凶极恶般的高傲，或许该归咎于我那位好母亲。我有个习惯，人前爱吹嘘我的出身，吹嘘我的车马、花园、酒窖和仆从何等排场——而且当着那些对我真实处境一清二楚的人的面。若是男孩子们胆敢讥笑，我就把他们打服，哪怕拼上性命；多少回我被人抬回家，差不多被一个或几个打得半死——母亲问起来，我就说是“家宅内斗”。

===Original===

“Support your name with your blood, Reddy my boy,” would that saint say, with the tears in her eyes; and so would she herself have done with her voice, ay, and her teeth and nails.
Thus, at fifteen, there was scarce a lad of twenty, for half-a-dozen miles round, that I had not beat for one cause or other. There were the vicar’s two sons of Castle Brady﻿—in course I could not associate with such beggarly brats as them, and many a battle did we have as to who should take the wall in Brady’s Town; there was Pat Lurgan, the blacksmith’s son, who had the better of me four times before we came to the crowning fight, when I overcame him; and I could mention a score more of my deeds of prowess in that way, but that fisticuff facts are dull subjects to talk of, and to discuss before high-bred gentlemen and ladies.
However, there is another subject, ladies, on which I must discourse, and *that* is never out of place. Day and night you like to hear of it: young and old, you dream and think of it. Handsome and ugly (and, faith, before fifty, I never saw such a thing as a plain woman), it’s the subject next to the hearts of all of you; and I think you guess my riddle without more trouble. *Love!* sure the word is formed on purpose out of the prettiest soft vowels and consonants in the language, and he or she who does not care to read about it is not worth a fig, to my thinking.
My uncle’s family consisted of ten children; who, as is the custom in such large families, were divided into two camps, or parties; the one siding with their mamma, the other taking the part of my uncle in all the numerous quarrels which arose between that gentleman and his lady. Mrs. Brady’s faction was headed by Mick, the eldest son, who hated me so, and disliked his father for keeping him out of his property: while Ulick, the second brother, was his father’s own boy; and, in revenge, Master Mick was desperately afraid of him. I need not mention the girls’ names; I had plague enough with them in afterlife, Heaven knows; and one of them was the cause of all my early troubles: this was (though to be sure all her sisters denied it) the belle of the family, Miss Honoria Brady by name.

===Chinese===

“用你的血来撑住你的姓氏，雷迪小子，”那位圣人会这样说，眼里含着泪花；她自己呢，也会用嗓子——哼，还有牙齿和指甲——来撑的。
就这样，十五岁上，方圆好几英里内，二十岁的小伙子几乎没有一个，不曾因这样那样的缘故挨过我的打。比如布雷迪城堡牧师家的两个儿子——我自然不屑同那样寒酸的顽童为伍，为了在布雷迪镇上走路谁该占着墙根，我们打过多少仗；还有铁匠的儿子帕特·勒根，他赢过我四回，直到最后那场决定性的大战，我才战胜了他；这类英勇事迹我还能举出二十件，只是拳头上的事实在无趣，不宜在高贵的先生太太们面前谈论。
不过，太太们，还有另一个题目是我必须谈的，而*那个*题目永远不会不合时宜。你们日夜爱听：不分老少，你们梦着它、想着它。美的丑的（说真的，五十岁以前，我从没见过一个相貌平庸的女人），它是你们所有人心中最贴近的题目；我想你们不必费神就猜到了我的谜语。*爱情*！这个词分明是特意从语言里最柔美的元音和辅音中造出来的；依我看，谁不爱读它，谁就一文不值。
舅舅家有十个孩子；按这种大家庭的惯例，他们分成两营、两党：一派站在妈妈一边，另一派在那位绅士同太太之间发生的无数次争吵中都替舅舅帮腔。布雷迪太太那一党的头领是长子米克——就是恨我恨得要死的那个——他怨父亲不让他掌管家业；次子尤利克则是他爹的心尖子；作为报复，米克少爷怕他怕得要死。姑娘们的名字就不必一一列举了；老天知道，后来我在她们身上吃的苦头够多了；其中一位，是我早年一切烦恼的根由：她便是（虽说她的姐妹们一概否认）全家的美人儿——霍诺丽娅·布雷迪小姐。

===Original===

She said she was only nineteen at the time; but I could read the flyleaf in the family Bible as well as another (it was one of the three books which, with the backgammon-board, formed my uncle’s library), and know that she was born in the year ’37, and christened by Doctor Swift, Dean of St. Patrick’s, Dublin: hence she was three-and-twenty years old at the time she and I were so much together.
When I come to think about her now, I know she never could have been handsome; for her figure was rather of the fattest, and her mouth of the widest; she was freckled over like a partridge’s egg, and her hair was the colour of a certain vegetable which we eat with boiled beef, to use the mildest term. Often and often would my dear mother make these remarks concerning her; but I did not believe them then, and somehow had gotten to think Honoria an angelical being, far above all the other angels of her sex.
And as we know very well that a lady who is skilled in dancing or singing never can perfect herself without a deal of study in private, and that the song or the minuet which is performed with so much graceful ease in the assembly-room has not been acquired without vast labour and perseverance in private; so it is with the dear creatures who are skilled in coquetting. Honoria, for instance, was always practising, and she would take poor me to rehearse her accomplishment upon; or the exciseman, when he came his rounds, or the steward, or the poor curate, or the young apothecary’s lad from Brady’s Town: whom I recollect beating once for that very reason. If he is alive now I make him my apologies. Poor fellow! as if it was *his* fault that he should be a victim to the wiles of one of the greatest coquettes (considering her obscure life and rustic breeding) in the world.
If the truth must be told﻿—and every word of this narrative of my life is of the most sacred veracity﻿—my passion for Nora began in a very vulgar and unromantic way. I did not save her life; on the contrary, I once very nearly killed her, as you shall hear.

===Chinese===

她说她当年只有十九岁；可家里的《圣经》扉页，我同别人一样认得字（那本书连同十五子棋盘，构成舅舅藏书室的另外两件），上面写着她生于三七年，在都柏林圣帕特里克教堂教长斯威夫特博士手里受的洗礼：可见她同我形影不离那阵子，已经二十有三了。
如今回想起来，我知道她那时候绝算不上漂亮：她身段偏胖，嘴巴偏阔；满脸雀斑，活像斑鸠蛋；头发的颜色，说得最客气些，同我们配煮牛肉吃的那种蔬菜一个颜色。我亲爱的母亲过去常这样议论她；可我当时不肯信，不知怎么竟认定霍诺丽娅是天仙一般的人物，远远高出于她同性别的所有天使之上。
我们都很清楚：一位精于舞蹈或歌唱的女士，若不在私下下大量苦功，绝难臻于完美；舞会厅里那般优雅从容的一支歌、一段小步舞，背后不知有多少私下的辛劳与坚持；那些精于卖弄风情的可爱造物，道理也是一样。比如霍诺丽娅，她无时无刻不在练习，而可怜的我便被抓去当她演练手段的对象；有时轮到收税官巡到、管家、可怜的副牧师，或是布雷迪镇上药店的小学徒——后者我记得还为此挨过我一顿打。他若如今还在世，我在此向他赔罪。可怜的人！这难道是*他*的错吗——谁叫他会沦为一桩世上数得着的风流手段的猎物（就她那湮没无闻的生涯和乡野教养而论）。
倘若必须说出真相——我这部生平自述的每一个字都具有最神圣的真实性——我对诺拉的痴情，起初的方式既庸俗又毫无浪漫可言。我没有救过她的命；恰恰相反，有一回我险些要了她的命，下文您就听到。

===Original===

I did not behold her by moonlight playing on the guitar, or rescue her from the hands of ruffians, as Alfonso does Lindamira in the novel; but one day, after dinner at Brady’s Town, in summer, going into the garden to pull gooseberries for my dessert, and thinking only of gooseberries, I pledge my honour, I came upon Miss Nora and one of her sisters, with whom she was friends at the time, who were both engaged in the very same amusement.
“What’s the Latin for gooseberry, Redmond?” says she. She was always “poking her fun,” as the Irish phrase it.
“I know the Latin for goose,” says I.
“And what’s that?” cries Miss Mysie, as pert as a peacock.
“Bo to you!” says I (for I had never a want of wit); and so we fell to work at the gooseberry-bush, laughing and talking as happy as might be. In the course of our diversion Nora managed to scratch her arm, and it bled, and she screamed, and it was mighty round and white, and I tied it up, and I believe was permitted to kiss her hand; and though it was as big and clumsy a hand as ever you saw, yet I thought the favour the most ravishing one that was ever conferred upon me, and went home in a rapture.
I was much too simple a fellow to disguise any sentiment I chanced to feel in those days; and not one of the eight Castle Brady girls but was soon aware of my passion, and joked and complimented Nora about her bachelor.
The torments of jealousy the cruel coquette made me endure were horrible. Sometimes she would treat me as a child, sometimes as a man. She would always leave me if ever there came a stranger to the house.
“For after all, Redmond,” she would say, “you are but fifteen, and you haven’t a guinea in the world.” At which I would swear that I would become the greatest hero ever known out of Ireland, and vow that before I was twenty I would have money enough to purchase an estate six times as big as Castle Brady. All which vain promises, of course, I did not keep; but I make no doubt they influenced me in my very early life, and caused me to do those great actions for which I have been celebrated, and which shall be narrated presently in order.
I must tell one of them, just that my dear young lady readers may know what sort of a fellow Redmond Barry was, and what a courage and undaunted passion he had. I question whether any of the jenny-jessamines of the present day would do half as much in the face of danger.

===Chinese===

我既没有在月光下看她弹吉他，也没有像小说里的阿方索救琳达米拉那样，把她从恶徒手中解救出来；而是某年夏天，在布雷迪镇吃过午饭，我到园子里摘醋栗当饭后果子——我以名誉担保，心里想的只有醋栗——却撞见诺拉小姐和她的一位妹妹（当时她俩正要好），双双忙于同一桩乐事。
“醋栗的拉丁文怎么说，雷德蒙？”她说。她总爱“逗趣取乐”——照爱尔兰人的说法。
“鹅的拉丁文我倒知道。”我说。
“是什么呀？”米茜小姐嚷道，神气得像只开屏的孔雀。
“嘎——去你的！”我说（我一向不缺急智）；于是我们在醋栗丛边干开了活，又说又笑，快活极了。玩闹中间，诺拉不小心划破了胳膊，出了血，她尖叫起来；那胳膊真是又圆又白，我替她包扎起来，并且相信获准吻了她的手；虽说那是一只您所能见过的最肥最大、最笨拙的手，我却觉得这份恩宠是平生所受最销魂的一次，回家时乐得魂儿都要飞了。
我那时是个再单纯不过的家伙，心里偶然起了什么情愫，丝毫不会遮掩；布雷迪城堡的八位姑娘，没有一位不很快看穿我的痴情，都拿诺拉的“情郎”打趣、道贺。这位无情的风流娇娘叫我忍受的醋海煎熬，实在可怕。她忽而拿我当孩子，忽而拿我当男人。家里一来生客，她总是撇下我。
“毕竟，雷德蒙，”她会说，“你才十五岁，而且身无一枚几尼。”听到这话我便发誓，要成为爱尔兰之外闻所未闻的最伟大的英雄，并赌咒说不出二十岁，我定要挣下足以买下六座布雷迪城堡的产业。这些虚夸的诺言，我自然一条也没兑现；但我毫不怀疑，它们在我极年幼时影响了我，促使我干下那些使我名扬四海的伟大功业——这些功业稍后自当按序叙述。其中一件我必须讲讲，好让我亲爱的青年女读者们知道雷德蒙·巴里是个怎样的人物，有着怎样的胆魄和不屈的激情。今日那些娇滴滴的“素馨花”们，面对危险能否做到一半，我很怀疑。

===Original===

About this time, it must be premised, the United Kingdom was in a state of great excitement from the threat generally credited of a French invasion. The Pretender was said to be in high favour at Versailles, a descent upon Ireland was especially looked to, and the noblemen and people of condition in that and all other parts of the kingdom showed their loyalty by raising regiments of horse and foot to resist the invaders. Brady’s Town sent a company to join the Kilwangan regiment, of which Master Mick was the captain; and we had a letter from Master Ulick at Trinity College, stating that the University had also formed a regiment, in which he had the honour to be a corporal. How I envied them both! especially that odious Mick as I saw him in his laced scarlet coat, with a ribbon in his hat, march off at the head of his men. He, the poor spiritless creature, was a captain, and I nothing﻿—I who felt I had as much courage as the Duke of Cumberland himself, and felt, too, that a red jacket would mightily become me! My mother said I was too young to join the new regiment; but the fact was, that it was she herself who was too poor, for the cost of a new uniform would have swallowed up half her year’s income, and she would only have her boy appear in a way suitable to his birth, riding the finest of racers, dressed in the best of clothes, and keeping the genteelest of company.
Well, then, the whole country was alive with war’s alarums, the three kingdoms ringing with military music, and every man of merit paying his devoirs at the court of Bellona, whilst poor I was obliged to stay at home in my fustian jacket and sigh for fame in secret. Mr. Mick came to and fro from the regiment, and brought numerous of his comrades with him. Their costume and swaggering airs filled me with grief, and Miss Nora’s unvarying attentions to them served to make me half wild. No one, however, thought of attributing this sadness to the young lady’s score, but rather to my disappointment at not being allowed to join the military profession.
Once the officers of the Fencibles gave a grand ball at Kilwangan, to which, as a matter of course, all the ladies of Castle Brady (and a pretty ugly coachful they were) were invited.

===Chinese===

首先得交代一下：大约在这个时期，法军入侵的威胁人人信以为真，联合王国上下群情激奋。据说僭位者在凡尔赛正大受恩宠，人们尤其预料法军将在爱尔兰登陆；王国那一带及各地的贵族与有身份的人士纷纷募集骑兵团、步兵团以御入侵者，以示忠心。布雷迪镇派出一个连队并入基尔万根团，米克少爷当上了上尉；我们又收到三一学院尤利克少爷的来信，说大学也编成了一个团，他荣任下士。我多么羡慕他们俩啊！尤其是那个可憎的米克——眼看着他穿着镶花边的猩红军装、帽上缀着缎带，神气十足地率队开拔。他，那个没出息的可怜虫，做上了上尉，而我什么都不是——我自觉胆气不输坎伯兰公爵本人，而且深信那件红军装穿在我身上必定英气逼人！母亲说我年纪太小，进不了新军团；实情却是她自家太穷：一套新军装的花费会吞掉她半年的收入，而她只肯让她的儿子以配得上出身的方式露面——骑最好的赛马，穿最好的衣裳，交最体面的朋友。
于是乎，举国上下战鼓催征，三岛回荡着军乐，每一位有志之士都在柏罗娜战神的宫廷里朝拜献殷勤，唯独可怜的我，只得穿着粗布外衣闷坐家中，暗自渴慕功名。米克少爷在军团里来来去去，带回许多战友。他们的装束和趾高气扬的做派使我满心悲苦，而诺拉小姐对他们一成不变的殷勤，更叫我半疯。不过，谁也没有想到把这份愁苦记在那位小姐账上，倒都以为是因为我不获准从军，失望所致。有一次，国防军军官们在基尔万根举办盛大舞会，布雷迪城堡全体女眷照例受邀（满满一车，那模样可实在不敢恭维）。

===Original===

I knew to what tortures the odious little flirt of a Nora would put me with her eternal coquetries with the officers, and refused for a long time to be one of the party to the ball. But she had a way of conquering me, against which all resistance of mine was in vain. She vowed that riding in a coach always made her ill. “And how can I go to the ball,” said she, “unless you take me on Daisy behind you on the pillion?” Daisy was a good blood-mare of my uncle’s, and to such a proposition I could not for my soul say no; so we rode in safety to Kilwangan, and I felt myself as proud as any prince when she promised to dance a country-dance with me.
When the dance was ended, the little ungrateful flirt informed me that she had quite forgotten her engagement; she had actually danced the set with an Englishman! I have endured torments in my life, but none like that. She tried to make up for her neglect, but I would not. Some of the prettiest girls there offered to console me, for I was the best dancer in the room. I made one attempt, but was too wretched to continue, and so remained alone all night in a state of agony. I would have played, but I had no money; only the gold piece that my mother bade me always keep in my purse as a gentleman should. I did not care for drink, or know the dreadful comfort of it in those days; but I thought of killing myself and Nora, and most certainly of making away with Captain Quin!
At last, and at morning, the ball was over. The rest of our ladies went off in the lumbering creaking old coach; Daisy was brought out, and Miss Nora took her place behind me, which I let her do without a word. But we were not half-a-mile out of town when she began to try with her coaxing and blandishments to dissipate my ill-humour.
“Sure it’s a bitter night, Redmond dear, and you’ll catch cold without a handkerchief to your neck.” To this sympathetic remark from the pillion, the saddle made no reply.
