“Did you and Miss Clancy have a pleasant evening, Redmond? You were together, I saw, all night.” To this the saddle only replied by grinding his teeth, and giving a lash to Daisy.
“O mercy! you’ll make Daisy rear and throw me, you careless creature you: and you know, Redmond, I’m so timid.” The pillion had by this got her arm round the saddle’s waist, and perhaps gave it the gentlest squeeze in the world.
“I hate Miss Clancy, you know I do!”

===Chinese===

我深知那个可憎的小卖俏货诺拉，会拿她同军官们没完没了的调情怎样折磨我，所以好长时间都拒绝随他们赴会。但她自有一套征服我的手段，我的一切抵抗都属徒劳。她赌咒说坐马车总叫她犯病。“你不带我骑‘雏菊’、坐在你身后的鞍垫上，我可怎么去舞会呀？”她说。雏菊是舅舅一匹好血统的母马，这样的提议我是万万无法拒绝的；于是我们平安骑到基尔万根，她答应同我跳一支乡土舞，我自觉尊荣不输任何亲王。
舞曲终了，那个没良心的小骚货告诉我，她把同我的约会忘了个干净；她竟然同一个英国人跳完了整场！我一生受过种种酷刑般的煎熬，但没有一回及得上这次。她设法弥补自己的怠慢，我不依。在场几位最美的姑娘主动来安慰我，因为我是全厅最好的舞客。我勉强试了一曲，实在愁肠百结，跳不下去，只得整夜独自枯坐，痛苦万状。我本想赌钱，可身无分文；钱袋里只有那枚金币，母亲叮嘱我，作为绅士要永远把它留在袋里。那时节我还不嗜酒，也不懂酒中那可怕的慰藉；只是一心想着杀死我自己和诺拉，而且百分之百想干掉奎因上尉！
舞会终于熬到了清晨。其余的太太小姐们坐上那辆吱嘎作响的笨重旧马车走了；雏菊被牵了出来，诺拉小姐坐上我身后的鞍垫——我一声不吭由她去了。可出镇不到半英里，她便开始施展哄骗和媚术，想驱散我的坏脾气。
“这天可真冷哪，亲爱的雷德蒙，脖子上不围条手绢要着凉的。”对鞍垫这边送来的同情话，马鞍这边一声不吭。
“你同克兰西小姐玩得开心吗，雷德蒙？我看你们整晚都待在一起。”对此，马鞍这边只报以咬牙切齿，朝雏菊猛抽了一鞭。
“哎呀天哪！你会叫雏菊人立起来把我摔了的，你这个冒失鬼：你知道的，雷德蒙，我胆子最小了。”这时鞍垫已将手臂环住了马鞍的腰，兴许还给了它世界上最轻柔的一捏。
“我恨克兰西小姐，你知道我恨她！”

===Original===

answers the saddle; “and I only danced with her because﻿—because﻿—the person with whom I intended to dance chose to be engaged the whole night.”
“Sure there were my sisters,” said the pillion, now laughing outright in the pride of her conscious superiority; “and for me, my dear, I had not been in the room five minutes before I was engaged for every single set.”
“Were you obliged to dance five times with Captain Quin?” said I; and oh! strange delicious charm of coquetry, I do believe Miss Nora Brady at twenty-three years of age felt a pang of delight in thinking that she had so much power over a guileless lad of fifteen. Of course she replied that she did not care a fig for Captain Quin: that he danced prettily, to be sure, and was a pleasant rattle of a man; that he looked well in his regimentals too; and if he chose to ask her to dance, how could she refuse him?
“But you refused me, Nora.”
“Oh! I can dance with you any day,” answered Miss Nora, with a toss of her head; “and to dance with your cousin at a ball, looks as if you could find no other partner. Besides,” said Nora﻿—and this was a cruel, unkind cut, which showed what a power she had over me, and how mercilessly she used it﻿—“besides, Redmond, Captain Quin’s a man and you are only a boy!”
“If ever I meet him again,” I roared out with an oath, “you shall see which is the best man of the two. I’ll fight him with sword or with pistol, captain as he is. A man indeed! I’ll fight any man﻿—every man! Didn’t I stand up to Mick Brady when I was eleven years old?﻿—Didn’t I beat Tom Sullivan, the great hulking brute, who is nineteen?﻿—Didn’t I do for the Scotch usher? O Nora, it’s cruel of you to sneer at me so!”
But Nora was in the sneering mood that night, and pursued her sarcasms; she pointed out that Captain Quin was already known as a valiant soldier, famous as a man of fashion in London, and that it was mighty well of Redmond to talk and boast of beating ushers and farmers’ boys, but to fight an Englishman was a very different matter.
Then she fell to talk of the invasion, and of military matters in general; of King Frederick (who was called, in those days, the Protestant hero), of Monsieur Thurot and his fleet, of Monsieur Conflans and his squadron, of Minorca, how it was attacked, and where it was; we both agreed it must be in America, and hoped the French might be soundly beaten there.

===Chinese===

马鞍答了话；“我同她跳舞，只是因为——因为——我原打算同跳的那一位，偏叫人占了一整晚。”
“那儿不是还有我的姐姐妹妹们吗，”鞍垫说，这回干脆笑出了声，怀着自知稳操胜券的得意；“至于我，亲爱的，我进厅还不到五分钟，每一场舞就都有主了。”
“那你犯得着同奎因上尉跳五回吗？”我说；啊！风骚竟有这般奇妙甜美的魅力——我深信二十三岁的诺拉·布雷迪小姐一想到自己竟能把一个十五岁的天真少年玩弄于股掌，心头必是甜丝丝地一颤。她自然回答说，奎因上尉在她眼里一文不值：不过他舞跳得确实漂亮，人也是个有趣的快嘴儿；穿上军装也体面；人家既然开口请她跳舞，她怎么好回绝？
“可你回绝了我，诺拉。”
“嗐！跟你跳舞哪天不成？”诺拉小姐一甩头答道；“舞会上同自己表弟跳舞，倒像找不着别的舞伴似的。再说了，”诺拉道——这一刀又狠又不留情，足见她对我的操控之力，也足见她用得多么冷酷——“再说了，雷德蒙，奎因上尉是个男人，你不过是个孩子！”
“往后我再碰见他，”我赌咒发誓地吼道，“就叫你瞧瞧我们俩哪个是真汉子。他当他的上尉，我照样用剑、用枪跟他干。男人，哼！我谁都敢斗——人人都敢！我十一岁那年不就同米克·布雷迪交过手？——十九岁的大个子、粗胚汤姆·沙利文，不也叫我打了？——那个苏格兰助教不叫我料理了？哦诺拉，你这样讥笑我，太狠心了！”
可诺拉那晚正处在讥诮的兴头上，冷嘲热讽不肯罢休；她指出，奎因上尉是出了名的骁勇军人，在伦敦以时髦人物著称；雷德蒙吹嘘自己打败过助教和农家小子固然不坏，可要同一个英国人交手，就完全是另一回事了。接着她谈起入侵，谈起一般的军情：谈到腓特烈国王（那时节人称新教英雄），谈到蒂罗先生和他的舰队、孔夫朗先生和他的分舰队，谈到米诺卡——它怎样受了攻击，又坐落在何处；我们俩一致断定它准是在美洲，还盼望法国人在那儿被痛揍一顿。

===Original===

I sighed after a while (for I was beginning to melt), and said how much I longed to be a soldier; on which Nora recurred to her infallible “Ah! now, would you leave me, then? But, sure, you’re not big enough for anything more than a little drummer.” To which I replied, by swearing that a soldier I would be, and a general too.
As we were chattering in this silly way, we came to a place that has ever since gone by the name of Redmond’s Leap Bridge. It was an old high bridge, over a stream sufficiently deep and rocky, and as the mare Daisy with her double load was crossing this bridge, Miss Nora, giving a loose to her imagination, and still harping on the military theme (I would lay a wager that she was thinking of Captain Quin)﻿—Miss Nora said, “Suppose now, Redmond, you, who are such a hero, was passing over the bridge, and the inimy on the other side?”
“I’d draw my sword, and cut my way through them.”
“What, with me on the pillion? Would you kill poor me?” (This young lady was perpetually speaking of “poor me!”)
“Well, then, I’ll tell you what I’d do. I’d jump Daisy into the river, and swim you both across, where no enemy could follow us.”
“Jump twenty feet! you wouldn’t dare to do any such thing on Daisy. There’s the Captain’s horse, Black George, I’ve heard say that Captain Qui﻿—”
She never finished the word, for, maddened by the continual recurrence of that odious monosyllable, I shouted to her to “hold tight by my waist,” and, giving Daisy the spur, in a minute sprang with Nora over the parapet into the deep water below. I don’t know why, now﻿—whether it was I wanted to drown myself and Nora, or to perform an act that even Captain Quin should crane at, or whether I fancied that the enemy actually was in front of us, I can’t tell now; but over I went. The horse sank over his head, the girl screamed as she sank and screamed as she rose, and I landed her, half fainting, on the shore, where we were soon found by my uncle’s people, who returned on hearing the screams. I went home, and was ill speedily of a fever, which kept me to my bed for six weeks; and I quitted my couch prodigiously increased in stature, and, at the same time, still more violently in love than I had been even before.

===Chinese===

过了一阵我叹了口气（因为我开始软化了），说自己是多么渴望当兵；诺拉便使出她那万灵的杀手锏：“啊！这么说，你要丢下我喽？再说，你那小身板，除了当个小鼓手还能当什么。”对此我以赌咒作答：兵我是一定要当的，还要当将军。
我们正这么傻里傻气地闲扯，来到了一个地方——打那以后，它就得名“雷德蒙飞身桥”。那是一座又高又老的桥，横跨一条水深石多的溪流。母马雏菊驮着我们双重的载重正过桥时，诺拉小姐任由想象力驰骋，还在军事题目上打转（我敢打赌她心里想的是奎因上尉）——只听诺拉小姐说：“雷德蒙，假如你这大英雄过桥时，对岸来了敌兵，你怎么办？”
“我就拔出剑，从他们中间杀出一条路。”
“什么？鞍垫上还坐着我呢？你要害死可怜的我吗？”（这位小姐开口闭口“可怜的我”！）
“好，那我告诉你我会怎么办。我让雏菊跳进河里，驮着咱们俩游到对岸，敌人就追不上咱们了。”
“跳二十英尺！谅你不敢骑雏菊玩这种把戏。人家上尉那匹马，黑乔治，我可是听说奎因上尉——”
这个词她永远没能说完，因为那个可憎的音节一而再、再而三地冒出来，把我气疯了；我大喊一声“抱紧我的腰”，一磕雏菊的马刺，转眼便带着诺拉越过桥栏，跃入下面深水之中。如今我说不清为什么——是我想要同诺拉一起淹死，还是要干一件连奎因上尉都不敢伸脖子的壮举，还是我真把敌军想象到了眼前，我说不清了；反正我跳了。马整个没入水中，姑娘下沉时尖叫，浮起时还尖叫；我把她半昏半醒地拖上岸，舅舅家的人听见尖叫赶了回来，很快寻到了我们。我回了家，很快发起高烧，一病六周；下床时个子蹿了一大截，与此同时，那痴情比病前更要炽烈三分。

===Original===

At the commencement of my illness, Miss Nora had been pretty constant in her attendance at my bedside, forgetting, for the sake of me, the quarrel between my mother and her family; which my good mother was likewise pleased, in the most Christian manner, to forget. And, let me tell you, it was no small mark of goodness in a woman of her haughty disposition, who, as a rule, never forgave anybody, for my sake to give up her hostility to Miss Brady, and to receive her kindly. For, like a mad boy as I was, it was Nora I was always raving about and asking for; I would only accept medicines from her hand, and would look rudely and sulkily upon the good mother, who loved me better than anything else in the world, and gave up even her favourite habits, and proper and becoming jealousies, to make me happy.
As I got well, I saw that Nora’s visits became daily more rare: “Why don’t she come?” I would say, peevishly, a dozen times in the day; in reply to which query, Mrs. Barry would be obliged to make the best excuses she could find﻿—such as that Nora had sprained her ankle, or that they had quarrelled together, or some other answer to soothe me. And many a time has the good soul left me to go and break her heart in her own room alone, and come back with a smiling face, so that I should know nothing of her mortification. Nor, indeed, did I take much pains to ascertain it: nor should I, I fear, have been very much touched even had I discovered it; for the commencement of manhood, I think, is the period of our extremest selfishness. We get such a desire then to take wing and leave the parent nest, that no tears, entreaties, or feelings of affection will counterbalance this overpowering longing after independence. She must have been very sad, that poor mother of mine﻿—Heaven be good to her!﻿—at that period of my life; and has often told me since what a pang of the heart it was to her to see all her care and affection of years forgotten by me in a minute, and for the sake of a little heartless jilt, who was only playing with me while she could get no better suitor. For the fact is, that during the last four weeks of my illness, no other than Captain Quin was staying at Castle Brady, and making love to Miss Nora in form. My mother did not dare to break this news to me, and you may be sure that Nora herself kept it a secret: it was only by chance that I discovered it.

===Chinese===

我病倒之初，诺拉小姐倒是常常守在我床边看护，为了我的缘故，把母亲同她家的旧怨撇在一边；我那位好母亲也以极基督徒的宽宏，乐于把那场过节一并忘掉。而且请听我说，以她那样高傲的性子——照例是从来不饶人的——竟肯为我捐弃对布雷迪小姐的敌意，还亲切地接待她，这实在不是小的美德。因为我这孩子疯魔了，满口胡话，喊的问的都是诺拉；药只肯从她手里吃，对那位好母亲——她爱我胜过世上一切，连自己的心头习惯、那份理所应当也无可非议的醋意都肯抛下，只为让我开心——反倒恶声恶气、绷着脸子。
随着病体渐愈，我眼看诺拉的探望一天稀似一天：“她怎么不来？”我一天要烦躁地问上十几遍；对这个质问，巴里太太只好搜罗出最好听的托词——什么诺拉崴了脚脖子啦，什么她们妯娌间拌了嘴啦，或是别的什么宽慰我的话。多少回，这个好人撇下我，独自到她房里去心碎，再挂着笑脸回来，好叫我对她受的委屈一无所知。说实在的，我也没费什么心去打听；恐怕即便发现了，也不会怎么动心；因为男子生涯的开端，我想，正是人最最自私的时期。那时节我们一心想振翅离巢，任什么眼泪、恳求、恩爱之情，都压不过这股对独立的强烈渴望。我那可怜的母亲，在我人生的那段日子里必是十分伤心——愿上天善待她！——她后来常对我说，眼看着自己多年来的照料疼爱，转眼间被我忘个干净，而且是为了一个没心没肺的小薄幸——她趁一时钓不着更好的主儿，只拿我解闷——她心里是何等针扎似的疼。实情是：我病中的最后四个星期，住在布雷迪城堡的不是别人，正是奎因上尉，而且正经八百地向诺拉小姐求起爱来。母亲不敢把这消息向我戳破，诺拉本人自然是守口如瓶：我是碰巧才发现的。

===Original===

Shall I tell you how? The minx had been to see me one day, as I sat up in my bed, convalescent; she was in such high spirits, and so gracious and kind to me, that my heart poured over with joy and gladness, and I had even for my poor mother a kind word and a kiss that morning. I felt myself so well that I ate up a whole chicken, and promised my uncle, who had come to see me, to be ready against partridge-shooting, to accompany him, as my custom was.
The next day but one was a Sunday, and I had a project for that day which I determined to realise, in spite of all the doctor’s and my mother’s injunctions: which were that I was on no account to leave the house, for the fresh air would be the death of me.
Well, I lay wondrous quiet, composing a copy of verses, the first I ever made in my life; and I give them here, spelt as I spelt them in those days when I knew no better. And though they are not so polished and elegant as “Ardelia ease a Lovesick Swain,” and “When Sol bedecks the Daisied Mead,” and other lyrical effusions of mine which obtained me so much reputation in after life, I still think them pretty good for a humble lad of fifteen:﻿—

> On Brady’s tower there grows a flower,

It is the loveliest flower that blows﻿—

At Castle Brady there lives a lady

(And how I love her no one knows):

Her name is Nora, and the goddess Flora

Presents her with this blooming rose.
> “O Lady Nora,” says the goddess Flora,

“I’ve many a rich and bright parterre;

In Brady’s towers there’s seven more flowers,

But you’re the fairest lady there:

Not all the county, nor Ireland’s bounty,

Can projuice a treasure that’s half so fair!”
> What cheek is redder? sure roses fed her!

Her hair is maregolds, and her eye of blew

Beneath her eyelid is like the vi’let,

That darkly glistens with gentle jew?

The lily’s nature is not surely whiter

Than Nora’s neck is﻿—and her arrums too.
> “Come, gentle Nora,” says the goddess Flora,

“My dearest creature, take my advice,

There is a poet, full well you know it,

Who spends his lifetime in heavy sighs﻿—

Young Redmond Barry, ’tis him you’ll marry,

If rhyme and raisin you’d choose likewise.”

===Chinese===

要我说说是怎么发现的吗？有一天，那小妖精来看我，那时我正处在恢复期，坐在床上；她兴致那样高，对我那样和蔼亲切，我的心满得直往外溢欢喜，那天早晨我甚至对可怜的母亲也有了好脸色，还亲了她一下。我自觉身子大好，吃了一整只鸡，还向前来看我的舅舅保证，到猎鹧鸪的季节准随他去，像往常一样。
隔一天便是礼拜天，我那天有一桩计划，不管医生和母亲怎样三令五申——说什么我万万不可出门，新鲜空气会要了我的命——我都打定主意要付诸实行。
于是我躺得乖乖的，作了一首诗——平生第一首；在此照录，拼法一仍其旧，全按当年少不更事时的原样。虽说它们不及《阿尔德里娅，宽慰相思的少年》《当太阳装点雏菊如茵的草畔》以及我后来赖以大获声誉的其他抒情诗那样精致典雅，我仍以为，一个十五岁的谦卑少年能写出这样的诗，算是不错了：——

> 布雷迪塔上生着一朵花，

朵朵开里数它最可夸——

布雷迪城堡住着一位小姐

（我爱她多深谁也不知）：

她名叫诺拉，花神弗洛拉

把盛开的玫瑰赠给她。
> “哦诺拉小姐，”花神弗洛拉说，

“我有好多华美明艳的花坛；

布雷迪的塔楼里还有七朵花，

可数你是那儿最美的姑娘：

凭全郡的家业，凭爱尔兰的富足，

也‘产’不出一半这样美的宝藏！”
> 什么脸蛋儿更红？准是玫瑰喂大了她！

她的头发是金盏花，眼珠是“兰”色，

睫毛底下像紫罗“兰”，

幽幽地闪着温柔的“露”珠？

百合的洁白也未必赛得过

诺拉的脖颈——还有她的胳膊。
> “来吧，温柔的诺拉，”花神弗洛拉说，

“我最亲爱的小东西，听我劝一句，

有一位诗人，你心里雪亮，

他整日价唉声叹气——

年轻的雷德蒙·巴里，你要嫁的是他，

只要你也中意诗韵和‘道理’。”

===Original===

On Sunday, no sooner was my mother gone to church, than I summoned Phil the valet, and insisted upon his producing my best suit, in which I arrayed myself (although I found that I had shot up so in my illness that the old dress was woefully too small for me), and, with my notable copy of verses in my hand, ran down towards Castle Brady, bent upon beholding my beauty. The air was so fresh and bright, and the birds sang so loud amidst the green trees, that I felt more elated than I had been for months before, and sprang down the avenue (my uncle had cut down every stick of the trees, by the way) as brisk as a young fawn. My heart began to thump as I mounted the grass-grown steps of the terrace, and passed in by the rickety hall-door. The master and mistress were at church, Mr. Screw the butler told me (after giving a start back at seeing my altered appearance, and gaunt lean figure), and so were six of the young ladies.
“Was Miss Nora one?” I asked.
“No, Miss Nora was not one,” said Mr. Screw, assuming a very puzzled, and yet knowing look.
“Where was she?” To this question he answered, or rather made believe to answer, with usual Irish ingenuity, and left me to settle whether she was gone to Kilwangan on the pillion behind her brother, or whether she and her sister had gone for a walk, or whether she was ill in her room; and while I was settling this query, Mr. Screw left me abruptly.
I rushed away to the back court, where the Castle Brady stables stand, and there I found a dragoon whistling the “Roast Beef of Old England,” as he cleaned down a cavalry horse. “Whose horse, fellow, is that?” cried I.
“Feller, indeed!” replied the Englishman: “the horse belongs to my captain, and he’s a better *feller* nor you any day.”
I did not stop to break his bones, as I would on another occasion, for a horrible suspicion had come across me, and I made for the garden as quickly as I could.
I knew somehow what I should see there. I saw Captain Quin and Nora pacing the alley together. Her arm was under his, and the scoundrel was fondling and squeezing the hand which lay closely nestling against his odious waistcoat. Some distance beyond them was Captain Fagan of the Kilwangan regiment, who was paying court to Nora’s sister Mysie.

===Chinese===

礼拜天，母亲刚一上教堂，我就唤来男仆菲尔，硬要他捧出我最好的衣裳，穿戴齐整（虽说大病一场蹿了个子，那身旧衣裳已小得可怜），手里攥着我那首得意诗作，一路跑向布雷迪城堡，一心要去见我的美人。空气那样新鲜明亮，绿树间鸟鸣那样喧闹，我几个月来不曾这样意气飞扬，沿着林荫道一路雀跃（顺便说一句，道旁的树木舅舅已砍得一根不剩），轻快得像头小鹿。踏上长满青草的露台台阶、穿过摇摇晃晃的正厅大门时，我的心开始怦怦直跳。主人和主母都上教堂了，管家斯克鲁先生告诉我（他一见我大变的模样和瘦长的身形，吓得倒退了一步），六位小姐也去了。
“诺拉小姐去了吗？”我问。
“没去，诺拉小姐没去，”斯克鲁先生说，脸上摆出一副既迷惑又心知肚明的神情。
“那她在哪儿？”对这个问题，他以爱尔兰人惯有的机巧作了回答——或者不如说装作回答——留下我自己去琢磨：她究竟是骑着马、坐在哥哥身后的鞍垫上去基尔万根了呢，还是同她妹妹散步去了，还是病在房里；正当我琢磨不决，斯克鲁先生突然撇下我走了。
我冲到后面的场院——布雷迪城堡的马厩就在那儿——只见一名龙骑兵正在刷洗一匹军马，一边吹着《老英格兰烤牛肉》的口哨。“那匹马是谁的，家伙？”我喊道。
“‘家伙’，亏你说得出口！”那英国人答道，“马是我们上尉的，而且不论哪天，他都是个比你像样的*家伙*。”
换作别的时候，我非停下来打断他的骨头不可；可这会儿一个可怕的疑心掠过心头，我便直奔花园而去。
不知怎么，我已料到将在那里看见什么。我看见奎因上尉同诺拉并肩在小径上踱步。她的胳膊挽着他的臂弯，那无耻之徒正把那只紧紧贴在他可憎背心上的手又抚又捏。离他们不远，基尔万根团的费根上尉正在向诺拉的妹妹米茜献殷勤。

===Original===

I am not afraid of any man or ghost; but as I saw that sight my knees fell a-trembling violently under me, and such a sickness came over me, that I was fain to sink down on the grass by a tree against which I leaned, and lost almost all consciousness for a minute or two: then I gathered myself up, and, advancing towards the couple on the walk, loosened the blade of the little silver-hilted hanger I always wore in its scabbard; for I was resolved to pass it through the bodies of the delinquents, and spit them like two pigeons. I don’t tell what feelings else besides those of rage were passing through my mind; what bitter blank disappointment, what mad wild despair, what a sensation as if the whole world was tumbling from under me; I make no doubt that my reader hath been jilted by the ladies many times, and so bid him recall his own sensations when the shock first fell upon him.
“No, Norelia,” said the Captain (for it was the fashion of those times for lovers to call themselves by the most romantic names out of novels), “except for you and four others, I vow before all the gods, my heart has never felt the soft flame!”
“Ah! you men, you men, Eugenio!” said she (the beast’s name was John), “your passion is not equal to ours. We are like﻿—like some plant I’ve read of﻿—we bear but one flower and then we die!”
“Do you mean you never felt an inclination for another?” said Captain Quin.
“Never, my Eugenio, but for thee! How can you ask a blushing nymph such a question?”
“Darling Norelia!” said he, raising her hand to his lips.
I had a knot of cherry-coloured ribands, which she had given me out of her breast, and which somehow I always wore upon me. I pulled these out of my bosom, and flung them in Captain Quin’s face, and rushed out with my little sword drawn, shrieking, “She’s a liar﻿—she’s a liar, Captain Quin! Draw, sir, and defend yourself, if you are a man!” and with these words I leapt at the monster, and collared him, while Nora made the air echo with her screams; at the sound of which the other captain and Mysie hastened up.
Although I sprang up like a weed in my illness, and was now nearly attained to my full growth of six feet, yet I was but a lath by the side of the enormous English captain, who had calves and shoulders such as no chairman at Bath ever boasted.

===Chinese===

人也好鬼也好，我都不怕；可一看见那情形，我的双膝在腿上剧烈打起颤来，一阵恶心眩晕涌上来，我只得顺着倚着的那棵树瘫坐到草地上，有一两分钟几乎失了知觉：随后我挣扎起身，朝小径上的那对儿走去，一面松动了我素来佩戴的那把银柄小弯刀的刀刃；因为我打定主意，要把这对罪人一剑串个透心凉，像穿两只鸽子似的。除了狂怒之外我心里还翻涌着什么，我不说了；那是何等苦涩空落的失望，何等疯狂暴烈的绝望，何等天塌地陷、整个世界从脚底下崩塌的感觉；我毫不怀疑，我的读者诸君被小姐们甩过的回数必定不少，那就请诸位回想一下，当头一棒初落下时的滋味吧。
“不，诺蕾莉娅，”上尉说（那时节情人们时兴互称小说里最罗曼蒂克的名字），“除了你和另外四位，我对诸神起誓，我的心从不曾燃过柔情的火焰！”
“啊！你们男人哪，你们男人，欧金尼奥！”她说（那畜生名叫约翰），“你们的热情抵不上我们的。我们好比——好比我在书上读到过的一种草木——只开一次花，开过便枯死！”
“你是说，你从没对别人动过心？”奎因上尉问。
“从来没有，我的欧金尼奥，心里只有你！这样的问题，你怎么问得出口，问一位脸皮薄的仙女？”
“亲爱的诺蕾莉娅！”他说着，把她的手举到唇边。
我怀里带着一束樱桃色的缎带，是她从胸前摘下给我的，也不知怎么，我一直贴身带着。我把它从怀里掏出，摔在奎因上尉脸上，拔出小剑冲上前，尖声喊道：“她撒谎——她撒谎，奎因上尉！拔剑吧，先生，你要还是个男人，就自卫！”话音未落，我便朝那怪物扑去，一把揪住他的领子；诺拉的尖叫响彻四野，闻声之下，另一位上尉和米茜急忙赶来。
大病一场，我像野草似的疯长，如今已近六英尺的全高；可站在那魁伟的英国上尉旁边，我不过是一根板条——他那样的小腿和肩膀，连巴斯的轿夫头子也自愧不如。

===Original===

He turned very red, and then exceedingly pale at my attack upon him, and slipped back and clutched at his sword﻿—when Nora, in an agony of terror, flung herself round him, screaming, “Eugenio! Captain Quin, for Heaven’s sake spare the child﻿—he is but an infant.”
“And ought to be whipped for his impudence,” said the Captain; “but never fear, Miss Brady, I shall not touch him; your *favourite* is safe from me.” So saying, he stooped down and picked up the bunch of ribands which had fallen at Nora’s feet, and handing it to her, said in a sarcastic tone, “When ladies make presents to gentlemen, it is time for *other* gentlemen to retire.”
“Good heavens, Quin!” cried the girl; “he is but a boy.”
“I am a man,” roared I, “and will prove it.”
“And don’t signify any more than my parrot or lapdog. Mayn’t I give a bit of riband to my own cousin?”
“You are perfectly welcome, miss,” continued the Captain, “as many yards as you like.”
“Monster!” exclaimed the dear girl; “your father was a tailor, and you are always thinking of the shop. But I’ll have my revenge, I will! Reddy, will you see me insulted?”
“Indeed, Miss Nora,” says I, “I intend to have his blood as sure as my name’s Redmond.”
“I’ll send for the usher to cane you, little boy,” said the Captain, regaining his self-possession; “but as for you, miss, I have the honour to wish you a good day.”
He took off his hat with much ceremony, made a low conge, and was just walking off, when Mick, my cousin, came up, whose ear had likewise been caught by the scream.
“Hoity-toity! Jack Quin, what’s the matter here?” says Mick; “Nora in tears, Redmond’s ghost here with his sword drawn, and you making a bow?”
“I’ll tell you what it is, Mr. Brady,” said the Englishman: “I have had enough of Miss Nora, here, and your Irish ways. I ain’t used to ’em, sir.”
“Well, well! what is it?” said Mick good-humouredly (for he owed Quin a great deal of money as it turned out); “we’ll make you used to our ways, or adopt English ones.”
“It’s not the English way for ladies to have two lovers” (the “Henglish way,” as the captain called it), “and so, Mr. Brady, I’ll thank you to pay me the sum you owe me, and I’ll resign all claims to this young lady. If she has a fancy for schoolboys, let her take ’em, sir.”
“Pooh, pooh! Quin, you are joking,” said Mick.
“I never was more in earnest,” replied the other.
“By Heaven, then, look to yourself!”

===Chinese===

遭我这一袭，他先是涨红了脸，随即刷地惨白，退后一步去抓剑——诺拉吓得魂飞魄散，扑上去抱住他，尖叫道：“欧金尼奥！奎因上尉，看在老天爷份上饶了这孩子吧——他不过是个娃娃。”
“娃娃正该为他的无礼吃顿鞭子，”上尉说，“不过别怕，布雷迪小姐，我不碰他；您的*心肝儿*在我手里保平安。”说着，他俯身拾起落在诺拉脚边的那束缎带，递还给她，语带讥讽地说：“女士们向先生们赠礼的时候，就是*别的*先生们该告退的时候了。”
“天哪，奎因！”姑娘喊道，“他不过是个孩子。”
“我是条汉子，”我吼道，“且要证明给你看。”
“证明也不过同我的鹦鹉、哈巴狗一个分量。把一截缎带送给自己表弟，难道不行吗？”
“您请便，小姐，”上尉接着说，“要几码给几码。”
“怪物！”可爱的姑娘喊道，“你父亲是个裁缝，你就总惦记着铺子里的尺寸。可我要报复的，一定报复！雷迪，你眼看我受辱不管吗？”
“您放心，诺拉小姐，”我说，“我名唤雷德蒙，就一定要他的血。”
“我叫人去请助教先生来给你一顿教鞭，小娃娃，”上尉恢复了几分镇定，说，“至于您，小姐，我荣幸地祝您日安。”
他煞有介事地脱帽，深深打了一躬，正要举步走开，我表哥米克来了——尖叫声同样把他引了过来。
“嗬，好家伙！杰克·奎因，这儿唱的哪一出？”米克说，“诺拉挂着泪，雷德蒙的鬼魂提着剑，你倒在这儿作揖打躬？”
“我明说了吧，布雷迪先生，”那英国人说，“这位诺拉小姐，还有你们爱尔兰的做派，我都受够了。我不惯这些，先生。”
“好，好！到底怎么了？”米克脾气很好地问（后来才知道，他欠了奎因一大笔钱），“我们叫你习惯我们的做派，不然就学英国规矩。”
“女士们脚踩两只船，这可不合英国规矩”（上尉把它念成“英赫国规矩”），“所以，布雷迪先生，劳您的驾，把欠我的钱还我，我对这位小姐的一切权利也一并放弃。她既爱学堂里的娃娃，就让她抱着娃娃去吧，先生。”
“呸，呸！奎因，你说笑呢。”米克说。
“我从没这么正经过。”对方答道。
“那就对天起誓，你自己留神！”

===Original===

shouted Mick. “Infamous seducer! infernal deceiver!﻿—you come and wind your toils round this suffering angel here﻿—you win her heart and leave her﻿—and fancy her brother won’t defend her? Draw this minute, you slave! and let me cut the wicked heart out of your body!”
“This is regular assassination,” said Quin, starting back; “there’s two on ’em on me at once. Fagan, you won’t let ’em murder me?”
“ ’Faith!” said Captain Fagan, who seemed mightily amused, “you may settle your own quarrel, Captain Quin;” and coming over to me, whispered, “At him again, you little fellow.”
“As long as Mr. Quin withdraws his claim,” said I, “I, of course, do not interfere.”
“I do, sir﻿—I do,” said Mr. Quin, more and more flustered.
“Then defend yourself like a man, curse you!” cried Mick again. “Mysie, lead this poor victim away﻿—Redmond and Fagan will see fair play between us.”
“Well now﻿—I don’t﻿—give me time﻿—I’m puzzled﻿—I﻿—I don’t know which way to look.”
“Like the donkey betwixt the two bundles of hay,” said Mr. Fagan drily, “and there’s pretty pickings on either side.”

===Chinese===

米克喊道。“无耻的诱奸犯！恶毒的骗子！——你跑来把罗网缠住这位受害的天使——骗取她的心再把她甩掉——还当她哥哥不会替她出头？这分钟就拔剑，你这奴才！让我把你那颗黑心从你腔子里挖出来！”
“这分明是蓄意谋杀，”奎因倒退一步说，“一下子来了两个对付我一个。费根，你总不能眼看他们杀了我吧？”
“当真！”费根上尉说，他似乎被逗得不轻，“你们自家的事自家了，奎因上尉。”说着踱到我这边，悄声道：“再上，小家伙。”
“只要奎因先生撤回他的权利主张，”我说，“我自然不再插手。”
“我撤，先生——我撤。”奎因先生愈发慌张地说。
“那就像个男人一样自卫，该死的！”米克又喊道，“米茜，把这可怜的受害者带走——雷德蒙和费根给咱们做公正人。”
“这个——我不——给我点工夫——我懵了——我——我不知往哪儿看才好。”
“就像那头站在两捆干草之间的驴子，”费根先生干巴巴地说，“两边都有得挑呢。”

## VI The Crimp Wagon﻿—Military Episodes / 兵贩子马车——军旅纪事
===Original===

The covered wagon to which I was ordered to march was standing, as I have said, in the courtyard of the farm, with another dismal vehicle of the same kind hard by it. Each was pretty well filled with a crew of men, whom the atrocious crimp who had seized upon me, had enlisted under the banners of the glorious Frederick; and I could see by the lanterns of the sentinels, as they thrust me into the straw, a dozen dark figures huddled together in the horrible moving prison where I was now to be confined. A scream and a curse from my opposite neighbour showed me that he was most likely wounded, as I myself was; and, during the whole of the wretched night, the moans and sobs of the poor fellows in similar captivity kept up a continual painful chorus, which effectually prevented my getting any relief from my ills in sleep. At midnight (as far as I could judge) the horses were put to the wagons, and the creaking lumbering machines were put in motion. A couple of soldiers, strongly armed, sat on the outer bench of the cart, and their grim faces peered in with their lanterns every now and then through the canvas curtains, that they might count the number of their prisoners. The brutes were half-drunk, and were singing love and war songs, such as “O Gretchen mein Täubchen, mein Herzenstrompet, mein Kanon, mein Heerpauk und meine Musket,” “Prinz Eugen der edle Ritter,” and the like; their wild whoops and yodels making doleful discord with the groans of us captives within the wagons. Many a time afterwards have I heard these ditties sung on the march, or in the barrack-room, or round the fires as we lay out at night.
I was not near so unhappy, in spite of all, as I had been on my first enlisting in Ireland. At least, thought I, if I am degraded to be a private soldier there will be no one of my acquaintance who will witness my shame; and that is the point which I have always cared for most. There will be no one to say, “There is young Redmond Barry, the descendant of the Barrys, the fashionable young blood of Dublin, pipeclaying his belt and carrying his brown Bess.” Indeed, but for that opinion of the world, with which it is necessary that every man of spirit should keep upon equal terms, I, for my part, would have always been contented with the humblest portion.

===Chinese===

如前所说，奉命押我前往的那辆篷车，正停在农庄的院子里，旁边紧挨着另一辆同样阴惨的车子。两辆车都塞满了人——都是那个抓了我的万恶兵贩子，替光荣的腓特烈旗下募来的兵。哨兵的灯笼照着，他们把我往草堆里一推，我看见十来条黑影蜷缩在这座可怕的移动监狱里——如今我也将被囚在其中。对面邻座的一声惨叫加一句咒骂，告诉我他多半也负了伤，同我一样；整整一个悲惨的夜晚，这些同样被囚的可怜人唉哼呜咽，此起彼伏，凑成一片持续不断的苦楚合唱，叫我连从睡梦中求一点解脱都不得。半夜前后（我估摸着），马匹套上了车，吱嘎作响的笨重机器开动了。两名全副武装的士兵坐在车外的条凳上，不时提着灯笼从帆布帘缝里探进狰狞的脸，清点俘虏的数目。这两个畜生半醉着，唱着情歌军歌，什么《哦格蕾琴，我的小鸽子，我的心之号角，我的大炮，我的战鼓和我的火枪》啦，《高贵骑士欧根亲王》啦，等等；他们狂野的呼喝与约德尔调，同我们车中俘虏的呻吟凑成凄凉的噪音。后来在行军途中、在营房里、在夜里露宿的篝火旁，这些小调我听过何止千百遍。
尽管如此，我不像当初在爱尔兰头一回被骗入伍时那样痛不欲生。至少，我想，即便我沦落成一名列兵，也没有一个熟人会目睹我的耻辱；而这正是我一向最看重的一点。不会有人说：“瞧，雷德蒙·巴里少爷，巴里家的后代，都柏林的风流哥儿，正给腰带刷白土、扛着他的褐贝丝呢。”说真的，若不是为了那份世人的舆论——凡是有血性的人，都非得同它平起平坐不可——就我而言，安于最卑微的命运原也无妨。

===Original===

Now here, to all intents and purposes, one was as far removed from the world as in the wilds of Siberia, or in Robinson Crusoe’s Island. And I reasoned with myself thus:﻿—“Now you are caught, there is no use in repining: make the best of your situation, and get all the pleasure you can out of it. There are a thousand opportunities of plunder, etc., offered to the soldier in wartime, out of which he can get both pleasure and profit: make use of these, and be happy. Besides, you are extraordinarily brave, handsome, and clever: and who knows but you may procure advancement in your new service?”
In this philosophical way I looked at my misfortunes, determining not to be cast down by them; and bore woes and my broken head with perfect magnanimity. The latter was, for the moment, an evil against which it required no small powers of endurance to contend; for the jolts of the wagon were dreadful, and every shake caused a throb in my brain which I thought would have split my skull. As the morning dawned, I saw that the man next me, a gaunt yellow-haired creature, in black, had a cushion of straw under his head.
“Are you wounded, comrade?” said I.
“Praised be the Lord,” said he, “I am sore hurt in spirit and body, and bruised in many members; wounded, however, am I not. And you, poor youth?”
“I am wounded in the head,” said I, “and I want your pillow: give it me﻿—I’ve a clasp-knife in my pocket!” and with this I gave him a terrible look, meaning to say (and mean it I did, for look you, *à la guerre c’est à la guerre*, and I am none of your milksops) that, unless he yielded me the accommodation, I would give him a taste of my steel.
“I would give it thee without any threat, friend,” said the yellow-haired man meekly, and handed me over his little sack of straw.
He then leaned himself back as comfortably as he could against the cart, and began repeating, “Ein’ feste Burg ist unser Gott,” by which I concluded that I had got into the company of a parson. With the jolts of the wagon, and accidents of the journey, various more exclamations and movements of the passengers showed what a motley company we were. Every now and then a countryman would burst into tears; a French voice would be heard to say, “*O mon Dieu!﻿—mon Dieu!

===Chinese===

而在这儿，无论从哪方面说，人都同红尘隔得远远的，仿佛身处西伯利亚荒原，或鲁滨逊·克鲁索的海岛。我这样开导自己：“如今你既已落网，怨天尤人无用：把处境往好里过，能捞多少乐子捞多少乐子。战时摆在士兵面前的，打劫啦等等的机会成百上千，既可得乐又可得利：善用它们，快活起来。再说，你勇气、相貌、头脑样样出众：谁保得准你在新差事里不会高升呢？”
我便以这等通达的眼光看待自己的不幸，决意不为之消沉；怀着十足的豁达，承受苦难和我那破了口的脑袋。后者眼下就是一桩磨难，没有相当的本事还真熬不住：车的颠簸可怕极了，每颠一下，脑仁里就轰然一跳，跳得我直以为脑壳要裂开。天蒙蒙亮时，我看见旁边那人——一个瘦长的黄头发家伙，穿一身黑——头底下垫着个草枕。
“你受伤了吗，战友？”我说。
“赞美主，”他说，“我在灵里身上都伤得厉害，好几处青了肿了；不过刀枪伤是没有的。你呢，可怜的年轻人？”
“我头上挂了彩，”我说，“我要你的枕头：给我——我兜里有把折刀！”说着狠狠瞪了他一眼，意思是（我也真是这个意思，要知道，*战场上就得按战场的规矩*，我可不是那号娘儿们腔），他若不把这个舒服让出来，我就叫他尝尝我的刀尖。
“不用吓唬我也给你，朋友。”黄头发男人温顺地说，把他那小袋草递了过来。
随后他尽量往车厢上舒舒服服一靠，念诵起《上主是我坚固保障》来，我由此断定，同行的有一位牧师。随着车的颠簸和一路的磕碰，乘客们更多的叫嚷和动静，显出我们这伙人是何等五方杂处。时不时有个乡下汉子放声大哭；一个法国口音嚷着“*哦我的天！——我的天！

===Original===

*” a couple more of the same nation were jabbering oaths and chattering incessantly; and a certain allusion to his own and everybody else’s eyes, which came from a stalwart figure at the far corner, told me that there was certainly an Englishman in our crew.
But I was spared soon the tedium and discomforts of the journey. In spite of the clergyman’s cushion, my head, which was throbbing with pain, was brought abruptly in contact with the side of the wagon; it began to bleed afresh: I became almost lightheaded. I only recollect having a draught of water here and there; once stopping at a fortified town, where an officer counted us:﻿—all the rest of the journey was passed in a drowsy stupor, from which, when I awoke, I found myself lying in a hospital bed, with a nun in a white hood watching over me.
“They are in sad spiritual darkness,” said a voice from the bed next to me, when the nun had finished her kind offices and retired: “they are in the night of error, and yet there is the light of faith in those poor creatures.”
It was my comrade of the crimp wagon, his huge broad face looming out from under a white nightcap, and ensconced in the bed beside.
“What! you there, Herr Pastor?” said I.
“Only a candidate, sir,” answered the white nightcap. “But, praised be Heaven! you have come to. You have had a wild time of it. You have been talking in the English language (with which I am acquainted) of Ireland, and a young lady, and Mick, and of another young lady, and of a house on fire, and of the British Grenadiers, concerning whom you sung us parts of a ballad, and of a number of other matters appertaining, no doubt, to your personal history.”
“It has been a very strange one,” said I; “and, perhaps, there is no man in the world, of my birth, whose misfortunes can at all be compared to mine.”
I do not object to own that I am disposed to brag of my birth and other acquirements; for I have always found that if a man does not give himself a good word, his friends will not do it for him.
“Well,” said my fellow-patient, “I have no doubt yours is a strange tale, and shall be glad to hear it anon; but at present you must not be permitted to speak much, for your fever has been long, and your exhaustion great.”
“Where are we?” I asked; and the candidate informed me that we were in the bishopric and town of Fulda, at present occupied by Prince Henry’s troops.

===Chinese===

*”；同国的另外两个骂骂咧咧，絮叨个不停；远角上一条大汉口口声声不离他自己的眼睛和别人的眼睛，我一听便知，我们这伙人里必定有个英国人。
好在旅程的烦闷与辛苦不久便与我无缘了。虽有牧师的草枕垫着，我那突突作痛的脑袋还是猛地撞上了车厢板；伤口又出了血：我几乎发起昏来。我只记得东一茬西一茬地喝过几口水；在一座设防的城镇停过一回，一位军官点了我们的人数：其余的路程都在昏沉的迷睡中过去；一觉醒来，我发现自己躺在医院床上，一位戴白头巾的修女看护着我。
“她们处在可悲的灵性黑暗中，”修女行完善举退出去后，邻床一个声音说，“她们身处谬误之夜，然而那些可怜人身上自有信仰之光。”
正是兵贩子马车上的那位同伴，他从白色睡帽底下露出宽宽的大脸盘，安卧在旁边的病床上。
“怎么！是您哪，牧师先生？”我说。
“不过是位候补牧师罢了，先生，”白睡帽答道，“不过，赞美上天！您醒过来了。您先前可闹得凶。您一直说英语（这门语言我通晓），说爱尔兰，说一位年轻小姐，说米克，又说另一位年轻小姐，说一座着火的房子，还说英国掷弹兵——关于他们，您还给我们唱了一段歌谣——还有许多别的事，无疑都同您本人的经历有关。”
“我的经历确实离奇，”我说，“世上出身同我一样的人里头，恐怕没有谁的厄运比得上我的。”
我不否认，我素有夸耀自家出身和其他造诣的癖好；因为我一向发现，一个人若不替自己说好话，朋友是不会替他说的。
“唔，”我的病友说，“您的故事想必离奇，回头我乐意领教；只是眼下可不许您多说话，您的热病拖得久，元气亏得厉害。”
“我们在哪儿？”我问；候补牧师告诉我，我们在富尔达主教辖区和富尔达城里，眼下由亨利亲王的部队占领。城郊同法国前哨打过一场小仗，一发子弹穿进车厢，可怜的候补牧师就是那时负的伤。

===Original===

There had been a skirmish with an out-party of French near the town, in which a shot entering the wagon, the poor candidate had been wounded.
As the reader knows already my history, I will not take the trouble to repeat it here, or to give the additions with which I favoured my comrade in misfortune. But I confess that I told him ours was the greatest family and finest palace in Ireland, that we were enormously wealthy, related to all the peerage descended from the ancient kings, etc.; and, to my surprise, in the course of our conversation, I found that my interlocutor knew a great deal more about Ireland than I did. When, for instance, I spoke of my descent﻿—
“From which race of kings?” said he.
“Oh!” said I (for my memory for dates was never very accurate), “from the old ancient kings of all.”
“What! can you trace your origin to the sons Japhet?” said he.
“ ’Faith, I can,” answered I, “and farther too﻿—Nebuchadnezzar, if you like.”
“I see,” said the candidate, smiling, “that you look upon those legends with incredulity. These Partholans and Nemedians, of whom your writers fondly make mention, cannot be authentically vouched for in history. Nor do I believe that we have any more foundation for the tales concerning them, than for the legends relative to Joseph of Arimathea and King Bruce which prevailed two centuries back in the sister island.”
And then he began a discourse about the Phoenicians, the Scyths or Goths, the Tuath de Danans, Tacitus, and King MacNeil; which was, to say the truth, the very first news I had heard of those personages. As for English, he spoke it as well as I, and had seven more languages, he said, equally at his command; for, on my quoting the only Latin line that I knew, that out of the poet Homer, which says﻿—

> “*As in praesenti perfectum fumat in avi*,”

===Chinese===

我的经历读者诸君已经知道，此处不再费神重复，也不细说我对这位患难同伴添油加醋的部分。但我承认，我对他说，我们家是爱尔兰第一等的门第、第一等的府邸，我们家资巨万，同古代列王传下来的所有贵族都沾亲带故，等等；不料谈着谈着，我吃惊地发现，这位对话者对爱尔兰的了解远比我自己多。比如，我谈起我的世系时——
“从哪一支王系传下来的？”他问。
“哦！”我说（我对年代的记性向来不大准），“从所有的老古董国王传下来的呗。”
“什么！你能把家世上溯到雅弗的诸子？”他说。
“当真可以，”我答道，“还能更远——远到尼布甲尼撒，你若乐意。”
“我明白了，”候补牧师含笑说，“您对那些传说是不信的。你们的作家津津乐道的什么帕索兰人、内梅德人，史册上并无确凿凭证。我看那些故事，同两百年前海峡对面那座岛上流传的亚利马太的约瑟、布鲁斯王的传说，同样是空穴来风。”
接着他开讲腓尼基人、斯基泰人还是哥特人、达努神族、塔西佗、麦克尼尔王；说实在的，这些名号我还是头一回听闻。至于英语，他说得同我一样好；他还说另有七种语言同样烂熟；因为当我念出我唯一会的那句拉丁文——出自诗人荷马的那句——

> “*As in praesenti perfectum fumat in avi*”，

===Original===

he began to speak to me in the Roman tongue; on which I was fain to tell him that we pronounced it in a different way in Ireland, and so got off the conversation.
My honest friend’s history was a curious one, and it may be told here in order to show of what motley materials our levies were composed:﻿—
“I am,” said he, “a Saxon by birth, my father being pastor of the village of Pfannkuchen, where I imbibed the first rudiments of knowledge. At sixteen (I am now twenty-three), having mastered the Greek and Latin tongues, with the French, English, Arabic, and Hebrew; and having come into possession of a legacy of a hundred rixdalers, a sum amply sufficient to defray my University courses, I went to the famous academy of Gottingen, where I devoted four years to the exact sciences and theology. Also, I learned what worldly accomplishments I could command; taking a dancing-tutor at the expense of a groschen a lesson, a course of fencing from a French practitioner, and attending lectures on the great horse and the equestrian science at the hippodrome of a celebrated cavalry professor. My opinion is, that a man should know everything as far as in his power lies: that he should complete his cycle of experience; and, one science being as necessary as another, it behoves him, according to his means, to acquaint himself with all. For many branches of personal knowledge (as distinguished from spiritual: though I am not prepared to say that the distinction is a correct one), I confess I have found myself inapt. I attempted tight-rope dancing, with a Bohemian artist who appeared at our academy; but in this I failed lamentably, breaking my nose in the fall which I had. I also essayed to drive a coach-and-four, which an English student, Herr Graf Lord von Martingale, drove at the University. In this, too, I failed; oversetting the chariot at the postern, opposite the Berliner gate, with his Lordship’s friend, Fräulein Miss Kitty Coddlins within. I had been instructing the young lord in the German language when the above accident took place, and was dismissed by him in consequence. My means did not permit me further to pursue this curriculum (you will pardon me the joke), otherwise I have no doubt, I should have been able to take a place in any hippodrome in the world, and to handle the ribbons (as the high-well-born lord used to say) to perfection.

===Chinese===

他便用罗马话对我讲起来；我只得告诉他，这语言我们在爱尔兰另有念法，就这样把话头岔开了。
我这位忠厚朋友的历史颇奇特，不妨在此讲一讲，也好让各位看看，我们的兵源都是些什么七拼八凑的材料：——
“我，”他说，“生为萨克森人，家父是普凡库兴村的牧师，我在那儿汲取了最初的知识启蒙。十六岁上（今年二十三），我已通晓希腊、拉丁两种语言，兼及法语、英语、阿拉伯语和希伯来语；又继承了一百塔勒的遗产，这笔钱足够支付大学的全部课程，我便进了著名的格丁根学院，四年专攻精密科学与神学。世俗的才艺，力所能及的我也学：请舞蹈教师每课一戈罗申；跟一位法国师傅学了一期剑术；还在一位著名骑兵教授的跑马场里，听讲骏马与骑术之学。依我的见解，一个人力所能及，就该无所不晓：应当走完他经验的全程；学问既彼此同样必需，人就当量其财力，门门都通。至于许多身手的学问（同灵性的学问相对而言：虽则这个分别是否站得住，我不敢妄断）——我承认自己不成。我随一位来我们学院献艺的波希米亚艺人学过走绳；可惜败得凄惨，一跤摔破了鼻子。我还试过驾驶四马大车——大学里有个英国学生马丁格尔伯爵老爷驾这个。这也不成；车在柏林门对面的后门口翻了，车里还坐着老爷的朋友科德林斯小姐。出事时我正教这位少年老爷德语，因此被他辞退了。我的财力不许我继续这套课程（这个玩笑请多包涵），否则我毫不怀疑，天下任何跑马场我都能谋个位子，把缰绳（那位高贵的老爷惯常说）执到十全十美。”

===Original===

“At the University I delivered a thesis on the quadrature of the circle, which, I think, would interest you; and held a disputation in Arabic against Professor Strumpff, in which I was said to have the advantage. The languages of Southern Europe, of course, I acquired; and, to a person well-grounded in Sanskrit, the Northern idioms offer no difficulty. If you have ever attempted the Russian you will find it child’s play; and it will always be a source of regret to me that I have been enabled to get no knowledge (to speak of) of Chinese; and, but for the present dilemma, I had intended to pass over into England for that purpose and get a passage in one of the English company’s ships to Canton.
“I am not of a saving turn, hence my little fortune of a hundred rixdalers, which has served to keep many a prudent man for a score of years, barely sufficed for five years’ studies; after which my studies were interrupted, my pupils fell off, and I was obliged to devote much time to shoe-binding in order to save money, and, at a future period, resume my academic course. During this period I contracted an attachment” (here the candidate sighed a little) “with a person, who, though not beautiful, and forty years of age, is yet likely to sympathise with my existence; and, a month since, my kind friend and patron, University Prorector Doctor Nasenbrumm, having informed me that the Pfarrer of Rumpelwitz was dead, asked whether I would like to have my name placed upon the candidate list, and if I were minded to preach a trial sermon? As the gaining of this living would further my union with my Amalia, I joyously consented, and prepared a discourse.
“If you like I will recite it to you﻿—No?﻿—Well, I will give you extracts from it upon our line of march. To proceed, then, with my biographical sketch, which is now very near a conclusion; or, as I should more correctly say, which has very nearly brought me to the present period of time: I preached that sermon at Rumpelwitz, in which I hope that the Babylonian question was pretty satisfactorily set at rest. I preached it before the Herr Baron and his noble family, and some officers of distinction who were staying at his castle. Mr.

===Chinese===

“在大学里，我宣读过一篇论化圆为方的论文，想必您会有兴趣；还用阿拉伯语同斯特鲁姆普夫教授进行过一场辩难，据说占了上风。南欧诸语言自然都在我掌握之中；而梵文根基扎实的人，北欧方言毫不费事。您若试过俄语，便知那不过是儿戏；只有中文，我至今未能（说得上）学到，此乃终身憾事；若非眼下这场困厄，我原打算为此渡海去英格兰，搭英国公司的船到广州去。
“我天生不会攒钱，那一百塔勒的小小家财，够许多精细人过二十年的，勉强供了我五年学业；此后学业中断，学生也散了，我只得花许多工夫纳鞋帮攒钱，指望日后再续学业。在此期间，我结下了一段情缘”（说到此处，候补牧师轻轻叹了口气）“对象虽说不上美，年已四十，却颇能同我的存在相怜相惜；一个月前，我那位好心的朋友兼恩主、大学副学监纳斯布鲁姆博士告知我，伦佩尔维茨的牧师故去了，问我可愿把名字列进候补名单，可愿去试讲一场布道？谋得这份俸禄便可促成我同阿玛莉亚的结合，我便欢然应允，着手预备讲章。
“您若有兴致，我可以背诵给您听——不必？——好，行军路上我再给您摘几段。那就接着讲我的小传——如今已近尾声；或者说得更确切些，它已几乎讲到眼下这个时辰了：那篇布道我在伦佩尔维茨讲过了，其中巴比伦问题，我希望是相当圆满地解决了。听讲的有男爵老爷和他高贵的家眷，还有几位借住他城堡的显赫军官。莫泽

===Original===

Doctor Moser of Halle followed me in the evening discourse; but, though his exercise was learned, and he disposed of a passage of Ignatius, which he proved to be a manifest interpolation, I do not think his sermon had the effect which mine produced, and that the Rumpelwitzers much relished it. After the sermon, all the candidates walked out of church together, and supped lovingly at the Blue Stag in Rumpelwitz.
“While so occupied, a waiter came in and said that a person without wished to speak to one of the reverend candidates, ‘the tall one.’ This could only mean me, for I was a head and shoulders higher than any other reverend gentleman present. I issued out to see who was the person desiring to hold converse with me, and found a man whom I had no difficulty in recognising as one of the Jewish persuasion.
“ ‘Sir,’ said this Hebrew, ‘I have heard from a friend, who was in your church today, the heads of the admirable discourse you pronounced there. It has affected me deeply, most deeply. There are only one or two points on which I am yet in doubt, and if your honour could but condescend to enlighten me on these, I think﻿—I think Solomon Hirsch would be a convert to your eloquence.’
“ ‘What are these points, my good friend?’ said I; and I pointed out to him the twenty-four heads of my sermon, asking him in which of these his doubts lay.
“We had been walking up and down before the inn while our conversation took place, but the windows being open, and my comrades having heard the discourse in the morning, requested me, rather peevishly, not to resume it at that period. I, therefore, moved on with my disciple, and, at his request, began at once the sermon; for my memory is good for anything, and I can repeat any book I have read thrice.
“I poured out, then, under the trees, and in the calm moonlight, that discourse which I had pronounced under the blazing sun of noon. My Israelite only interrupted me by exclamations indicative of surprise, assent, admiration, and increasing conviction. ‘Prodigious!’ said he;﻿—‘*Wunderschön!*’ would he remark at the conclusion of some eloquent passage; in a word, he exhausted the complimentary interjections of our language: and to compliments what man is averse?

===Chinese===

博士——哈勒来的——接着讲了晚堂；不过，尽管他的功课够渊博，还料理了一段伊格纳修斯——证明那是明白的伪窜——我并不认为他那篇布道有我那篇的效力，伦佩尔维茨人也不怎么领他的情。布道完毕，众候补牧师一同走出教堂，在伦佩尔维茨的蓝鹿客栈亲亲热热地用了晚饭。
“正吃着，伙计进来禀报，说外头有个人求见可敬的候补牧师中的一位——‘高的那位’。这只能是指我，因为在场的可敬先生们，没一个不是比我矮一头加半肩。我出去看是谁要同我攀谈，一见那人，便毫不费力地认出他是犹太教门里的。
“‘先生，’这位希伯来人说，‘我有位朋友今天在贵教堂，听得了您那篇令人叹服的讲章的条目。我深受感动，感动至深。只有一两点我尚存疑惑，先生若肯屈尊为我解惑，我想——我想所罗门·赫施定要皈依在您的雄辩之下了。’
“‘是哪些点呢，我的好朋友？’我说；我把讲章的二十四个条目一一指给他看，问他的疑惑落在哪一条。
“‘谈话时我们在客栈门前来回踱着，可窗户开着，同伴们上午已听过那篇布道，便有些不耐烦地求我，那会儿别再讲一遍。于是我带着我的门徒挪了地方，应他的请求，当场开讲那篇布道；因为我的记性什么都装得下，凡读过的书，读过三遍便可背诵。
“‘就这样，在树影之下，静静的月光里，我把正午烈日下讲过的那篇布道又倾吐了一遍。我的以色列人只以惊叹打断我，那惊叹里有诧异，有首肯，有倾慕，有与日俱增的信服。‘神了！’他说；——每逢一段雄文收梢，便叹一声‘*妙不可言！*’；一句话，他把我们语言里表敬叹的感叹词用了个遍：而世上哪个男人同恭维有仇呢？”

===Original===

I think we must have walked two miles when I got to my third head and my companion begged I would enter his house, which we now neared, and partake of a glass of beer; to which I was never averse.
“That house, sir, was the inn at which you, too, if I judge aright, were taken. No sooner was I in the place, than three crimps rushed upon me, told me I was a deserter, and their prisoner, and called upon me to deliver up my money and papers; which I did with a solemn protest as to my sacred character. They consisted of my sermon in MS., Prorector Nasenbrumm’s recommendatory letter, proving my identity, and three groschen four pfennigs in bullion. I had already been in the cart twenty hours when you reached the house. The French officer, who lay opposite you (he who screamed when you trod on his foot, for he was wounded), was brought in shortly before your arrival. He had been taken with his epaulets and regimentals, and declared his quality and rank; but he was alone (I believe it was some affair of love with a Hessian lady which caused him to be unattended); and as the persons into whose hands he fell will make more profit of him as a recruit than as a prisoner, he is made to share our fate. He is not the first by many scores so captured. One of M. de Soubise’s cooks, and three actors out of a troop in the French camp, several deserters from your English troops (the men are led away by being told that there is no flogging in the Prussian service), and three Dutchmen were taken besides.”
“And you,” said I﻿—“you who were just on the point of getting a valuable living﻿—you who have so much learning, are you not indignant at the outrage?”
“I am a Saxon,” said the candidate, “and there is no use in indignation. Our government is crushed under Frederick’s heel these five years, and I might as well hope for mercy from the Grand Mogul. Nor am I, in truth, discontented with my lot; I have lived on a penny bread for so many years, that a soldier’s rations will be a luxury to me. I do not care about more or less blows of a cane; all such evils are passing, and therefore endurable. I will never, God willing, slay a man in combat; but I am not unanxious to experience on myself the effect of the war-passion, which has had so great an influence on the human race.

===Chinese===

我看走到第三条目时，足有两英里了；同伴求我进他现在临近的家，喝杯啤酒；对啤酒我向无恶感。
“那座房子，先生，就是我揣度您也一并遭擒的那家客栈。我一进门，三个兵贩子便扑上来，说我是逃兵，是他们的囚犯，勒令我交出钱财文书；我郑重声明白己的神圣身份，照交了。计有：布道稿一份，副学监纳斯布鲁姆的荐书一封——足证我身份——以及铸币三戈罗申又四芬尼。
“您到客栈时，我在车里已关了二十个钟头。躺在您对面的那位法国军官（您踩了他一脚他惨叫的那位，他带着伤），是您到前不久才押来的。他被俘时肩章军服俱全，也报了身份军阶；可他是孤身一人（我想是同一位黑森夫人的一段情事叫他没带随从）；而落到这些人手里，把他当新兵比当俘虏更有油水，他就只好同我们共命运了。这么被掳来的，前前后后几十个不止。苏比斯先生手下的一名厨子、法国营中一个戏班的三个戏子、几个从你们英国部队开小差的（那些人是听了‘普鲁士军里不打鞭子’的话才上钩的），还有三个荷兰人，都是这么抓来的。”
“那您呢，”我说——“您眼看就要到手一份肥美的圣俸——您这样满腹学问的人，对这场横暴不愤慨吗？”
“我是萨克森人，”候补牧师说，“愤慨无用。我们的政府被腓特烈的脚跟踩了五年，我还不如去求大莫卧儿皇帝开恩。说实在的，我对自己的命运也没什么不满；一便士的面包我吃了这些年，士兵的口粮于我就是盛宴。藤条多几下少几下我不在意；这类祸患都是过眼的，因而受得住。上帝容许的话，我永不在战斗中杀人；但战争激情对人类影响如彼之大，我倒真想在自身体验一番它的效力。”

===Original===

It was for the same reason that I determined to marry Amalia, for a man is not a complete Mensch until he is the father of a family; to be which is a condition of his existence, and therefore a duty of his education. Amalia must wait; she is out of the reach of want, being, indeed, cook to the Frau Prorectorinn Nasenbrumm, my worthy patron’s lady. I have one or two books with me, which no one is likely to take from me, and one in my heart which is the best of all. If it shall please Heaven to finish my existence here, before I can prosecute my studies further, what cause have I to repine? I pray God I may not be mistaken, but I think I have wronged no man, and committed no mortal sin. If I have, I know where to look for forgiveness; and if I die, as I have said, without knowing all that I would desire to learn, shall I not be in a situation to learn *everything*, and what can human soul ask for more?
“Pardon me for putting so many *I*’s in my discourse,” said the candidate, “but when a man is talking of himself, ’tis the briefest and simplest way of talking.”
In which, perhaps, though I hate egotism, I think my friend was right. Although he acknowledged himself to be a mean-spirited fellow, with no more ambition than to know the contents of a few musty books, I think the man had some good in him; especially in the resolution with which he bore his calamities. Many a gallant man of the highest honour is often not proof against these, and has been known to despair over a bad dinner, or to be cast down at a ragged-elbowed coat. *My* maxim is to bear all, to put up with water if you cannot get Burgundy, and if you have no velvet to be content with frieze. But Burgundy and velvet are the best, *bien entendu*, and the man is a fool who will not seize the best when the scramble is open.
The heads of the sermon which my friend the theologian intended to impart to me, were, however, never told; for, after our coming out of the hospital, he was drafted into a regiment quartered as far as possible from his native country, in Pomerania; while I was put into the Bülow regiment, of which the ordinary headquarters were Berlin. The Prussian regiments seldom change their garrisons as ours do, for the fear of desertion is so great, that it becomes necessary to know the face of every individual in the service; and, in time of peace, men live and die in the same town.

===Chinese===

我决定娶阿玛莉亚也是为了同样的缘故：人不到做了一家之父，就不算完全的“人”（Mensch）；做父亲是人生在世的一个条件，因而也是他教养的一项义务。阿玛莉亚只好等；她衣食无忧——她正是我可敬恩主的太太纳斯布鲁姆副学监夫人的厨娘。我身边带着一两本书，大概没人会拿走；心里还揣着一本，那是最好的一本。倘若上天乐意，在我深造之前就在此地了结我的存在，我有什么可抱怨的？我求上天保佑我没想错：我不曾亏欠过谁，也不曾犯过死罪。倘若有，我知道上哪儿求饶恕；而若如我方才所说，我至死未能尽知愿学的一切，那时我不正可以学习*一切*吗？人的灵魂还能别求什么呢？
“请原谅我话里这么多的‘我’，”候补牧师说，“人讲起自己来，这是最简便的说法。”
这话我想他是对的，虽说素来厌恶自我主义。他自认是个胸无大志的人，志向不过是知晓几本霉旧书的内容；但我认为这人身上自有善处，尤其是承受灾难的那份坚毅。多少气概冲天、荣誉至上的好汉偏偏经不起这个：为一顿坏饭灰心的，为一件肘上磨破的外套消沉的，都大有人在。*我的*座右铭是：一切皆可忍受；喝不上勃艮第，白水也将就；没有天鹅绒，粗呢也知足。不过勃艮第和天鹅绒终归是最好的，*这是自然*；一旦争夺开场还不去抢最好的，那人是傻瓜。
只是我那位神学家朋友打算传授给我的布道条目，终究没能讲成：出医院后，他被拨进一支驻防地离他故乡尽可能远的团队，在波美拉尼亚；我则进了比洛团——团部常驻柏林。普鲁士团队不像我们的那样常换防地；他们怕逃兵怕得厉害，以致必须认得军中每一张脸；太平年月，士兵在同一座城里生，也在同一座城里死。

===Original===

This does not add, as may be imagined, to the amusements of the soldier’s life. It is lest any young gentleman like myself should take a fancy to a military career, and fancy that of a private soldier a tolerable one, that I am giving these, I hope, moral descriptions of what we poor fellows in the ranks really suffered.
As soon as we recovered, we were dismissed from the nuns and the hospital to the town prison of Fulda, where we were kept like slaves and criminals, with artillerymen with lighted matches at the doors of the courtyards and the huge black dormitory where some hundreds of us lay; until we were despatched to our different destinations. It was soon seen by the exercise which were the old soldiers amongst us, and which the recruits; and for the former, while we lay in prison, there was a little more leisure: though, if possible, a still more strict watch kept than over the broken-spirited yokels who had been forced or coaxed into the service. To describe the characters here assembled would require Mr. Gilray’s own pencil. There were men of all nations and callings. The Englishmen boxed and bullied; the Frenchmen played cards, and danced, and fenced; the heavy Germans smoked their pipes and drank beer, if they could manage to purchase it. Those who had anything to risk gambled, and at this sport I was pretty lucky, for, not having a penny when I entered the depot (having been robbed of every farthing of my property by the rascally crimps), I won near a dollar in my very first game at cards with one of the Frenchmen; who did not think of asking whether I could pay or not upon losing. Such, at least, is the advantage of having a gentlemanlike appearance; it has saved me many a time since by procuring me credit when my fortunes were at their lowest ebb.
Among the Frenchmen there was a splendid man and soldier, whose real name we never knew, but whose ultimate history created no small sensation, when it came to be known in the Prussian army. If beauty and courage are proofs of nobility, as (although I have seen some of the ugliest dogs and the greatest cowards in the world in the noblesse) I have no doubt courage and beauty are, this Frenchman must have been of the highest families in France, so grand and noble was his manner, so superb his person.

===Chinese===

可以想见，这并不会给士兵的生活添什么乐趣。我之所以写下这些——但愿算得上道德教谕——描述我们行伍中的可怜人真正受的苦，正是要叫哪位同我当年一样的少爷打消从军的兴致，别把当列兵想成一份还过得去的营生。
伤一好，修女和医院便不管我们了，把我们移交给富尔达的城监，当奴隶和罪犯一般关押：院门口和那间黑黢黢的大宿舍门前，站着拿点火棒的炮兵——宿舍里躺着几百号人；直到我们被遣往各自的去处。操练几回，谁是老兵谁是新兵很快见分晓；我们在押期间，前者的闲工夫略多些：虽说对前者的看守，若有可能，比对那些被强征或诓骗入伍、意志消沉的乡巴佬还要严。要描画此处聚集的人物，非得吉尔雷先生亲执画笔不可。各国各族、三教九流的人都有。英国人打拳逞霸；法国人打牌、跳舞、击剑；笨重的德国人抽着烟斗灌啤酒——只要买得着。有俩钱的都赌；这项营生我手气不坏——进收容所时我身无分文（财产被那帮无赖兵贩子搜刮得分文不剩），头一局同法国人打牌就赢了将近一块银元；那位输了钱压根没想到问问我付不付得出。这至少是生就一副绅士模样的好处；后来多少回，我落魄到最低谷时，都靠它赊出信用来救急。
法国人群里有一位堂堂的人物和军人，真名我们始终不知；而他的结局传开之后，在普鲁士军中掀起不小的震动。倘若美貌与勇气真是高贵的凭证——这一点我毫不怀疑（虽说贵族堆里天下最丑的嘴脸和最怕事的懦夫我都见过）——那么这位法国人必定出自法兰西最高门的世家：他的举止那样雍容高贵，他的人品那样堂皇出众。

===Original===

He was not quite so tall as myself, fair, while I am dark, and, if possible, rather broader in the shoulders. He was the only man I ever met who could master me with the small-sword; with which he would pink me four times to my three. As for the sabre, I could knock him to pieces with it; and I could leap farther and carry more than he could. This, however, is mere egotism. This Frenchman, with whom I became pretty intimate﻿—for we were the two cocks, as it were, of the depot, and neither had any feeling of low jealousy﻿—was called, for want of a better name, Le Blondin, on account of his complexion. He was not a deserter, but had come in from the Lower Rhine and the bishoprics, as I fancy; fortune having proved unfavourable to him at play probably, and other means of existence being denied him. I suspect that the Bastile was waiting for him in his own country, had he taken a fancy to return thither.
He was passionately fond of play and liquor, and thus we had a considerable sympathy together: when excited by one or the other, he became frightful. I, for my part, can bear, without wincing, both ill luck and wine; hence my advantage over him was considerable in our bouts, and I won enough money from him to make my position tenable. He had a wife outside (who, I take it, was the cause of his misfortunes and separation from his family), and she used to be admitted to see him twice or thrice a week, and never came empty-handed﻿—a little brown bright-eyed creature, whose ogles had made the greatest impression upon all the world.
This man was drafted into a regiment that was quartered at Neiss in Silesia, which is only at a short distance from the Austrian frontier; he maintained always the same character for daring and skill, and was, in the secret republic of the regiment﻿—which always exists as well as the regular military hierarchy﻿—the acknowledged leader. He was an admirable soldier, as I have said; but haughty, dissolute, and a drunkard. A man of this mark, unless he takes care to coax and flatter his officers (which I always did), is sure to fall out with them. Le Blondin’s captain was his sworn enemy, and his punishments were frequent and severe.

===Chinese===

他个子比我略矮，头发浅色，我是深色；肩膀呢，若说有分别，他倒比我更宽些。平生所遇，唯此人能在短剑上胜我：比试起来，他刺中我四剑，我才还他三剑。至于马刀，我能把他剁成碎片；跳远和负重我也胜他。不过这纯属自夸了。这位法国人同我相当亲厚——我们在收容所里好比两只斗鸡，彼此又全无卑劣的妒意——因为没别的名字，众人就着他那浅金的发色，管他叫“小金发”。他不是逃兵，我料想是从下莱茵和各主教区那头流落来的；多半是赌运不济，别的生计又断了门路。我疑心他若想回国，巴士底狱正在老家候着他。
他狂爱赌和酒，这一点我们气味相投：两样里随便哪样上了头，他就变得狰狞可怕。至于我，霉运和酒都撼不动我；因此交起手来我大占上风，从他身上赢的钱，足够我站稳脚跟。他在外面有个妻子（我揣度，他的倒霉和同家里的决裂，正是拜她所赐）；她每星期获准来探两三回，从不空手来——一个棕色皮肤、眼睛发亮的小巧娘们儿，那媚眼抛得天下轰动。
此人后来拨入驻在西里西亚奈斯的团队，那儿离奥地利边境不远；他胆大艺高的名声始终不减，在团队的秘密共和国里——每个团队除了正式的军阶建制，总有这么一个秘密共和国——他是公认的首领。如前所说，他是出色的军人；却又傲慢、放荡、贪杯。这路人，除非小心讨好奉承军官（这一层我一向做得很到家），必同他们闹翻。小金发的上尉是他的死敌，他的刑罚挨得又勤又重。

===Original===

His wife and the women of the regiment (this was after the peace) used to carry on a little commerce of smuggling across the Austrian frontier, where their dealings were winked at by both parties; and in obedience to the instructions of her husband, this woman, from every one of her excursions, would bring in a little powder and ball: commodities which are not to be procured by the Prussian soldier, and which were stowed away in secret till wanted. They *were* to be wanted, and that soon.
Le Blondin had organised a great and extraordinary conspiracy. We don’t know how far it went, how many hundreds or thousands it embraced; but strange were the stories told about the plot amongst us privates: for the news was spread from garrison to garrison, and talked of by the army, in spite of all the Government efforts to hush it up﻿—hush it up, indeed! I have been of the people myself; I have seen the Irish rebellion, and I know what is the freemasonry of the poor.
He made himself the head of the plot. There were no writings nor papers. No single one of the conspirators communicated with any other than the Frenchman; but personally he gave his orders to them all. He had arranged matters for a general rising of the garrison, at twelve o’clock on a certain day: the guardhouses in the town were to be seized, the sentinels cut down, and﻿—who knows the rest? Some of our people used to say that the conspiracy was spread through all Silesia, and that Le Blondin was to be made a general in the Austrian service.
At twelve o’clock, and opposite the guardhouse by the Böhmer-Thor of Neiss, some thirty men were lounging about in their undress, and the Frenchman stood near the sentinel of the guardhouse, sharpening a wood hatchet on a stone. At the stroke of twelve, he got up, split open the sentinel’s head with a blow of his axe, and the thirty men, rushing into the guardhouse, took possession of the arms there, and marched at once to the gate. The sentry there tried to drop the bar, but the Frenchman rushed up to him, and, with another blow of the axe, cut off his right hand, with which he held the chain. Seeing the men rushing out armed, the guard without the gate drew up across the road to prevent their passage; but the Frenchman’s thirty gave them a volley, charged them with the bayonet, and brought down several, and the rest flying, the thirty rushed on.

===Chinese===

他的妻子和团队的女人们（这是讲和以后的事）常年在奥地利边境做点走私小买卖，两边都睁一只眼闭一只眼；这女人遵照丈夫的嘱咐，每趟出门都捎回一点火药和弹丸：这两样东西普鲁士士兵是弄不到的，都秘密囤着，以备不时之需。而这“不时”，来得很快。
小金发组织了一场惊天动地的大阴谋。它蔓延多广，裹挟了几百几千人，我们无从知道；但这些故事在我们列兵中间传得神乎其神：消息从一个驻地传到另一个驻地，全军都在议论——任凭政府如何遮掩——遮掩得了吗！我自己就当过下等人；我见过爱尔兰的叛乱，我懂得穷人间那套同会般的暗号。
阴谋的首脑就是他本人。没有文书，没有字纸。密谋者彼此互不相通，只各自同那法国人单线联络；号令全由他亲口传达。他把一切都布置好，只等某个日子的正午十二点，全城驻军一齐举事：占各处卫兵室，砍倒哨兵，然后——后面的事谁说得清呢？我们中间有人常说，那阴谋遍布整个西里西亚，还说小金发将在奥军里受衔将军。
十二点，在奈斯波希米亚门旁的卫兵室对面，三十来个人穿着便装东游西荡，那法国人站在卫兵室哨兵近旁，在石头上磨一把劈柴斧。十二点钟声一响，他起身一斧劈开哨兵的脑袋；三十条汉子冲进卫兵室，夺了里面的枪械，直奔城门。门上的哨兵要放闸杆，法国人扑上去，又是一斧，剁下他握着铁链的右手。门外的守卫见人们持械冲出，横列路上阻拦；那三十人一排枪，接着端刺刀冲锋，搠倒几个，其余的跑了，三十人继续向前。

===Original===

The frontier is only a league from Neiss, and they made rapidly towards it.
But the alarm was given in the town, and what saved it was that the clock by which the Frenchman went was a quarter of an hour faster than any of the clocks in the town. The generale was beat, the troops called to arms, and thus the men who were to have attacked the other guardhouses, were obliged to fall into the ranks, and their project was defeated. This, however, likewise rendered the discovery of the conspirators impossible, for no man could betray his comrade, nor, of course, would he criminate himself.
Cavalry was sent in pursuit of the Frenchman and his thirty fugitives, who were, by this time, far on their way to the Bohemian frontier. When the horse came up with them, they turned, received them with a volley and the bayonet, and drove them back. The Austrians were out at the barriers, looking eagerly on at the conflict. The women, who were on the lookout too, brought more ammunition to these intrepid deserters, and they engaged and drove back the dragoons several times. But in these gallant and fruitless combats much time was lost, and a battalion presently came up, and surrounded the brave thirty; when the fate of the poor fellows was decided. They fought with the fury of despair: not one of them asked for quarter. When their ammunition failed, they fought with the steel, and were shot down or bayoneted where they stood. The Frenchman was the very last man who was hit. He received a bullet in the thigh, and fell, and in this state was overpowered, killing the officer who first advanced to seize him.
He and the very few of his comrades who survived were carried back to Neiss, and immediately, as the ringleader, he was brought before a council of war. He refused all interrogations which were made as to his real name and family. “What matters who I am?” said he; “you have me and will shoot me. My name would not save me were it ever so famous.” In the same way he declined to make a single discovery regarding the plot. “It was all my doing,” he said; “each man engaged in it only knew me, and is ignorant of every one of his comrades. The secret is mine alone, and the secret shall die with me.” When the officers asked him what was the reason which induced him to meditate a crime so horrible?﻿—“It was your infernal brutality and tyranny,” he said.

===Chinese===

边境离奈斯只有一里格，他们飞快地朝那儿赶。
可城里的警钟响了；救了这座城的，是那法国人对时的钟，比城里所有的钟快上一刻钟。集合鼓敲响，军队紧急集合；本来该去攻打其余卫兵室的人，只得归队站排，计划就此落空。不过这一来，密谋者也再无从查起：没人能出卖同伴，当然更没人肯把自己搭进去。
骑兵奉命追击那法国人和他的三十名逃亡者；此时他们已在奔赴波希米亚边境的大道上走出去老远。骑兵追上，他们回身应战，一排枪加刺刀，把骑兵打了回去。奥地利人涌到关卡边，热切地观战。女人们也在守望，给这些无畏的逃亡者送来更多弹药；他们接战好几回，回回都把龙骑兵击退。可这些英勇而徒劳的战斗耗掉了太多时间；不一会儿一个营赶到，把勇敢的三十人团团围住；可怜人的命运就此定了。他们以绝望的狂怒作战：没有一个人讨饶。弹药打光了，就用铁刃拼杀，一个个原地中弹倒下，或被刺刀搝穿。那法国人是最后一个挂彩的。一颗子弹打中他的大腿，他倒下了，纵然如此，还结果了头一个上前捉他的军官，才被制服。
他同极少数活下来的伙伴被抬回奈斯；作为首犯，他立即被提交军事法庭。对真名和家世的盘问，他一概拒绝。“我是谁，有什么要紧？”他说，“人已落在你们手里，你们要枪毙我。名字再响亮，也救不了我。”关于阴谋，他也同样一字不吐。“那全是我一个人干的，”他说，“参与者各自只认得我，对任何一个同伴都一无所知。秘密只属于我一人，也将随我一同死去。”军官们问他，何以要谋划如此骇人的罪行？——“就是你们那地狱般的残暴与专横。”他说。

===Original===

“You are all butchers, ruffians, tigers, and you owe it to the cowardice of your men that you were not murdered long ago.”
At this his captain burst into the most furious exclamations against the wounded man, and rushing up to him, struck him a blow with his fist. But Le Blondin, wounded as he was, as quick as thought seized the bayonet of one of the soldiers who supported him, and plunged it into the officer’s breast. “Scoundrel and monster,” said he, “I shall have the consolation of sending you out of the world before I die.” He was shot that day. He offered to write to the King, if the officers would agree to let his letter go sealed into the hands of the postmaster; but they feared, no doubt, that something might be said to inculpate themselves, and refused him the permission. At the next review Frederick treated them, it is said, with great severity, and rebuked them for not having granted the Frenchman his request. However, it was the King’s interest to conceal the matter, and so it was, as I have said before, hushed up﻿—so well hushed up, that a hundred thousand soldiers in the army knew it; and many’s the one of us that has drunk to the Frenchman’s memory over our wine, as a martyr for the cause of the soldier. I shall have, doubtless, some readers who will cry out at this, that I am encouraging insubordination and advocating murder. If these men had served as privates in the Prussian army from 1760 to 1765, they would not be so apt to take objection. This man destroyed two sentinels to get his liberty; how many hundreds of thousands of his own and the Austrian people did King Frederick kill because he took a fancy to Silesia? It was the accursed tyranny of the system that sharpened the axe which brained the two sentinels of Neiss: and so let officers take warning, and think twice ere they visit poor fellows with the cane.
I could tell many more stories about the army; but as, from having been a soldier myself, all my sympathies are in the ranks, no doubt my tales would be pronounced to be of an immoral tendency, and I had best, therefore, be brief.

===Chinese===

“你们全是一群屠夫、恶棍、老虎；全亏你们的兵胆小，你们才活到今天没被宰掉。”
这话惹得他的上尉冲那伤者破口大骂，扑上去一拳打来。可小金发虽带着重伤，迅雷般夺过身边扶他的士兵的刺刀，扎进了那军官的胸膛。“恶棍，怪物，”他说，“我死之前，还赶得及送你出这个世界，也算一件安慰。”
当天他就被枪决了。他表示愿给国王写信，只要军官们答应让信原封交到驿站长手里；可他们无疑怕信里说出什么牵连自己的话，拒绝了他。据说，翌年阅兵时，腓特烈对他们大加训斥，责备他们不该驳回那法国人的请求。不过，把事情捂住正合国王的利益；于是如我所说，这事被捂住了——捂得那么严实，全军十万士兵个个知道；我们多少人举着酒杯，追怀那位法国人，敬他是士兵事业的一位殉道者。
无疑会有读者对此大叫大嚷，说我这是鼓动犯上、宣扬谋杀。这班先生们若在 1760 到 1765 年间当过普鲁士军的列兵，就不会这么轻易表示反对了。此人为求自由杀了两个哨兵；腓特烈国王只因看上了西里西亚，又屠了他本国和奥地利的几十万生灵？磨快那把劈开奈斯两个哨兵脑袋的斧头的，正是那制度万恶的暴虐：愿军官们引以为戒，举藤条打可怜人之前，三思而后行。
关于这支军队，我还能讲许多故事；只是我自己当过兵，同情全在行伍这边，我讲的必定要被判为伤风败俗，所以长话短说为妙。

===Original===

Fancy my surprise while in this depot, when one day a well-known voice saluted my ear, and I heard a meagre young gentleman, who was brought in by a couple of troopers and received a few cuts across the shoulders from one of them, say in the best English, “You infernal *wascal*, I’ll be *w*evenged for this. I’ll *wite* to my ambassador, as sure as my name’s Fakenham of Fakenham.” I burst out laughing at this: it was my old acquaintance in *my* corporal’s coat. Lischen had sworn stoutly, that he was really and truly the private, and the poor fellow had been drafted off, and was to be made one of us. But I bear no malice, and having made the whole room roar with the story of the way in which I had tricked the poor lad, I gave him a piece of advice, which procured him his liberty. “Go to the inspecting officer,” said I; “if they once get you into Prussia it is all over with you, and they will never give you up. Go now to the commandant of the depot, promise him a hundred﻿—five hundred guineas to set you free; say that the crimping captain has your papers and portfolio” (this was true); “above all, show him that you have the means of paying him the promised money, and I will warrant you are set free.” He did as I advised, and when we were put on the march Mr. Fakenham found means to be allowed to go into hospital, and while in hospital the matter was arranged as I had recommended. He had nearly, however, missed his freedom by his own stinginess in bargaining for it, and never showed the least gratitude towards me his benefactor.
I am not going to give any romantic narrative of the Seven Years’ War. At the close of it, the Prussian army, so renowned for its disciplined valour, was officered and under-officered by native Prussians, it is true; but was composed for the most part of men hired or stolen, like myself, from almost every nation in Europe. The deserting to and fro was prodigious. In my regiment (Bülow’s) alone before the war, there had been no less than 600 Frenchmen, and as they marched out of Berlin for the campaign, one of the fellows had an old fiddle on which he was flaying a French tune, and his comrades danced almost, rather than walked, after him, singing, “*Nous allons en France.*” Two years after, when they returned to Berlin, there were only six of these men left; the rest had fled or were killed in action.

===Chinese===

请想我在收容所里有多惊讶：一天，一个熟悉的声音传入耳中——只见一位瘦削的少年老爷被两名骑兵押了进来，其中一个还朝他肩背抽了几鞭，他用一口顶地道的英语嚷道：“你这该死的*混仗*，这仇我非报不可，我要给我的大使*写姓*告状，我名唤法肯汉的法肯汉，半点不假。”我放声大笑：这正是穿走*我*那身下士军装的旧相识。利卿曾一口咬死，他就是那个货真价实的列兵；这可怜虫就这样被拨了来，要编进我们中间。不过我不记仇；我把捉弄这可怜少年的经过讲得满屋哄笑之后，送了他一条主意，替他挣回了自由。“去找视察军官，”我说，“他们一旦把你弄进普鲁士本土，你就完了，再也放不出来。现在就去见收容所的指挥官，许他一百——五百几尼放你走；就说抓你的那个兵贩子上尉扣着你的证件和皮包”（这是实话）；“最要紧的，让他看见你拿得出许下的那笔钱，我保你脱身。”他依计而行；开拔的时候，法肯汉先生设法获准进了医院，人住着医院，事情就照我出的主意办妥了。只是他讲价时那股抠门劲儿险些误了自由；而且对恩人我，他从头到尾没露过一丝感激。
我不打算把七年战争写成什么传奇。到战争结束时，这支以纪律严明、剽勇善战闻名天下的普鲁士军队，军官和军士固然是普鲁士本邦人；兵员却大半同我一样，是从欧洲几乎每个民族雇来或偷来的。开小差来来去去的多得惊人。单说我那个团（比洛团），战前法国人不下六百；开拔出柏林上前线那天，有个家伙把一把旧提琴锯着一支法国曲子，同伴们跟在他后头几乎不是走而是跳，齐声唱着“*我们回法兰西去。*”两年后回柏林，这些人只剩六个；其余的不是逃了，就是阵亡了。

===Original===

The life the private soldier led was a frightful one to any but men of iron courage and endurance. There was a corporal to every three men, marching behind them, and pitilessly using the cane; so much so that it used to be said that in action there was a front rank of privates and a second rank of sergeants and corporals to drive them on. Many men would give way to the most frightful acts of despair under these incessant persecutions and tortures; and amongst several regiments of the army a horrible practice had sprung up, which for some time caused the greatest alarm to the Government. This was a strange frightful custom of *child-murder*. The men used to say that life was unbearable, that suicide was a crime; in order to avert which, and to finish with the intolerable misery of their position, the best plan was to kill a young child, which was innocent, and therefore secure of heaven, and then to deliver themselves up as guilty of the murder. The King himself﻿—the hero, sage, and philosopher, the prince who had always liberality on his lips and who affected a horror of capital punishments﻿—was frightened at this dreadful protest, on the part of the wretches whom he had kidnapped, against his monstrous tyranny; but his only means of remedying the evil was strictly to forbid that such criminals should be attended by any ecclesiastic whatever, and denied all religious consolation.
The punishment was incessant. Every officer had the liberty to inflict it, and in peace it was more cruel than in war. For when peace came the King turned adrift such of his officers as were not noble; whatever their services might have been. He would call a captain to the front of his company and say, “He is not noble, let him go.” We were afraid of him somehow, and were cowed before him like wild beasts before their keeper. I have seen the bravest men of the army cry like children at a cut of the cane; I have seen a little ensign of fifteen call out a man of fifty from the ranks, a man who had been in a hundred battles, and he has stood presenting arms, and sobbing and howling like a baby, while the young wretch lashed him over the arms and thighs with the stick. In a day of action this man would dare anything. A button might be awry *then* and nobody touched him; but when they had made the brute fight, then they lashed him again into subordination.

===Chinese===

列兵过的日子，除了钢筋铁骨的好汉，谁过谁怕。每三个兵配一个下士，跟在身后，抡藤条毫不留情；以至于人们常说：打仗时前排是列兵，第二排是逼着他们往前的中士和下士。在这无休无止的凌虐酷刑之下，多少人干出最骇人的绝望勾当；全军好几个团队里竟生出一种可怕的习俗，一时令政府大为惊恐。那便是一种离奇可怖的*杀童*习俗。兵们常说：这日子没法过；自杀又是罪；为躲开这罪、又了断那难堪的苦楚，最好的法子是杀一个幼童——孩子无辜，稳进天堂——然后自首服罪。连国王本人——那位英雄、圣哲、哲学家，那位把开明挂在嘴边、对极刑佯作深恶痛绝的君王——也被这可怕的抗议吓着了：抗议来自他被绑架来的苦命人，矛头直指他的暴政；而他唯一的补救之道，是严令此类罪犯不得有任何教士探视，剥夺其一切宗教慰藉。
鞭打从不消停。每个军官都有权动手，太平年月比战时更狠。因为太平一到来，国王就把军中非贵族出身的军官扫地出门，不管他们立过什么功。他会把一名上尉叫到全连队列前，说：“此人非贵族，叫他走。”不知怎么，我们怕他，在他面前发怵，像野兽见了饲养员。我见过全军最勇的汉子挨一藤条哭得像孩子；我见过一个十五岁的小旗手把五十岁的老兵从队列里叫出来——身经百战的人——笔直举着枪立正，呜呜咽咽哭得像娃娃，任那小畜生用棍子抽他的胳膊和大腿。打起仗来，这人什么都敢。那时候一颗扣子歪了*也*没人碰他；可等他们驱使这头牲口打完仗，又用鞭子把他抽回服从里去。

===Original===

Almost all of us yielded to the spell﻿—scarce one could break it. The French officer I have spoken of as taken along with me, was in my company, and caned like a dog. I met him at Versailles twenty years afterwards, and he turned quite pale and sick when I spoke to him of old days. “For God’s sake,” said he, “don’t talk of that time: I wake up from my sleep trembling and crying even now.”
As for me, after a very brief time (in which it must be confessed I tasted, like my comrades, of the cane) and after I had found opportunities to show myself to be a brave and dexterous soldier, I took the means I had adopted in the English army to prevent any further personal degradation. I wore a bullet around my neck, which I did not take the pains to conceal, and I gave out that it should be for the man or officer who caused me to be chastised. And there was something in my character which made my superiors believe me; for that bullet had already served me to kill an Austrian colonel, and I would have given it to a Prussian with as little remorse. For what cared I for their quarrels, or whether the eagle under which I marched had one head or two? All I said was, “No man shall find me tripping in my duty; but no man shall ever lay a hand upon me.” And by this maxim I abided as long as I remained in the service.
I do not intend to make a history of battles in the Prussian any more than in the English service. I did my duty in them as well as another, and by the time that my moustache had grown to a decent length, which it did when I was twenty years of age, there was not a braver, cleverer, handsomer, and I must own, wickeder soldier in the Prussian army. I had formed myself to the condition of the proper fighting beast; on a day of action I was savage and happy; out of the field I took all the pleasure I could get, and was by no means delicate as to its quality or the manner of procuring it. The truth is, however, that there was among our men a much higher tone of society than among the clumsy louts in the English army, and our service was generally so strict that we had little time for doing mischief. I am very dark and swarthy in complexion, and was called by our fellows the “Black Englander,” the “Schwartzer Englander,” or the English Devil. If any service was to be done, I was sure to be put upon it.

===Chinese===

我们几乎人人都折服在这魔咒之下——能挣破的没有几个。我说过同我一道被掳来的那位法国军官，就在我连里，挨藤条像条狗。二十年后我在凡尔赛遇见他，一提当年，他脸都白了，直犯恶心。“看在上帝份上，”他说，“别提那段日子：直到如今，我从睡里惊醒，还会发抖、还会哭。”
至于我，过了一小段日子（得承认，同伙伴们一样，藤条的滋味我也尝过），又寻着机会显出自己是个又勇又利落的兵之后，便用上了当年在英国军队里的法子，免得人格再受糟践。我脖子上挂一颗子弹，毫不遮掩，并放出话去：谁——兵也好军官也好——害我挨罚，这颗子弹就是谁的。而我这个人身上自有一股什么，叫上司们信得过：那颗子弹先前已替我结果过一名奥地利上校，送给一个普鲁士人，我也绝不手软。他们的争端关我何事？我旗下的鹰徽一个头还是两个头，与我何干？我只有一句话：“职守之内，无人能挑我的错；但任何人也休想碰我一指头。”只要还在军中一天，我就守着这条格言一天。
在普鲁士军中同在英国军中一样，我不打算写一部战史。仗里我尽职尽责不输旁人；等到我唇髭蓄到体面的长度——那是我二十岁上——普鲁士全军里，再找不出比我更勇、更精、更俊、也得承认——更坏的兵了。我已把自己修炼成一头合格的斗兽：临阵之日凶悍而快活；下了战场，能捞的乐子照单全收，至于成色如何、来路怎样，一概不讲究。不过实情是，我们这帮人中间的风气，比英国军里那帮粗坯高出许多；而且军纪一般严得厉害，作恶的时间都挤不出。我肤色黝黑，伙伴们管我叫“黑英国佬”，或者英国魔鬼。有什么差事要办，必定落在我头上。

===Original===

I got frequent gratifications of money, but no promotion; and it was on the day after I had killed the Austrian colonel (a great officer of Uhlans, whom I engaged﻿—singly and on foot) that General Bülow, my colonel, gave me two Frederics-d’or in front of the regiment, and said, “I reward thee now; but I fear I shall have to hang thee one day or other.” I spent the money, and that I had taken from the colonel’s body, every groschen, that night with some jovial companions; but as long as war lasted was never without a dollar in my purse.

===Chinese===

金钱的犒赏我得了不少，升迁却没有；就在我杀了那奥地利上校（一位枪骑兵的大官，我单身步行同他交的手）的第二天，我们团长比洛将军当着全团的面赏了我两枚腓特烈金币，说：“现在我犒赏你；可我怕早晚有一天得亲手绞死你。”那笔钱，连同从上校身上取来的，当晚同几个快活朋友花了个精光，一个戈罗申不剩；不过只要战争还打着，我的钱袋里从不缺一块银元。

## XII Tragical History of Princess of X﻿—﻿—﻿ / XII X——公主的悲惨历史
===Original===

More than twenty years after the events described in the past chapters, I was walking with my Lady Lyndon in the Rotunda at Ranelagh. It was in the year 1790; the emigration from France had already commenced, the old counts and marquises were thronging to our shores: not starving and miserable, as one saw them a few years afterwards, but unmolested as yet, and bringing with them some token of their national splendour. I was walking with Lady Lyndon, who, proverbially jealous and always anxious to annoy me, spied out a foreign lady who was evidently remarking me, and of course asked who was the hideous fat Dutchwoman who was leering at me so? I knew her not in the least. I felt I had seen the lady’s face somewhere (it was now, as my wife said, enormously fat and bloated); but I did not recognise in the bearer of that face one who had been among the most beautiful women in Germany in her day.
It was no other than Madame de Liliengarten, the mistress, or as some said the morganatic wife, of the old Duke of X﻿—﻿—, Duke Victor’s father. She had left X﻿—﻿— a few months after the elder Duke’s demise, had gone to Paris, as I heard, where some unprincipled adventurer had married her for her money; but, however, had always retained her quasi-royal title, and pretended, amidst the great laughter of the Parisians who frequented her house, to the honours and ceremonial of a sovereign’s widow. She had a throne erected in her stateroom, and was styled by her servants and those who wished to pay court to her, or borrow money from her, “Altesse.” Report said she drank rather copiously﻿—certainly her face bore every mark of that habit, and had lost the rosy, frank, good-humoured beauty which had charmed the sovereign who had ennobled her.
Although she did not address me in the circle at Ranelagh, I was at this period as well known as the Prince of Wales, and she had no difficulty in finding my house in Berkeley Square; whither a note was next morning despatched to me. “An old friend of Monsieur de Balibari,” it stated (in extremely bad French), “is anxious to see the Chevalier again and to talk over old happy times. Rosina de Liliengarten (can it be that Redmond Balibari has forgotten her?) will be at her house in Leicester Fields all the morning, looking for one who would never have passed her by *twenty years* ago.”

===Chinese===

前几章所述的那些事，一晃过去二十多年，一日，我正陪林登伯爵夫人在拉尼拉格（Ranelagh）的圆形大厅里散步。其时是1790年；法兰西的流亡潮已经开场，老伯爵们、老侯爵们正成群结队涌向我们的海岸：还不像几年后人们看到的那样饥寒交迫、狼狈不堪，而是尚无人惊扰，还随身带着几分本国的富贵气派。我正同林登伯爵夫人挽臂而行——她那妒忌的性子远近闻名，总巴望惹我不痛快——一眼认出一位外国太太正明摆着打量我，便理所当然地问：那个冲我挤眉弄眼的、奇丑的荷兰胖女人是谁？我压根儿不认得她。只觉得那张脸在哪儿见过（如今，照我太太的说法，是肥得吓人、肿得发亮）；可在这张脸的主人身上，我全然认不出当年德意志数一数二的美人。
她不是别人，正是莉莲嘉登夫人（Madame de Liliengarten）——老X——公爵、即维克托公爵之父的情妇，也有人说，是贵贱通婚的妻子。老公爵薨后几个月，她离开了X——，据我后来听说，去了巴黎，叫一个没廉耻的冒险家图她的钱财娶了去；不过她始终保留着那份半王家头衔，在常出入她府上的巴黎人的哄堂大笑声中，端着君王遗孀的仪注与尊荣。她在正厅里设了一座宝座，仆役们，以及那些想向她献媚、或是想向她借钱的，都称她“殿下”（Altesse）。传闻说她酒喝得很凶——她那张脸也确实处处带着这个习惯的印记，早已失去当年那种红润、坦率、欢畅的美色——正是那美色，曾迷住给她授爵的君王。
虽然在拉尼拉格的圈子里她没有上前同我搭话，但我在这个时期的名头，同威尔士亲王不相上下，她毫不费事便打听到了我在伯克利广场的宅邸；第二天一早，一张便条便递到了我府上。“德·巴利巴里先生（Monsieur de Balibari）的一位老朋友，”便条上写道（用的是极其蹩脚的法文），“渴望再见骑士一面，叙叙当年快活的时光。罗西娜·德·莉莲嘉登（Rosina de Liliengarten）（莫非雷德蒙·巴利巴里已把她忘了？）整个上午都在莱斯特菲尔兹（Leicester Fields）家中守候，等一位*二十年前*绝不会与她擦肩而过的人。”

===Original===

Rosina of Liliengarten it was indeed﻿—such a full-blown Rosina I have seldom seen. I found her in a decent first-floor in Leicester Fields (the poor soul fell much lower afterwards) drinking tea, which had somehow a very strong smell of brandy in it; and after salutations, which would be more tedious to recount than they were to perform, and after further straggling conversation, she gave me briefly the following narrative of the events in X﻿—﻿—, which I may well entitle the “Princess’s Tragedy.”
“You remember Monsieur de Geldern, the Police Minister. He was of Dutch extraction, and, what is more, of a family of Dutch Jews. Although everybody was aware of this blot in his scutcheon, he was mortally angry if ever his origin was suspected; and made up for his fathers’ errors by outrageous professions of religion, and the most austere practices of devotion. He visited church every morning, confessed once a week, and hated Jews and Protestants as much as an inquisitor could do. He never lost an opportunity of proving his sincerity, by persecuting one or the other whenever occasion fell in his way.
“He hated the Princess mortally; for her Highness in some whim had insulted him with his origin, caused pork to be removed from before him at table, or injured him in some such silly way; and he had a violent animosity to the old Baron de Magny, both in his capacity of Protestant, and because the latter in some haughty mood had publicly turned his back upon him as a sharper and a spy. Perpetual quarrels were taking place between them in council; where it was only the presence of his august masters that restrained the Baron from publicly and frequently expressing the contempt which he felt for the officer of police.
“Thus Geldern had hatred as one reason for ruining the Princess, and it is my belief he had a stronger motive still﻿—interest. You remember whom the Duke married, after the death of his first wife?﻿—a princess of the house of F﻿—﻿—.
